\hypertarget{reading-this-mathematical-appendix}{%
\section{Reading this mathematical
appendix}\label{reading-this-mathematical-appendix}}

This appendix is an integral mathematical part of the article, intended
for refereeing with it. It contains the longer corner arguments used by
the direct fifteen-source proof. The alternative certificate proof,
executable audits, and Lean implementation are described separately.

\textbf{Editorial status.} This is a consolidation draft. The arguments
have been collected and cross-linked from the proof-development notes. A
final editorial pass is still needed to shorten repetitions and
standardise all local notation. No claim is made that this appendix
follows the existing Lean proof route.

A directed corner \(ab\) abbreviates the complementary cyclic
tricoloured corner. \(C(S)=S\to V\setminus S\), where
\(V=\{0,1,2,3,4\}\), is an adjacent class. Its pole pair is the
two-element side, and a repeated colour lies outside that pair. An
opposite variety supplies both directions of the edges of a four-cycle.
Every multiplicity refers to physical source copies. Hall matchings
select distinct copies; repeated entries assigned to one variety use
separate copies. An \emph{own loop} in the arguments below is the
article's \emph{own completion}: a matching of the six tricoloured
corners after two class copies are reserved.

Arguments are ordered so that the recorded dependencies precede their
uses. This includes explicit file references and the implicit
dependencies reviewed in the bounded simplification pass; it is not a
certification that every implicit dependency has been found. Internal
divisions retain local numbers: ``Section 3'' means local division 3 of
that argument unless another argument is named. Links to another
argument replace the research filenames. The prescribed-family
eight-plus-three lemma is proved in the main article's shared geometric
tools, independently of the corner threshold.

The proof uses mathematical case exhaustion and the displayed Hall
tables. Programs used to discover or audit these tables are not premises
of the proof.

The local names \(O_{60},\ldots,O_{74}\) (also printed O60, etc.) denote
the following bidirected four-cycles. Each row means all edges joining
the two displayed pairs; the remaining colour is repeated.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrl@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Name & Repeated colour & Bipartition \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
O60 & 0 & 12 / 34 \\
O61 & 0 & 13 / 24 \\
O62 & 0 & 14 / 23 \\
O63 & 1 & 02 / 34 \\
O64 & 1 & 03 / 24 \\
O65 & 1 & 04 / 23 \\
O66 & 2 & 01 / 34 \\
O67 & 2 & 03 / 14 \\
O68 & 2 & 04 / 13 \\
O69 & 3 & 01 / 24 \\
O70 & 3 & 02 / 14 \\
O71 & 3 & 04 / 12 \\
O72 & 4 & 01 / 23 \\
O73 & 4 & 02 / 13 \\

\end{longtable}

\hypertarget{arg-1}{%
\section{Argument 1: Cut intersections on an opposite
target}\label{arg-1}}

\hypertarget{two-elementary-facts-about-adjacent-cuts-on-an-opposite-target}{%
\subsection*{1. Two elementary facts about adjacent cuts on an opposite
target}\label{two-elementary-facts-about-adjacent-cuts-on-an-opposite-target}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Two elementary facts about adjacent
cuts on an opposite target}

Fix an opposite target T. Every adjacent class meets T in two or four
directed positions, on two or four underlying edges respectively. It
never contains both directions of an edge.

At most two adjacent classes have their T-intersection inside any
specified two-position set. A two-position pattern is either a star,
which determines the class uniquely, or two opposite edges, with the
fifth colour free to join either side, which gives two classes.

At most three adjacent classes have their T-intersection inside a
specified three-position set. To see this, no four-position pattern fits
there. If two positions are the reverse directions of an edge, the only
possible two-position patterns pair the third position with one of them,
giving at most two classes. Otherwise the three positions lie on a
three-edge path in the target four-cycle. The two end edges can give one
opposite-edge pattern, with two classes. When that pattern is valid, the
middle position forms a valid directed star with at most one end, adding
at most one class. If the end-edge pattern is invalid, only the two
possible stars remain. The bound is three in all cases.

In particular, ten adjacent sources with class cap three cannot all have
their target intersections inside three positions.

\hypertarget{arg-2}{%
\section{Argument 2: Five opposite sources with two neighbouring
pairs}\label{arg-2}}

\textbf{Lemma.} Let \(P,Q\) be opposite varieties sharing four directed
corners, and let \(R\) be any other opposite variety. The sources

\[
2P+2Q+R+2A+B
\]

assemble an opposite target whenever \(A,B\) are different adjacent
tricolour classes.

Write

\[
I=P\cap Q,\qquad J=P\setminus Q,\qquad K=Q\setminus P,
\qquad T=P\mathbin\triangle Q.
\]

Here \(I,J,K\) are two-edge sets, with each edge denoting both of its
directed corners. The line targets are \(P=I\cup J\), \(Q=I\cup K\), and
\(T=J\cup K\). Their union is a bidirected \(K_4\) or \(K_{2,3}\).

\hypertarget{adjacent-restrictions-to-a-line}{%
\subsection*{1. Adjacent restrictions to a
line}\label{adjacent-restrictions-to-a-line}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Adjacent restrictions to a line}

An adjacent cut uses at most one direction of an undirected edge. It
therefore meets each of \(I,J,K\) in at most two positions. Its
intersection with any opposite target has size two or four. Consequently
its three block counts are precisely one of

\[
(1,1,1),\qquad (2,2,0)\text{ and its permutations},\qquad (2,2,2).
\]

Moreover a restriction with counts \((1,1,1)\) determines the adjacent
class uniquely. On a \(K_4\) line union its three cut edges form a
directed star; their orientation fixes the cut on the four vertices, and
the fifth colour must join the singleton side to make the side sizes two
and three. On a \(K_{2,3}\) line union a three-edge cut meets all five
vertices, so its directions determine both sides of the cut.

For \(R\) outside the line, exactly one of \(P,Q,T\) is its high target:
its intersections with the line targets have two, one and one undirected
edges respectively. We consider its high target first.

\hypertarget{r-has-high-target-p-or-q-or-rt}{%
\subsection*{2. R has high target P or Q, or
R=T}\label{r-has-high-target-p-or-q-or-rt}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. R has high target P or Q, or R=T}

Interchange \(P,Q\) if necessary to make the high target \(P\). The five
opposite sources can fill the five unreserved target corners whenever
the three holes reserved for the adjacent sources satisfy one of these
conditions:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Target & Sufficient distribution of the three holes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(P=I\cup J\) & At least one hole in \(J\) \\
\(Q=I\cup K\) & At least two holes in \(K\) \\
\(T=J\cup K\) & One hole in \(J\), two in \(K\) \\
\end{longtable}

These rules follow directly by allocating the two copies confined to one
block first. On \(P\), the two \(Q\) copies require two \(I\) positions;
\(R\) sees an edge in each of \(I,J\), and the two \(P\) copies are
universal. If exactly one hole lies in \(J\), then \(R\) and the two
\(P\) copies fill its three remaining positions. If two or three holes
lie in \(J\), its remaining positions use \(P\) copies, while \(R\)
fills an available \(I\) corner. On \(Q\), \(R\) sees one \(I\) edge,
and the two or three holes in \(K\) leave that edge available. On \(T\),
the two \(P\) copies and \(R\) fill the three remaining \(J\) positions,
and the two \(Q\) copies fill \(K\). The same three sufficient rules
hold when \(R=T\): on \(P\), assign \(R\) an available \(J\) position,
the two \(Q\) copies to \(I\), and the two universal \(P\) copies to the
remainder. The inequality on the holes leaves at least one \(J\) and two
\(I\) positions. The argument on \(Q\) is symmetric, and on \(T\) the
source \(R\) is universal.

If \(A\) has block counts \((1,1,1)\), write its corners as
\(a_I,a_J,a_K\). If \(B\) has a \(P\)-corner outside \(\{a_I,a_J\}\),
reserve those two corners for the \(A\) copies and the new corner for
\(B\); the first rule applies. Otherwise \(B\)'s \(P\)-intersection is
exactly \(\{a_I,a_J\}\), and its block counts must also be \((1,1,1)\).
If its \(K\) corner differs from \(a_K\), reserve \(a_I,a_K\) and that
new \(K\) corner on \(Q\); the second rule applies. If it does not
differ, the two classes have the same three-corner restriction and hence
are equal, contrary to the hypothesis.

If \(A\) has two corners in both \(I,J\), use target \(P\). The two
\(A\) copies can choose distinct corners, including a \(J\) corner,
while avoiding any selected \(B\) corner.

If \(A\) has counts \((2,0,2)\), use \(P\) with the two \(A\) holes in
\(I\) when \(B\) has a \(J\) corner. Otherwise \(B\) also has counts
\((2,0,2)\); use \(Q\) with the two \(A\) holes in \(K\) and a \(B\)
hole in \(I\).

Finally suppose \(A\) has counts \((0,2,2)\). If \(B\) has an \(I\)
corner, use \(P\) with the two \(A\) holes in \(J\). Otherwise \(B\)
also has counts \((0,2,2)\); use \(T\) with the two \(A\) holes in \(K\)
and a \(B\) hole in \(J\). This exhausts all adjacent block profiles.

\hypertarget{r-has-high-target-t}{%
\subsection*{3. R has high target T}\label{r-has-high-target-t}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. R has high target T}

Now \(R\) sees an edge in each of \(J,K\), and one edge in each of the
targets \(P,Q\). The following three completion rules suffice:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Target & Sufficient distribution of the three holes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(P\) & Two in \(I\), one in \(J\) \\
\(Q\) & Two in \(I\), one in \(K\) \\
\(T\) & At least one in each of \(J,K\) \\
\end{longtable}

For the first rule, the two \(Q\) copies fill the two remaining \(I\)
positions; \(R\) and the two \(P\) copies fill the three \(J\)
positions. Only one \(J\) corner has been removed, so \(R\)'s two-corner
intersection there survives. The second rule is symmetric. In the third
rule the remaining block sizes are three and two, and \(R\) joins the
doubled variety on the larger block. Again only one corner of that block
was removed.

If \(A\) has counts \((1,1,1)\), first use \(T\). The two \(A\) holes
occupy its distinct \(J,K\) corners. Any new \(B\) corner there gives
the third hole and completes the assembly. Otherwise \(B\) agrees on
both corners and has profile \((1,1,1)\). Since \(B\ne A\), uniqueness
forces a different \(I\) corner. On \(P\), use the two different \(I\)
corners and the \(A\) corner in \(J\).

If \(A\) has two corners in both \(J,K\), use \(T\), choosing one \(A\)
hole in each block while avoiding the chosen \(B\) hole. If \(A\) has
counts \((2,0,2)\), a \(B\) corner in \(J\) permits two \(A\) holes in
\(K\) on \(T\). If no such corner exists, \(B\) also has profile
\((2,0,2)\); use the two \(A\) corners in \(I\) and one \(B\) corner in
\(K\) on \(Q\). The profile \((2,2,0)\) is symmetric. All cases are now
covered.

\hypertarget{consequence}{%
\subsection*{Consequence}\label{consequence}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Consequence}

In a fourteen-pool with five diffuse opposite sources and an adjacent
class of size at least three, the opposite profile \(2,2,1\) is settled
whenever the doubled varieties share four directed corners. There are
other adjacent classes available, since the adjacent total is nine and
each class has multiplicity at most three after the four-class case is
removed. Select two copies of the triple class and one source from
another class, then apply the lemma.

\hypertarget{arg-3}{%
\section{Argument 3: Two four-cycle line normal forms}\label{arg-3}}

\hypertarget{the-six-edge-graph-is-a-line-union}{%
\subsection*{3. The six-edge graph is a line
union}\label{the-six-edge-graph-is-a-line-union}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. The six-edge graph is a line union}

Let \(P,Q\) be four-cycles sharing exactly one edge \(e\). Let disjoint
two-edge sets \(F\subset P\setminus Q\) and \(G\subset Q\setminus P\)
have six-edge complement \(L\) in \(K_5\). Suppose \(L\) contains at
least two distinct four-cycles. Two distinct four-cycles on five
vertices share at most two edges. Since their union lies in a six-edge
graph, they must share exactly two, and their union equals \(L\).

There are exactly two possibilities. If their common two edges are
disjoint, their union is \(K_4\). If their common two edges are
adjacent, their union is \(K_{2,3}\). In each case the union contains
exactly three four-cycles: the two given ones and their symmetric
difference.

The source \(P\) has exactly two edges outside \(L\), namely \(F\);
\(Q\) has the complementary two outside edges \(G\). Their two-edge
intersections with \(L\) share the edge \(e\). We now show that some
line target \(T\subset L\) contains both of those two-edge
intersections.

\hypertarget{k4-line-union}{%
\subsubsection*{K4 line union}\label{k4-line-union}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{K4 line union}

Let \(L\) be the complete graph on \(1,2,3,4\); its complement is the
star at colour 0. Since \(F,G\) partition the four star edges, relabel
so that

\[
P=\{01,02,13,23\},\qquad
Q=\{03,04,13,14\}.
\]

To justify the normal form, \(P\)'s two star edges have endpoints 1 and
2, and its fourth vertex may be called 3. The other two star edges
belong to \(Q\), whose fourth vertex is 1 or 2; exchange those two
labels if necessary. The target

\[
T=\{13,23,24,14\}
\]

contains \(P\cap L=\{13,23\}\) and \(Q\cap L=\{13,14\}\).

\hypertarget{k23-line-union}{%
\subsubsection*{K2,3 line union}\label{k23-line-union}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{K2,3 line union}

Let \(L\) have parts \(\{0,1\}\) and \(\{2,3,4\}\). Its complement
consists of edge \(01\) and triangle \(234\). Exactly one of \(F,G\),
say \(F\), contains \(01\). Relabel the three other colours so that

\[
F=\{01,23\},\qquad G=\{24,34\}.
\]

The cycle \(P\) closes the two disjoint edges of \(F\) with \(02,13\) or
\(03,12\); choose the former by exchanging 2 and 3. The cycle \(Q\)
closes the path \(2,4,3\) through colour 0 or 1. The simultaneous
exchanges \(0\leftrightarrow1\) and \(2\leftrightarrow3\) preserve
\(P\), so choose colour 0. Hence

\[
P=\{01,23,02,13\},\qquad
Q=\{24,34,02,03\}.
\]

The line target

\[
T=\{02,03,12,13\}
\]

contains both \(P\cap L=\{02,13\}\) and \(Q\cap L=\{02,03\}\).

\hypertarget{arg-4}{%
\section{Argument 4: Six diffuse opposite cubes and three cubes in one
adjacent class}\label{arg-4}}

\textbf{Lemma.} Six opposite-repeat sources, with no opposite variety
occurring more than twice, together with three sources from one adjacent
tricolour class, always assemble an opposite target. Thus nine sources
suffice.

The repeated colours of the three adjacent sources are irrelevant.

\hypertarget{the-two-by-three-grid}{%
\subsection*{1. The two-by-three grid}\label{the-two-by-three-grid}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. The two-by-three grid}

Normalize the adjacent class to

\[
A=\{0,1\}\longrightarrow\{2,3,4\}.
\]

Its six directed corners are a grid with two rows and three columns.
Each opposite source meets this grid in one of the following fifteen
patterns:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  two positions in different columns, one position in each; there are
  twelve such patterns;
\item
  a complete two-column rectangle; there are three such patterns.
\end{itemize}

Each pattern determines the opposite variety uniquely. In particular
every pattern meets exactly two columns. An opposite source has the
reversed versions of the same corners as well.

Let \(r(X)\) be the maximum number of corners in a grid subset \(X\)
that can be assigned to different opposite sources. This is the rank
function of a transversal matroid.

If some two-column rectangle has rank four, assign four opposite sources
to its four reverse corners. Any unused opposite source meets the
forward rectangle: it meets two of the three columns, so at least one of
the selected two. That source and the three \(A\) sources fill the four
forward positions. We have an opposite assembly.

We may therefore suppose that all three two-column rectangles have rank
at most three. If the columns are \(X,Y,Z\), submodularity gives

\[
2r(X\cup Y\cup Z)
\le r(X\cup Y)+r(X\cup Z)+r(Y\cup Z)\le9.
\]

Consequently the six-corner grid has matching rank at most four.

\hypertarget{a-hall-cover-with-at-most-four-positions}{%
\subsection*{2. A Hall cover with at most four
positions}\label{a-hall-cover-with-at-most-four-positions}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. A Hall cover with at most four
positions}

By the matching-rank form of Hall's theorem, there is a grid subset
\(H\) such that

\[
|H|+\#\{\text{opposite sources whose pattern is not contained in }H\}
\le4.
\]

We may take \(|H|\le4\). A set of at most one position contains no
opposite pattern. A two-position set contains at most one variety, hence
at most two of the six sources. Those cases contradict the displayed
inequality. Thus only the following possibilities remain:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(|H|=3\), and at least five of the six sources have patterns in
  \(H\);
\item
  \(|H|=4\), and all six source patterns lie in \(H\).
\end{enumerate}

\hypertarget{three-positions}{%
\subsubsection*{Three positions}\label{three-positions}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Three positions}

If the three positions use only two columns, they contain just two
opposite patterns, allowing at most four sources. Hence they occupy
three different columns. Their three two-position subsets are the only
available patterns.

The corresponding three opposite varieties share four corners pairwise.
This can be seen by closing each two-edge path or matching to its unique
opposite four-cycle; the three cycles form one of the lines described in
the neighbouring-pairs lemma. Equivalently, up to row exchange and
column permutations the grid positions are either \(02,03,04\) or
\(02,03,14\). The varieties in the first case are \(O63,O64,O65\), and
in the second case \(O65,O68,O71\); each pair has four corners in
common.

At least five copies among these three varieties, each with capacity
two, force two doubled varieties. These are neighbouring opposite
varieties. The other two opposite sources and any two of the adjacent
sources now give the neighbouring-pairs construction.

\hypertarget{four-positions-in-two-columns}{%
\subsubsection*{Four positions in two
columns}\label{four-positions-in-two-columns}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Four positions in two columns}

Here \(H\) is a complete rectangle. Its six opposite sources must match
all four positions. To see this directly, an absent position would
confine all six sources to the other three positions, which contain only
two varieties and hence at most four sources. A deficient pair of
positions would confine at least five sources to the other two, which
contain at most one variety and hence two sources. A deficient triple
would confine at least four sources to one position, which contains no
pattern. Hall's remaining inequality is immediate.

This contradicts our assumption that every rectangle has rank at most
three.

\hypertarget{four-positions-in-all-three-columns}{%
\subsection*{3. Four positions in all three
columns}\label{four-positions-in-all-three-columns}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Four positions in all three
columns}

The remaining shape has two positions in one column and one in each of
the other columns. It contains exactly five opposite patterns: the five
two-position pairs lying in different columns. Let \(T\) be the opposite
variety determined by the two singleton-column positions.

Two elementary facts about these five varieties finish the proof:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  each of them shares at least two undirected corner edges with \(T\);
\item
  each undirected edge of \(T\) is absent from exactly two of the five
  varieties.
\end{itemize}

There are only two grid shapes up to row exchange and column
permutation. The following table verifies both facts explicitly. An
undirected edge denotes its two directed corners. The last column lists,
for each edge of \(T\), the three varieties containing it.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Grid positions \(H\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Five varieties
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Central target \(T\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Edge supports within the five varieties
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(02,12,03,04\) & \(63,64,65,70,73\) & \(63=\{03,04,23,24\}\) &
\(03:63,65,73;\quad04:63,64,70;\quad23:63,64,73;\quad24:63,65,70\) \\
\(02,12,03,14\) & \(61,65,68,71,73\) & \(68=\{01,03,14,34\}\) &
\(01:68,71,73;\quad03:65,68,73;\quad14:61,68,71;\quad34:61,65,68\) \\
\end{longtable}

For self-contained decoding, the varieties appearing in this table have
the following undirected edge sets:

\[
\begin{array}{c|l@{\qquad}c|l}
61&12,14,23,34&63&03,04,23,24\\
64&02,04,23,34&65&02,03,24,34\\
68&01,03,14,34&70&01,04,12,24\\
71&01,02,14,24&73&01,03,12,23.
\end{array}
\]

Since there are six opposite sources and each variety occurs at most
twice, the two varieties missing any given target edge account for at
most four sources. Every edge of \(T\) therefore has at least two source
copies available. Each source meets at least two edges of \(T\). The
robust-six criterion from the neighbouring-pairs note applies: the six
sources can fill any six of its eight corners.

Two adjacent sources can choose distinct compatible corners of \(T\),
since each has at least two. Fill the remaining six corners with the
opposite sources. This proves the lemma.

\hypertarget{consequences-and-an-independent-check}{%
\subsection*{Consequences and an independent
check}\label{consequences-and-an-independent-check}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Consequences and an independent check}

In the diffuse fourteen-source problem, a pool with at least six
opposite sources and an adjacent class of multiplicity at least three is
soluble. Thus, after this lemma, any remaining failure with six or more
opposite sources has every adjacent class of size at most two.

The standalone checker \texttt{sixO\_threeA.cpp} independently checks
every six-opposite inventory with multiplicity at most two against a
fixed adjacent class. Colour permutation and reversal cover all classes.
Its output is:

\begin{verbatim}
Checked 28665; no exception
\end{verbatim}

This check is a verification of the statement, separate from the
elementary proof above.

\hypertarget{arg-5}{%
\section{Argument 5: Five opposite sources with two nonneighbouring
pairs}\label{arg-5}}

\textbf{Lemma.} A fourteen-source pool with exactly five opposite
sources, no opposite variety occurring more than twice, and an adjacent
class of multiplicity at least three is soluble.

We may assume every adjacent class has at most three copies, using the
separately established four-class lemma. The preceding two notes settle
the cases with at most one doubled opposite variety, and with two
doubled varieties sharing four corners. It remains to handle

\[
2P+2Q+C,
\]

where \(P,Q\) share only one undirected edge and \(C\ne P,Q\). There are
nine adjacent sources in the pool, each class occurring at most three
times. Normalize a triple class to \(A=C(01)\).

\hypertarget{two-possible-grid-shapes}{%
\subsection*{1. Two possible grid
shapes}\label{two-possible-grid-shapes}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Two possible grid shapes}

If the opposite sources match all four corners of a two-column rectangle
of the \(A\) grid, four opposite sources fill its reverse, and the fifth
opposite source plus three \(A\) fill its forward corners. Otherwise the
three rectangle ranks are at most three. As before, the full grid has
rank at most four, and Hall gives a cover \(H\) of size three or four,
with at most one source outside a three-position cover and none outside
a four-position cover.

Both doubled source patterns must lie in \(H\): putting one of them
outside would put two source copies outside. Neither doubled pattern is
a whole rectangle. Such a rectangle would force \(H\) to be that
rectangle; five capped opposite sources on it match all four positions.
Thus the two patterns each contain two grid positions.

If they share a position, their union has three positions. These cannot
occupy all three columns: the three two-position patterns on one
position per column form a line of pairwise neighbouring varieties.
Since \(P,Q\) are nonneighbouring, the union has column sizes \(2,1\).

If the patterns are disjoint, their four positions cannot form a
two-column rectangle for the same matching reason. Their column sizes
are \(2,1,1\), and \(C\)'s pattern must also lie in those four
positions. These are the only two shapes.

\hypertarget{overlapping-patterns}{%
\subsection*{2. Overlapping patterns}\label{overlapping-patterns}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Overlapping patterns}

Normalize the patterns to

\[
P\cap A=\{02,03\},\qquad Q\cap A=\{12,03\}.
\]

The opposite varieties are

\[
P=O65=\{02,03,24,34\},\qquad
Q=O73=\{01,03,12,23\},
\]

where undirected edges denote both corner orientations.

If \(C\) contains edge \(13\), its corner \(13\), one \(P\) corner
\(02\), one \(Q\) corner \(12\), and another \(P\) or \(Q\) corner
\(03\) match the rectangle on columns 2 and 3. We are done.

Otherwise \(C\) contains edge \(04\) or \(14\). Indeed the three
forbidden edges \(13,14,04\) form a four-vertex path. Exactly two
four-cycles on five vertices avoid that path: they omit one or the other
internal vertex and are precisely \(P,Q\). Omitting an endpoint leaves a
vertex of degree one in the available four-vertex graph, and omitting
the fifth vertex leaves only three available edges. Since \(C\ne P,Q\),
it must meet the path.

If \(C\) contains \(04\), use target \(T=O63=\{03,04,23,24\}\). Assign
two \(A\) copies to \(03,04\) and \(C\) to \(40\). The five remaining
positions are

\[
E=\{23,32,24,42,30\}.
\]

The two \(P\) copies see \(30,24,42\), and the two \(Q\) copies see
\(30,23,32\). They can fill any four of these five positions: the two
three-position sets share one position, and each has two private
positions. Any adjacent source meeting \(E\) supplies the remaining
corner.

Only the class \(A\) avoids \(E\). Avoiding both directions of \(23\)
and \(24\) puts colours \(2,3,4\) on one side of the cut and \(0,1\) on
the other. Avoiding \(30\) determines the orientation to be \(A\). Since
there are nine adjacent sources and only three can belong to \(A\), a
source meeting \(E\) exists.

If \(C\) instead contains \(14\), use \(T=O68=\{01,03,14,34\}\). Put two
\(A\) copies at \(03,14\) and \(C\) at \(41\). The five remaining
positions are

\[
E=\{01,10,30,34,43\}.
\]

The two \(P\) copies see \(30,34,43\), and the two \(Q\) copies see
\(30,01,10\), so again they fill any four. A class avoiding \(E\) must
keep \(0,1\) together, keep \(3,4\) together, and orient the former pair
toward the latter. Colour 2 can join either side. Thus at most two
classes avoid \(E\); their combined multiplicity is at most six, and one
of the nine adjacent sources meets \(E\). This closes the overlapping
case.

\hypertarget{disjoint-patterns-two-explicit-ways-to-complete}{%
\subsection*{3. Disjoint patterns: two explicit ways to
complete}\label{disjoint-patterns-two-explicit-ways-to-complete}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Disjoint patterns: two explicit
ways to complete}

There are three configurations up to exchange of the rows and
permutation of the columns. The table lists all of them. Each source
variety is determined by its two grid positions, so the three possible
values of \(C\) are the remaining two-position patterns in the four
positions.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1667}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1667}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1667}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1667}}

\endlastfoot
\(65,70\) & \(02,03;12,04\) & \(63=\{03,04,23,24\}\) & \(24\) & \(23\) &
\(63,64,73\) \\
\(61,65\) & \(12,14;02,03\) & \(68=\{01,03,14,34\}\) & \(34\) & \(01\) &
\(68,71,73\) \\
\(71,73\) & \(02,14;12,03\) & \(68=\{01,03,14,34\}\) & \(01\) & \(34\) &
\(61,65,68\) \\
\end{longtable}

In each row, let \(a\) be the \(A\)-corner of \(P\) that belongs to
\(T\), and let \(b\) be the corresponding \(Q\) corner. The target \(T\)
consists of the four undirected edges underlying \(a,b,d,e\). Every
permitted \(C\) contains \(e\) and at least one of the edges underlying
\(a,b\).

\textbf{First completion.} If any adjacent source has a corner on edge
\(e\), assign it there and \(C\) to the reverse corner of \(e\). Assign
two \(A\) copies to \(a,b\). The remaining four positions are the
reverse corners of \(a,b\) and the two directions of \(d\). The two
\(P\) copies fill the reverse of \(a\) and one direction of \(d\); the
two \(Q\) copies fill the reverse of \(b\) and the other direction of
\(d\).

\textbf{Second completion.} Suppose \(C\) contains the edge underlying
\(a\). Use target \(P\). Assign the two \(Q\) copies to both directions
of \(d\), assign \(C\) to the reverse of \(a\), and assign two \(A\)
copies to the two corners in \(P\cap A\). Three target positions remain.
Any adjacent source meeting one of them fills that position; the two
universal \(P\) copies fill the other two. When \(C\) contains the edge
underlying \(b\), the symmetric construction uses target \(Q\).

Here are the three-position sets supplied by the second completion:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
\(P,Q\)
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
If \(C\) contains the private P edge: target \(P\), remaining set
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\(65,70\) & Edge \(03\): \(\{20,34,43\}\) & Edge \(04\):
\(\{01,10,21\}\) \\
\(61,65\) & Edge \(14\): \(\{21,23,32\}\) & Edge \(03\):
\(\{20,24,42\}\) \\
\(71,73\) & Edge \(14\): \(\{20,24,42\}\) & Edge \(03\):
\(\{21,23,32\}\) \\
\end{longtable}

Fix either applicable second completion, and let \(E\) be the union of
its three-position set with both directions of \(e\). An adjacent source
meeting \(E\) enables one of the two completions. In every entry of the
table, \(E\) consists of two bidirected edges and one further directed
edge joining their components.

If the two bidirected edges are adjacent, avoiding them puts their three
vertices on the same side of the cut. The remaining two vertices must
occupy the other side, and the further directed edge fixes the
orientation. There is at most one avoiding class. If the two bidirected
edges are disjoint, each pair must stay together on opposite sides. The
further directed edge fixes their orientation, and the fifth colour has
two choices of side. There are at most two avoiding classes.

In either case those classes account for at most six adjacent sources.
There are nine. An adjacent source meeting \(E\) therefore exists, and
the pool assembles. This proves the lemma.

\hypertarget{combined-consequence}{%
\subsection*{Combined consequence}\label{combined-consequence}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Combined consequence}

Together with \protect\hyperlink{arg-4}{Argument 4} and the four-class
result, this shows that a still-failing fourteen-source pool with at
least five opposite sources, all opposite multiplicities at most two,
must have \textbf{every adjacent tricolour class of size at most two}.

\hypertarget{arg-6}{%
\section{Argument 6: Five opposite cubes, one doubled variety, and a
triple adjacent class}\label{arg-6}}

\textbf{Lemma.} A fourteen-source pool is soluble if it has exactly five
opposite sources, at most one opposite variety is repeated, every
opposite variety occurs at most twice, and one adjacent tricolour class
has at least three copies. Here we may assume that every adjacent class
has at most three copies, since the four-class case is already settled
separately.

Only opposite targets are used. The opposite inventory has profile
\(1^5\) or \(2,1,1,1\).

\hypertarget{the-grid-cover}{%
\subsection*{1. The grid cover}\label{the-grid-cover}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. The grid cover}

Let the triple adjacent class be \(A=C(01)\), with its two-by-three grid
of directed corners. If the five opposite sources match all four
positions of a two-column rectangle, use those four sources on the
reverse rectangle and the remaining opposite source plus three \(A\) on
the forward rectangle. We may therefore suppose every rectangle has rank
at most three.

As in the six-opposite proof, the grid has rank at most four. Hall's
theorem gives a cover \(H\) with

\[
|H|+\#\{\text{opposite sources with pattern not contained in }H\}\le4.
\]

A cover of size at most two is impossible. A four-position rectangle is
also impossible: five capped opposite sources supported on it match its
four corners. An absent corner would confine five sources to three
positions, containing only two varieties and hence at most four copies;
the other Hall inequalities are stronger still.

There are thus two cases.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  All five source patterns lie in four positions with column sizes
  \(2,1,1\).
\item
  Four source patterns lie in three positions, and the remaining source
  lies outside them.
\end{itemize}

In the second case the three positions occupy three distinct columns.
Otherwise they support only two varieties, which can provide at most
three sources when only one variety may be doubled. The three-position
case must therefore consist of \(2P,Q,R\), where \(P,Q,R\) are the three
varieties given by its two-position subsets, and one further variety
\(S\). The varieties \(P,Q,R\) form a line: their pairwise intersections
have two undirected edges, their union has six, and every union edge
belongs to two of the three cycles.

\hypertarget{selecting-a-target}{%
\subsection*{2. Selecting a target}\label{selecting-a-target}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Selecting a target}

In the four-position case, select the central target \(T\) joining the
two singleton-column positions, as in the preceding note. Every source
meets \(T\) in at least two undirected edges. Each target edge is absent
from only two of the five possible source varieties. Since at most one
variety is doubled, those two varieties account for at most three of the
five sources. Thus every target edge has at least two source copies.

If a variety \(D\ne T\) is doubled, the two \(A\)-compatible corners of
\(T\) include one outside \(D\cap T\). This follows directly from the
two central-target diagrams in the preceding note: each of the four
noncentral varieties contains exactly one of the two singleton-column
corners.

In the three-position case, select the high target \(T\) of \(S\) in the
line \(P,Q,R\). An opposite variety outside a line intersects its three
targets in \(2,1,1\) undirected edges, so this target exists. All five
sources then meet \(T\) in at least two undirected edges. The copies of
the line varieties themselves supply every target edge at least twice:
if \(T=P\), its two copies suffice; otherwise its own copy and a copy of
one of the other line varieties cover each edge.

If \(T\ne P\), the two \(A\)-compatible target corners lie in the two
distinct pairwise-intersection blocks of the line. In particular one is
outside \(P\cap T\). If \(T=P\), the doubled source is universal and
creates no exception.

Thus, in both cases, we have a target \(T\) with these properties:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  each of the five opposite sources sees at least two target edges;
\item
  each target edge is seen by at least two sources;
\item
  the target has exactly two corners in \(A\), denoted \(a,b\);
\item
  if the doubled variety \(D\) is not \(T\), at least one of \(a,b\)
  lies outside \(D\cap T\).
\end{enumerate}

\hypertarget{five-source-completion-after-the-two-a-corners-are-reserved}{%
\subsection*{3. Five-source completion after the two A corners are
reserved}\label{five-source-completion-after-the-two-a-corners-are-reserved}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Five-source completion after the
two A corners are reserved}

Reserve \(a,b\) for two \(A\) copies. The five opposite sources can fill
\textbf{any five of the remaining six positions}.

To prove this by Hall, a position set using one target edge has at least
two source neighbours. A set using at least three target edges sees all
five sources. A set using two target edges has at least four source
neighbours, except possibly when the sole variety avoiding those two
edges is the doubled variety \(D\). In that case it has three
neighbours, but the two edges are precisely the complement of
\(D\cap T\), and one of their four corners has already been reserved as
\(a\) or \(b\). At most three of their positions remain. Every Hall
inequality therefore holds for every set of at most five unreserved
positions.

Here we used the elementary uniqueness fact that, for a fixed target and
a fixed two-edge subset, exactly one other opposite variety has that
subset as its complete intersection with the target. It follows by
closing a two-edge path through the fifth colour, or by choosing the
other four-cycle containing a pair of opposite edges.

\hypertarget{an-additional-adjacent-source-exists}{%
\subsection*{4. An additional adjacent source
exists}\label{an-additional-adjacent-source-exists}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. An additional adjacent source
exists}

There are nine adjacent sources in the pool. At most two adjacent
tricolour classes have target intersection contained in \(\{a,b\}\).
Indeed every adjacent source meets an opposite target in at least two
corners on distinct underlying edges, so such an intersection must equal
\(\{a,b\}\). A two-corner pattern on a target four-cycle is either a
directed two-edge star, which determines one adjacent class, or consists
of two opposite edges, which determines at most two classes according to
the placement of the fifth colour.

Each class has at most three copies. Those classes account for at most
six of the nine adjacent sources. Some other adjacent source therefore
has a target corner \(c\notin\{a,b\}\). Use it at \(c\), the two \(A\)
copies at \(a,b\), and the five opposite sources on the remaining five
positions. This proves the lemma.

\hypertarget{remaining-five-opposite-profile}{%
\subsection*{Remaining five-opposite
profile}\label{remaining-five-opposite-profile}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Remaining five-opposite profile}

The argument leaves only opposite inventory \(2,2,1\). A separate
line-hole construction handles the case in which the two doubled
varieties share four directed corners. The remaining case with two
doubled opposite varieties sharing only two directed corners is proved
separately in \protect\hyperlink{arg-5}{Argument 5}.

\hypertarget{arg-7}{%
\section{Argument 7: Three opposite pairs and two arbitrary
sources}\label{arg-7}}

\textbf{Proved elementary lemma.} Two copies of each of any three
distinct opposite-repeat varieties, together with any two further
five-colour cubes, admit an opposite-repeat corner assembly.

There is no multiplicity restriction on the two additional cubes. This
is stronger than the corresponding special case of the earlier finite
eight-cube lemma. It does not settle all its six-opposite cases.

\hypertarget{undirected-notation-and-a-robust-six-observation}{%
\subsection*{1. Undirected notation and a robust-six
observation}\label{undirected-notation-and-a-robust-six-observation}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Undirected notation and a
robust-six observation}

Identify an opposite variety with its undirected four-cycle on the five
colours. Each edge denotes both directed corner types. Any two distinct
four-cycles on five vertices share one or two edges.

For a fixed four-cycle T and any two-edge subset F of T, exactly one
other four-cycle has intersection F with T. If the two edges of F are
adjacent, its remaining vertex must be the fifth colour. If the two
edges are disjoint, the alternative way to complete them on the same
four vertices is unique.

Suppose three distinct opposite varieties A,B,C each share at least two
edges with a target T, and their intersections together cover all four
edges of T. Then two copies of each A,B,C can fill \textbf{any six} of
T's eight corners. Indeed, a set of corners on one target edge has at
least two neighbouring sources. A set meeting two target edges has at
least four neighbours: at most one of A,B,C can have its intersection
with T confined to the complementary two edges. A set meeting three or
four target edges sees all six sources. These are Hall's inequalities
for every set of at most six corner positions.

The two arbitrary additional sources each have at least two corners in
common with T, so they can be assigned two distinct positions. The six
opposite sources fill the remaining positions.

\hypertarget{when-a-robust-six-target-exists}{%
\subsection*{2. When a robust-six target
exists}\label{when-a-robust-six-target-exists}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. When a robust-six target exists}

If one of A,B,C shares two edges with each of the other two, use it as
T. The preceding observation applies.

If exactly one pair, say A,B, shares two edges, take T=A
symmetric-difference B. This is again a four-cycle: if the common edges
are adjacent, the two alternative fourth vertices form the new cycle; if
they are disjoint, the alternative edge pairs form it. Its intersections
with A and B are complementary two-edge subsets, so already cover T.
Since C shares one edge with each A and B,

\begin{verbatim}
|C∩T| = |C∩A| + |C∩B| − 2|C∩A∩B|.
\end{verbatim}

The right side is either zero or two. It cannot be zero because two
four-cycles on five vertices always share an edge. Thus C also shares
two edges with T, and the robust-six observation applies.

It remains that A,B,C are pairwise one-edge-intersecting. Normalize two
of them to

\begin{verbatim}
X={13,14,23,24},   Y={01,04,13,34}.
\end{verbatim}

The third cannot have the omitted colour of X or Y, because two distinct
four-cycles on the same four vertices share two edges. Listing the three
cycles on each of the other four-vertex sets leaves only

\begin{verbatim}
{02,03,24,34}, {02,04,12,14},
{02,03,12,13}, {01,03,12,23}.
\end{verbatim}

For the third choice all three cycles share the edge13. The target
\{12,13,24,34\} meets them in the three two-edge sets \{13,24\},
\{13,34\}, \{12,13\}; these cover all four target edges, so the
robust-six observation applies again.

In each of the other three choices, the three pairwise intersection
edges form a path on four colours. All such triples are equivalent under
colour relabelling. To see uniqueness, the cycle containing the two
outside path edges is determined by avoiding the middle edge; each other
cycle consists of its two adjacent path edges completed through the
fifth vertex. We may therefore use the normal form

\begin{verbatim}
X={13,14,23,24} [O60],
Y={02,04,23,34} [O64],
Z={01,03,14,34} [O68].
\end{verbatim}

\hypertarget{the-remaining-normal-form}{%
\subsection*{3. The remaining normal
form}\label{the-remaining-normal-form}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. The remaining normal form}

For the doubled sources 2X+2Y+2Z, the following four targets have the
stated useful property: the six sources fill every set of six target
corners obtained by leaving any two distinct corners in H unused. Here
an undirected edge in H again denotes both directions.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Target T
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Forced edge filled by two core copies
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
H
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Opposite varieties avoiding H
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\{03,04,23,24\} {[}O63{]} & 03, by Z & \{04,23,24\} & Z,V \\
\{02,03,24,34\} {[}O65{]} & 24, by X & \{02,03,34\} & X,W \\
\{03,04,13,14\} {[}O66{]} & 04, by Y & \{03,13,14\} & Y,W \\
\{01,04,13,34\} {[}O67{]} & 13, by X & \{01,04,34\} & X,V \\
\end{longtable}

where

\begin{verbatim}
V={02,03,12,13} [O72],
W={01,04,12,24} [O70].
\end{verbatim}

The six-corner completion property is immediate in every row. The
indicated two core copies fill both directions of the forced edge. The
other four copies form two two-copy pools on H. Each pool fits two of
H's three edge pairs, and their intersection is one edge pair. Thus the
first pool's exclusive pair requires at most two copies, the second
pool's exclusive pair requires at most two, and all four copies together
fill any four of H's six corner positions.

Each H is a three-edge path on four vertices. An adjacent cut meets H
because a two-three cut cannot place all four path vertices on one side.
Its intersection contains one directed corner for every crossed path
edge. An opposite variety meets H in both directions of any shared edge.
The last column follows by considering the omitted vertex: a four-cycle
avoiding a three-edge path must omit an internal path vertex, and either
internal vertex determines exactly one such cycle.

\hypertarget{two-adjacent-additional-sources}{%
\subsubsection*{Two adjacent additional
sources}\label{two-adjacent-additional-sources}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Two adjacent additional sources}

The first two paths H are complementary edge sets in the complete graph
on \{0,2,3,4\}. A two-three cut splits these four vertices nontrivially,
and therefore crosses three or four of that complete graph's edges. It
consequently meets at least one of these two paths in at least two
corners. The other adjacent source meets that path in at least one
corner. Assign the sources to two distinct corners of H and complete
with the six opposite copies.

\hypertarget{one-adjacent-and-one-opposite-additional-source}{%
\subsubsection*{One adjacent and one opposite additional
source}\label{one-adjacent-and-one-opposite-additional-source}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{One adjacent and one opposite
additional source}

The two sets \{Z,V\} and \{X,W\} in the first two rows are disjoint.
Thus the additional opposite source meets H in at least one of these
rows, giving it at least two directed corners there. The adjacent source
has at least one corner there. Again distinct holes can be assigned and
the core completes the assembly.

\hypertarget{two-opposite-additional-sources}{%
\subsubsection*{Two opposite additional
sources}\label{two-opposite-additional-sources}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Two opposite additional sources}

If both meet H in any row, each has at least two directed corners in H,
and the same construction works. Failure of the first two rows would
therefore require one extra source from \{Z,V\} and the other from
\{X,W\}. The third row eliminates Z+X and V+X; the fourth eliminates
Z+W. Only V+W remains.

For V+W use the target \{12,14,23,34\} {[}O61{]}. The two extra sources
fill 12 and21. The six core sources fill the six directed corners on
\{14,23,34\}: X fits the first two edge pairs, Y the last two, and Z the
first and last. Hall's inequalities hold because each edge pair has four
neighbours, any two edge pairs see all six sources, and all six
positions see all six. This completes the last case.

\hypertarget{consequence-1}{%
\subsection*{Consequence}\label{consequence-1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Consequence}

A failing pool of size at least eight cannot contain three distinct
opposite varieties twice each. In particular, in the remaining diffuse
fourteen-cube branch, at most two opposite varieties can occur twice. An
elementary treatment of six-opposite cores with multiplicity profiles
(2,2,1,1), (2,1,1,1,1), or(1,1,1,1,1,1) is still needed for the full
eight-cube lemma.

\hypertarget{arg-8}{%
\section{Argument 8: Two neighbouring opposite pairs and two further
opposite sources}\label{arg-8}}

\textbf{Proved elementary lemma.} Let A,B be distinct opposite varieties
sharing four directed corners, equivalently two undirected edges. Take
two copies of each. Add two opposite sources C,D, neither of which is A
or B (they may equal each other), and any two further five-colour cubes.
These eight sources have an opposite-target assembly.

The proof uses the three-opposite-pairs lemma in the companion note.
There is no multiplicity restriction on the two arbitrary sources.

\hypertarget{the-line-of-three-four-cycles}{%
\subsection*{1. The line of three
four-cycles}\label{the-line-of-three-four-cycles}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. The line of three four-cycles}

Use undirected four-cycle notation, with each edge denoting both
directed corners. Put

\begin{verbatim}
I=A∩B, J=A\B, K=B\A, T=A symmetric-difference B=J∪K.
\end{verbatim}

Each of I,J,K has two edges, and T is a four-cycle. The three cycles
A=I∪J, B=I∪K, T=J∪K form a line: their union is either K4 (when I
consists of disjoint edges) or K2,3 (when I consists of adjacent edges).
Each union edge occurs in exactly two of the three cycles.

Every opposite variety E outside \{A,B,T\} has two edges in this union.
For a K4 union this follows because E uses the fifth vertex, leaving
only a two-edge path on the other three vertices. For a K2,3 union, the
number of crossing edges in the four-cycle E is even; it cannot be zero,
and if it were four E would be one of A,B,T. Thus it is two.

Since E meets each of A,B,T in at least one edge, its intersection sizes
with the three are therefore (2,1,1) in some order. Call the cycle with
intersection size two its high target. More precisely,

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  high A: one edge of I and one of J;
\item
  high B: one edge of I and one of K;
\item
  high T: one edge of J and one of K.
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{a-robust-six-criterion}{%
\subsection*{2. A robust-six criterion}\label{a-robust-six-criterion}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. A robust-six criterion}

Six opposite sources, with every variety occurring at most twice, can
fill any six corners of a target E if:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  each source intersects E in at least two undirected edges;
\item
  each edge of E is present in at least two source copies.
\end{itemize}

For Hall's theorem, one edge pair has at least two neighbours. A set
meeting two target edges has at least four: only one opposite variety
can have its E-intersection confined to the complementary two edges, and
that variety has at most two copies. A set meeting at least three target
edges sees all six sources. Thus every set of at most six positions has
enough neighbours.

If C or D equals T, or if C,D have the same high target, this criterion
immediately supplies the needed robust-six target. At A or B the two
copies of that variety provide the edge-frequency bound; at T the two A
and two B copies provide it because J and K partition T. The arbitrary
additional sources can then fill any two distinct compatible corners.

It remains that C,D have different high targets.

\hypertarget{two-elementary-six-corner-completion-rules}{%
\subsection*{3. Two elementary six-corner completion
rules}\label{two-elementary-six-corner-completion-rules}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Two elementary six-corner
completion rules}

The following rules are useful on target A=I∪J. Its source pattern
consists of two universal A copies, two B copies seeing I, one C copy
seeing one edge of I and one edge of J, and one other source D.

\textbf{Rule I.} If D sees one edge of I, the six sources can fill every
six-position set whose two omitted corners are not both in I.

If one omitted corner is in I and one in J, the remaining three J
corners are filled by C and the two A copies: C's two J corners cannot
both be omitted. The three remaining I corners are filled by D and the
two B copies. If both omitted corners are in J, the two A copies fill
the two remaining J positions. C,D can be assigned distinct I corners
(each has two), and the two B copies fill the rest.

\textbf{Rule II.} If D sees the single edge e in J, the six sources can
fill every six-position set unless the omitted pair consists of both
directions of e.

Assign D an available e corner. If three J positions remain, neither
omitted corner was in J; C still has a J corner and it joins the two A
copies there. The two B copies fill the two remaining I positions. If
two J positions remain, use the two A there, and C plus two B at the
three remaining I positions. If one J position remains, use one A there
and the other A, C and two B at the four I positions. Every stated
assignment has a compatible corner.

\hypertarget{different-high-targets}{%
\subsection*{4. Different high targets}\label{different-high-targets}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Different high targets}

First suppose C has high target A and D high target B. On target A, D
sees one edge of I, so Rule I applies. On target B the symmetric Rule I
applies. Every additional cube has a corner of the opposite target
T=J∪K. If that corner is in J, reserve it as a hole in A; if it is in K,
reserve it as a hole in B. The other additional cube has at least two
corners of the selected target and hence can fill a different position.
The omitted pair is not confined to I, so the corresponding six-corner
completion works.

Next suppose C has high target A and D high target T. On target A, D
sees an edge e of J, so Rule II applies. It yields an assembly unless
both additional cubes have all their A-compatible corners inside the
bidirected edge e.

An adjacent cut cannot have this property: it has at least two corners
of A, on distinct undirected edges. Both additional sources would
therefore be opposite varieties whose intersection with A is exactly e.
There are precisely two such varieties; one is D, and write R for the
other. (They are obtained by omitting one or the other of A's vertices
not on e.)

If an additional source is D, the pool contains two copies each of
A,B,D. If neither is D, both are R, and it contains two copies each of
A,B,R. In either case the three-opposite-pairs lemma applies.

The case of high targets B and T is symmetric. These exhaust the
possibilities, proving the lemma.

\hypertarget{consequence-for-the-diffuse-fourteen-cube-problem}{%
\subsection*{Consequence for the diffuse fourteen-cube
problem}\label{consequence-for-the-diffuse-fourteen-cube-problem}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Consequence for the diffuse
fourteen-cube problem}

If a pool has at least six opposite sources, every opposite variety
occurs at most twice, and two doubled varieties share four corners, it
is soluble. Choose those four sources, any two other opposite sources,
and any two further sources.

Combining this with the three-opposite-pairs lemma, a still-failing pool
with at least six opposite sources could have at most two doubled
opposite varieties; if it has two, they must share only two corners. The
cases with two such nonneighbouring doubled types, one doubled type, or
no doubled type are not settled by this note.

\hypertarget{arg-9}{%
\section{Argument 9: Avoided edges and common high
targets}\label{arg-9}}

\hypertarget{three-basic-facts-about-the-adjacent-pool}{%
\subsection*{1. Three basic facts about the adjacent
pool}\label{three-basic-facts-about-the-adjacent-pool}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Three basic facts about the
adjacent pool}

The following statements hold in a pool with no opposite assembly.

\hypertarget{nine-adjacent-cubes-cannot-all-avoid-two-edges}{%
\subsubsection*{Nine adjacent cubes cannot all avoid two
edges}\label{nine-adjacent-cubes-cannot-all-avoid-two-edges}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Nine adjacent cubes cannot all
avoid two edges}

If the two edges meet, a cut avoiding both must keep all three of their
vertices together. Only two reverse adjacent classes do this, with
combined capacity six, which cannot accommodate nine cubes.

If the edges are disjoint, each endpoint pair stays together. The pairs
lie on opposite sides of the cut; the fifth colour may join either side.
Thus there are four possible classes, two in each orientation between
the pairs, with capacity six per orientation. Nine cubes split with at
least three in each orientation. Four cubes in each orientation would
fill an opposite target between the pairs. If one orientation has only
three, use four cubes from the other orientation and those three,
together with any opposite source in the remaining position. An opposite
source meets that target in at least one underlying edge and supports
its required direction. Again there is an assembly.

\hypertarget{a-common-high-target-always-exists}{%
\subsection*{3. A common high target always
exists}\label{a-common-high-target-always-exists}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. A common high target always exists}

\textbf{Geometric lemma.} Any three distinct opposite varieties have an
opposite target on which all three are high.

If two of the varieties are neighbours (share two edges), they lie on a
line of three four-cycles inside a K4 or K2,3. If the third variety is
on that line, any line target works. If it is outside, it meets exactly
one line target in two edges and the others in one; choose its high line
target. This is the elementary line geometry in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-8}{Argument 8}.

Moreover this target is fully supported by the first two varieties: it
is either one of them, or their symmetric difference.

It remains to consider three pairwise nonneighbouring varieties.
Normalize the doubled variety and one single to

\begin{verbatim}
D=O60={13,14,23,24}, P=O67={01,04,13,34}.
\end{verbatim}

A variety meeting both only in one edge has exactly four possibilities:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2500}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Other single Q
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Edges of Q
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
A common high target
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Edge of target absent from D∪P∪Q
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
O65 & 02,03,24,34 & O62=\{12,13,24,34\} & 12 \\
O69 & 02,04,12,14 & O66=\{03,04,13,14\} & 03 \\
O72 & 02,03,12,13 & O62=\{12,13,24,34\} & none \\
O73 & 01,03,12,23 & O74=\{01,02,13,23\} & 02 \\
\end{longtable}

Here is a direct completeness check. If Q contains the common edge13, it
must avoid all other edges of D∪P, which uniquely gives O72. Otherwise
it uses one edge from D\textbackslash P, one from P\textbackslash D, and
two edges of the missing path1--2--0--3. Choose the latter two edges. If
they meet, closing their two-edge path through the allowed fourth vertex
gives O65 or O69. If they are disjoint, the only allowed closure gives
O73. The alternative closure in each case uses edge13, which is
excluded. The table is therefore a four-cycle classification, not a
source-inventory search.

Direct intersection verifies the three high-target conditions in every
row.

\hypertarget{arg-10}{%
\section{Argument 10: Eight opposite-repeat cubes with multiplicity cap
two}\label{arg-10}}

\textbf{Theorem.} Any eight opposite-repeat five-colour cubes, with no
variety occurring more than twice, assemble an opposite-repeat target.

An opposite variety is an undirected four-cycle of K5; each edge gives
both directed corner positions. This proof uses the elementary three-
pairs and neighbouring-pairs constructions in
\texttt{../opposite\_small\_lemma/}. It uses the line geometry from
\protect\hyperlink{arg-3}{Argument 3}, but does not assume the numerical
nine-source theorem or any exhaustive search.

\hypertarget{incidence-facts-and-a-five-source-core}{%
\subsection*{1. Incidence facts and a five-source
core}\label{incidence-facts-and-a-five-source-core}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Incidence facts and a five-source
core}

Every edge belongs to six opposite varieties. Every pair of distinct
edges belongs to two varieties, and is avoided by exactly five. For a
fixed target, each two-edge intersection belongs to exactly one other
variety; each one-edge intersection belongs to exactly two.

The five distinct varieties avoiding two fixed edges form a useful core.
They can fill any five corners of a suitable central target. The
following two normal forms cover adjacent and disjoint forbidden edges.
Each entry denotes a set of undirected edges.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Forbidden pair F
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Central target T
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Intersections of the other four avoiders with T
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\{01,02\} & \{13,14,23,24\} & \{14,23\}; \{13,24\}; \{23,24\};
\{13,14\} \\
\{01,23\} & \{02,03,12,13\} & \{12,13\}; \{02,03\}; \{03,13\};
\{02,12\} \\
\end{longtable}

Every target edge has three source neighbours. A corner set using two
edges has at least four neighbours, since only the unique variety with
the complementary two-edge intersection can avoid it. A set using three
edges sees all five sources. Hall therefore gives a matching of any five
target corners.

We need two elementary ways to fill the other three positions.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Three distinct opposite varieties can match three corners of any
  target: each has at least two corners, and at most two varieties can
  have their entire intersection in one bidirected edge.
\item
  Three opposite sources can also match three corners if one sees at
  least two target edges. Every two-source union has size at least two,
  while the three-source union then has size at least four.
\end{itemize}

Finally, any four-cycle containing the forbidden pair F meets its
central target T in two edges. For adjacent F, the cycle closes the
two-edge path through one other vertex, and its two closing edges belong
to T. For disjoint F, its other two edges join the two pairs and
likewise belong to T.

\hypertarget{zero-or-one-doubled-variety}{%
\subsection*{2. Zero or one doubled
variety}\label{zero-or-one-doubled-variety}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Zero or one doubled variety}

\hypertarget{eight-distinct-varieties}{%
\subsubsection*{Eight distinct
varieties}\label{eight-distinct-varieties}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Eight distinct varieties}

At most one undirected edge has source frequency at most one. Otherwise
two low-frequency edges would require at least5+5−2=8 omitted varieties,
whereas only seven varieties are omitted.

If the eight varieties contain all five avoiders of any two edges, use
their five-source core and the remaining three distinct sources.
Otherwise every two-edge target set has at least four neighbours: a
failure would require at least five sources avoiding those two edges.
Every three-edge target set has at least six neighbours, since only two
varieties avoid three edges of a four-cycle.

Choose a target avoiding the possible low-frequency edge. Its single
edges have at least two neighbours, its pairs at least four, its triples
at least six, and all four edges see all eight sources. These are all
Hall inequalities, proving the claim.

\hypertarget{one-doubled-variety-p}{%
\subsubsection*{One doubled variety P}\label{one-doubled-variety-p}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{One doubled variety P}

Use P as target. Its own two copies satisfy the one-edge inequalities.
Any three target edges have at least six neighbours, because at most two
single varieties avoid them. Hence failure would require a pair F⊂P
having at most three neighbours. All five avoiding varieties must then
occur among the six distinct single sources.

Use their five-source core. The remaining sources are two P copies and
one single. P contains F, so it sees two edges of the central target, by
Section1. These three sources match three target corners, and the
five-source core fills the rest.

\hypertarget{three-doubled-varieties-or-neighbouring-doubled-varieties}{%
\subsection*{3. Three doubled varieties, or neighbouring doubled
varieties}\label{three-doubled-varieties-or-neighbouring-doubled-varieties}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Three doubled varieties, or
neighbouring doubled varieties}

If at least three varieties are doubled, the three-pairs lemma assembles
using those six cubes and any two others.

If exactly two doubled varieties P,Q share two edges, the neighbouring-
pairs lemma applies to two copies of each, two further opposite sources,
and the two remaining sources.

The only remaining profile is 2P+2Q and four distinct single varieties,
with P,Q sharing exactly one edge e.

\hypertarget{the-one-edge-companion-is-present}{%
\subsection*{4. The one-edge companion is
present}\label{the-one-edge-companion-is-present}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. The one-edge companion is present}

There is a unique variety V meeting both P and Q only at e. Normalize

\begin{verbatim}
P={13,14,23,24},  Q={01,04,13,34},  V={02,03,12,13}.
\end{verbatim}

Suppose V is one of the four single sources. The other three singles are
distinct and differ from P,Q,V. Consider these three targets:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Target
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
P intersection
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Q intersection
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
V intersection
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
V-private edge
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\{12,13,24,34\} & \{13,24\} & \{13,34\} & \{13,12\} & 12 \\
\{03,04,13,14\} & \{13,14\} & \{13,04\} & \{13,03\} & 03 \\
\{01,02,13,23\} & \{13,23\} & \{13,01\} & \{13,02\} & 02 \\
\end{longtable}

\textbf{Claim.} Each target assembles as soon as an extra single source
contains its V-private edge.

Check source-side Hall. A union of one target edge can contain at most
two of the three extra singles and none of P,Q,V, so has at most two
sources. A union of two target edges can contain only one of the three
displayed anchor patterns. If it contains a P or Q pattern, it omits the
V-private edge and hence misses the extra source containing that edge;
it therefore contains at most two anchor copies and two extras. If it
contains the V pattern it contains at most one anchor copy and three
extras. In either case at most four sources are confined there.

A three-edge union can contain at most two anchor patterns. If they are
P,Q, it omits the V-private edge and therefore contains at most four
anchor copies and two extras. If one is V, it contains at most three
anchor copies and three extras. Thus at most six sources are confined to
three edges. All source-side Hall inequalities hold.

The only opposite varieties avoiding all three edges12,02,03 are P,Q.
Indeed those edges form the path1--2--0--3. Omitting an endpoint leaves
an internal vertex of degree one; omitting the isolated vertex4 leaves
only three allowed edges. Omitting either internal vertex gives the
unique avoiding four-cycle P or Q. Thus every extra single contains at
least one V-private edge in the table, and one target assembles.

\hypertarget{the-companion-is-absent}{%
\subsection*{5. The companion is absent}\label{the-companion-is-absent}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. The companion is absent}

Use P as target. Single-edge Hall inequalities hold because P occurs
twice. Three-edge inequalities also hold: a three-edge complement could
have three avoiding copies only from Q,Q,V, and V is absent. Thus a
failed P target has a two-edge obstruction F⊂P∖Q, avoided by both Q
copies and at least three of the four singles.

If all four singles avoid F, they and one Q are precisely the five
distinct avoiding varieties. Their central target has a five-source
completion. The remaining sources are P,P,Q; the remaining Q sees at
least two target edges because it is one of the five avoiding varieties.
These three sources fill the other three corners.

We may therefore assume exactly three singles avoid F. Likewise, if Q
does not assemble, exactly three singles avoid a two-edge set G⊂Q∖P. The
sets F,G are disjoint, and their complement L in K5 has six edges.

The two three-element sets of single avoiders have at least two members
in common. Thus L contains two distinct four-cycles. Their union must
have exactly six edges, and is either K4 or K2,3. It contains exactly
three four-cycles, the familiar line of opposite varieties.

There is a line target T containing both P∩L and Q∩L. Explicit normal
forms are:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Line union L
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
P
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Q
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
T
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Edge h of T outside P∪Q
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
K4 on1234 & \{01,02,13,23\} & \{03,04,13,14\} & \{13,23,24,14\} & 24 \\
K2,3 with parts01 and234 & \{01,23,02,13\} & \{24,34,02,03\} &
\{02,03,12,13\} & 12 \\
\end{longtable}

These are the two line normal forms justified in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-3}{Argument 3}, Section3.

If three single varieties avoid both F and G, all three line varieties
are present. Then 2P+2Q and those three line sources fill any seven
corners of T: a target edge has at least two neighbours, a pair has at
least five, and a triple sees all seven sources. The remaining single
completes the target.

Otherwise exactly two singles X,Y avoid both pairs. The other two, R,S,
satisfy

\begin{verbatim}
R avoids G and meets F;    S avoids F and meets G.
\end{verbatim}

In either normal form, the only four-cycle avoiding G and h while
meeting F is P itself. Since R differs from P, it must contain h. For
clarity, this small graph fact can be checked as follows.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  In the K4 form, avoiding G=\{03,04\} leaves vertex0 with possible
  neighbours1,2. A cycle meeting F=\{01,02\} must use both edges. Its
  other two edges close through3 or4; avoiding h=24 forces the route
  through3, which is P.
\item
  In the K2,3 form, G=\{24,34\} and h=12. A cycle using23 must also
  use02, because vertex2 then has only neighbours0,3; its only
  four-cycle closure is through13,01, giving P. A cycle using01 must
  leave vertex1 through13 or14. The route through4 cannot return in two
  steps, since24,34 are forbidden; the route through3 must use23,02.
  This again gives P.
\end{itemize}

Now consider the six-source core 2P+2Q+X+Y. Every source sees at least
two edges of T. Every target edge is covered by X or Y, because their
union is L. Three target edges also have P or Q copies, so their
frequencies are at least two. The remaining edge h acquires its second
neighbour from R.

A two-edge set has at least four neighbours already in the six-source
core: the only variety that can avoid it while meeting T in at least two
edges is the unique complementary-pair variety, with multiplicity at
most two. Every three-edge set sees all six core sources. All four edges
see all eight sources. Hall therefore gives a full assembly of T.

This completes all multiplicity profiles and proves the theorem.

\hypertarget{consequence-seven-opposite-sources-and-any-adjacent-cube}{%
\subsection*{Consequence: seven opposite sources and any adjacent
cube}\label{consequence-seven-opposite-sources-and-any-adjacent-cube}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Consequence: seven opposite sources
and any adjacent cube}

\textbf{Corollary.} Seven opposite sources with multiplicity cap two,
together with any adjacent-repeat cube A, assemble an opposite target
using all eight cubes.

The directed cut of A contains the forward halves of three opposite
varieties, one for each choice of two columns. If all three varieties
are already doubled among the seven sources, the three-pairs lemma
applies, with the seventh opposite source and A as the two extras.

Otherwise choose one of these three varieties R that is not doubled.
Replace A temporarily by R. The eight opposite sources have cap two, so
the theorem supplies an assembly. Let c be the corner assigned to the
temporary R. Its underlying edge crosses the two parts of A, so exactly
one of c and its reverse belongs to A. If c belongs to A, replace R by A
directly. Otherwise reverse every directed corner of the assignment.
This preserves the target and every actual opposite source, since their
corner sets are bidirected; the reversed c now belongs to A. Choosing
compatible proper rotations realizes the new assignment. This proves the
corollary.

\hypertarget{arg-11}{%
\section{Argument 11: Three opposite copies and the butterfly
support}\label{arg-11}}

This is a hand classification of the first four-corner Hall-cover orbit
for a fourteen-failure containing three identical opposite copies. It
uses the established adjacent-class bound five. Other Hall-cover orbits
are treated separately; this note does not assert completeness of the
full fourteen-failure classification.

Use O=±\{13,14,23,24\} and adjacent cuts

\begin{verbatim}
H1=C(01), H2=C(02), H3=C(124), H4=C(123),
U=C(12), V=C(012).
\end{verbatim}

Let their counts be x1,x2,x3,x4,u,v, totaling eleven. The three copies
of O are additional. Put L=x1+x2+v, R=x3+x4+u, so L+R=11.

\hypertarget{elementary-assembly-rules}{%
\subsection*{Elementary assembly
rules}\label{elementary-assembly-rules}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Elementary assembly rules}

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  If u≥4 or v≥4, two copies in that low class have the same repeated
  colour. Reserve them for the two bicoloured corners. Two further
  copies fill the two corners involving colour0. Three O and any
  remaining adjacent copy fill the other four corners. Thus u,v≤3.
\item
  If xi≥4 and its side total is at least six (L for i=1,2; R for i=3,4),
  reserve a same-repeat pair in Hi. Two remaining Hi copies fill its
  private pair of tricoloured corners. Two further copies from its side
  fill the other two corners outside O, and two O fill the last pair.
  For H1, these pairs are respectively \{02,12\}, \{03,04\}, and
  \{13,14\}.
\item
  If all four high classes are nonempty, form the opposite target
  ±\{12,14,23,34\}. One copy each of H1,H2,H3,H4 supplies 12,21,43,34.
  Three O and one remaining adjacent copy fill 14,41,23,32: every
  adjacent class in this family has 14 or23.
\item
  If exactly three high classes are nonempty, normalize x1=0. If x2+v≥2,
  build ±\{03,04,23,24\}: two copies from H2∪V supply 03,04; H3,H4
  supply40,30; three O and another adjacent copy supply23,32,24,42.
  Therefore failure forces x2=1,v=0. Then R=10 and u≤3, so one of x3,x4
  is at least four; rule2 gives an assembly.
\item
  If exactly two high classes in the same block are nonempty, normalize
  these to H1,H2. If u≥2, build ±\{01,02,14,24\}: H2,H1 supply01,02, two
  U supply10,20, and three O plus one remaining adjacent copy
  supply14,41,24,42. Otherwise u≤1, L≥10 and v≤3, so one of x1,x2 is at
  least four; rule2 applies.
\item
  If at most one high class is nonempty, its bound five and u,v≤3 force
  exactly the profile5,3,3; its side is at least eight, so rule2 again
  applies.
\end{enumerate}

Consequently exactly two high classes, one from each block, are
nonempty. Normalize them to X=H1 and Y=H3, with counts x,y.

\hypertarget{four-numerical-possibilities}{%
\subsection*{Four numerical
possibilities}\label{four-numerical-possibilities}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Four numerical possibilities}

We have x+y+u+v=11, x,y≤5 and u,v≤3. If x≥4, rule2 forces x+v≤5, hence
y+u≥6. If y≥4, rule2 would solve because its side is at least six. Thus
y≤3, and y=u=3, x+v=5. The symmetric argument holds if y≥4. If both
x,y≤3, the total forces three counts3 and one count2.

Up to swapping the two sides, only the following possibilities remain:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
x & y & u & v \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
2 & 3 & 3 & 3 \\
3 & 3 & 2 & 3 \\
3 & 4 & 1 & 3 \\
3 & 5 & 0 & 3 \\
\end{longtable}

\hypertarget{repeated-colour-pigeonhole}{%
\subsection*{Repeated-colour
pigeonhole}\label{repeated-colour-pigeonhole}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Repeated-colour pigeonhole}

In all four rows, any two different nonempty classes among X,Y,U,V must
have disjoint sets of repeated colours. Otherwise their two same-repeat
cubes supply the bicoloured corners of an assemblable target, as in this
table:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
