Reserved classes & Target cut \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
X,Y & Y in the first numerical row; X in the others \\
X,U & U \\
X,V & Y \\
Y,U & X \\
Y,V & Y \\
U,V & C(14) \\
\end{longtable}

Here are explicit checks of the tricoloured completions. After the
reservations, X can be completed whenever at least two X copies and at
least four copies of X∪V remain: two X supply02,12; two further X∪V
supply03,04; and two O supply13,14. The corresponding rule for Y
requires two Y and four Y∪U, supplying40,43; 10,20; and13,23. For target
U, two Y∪U supply10,20, and three O plus any remaining adjacent copy
fill its four other corners. These numerical requirements hold in every
row where the indicated pair is present.

For the last pair U,V, their only possible common repeated colour is0.
Use their bicoloured corners with singleton1 and4. Three Y supply
10,40,43, one X supplies12, and two O supply13,42. This completes the
tricoloured cut C(14).

Moreover all copies within U have distinct repeated colours, and the
same holds within V. A same-repeat pair within U leaves at least two Y∪U
for its two corners involving0; three O and another adjacent copy
complete it. The analogous statement for V uses X∪V.

The number of distinct repeated colours required is therefore at least
1+1+u+v. Only five colours exist. The first three rows require 8,7,6
respectively and are impossible. Only (x,y,u,v)=(3,5,0,3), or its
symmetric form (5,3,3,0), survives.

Hence every surviving coarse profile is three identical opposite copies
and adjacent-class sizes \(5,3,3\). Only this necessary profile is used
in the threshold proof.

\hypertarget{arg-12}{%
\section{Argument 12: Five copies in one class in an all-adjacent
fourteen-pool}\label{arg-12}}

\textbf{Theorem.} Fourteen adjacent-repeat cubes solve if some
tricoloured corner class occurs at least five times.

The six-class lemma already handles multiplicity at least six. Assume a
failing pool contains exactly five A=C(01), where C(S) denotes the cut
S→Sᶜ. Every adjacent class has count at most five.

The four classes disjoint from A are

\begin{verbatim}
B=C(234), Z23=C(23), Z24=C(24), Z34=C(34).
\end{verbatim}

Call their total z. Each has count at most three, since four A and four
copies of any one of these classes form an opposite target. Also z≤5:
six such disjoint sources match the six corners of C(234), and two
same-repeat A copies supply that target's bichromatic corners. This
six-corner matching follows from its three two-corner rows q→\{0,1\}: B
sees every row; each Z sees two; each missing-row class has count≤3.

Reserve a same-repeat pair among the five A for an A target's
bichromatic corners. The other three A can fill any three of its six
tricoloured corners. Therefore the nine outside sources have matching
rank at most two on A's six corners.

\hypertarget{a-useful-eight-cube-construction}{%
\subsection*{1. A useful eight-cube
construction}\label{a-useful-eight-cube-construction}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. A useful eight-cube construction}

Let D be a class meeting A in a single corner. Then

\begin{verbatim}
4A + D + any three sources disjoint from A
\end{verbatim}

has an opposite-target assembly.

To prove this, group the six corners of the reverse cut into the three
rows q→\{0,1\}, q=2,3,4. Each disjoint source sees all three rows or all
but one. D sees one corner in each of two different rows. We need a
four-position matching on two rows; four A then supply the forward half.

If two of the three disjoint sources miss the same row, choose the other
two rows. Those two sources are universal on the chosen half. D meets
it, and the third source has at least two corners in it, so D and that
source can be assigned distinct corners and the universal two finish. If
no missing row occurs twice, choose any two rows met by D. After D fills
a corner, the three other sources satisfy Hall on the other three: each
has a corner, any two see at least two, and all three see all three.

A related statement applies when a class T meets A in two corners of one
row of A. Such a T sees just one reverse corner, in some row q. The same
construction works unless all three disjoint sources are the unique
Z-class missing q. If two miss a different row, avoid that row; if
exactly two miss q, the third supplies the other corner of row q. The
case with no repeated missing row is as above.

\hypertarget{hall-cover-reduction}{%
\subsection*{2. Hall cover reduction}\label{hall-cover-reduction}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Hall cover reduction}

By Konig's theorem, matching rank at most two means that the nine
outside sources' incidences with A can be covered by at most two source
or corner vertices.

Two source vertices would leave at least seven disjoint sources,
contradicting z≤5. One corner plus one source vertex leaves at least
eight cubes in the union of the disjoint family and the unique class D
meeting A in that corner. Since D≤5, there are at least three disjoint
sources; since z≤5, D is nonempty. Section1 solves immediately.

It remains that all outside intersections lie in two A corners. Up to
the stabilizer of A, these form a column, a diagonal, or a row of its
two-by-three array.

\hypertarget{column-and-diagonal-covers}{%
\subsection*{3. Column and diagonal
covers}\label{column-and-diagonal-covers}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Column and diagonal covers}

For a column, normalize the two corners to03,13. The
nonzero-intersection classes are D0=C(024) and D1=C(124). If z≥3,
Section1 solves. Otherwise their counts total at least seven, so each is
at least two. Four A, two D0 and two D1 form the opposite target with
partition01∣24: A supplies02,04,12,14; D0 supplies21,41; D1
supplies20,40.

For a diagonal, normalize to03,14. The nonzero classes are D0=C(024) and
D1=C(123). Again z≤2. If both counts are at least three, build the
opposite target with partition04∣13:

\begin{verbatim}
A:03,14; D0:01,41,43; D1:10,30,34.
\end{verbatim}

Otherwise their counts are2 and5, and z=2. By symmetry D1 has count2. If
a disjoint source is B,Z23,orZ34, it supplies30 or34 in the displayed
target, leaving only two of D1's three positions. If instead the
disjoint sources are Z24, build the target01∣24 using

\begin{verbatim}
A:02,04,12,14; D1:20; D0:21,41; Z24:40.
\end{verbatim}

Thus column and diagonal covers always solve.

\hypertarget{row-cover-numerical-reduction}{%
\subsection*{4. Row cover: numerical
reduction}\label{row-cover-numerical-reduction}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Row cover: numerical reduction}

Normalize the row to13,14. Besides the disjoint sources, the allowed
outside classes are

\begin{verbatim}
D=C(124), E=C(123), T=C(12),
\end{verbatim}

with counts b,c,d.~Thus b+c+d+z=9. The shared-four bounds give b+d≤7 and
c+d≤7.

If z≥3 and D or E is present, Section1 solves. If both are absent, d≤5
forces z≥4. Choose three disjoint sources not all of the exceptional
type Z34; this is possible since each disjoint class has count≤3. The
second construction in Section1, applied to T, solves. Hence z≤2.

Suppose z=2. For a target with partition01∣24, four A supply the forward
half, one D supplies40, and another nonzero source supplies20. The two
disjoint copies can fill21,41 unless both are Z23 or both are Z34.
Similarly a target01∣23 works using E unless both disjoint copies are
Z24 or both are Z34. If both b,c are positive, the only shared exception
is two Z34, and then use target01∣34: four A supply its forward half, D
supplies40, E supplies30, and the two Z34 supply31,41. Thus that case
also solves. Since b+c+d=7\textgreater d's cap, at least one is
positive. Up to symmetry the only remaining forms have
b\textgreater0,c=0, b+d=7, and either two Z23 or two Z34.

If b≥4, reserve a same-repeat D pair. A disjoint source supplies20 (for
Z23) or40 (for Z34); two remaining D supply the other one of20,40 and43;
two T supply10,23; one A supplies13. If d≥4, reserve a same-repeat T
pair; two remaining T fill20,24, two D fill10,23, and two A fill13,14.
Since b+d=7 and both are at most five, one of these constructions
applies.

Consequently z≤1. A T-class of size at least four solves: reserve a
same-repeat pair, use two A at13,14, and match10,20,23,24 from the
remaining D,E,T copies. At least six such copies remain; 10 and20 see
all of them, while23 and24 each see at least two.

Likewise b≥4 solves unless b=4,d=0. Reserve two D for the bichromatic
positions and two for40,43; use A at13. The three remaining positions
10,20,23 can be matched because the residual D+T count at23 is b+d−4≥1
and at least four copies fit10,20. The symmetric statement holds for E.
If b=4,d=0, then c=5 when z=0 and the symmetric large-class construction
applies. When z=1, b=c=4. Every disjoint source meets at least one of
the private sets

\begin{verbatim}
{23,40,43} for D,    {24,30,34} for E.
\end{verbatim}

Use it there, two copies of the corresponding class at the other private
positions, two copies of the other class at10,20, and A at13 or14, after
reserving a same-repeat pair in the chosen four-copy class.

Thus every failure in this cover must satisfy b,c,d≤3. If z=0 then
b=c=d=3; if z=1 then (b,c,d) is a permutation of(3,3,2).

\hypertarget{row-cover-repeated-colour-contradiction}{%
\subsection*{5. Row cover: repeated-colour
contradiction}\label{row-cover-repeated-colour-contradiction}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. Row cover: repeated-colour
contradiction}

For all these residual counts, A,D,E,T are pairwise conflicting classes:
if two different classes share a repeated colour, a target can be
assembled. Also T has a loop: two T copies with the same repeat solve.
Here are direct verifications of these statements.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  A,D or A,E: reserve one from each for an A target. Four A fill
  02,03,04,12; remaining D,E,T copies fill13,14.
\item
  D,E: use the target of a three-copy class among D,E, reserving one
  from each. Two remaining copies of that target class fill its private
  pair40,43 or30,34. The other three non-A positions are supplied by T
  and the other class; one A supplies13 or14. If d=2, both b,c=3, so T
  fills23 (or24) and one of10,20, while the other class fills the
  remaining one.
\item
  A,T: use a T target. The reserved cubes supply poles1 and2. Two
  remaining A fill13,14; D,E,T match10,20,23,24.
\item
  D,T or E,T: use an A target, with poles0 and1 supplied by those two
  classes. Four A fill its private corners and other copies fill13,14.
\item
  Two T copies: use their T target. Two A fill13,14; D,E,T match the
  other four tricoloured corners. At least six copies remain in that
  pool.
\end{itemize}

The pole pairs are01,03,04,12 respectively. Therefore T uses d distinct
repeats, while each of A,D,E uses at least one, with all these colour
sets disjoint. This requires at least d+3 distinct colours. If d=3, it
requires six, impossible.

If d=2, then z=1 and b=c=3. The disjoint source meets40 or43, unless it
is Z23, in which case it meets30 and34. Consequently at least one of D,E
also has a loop: reserve two same-repeat cubes from that class, use its
last copy and the disjoint source for its two private corners, T at23
or24, the other class at10,20, and A at13 or14.

But A,D,E are pairwise conflicting and all have pole0, so none may
repeat0. A looped three-copy class among D,E needs three of the other
four colours; each of the other two classes needs a different colour.
This requires five colours from a four-colour set, a contradiction.

All Hall-cover cases are therefore impossible. This proves the theorem.

\hypertarget{arg-13}{%
\section{Argument 13: Six copies in one adjacent tricolour
class}\label{arg-13}}

This gives a self-contained hand proof of the concentration lemma used
elsewhere in the research notes.

\textbf{Lemma.} Every collection of fourteen five-colour cubes
containing at least six cubes from one adjacent tricoloured-corner class
is soluble.

Write C(S) for the directed cut S→Sᶜ, where \textbar S\textbar{} is two
or three. Its pole pair is its two-element side. Its three varieties are
indexed by their repeated colour, which lies outside that pair. Two
copies with the same repeat supply both bicoloured corners of either
orientation of the cut with that pole pair.

Normalize the chosen class to A=C(01). Three elementary observations
will be used.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The four adjacent classes disjoint from A in tricoloured corners are
  B=C(234), C=C(23), E=C(24), F=C(34). No opposite variety is disjoint
  from A. Four A and four copies of any one of these classes form an
  opposite target, by using four arcs in each direction between \{0,1\}
  and a suitable two-element subset of \{2,3,4\}.
\item
  The reverse cut B has six arcs grouped into three pairs q→\{0,1\},
  q∈\{2,3,4\}. A B source sees all three pairs, while C,E,F each miss
  one pair. Thus at least six sources from B,C,E,F, with each of C,E,F
  occurring at most three times, match all six arcs of B. Hall is
  immediate: a set of arcs confined to one pair has at least three
  neighbours; a set meeting two pairs sees every source.
\item
  Each intersection of another class with A has size0,1,2,4, or6. An
  intersection of size1 determines a unique adjacent class. Indeed, for
  an outset S the intersection has size
  \textbar S∩\{0,1\}\textbar·\textbar\{2,3,4\}\textbackslash S\textbar,
  which is one precisely when S consists of one of0,1 and two of2,3,4.
  Opposite sources have intersection size2 or4.
\end{itemize}

First suppose A occurs at least eight times. Reserve two copies with the
same repeated colour for the bicoloured corners and six further copies
for the tricoloured corners. If A occurs seven times and some outside
source shares a tricoloured corner with A, reserve a same-repeat pair,
use five A and that source for the six tricoloured corners. Otherwise
all seven outside sources lie in B,C,E,F. If one of those classes occurs
four times, the first observation solves. If not, the second observation
supplies the six tricoloured corners of B; two A copies with the same
repeat supply B's bicoloured corners. Hence no failure has a class of
size at least seven.

It remains to consider exactly six A copies. Reserve a same-repeat pair.
If two outside sources can be matched to two distinct A corners, the
four remaining A copies complete the target. Suppose this is not
possible. Let z be the number of outside sources disjoint from A. Each
of their four classes has at most three copies, or the first observation
solves. If z≥6, the second observation solves using two A for the
bicoloured corners of B.

Thus z≤5. At least three of the eight outside sources meet A. Since they
cannot match two distinct A corners, they all meet A in the same single
corner. By symmetry that corner is13, and all these sources belong to
D=C(124). Write m=8−z for their number. The previous paragraph excludes
m≥7, so 3≤m≤6.

The intersections of B,C,E,F with D are respectively

\begin{verbatim}
B: {20,40}; C: {20}; E: {20,23,40,43}; F: {40}.
\end{verbatim}

All are nonempty and avoid13, the corner supplied by A.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{m=6.} Reserve a same-repeat D pair. Four remaining D, one A
  for13, and any disjoint-from-A source for a different D corner fill
  its six tricoloured corners.
\item
  \textbf{m=5.} If two of the three disjoint sources match different D
  corners, use those two, one A for13, and three D for the remaining
  corners, after reserving a D pair. This fails only if all three
  disjoint sources are C, or all three are F. In the C case assemble the
  opposite target ±\{02,03,12,13\}: four A supply02,03,12,13; one D
  supplies20; and three C supply21,30,31. The F case is symmetric, using
  ±\{03,04,13,14\}.
\item
  \textbf{m=4.} If at least three of the four disjoint sources are E,
  reserve a D pair; the other two D, one A for13, and three E for three
  of20,23,40,43 complete the D target. Otherwise at most two are E.
  Three of the disjoint sources can then match21,30,31: the first corner
  sees B,C,E; the last two see B,C,F; the latter pool has at least two
  copies, the former at least one, and the union has four. Together with
  four A and one D for20, this assembles ±\{02,03,12,13\}.
\item
  \textbf{m=3.} There are five disjoint sources and each class count is
  at most three. The same two pools have respectively at least two and
  two sources, so three sources match21,30,31. Four A and one D again
  complete the same opposite target.
\end{itemize}

Every case gives an assembly, proving the lemma.

\hypertarget{arg-14}{%
\section{Argument 14: Four identical opposite copies}\label{arg-14}}

Status: a hand proof, using the six-in-one-adjacent-class lemma proved
in \protect\hyperlink{arg-13}{Argument 13}. The short
shared-four-corners lemma is recalled below. This does not classify
failures with at most three copies of every opposite variety.

\hypertarget{directed-corner-notation}{%
\subsection*{Directed-corner notation}\label{directed-corner-notation}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Directed-corner notation}

Colours are 0,1,2,3,4. Write \texttt{ab} for the directed tricoloured
corner a→b. An adjacent class is a cut C(S)=\{ab:a∈S,b∉S\}, with
\textbar S\textbar=2 or3. Its pole pair is the two-element side P of the
cut. There are three actual varieties in this class, indexed by their
repeated colour r∉P; their two additional corners are (r,r,p), p∈P. Two
copies having the same repeated colour can therefore supply both
bicoloured corners of that target class.

An opposite variety has eight tricoloured corners: both directions of a
K₂,₂ on four colours, omitting the repeated colour. Normalize an
opposite variety to O=±\{13,14,23,24\}, where ± adds reversed arcs.

We use the established fact that a fourteen-cube failure has at most
five copies in any adjacent class. Two adjacent classes sharing four
tricoloured corners have at most seven copies in total: if their total
were at least eight, choose a class with at least four copies, reserve
two with the same repeat for its bicoloured corners, use two remaining
copies of that class for its two private tricoloured corners, and fill
the four common corners from the remaining four copies of the two
classes. If the other class has fewer than four copies, the same
assignment still works because all remaining copies have all four common
corners. (The six-in-one-class lemma already handles the case of six or
more copies.)

\hypertarget{halls-cover-form}{%
\subsection*{Hall's cover form}\label{halls-cover-form}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Hall's cover form}

If a target with eight corner types cannot be assembled from fourteen
sources, Hall's theorem gives a subset C of its corners,
\textbar C\textbar=c≤7, such that at least 7+c sources have all their
target-compatible corners contained in C. If the target variety itself
occurs m times, these m copies do not belong to that group, so c≤7−m.

Every five-colour source has at least two corners of an opposite target;
its intersection with that target has size 2,4, or8. This follows
directly from the directed cut/biclique descriptions.

\hypertarget{five-identical-opposite-copies-suffice-in-a-fourteen-pool}{%
\subsection*{Five identical opposite copies suffice in a
fourteen-pool}\label{five-identical-opposite-copies-suffice-in-a-fourteen-pool}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Five identical opposite copies suffice
in a fourteen-pool}

Suppose m≥5 copies of O occur in a failure. The Hall cover has c≤2, so
c=2 and m=5. All nine other sources have their O-intersections contained
in this pair. Up to symmetry there are three nonempty possibilities:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  A row or column pair, e.g.~\{13,14\}: only the adjacent class C(01) is
  eligible, contradicting its bound five.
\item
  Two oppositely directed arcs on disjoint edges, e.g.~\{13,42\}: two
  adjacent classes sharing four corners are eligible, contradicting
  their combined bound seven.
\item
  A reversed pair, e.g.~\{13,31\}: precisely two other opposite
  varieties are eligible. Denote them U=±\{01,04,13,34\} and
  V=±\{02,03,12,13\}. If one occurs at least eight times it is itself an
  assembly; otherwise both occur at least twice. Four O, two U, and two
  V build T=±\{12,14,23,34\}: O supplies 14,41,23,32; U supplies 34,43;
  V supplies 12,21.
\end{enumerate}

Thus every opposite variety occurs at most four times in a failure.

\hypertarget{the-four-copy-theorem}{%
\subsection*{The four-copy theorem}\label{the-four-copy-theorem}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{The four-copy theorem}

\textbf{Theorem.} If a fourteen-cube failure contains four identical
opposite copies, its coarse profile is precisely O=4 and adjacent-class
counts 5,5. The two adjacent cuts share exactly one tricoloured corner.

Normalize the four copies to O. Hall gives c≤3. Covers with c≤2 are
impossible: the three eligible families above have capacities at most
5,7,8 respectively, whereas c=2 requires nine eligible sources. Thus
c=3, and all ten remaining sources have O-intersection contained in C.

There are five three-corner patterns up to the symmetries of O. Their
complete source lists are as follows. A cut is written by its source
side.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
C & Eligible adjacent cuts & Eligible opposite varieties \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
13,14,23 & 01; 124 & none \\
13,14,31 & 01 & U,V \\
13,14,32 & 01; 13; 013 & none \\
13,24,31 & 023; 23 & U,V \\
13,24,32 & none & none \\
\end{longtable}

The five patterns account for 8,16,16,8,8 of the 56 three-subsets,
respectively. This small geometric table can also be checked without
enumerating subsets: first distinguish whether C contains a reversed arc
pair, then whether its remaining arcs share an endpoint.

\hypertarget{first-row}{%
\subsubsection*{First row}\label{first-row}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{First row}

There are only two eligible adjacent classes. Each has capacity five, so
their counts are 5,5. They share only the corner 13. This is the
asserted configuration.

\hypertarget{second-row}{%
\subsubsection*{Second row}\label{second-row}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Second row}

Let g,u,v be the counts of C(01),U,V. The bounds g≤5 and u,v≤4, together
with g+u+v=10, force both u,v positive and at least one of them at least
two. If u≥2, assemble T=±\{12,14,23,34\} using four O for 14,41,23,32;
two U for 34,43; one V for 21; and one C(01) for 12. If v≥2, instead use
target T'=±\{03,04,23,24\}: four O supply 23,32,24,42; two V supply
03,30; one U supplies 40; and C(01) supplies 04. Both are assemblies, a
contradiction.

\hypertarget{third-row}{%
\subsubsection*{Third row}\label{third-row}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Third row}

Let X=C(01), Y=C(13), Z=C(013), with counts x,y,z. The pairs X,Z and Y,Z
share four corners, so x+z,y+z≤7. Since x+y+z=10, x,y≥3. At least one
count is at least four and hence that class contains two copies of one
repeated variety. Reserve those two for its bicoloured corners; the six
tricoloured corners can be filled as follows.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  If x=5, three X supply 02,03,04, one Y supplies 12, and two O supply
  13,14. If x=4, then z≥1: two X supply 02,03 and one Z supplies 04; the
  other assignments are the same.
\item
  If y=5, three Y supply 10,30,34, one X supplies 12, and two O supply
  32,14. If y=4, then z≥1: two Y supply 10,30 and one Z supplies 34; the
  other assignments are the same.
\item
  Otherwise z≥4, and the bounds force (x,y,z)=(3,3,4). Three X supply
  02,04,12, one Y supplies 34, and two O supply 32,14.
\end{itemize}

Each assignment uses distinct physical cubes and yields an assembly.

\hypertarget{fourth-row}{%
\subsubsection*{Fourth row}\label{fourth-row}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Fourth row}

Let X=C(023), Y=C(23), with counts a,b, and let u,v count U,V. We have
a,b≤5, a+b≤7, u,v≤4, and a+b+u+v=10, hence u+v≥3.

If u,v are both positive, use four O for 14,41,23,32, one U for 43, one
V for 12, and any two adjacent copies for 21,34. Both X and Y have both
of these last corners. There are at least two adjacent copies because
u+v≤8. This assembles T=±\{12,14,23,34\}.

Otherwise, by symmetry take v=0 and 3≤u≤4. Build target U. Three U
copies supply 10,40,43; two further copies from U∪X supply 01,04; one
remaining adjacent copy supplies 34; two O supply 13,31. These choices
are possible because u+a=10−b≥5 and a+b=10−u≥6.

\hypertarget{fifth-row}{%
\subsubsection*{Fifth row}\label{fifth-row}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Fifth row}

There is no eligible source, so the row is impossible.

This proves the coarse-profile theorem.

\hypertarget{arg-15}{%
\section{Argument 15: Elementary proofs for the two six-cut support
families}\label{arg-15}}

The support-poset statement concerns arbitrary adjacent cuts. The
butterfly profile statement concerns fourteen adjacent cubes with class
cap three. It is a conditional classification by \textbf{support
family}, not a proof that every fourteen-cube failure lies in one of
these families. In particular, it does not yet rule out a failure whose
tricoloured-corner union supports an opposite-repeat target.

Use colours \texttt{0,1,2,3,4}. An adjacent class is a directed complete
cut \texttt{S\ -\textgreater{}\ S\^{}c}, with
\texttt{\textbar{}S\textbar{}=2} or \texttt{3}. Its poles are its
two-element side, and the three varieties in the class are distinguished
by their repeated colour, which is not a pole. A variety with poles
\texttt{\{p,q\}} and repeated colour \texttt{r} has bichromatic corners
\texttt{(r,r,p)} and \texttt{(r,r,q)}.

A target cut is \textbf{robust} if its six tricoloured corners can still
be matched after any two source cubes are removed. Thus any two distinct
cubes supplying its two bichromatic corners complete a solution.

A robust triple in a failure must use each of its three allowed repeats
once. If another source has pole pair Q meeting its pole pair P in one
colour, that source must repeat the colour in \(P\setminus Q\).
Otherwise it shares an allowed repeat with a member of the triple, and
the two sources fill the robust target's bicoloured positions.

\hypertarget{why-there-are-only-two-support-families}{%
\subsection*{0. Why there are only two support
families}\label{why-there-are-only-two-support-families}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{0. Why there are only two support
families}

This reduction is also elementary; the enumeration of 4,231 labelled
posets is unnecessary.

For a collection of directed cuts, put \texttt{a\ \textless{}=\ b} when
the arc \texttt{a\ -\textgreater{}\ b} is absent from their union.
Equivalently every source outset containing \texttt{a} contains
\texttt{b}. Including reflexive pairs makes this a preorder. Break any
equivalence class into a linear order. The resulting partial order is
weaker, so all source outsets remain upsets; its incomparability graph
is unchanged. An opposite-repeat target is corner-supported exactly when
this incomparability graph contains a four-cycle (not necessarily
induced).

Consider a partial order on five points whose incomparability graph has
no four-cycle. It contains, as a weaker order, one of these three:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  two points below two other points (\texttt{K\_\{2,2\}}), with the
  fifth isolated;
\item
  a diamond \texttt{x\textless{}y,z\textless{}w}, with the fifth
  isolated;
\item
  a chain \texttt{x\textless{}y\textless{}z} together with
  \texttt{x\textless{}u} and \texttt{v\textless{}z}.
\end{itemize}

Here is a short completeness proof. If the height is at least four, a
four-chain contains the first order. If the height is three, choose
\texttt{x\textless{}y\textless{}z}, with remaining points \texttt{u,v}.
Suppose neither the first order nor a diamond occurs. Neither \texttt{u}
nor \texttt{v} is comparable with \texttt{y}, since such a comparison
gives two points below two others. Each can be above \texttt{x} or below
\texttt{z}, but not both, which would give a diamond. Avoiding an
incomparability four-cycle between \texttt{\{x,y\}} and \texttt{\{u,v\}}
forces one of \texttt{u,v} above \texttt{x}; avoiding one between
\texttt{\{y,z\}} and \texttt{\{u,v\}} forces one below \texttt{z}. They
are different, giving the third order.

If the height is at most two, split the points into nonisolated minima,
nonisolated maxima, and isolated points. An antichain has size at most
three, so these class sizes are \texttt{(2,2,1)}, \texttt{(2,3,0)}, or
\texttt{(3,2,0)}. In the first case every minimum must be below every
maximum: a missing comparison \texttt{a\textless{}z}, together with the
other minimum and the isolated point, gives an incomparability
four-cycle. In the \texttt{(2,3,0)} case each minimum has at least two
upper neighbours; otherwise it and the third maximum are incomparable
with the other two maxima. Two different two-element upper
neighbourhoods also give an incomparability four-cycle. Thus the minima
have two common upper neighbours, giving the first order. The last case
is dual.

The size-two and size-three upsets of the first order are exactly the
six butterfly cuts of Section 2. For a diamond, every such upset
contains its top and omits its bottom; conversely every size-two or
size-three subset with these properties is an upset. The same is true
for the third order: containing \texttt{x} would require four points,
and omitting \texttt{z} would leave at most one. Thus both of the latter
orders give exactly the six common-corner cuts of Section 1.

Consequently every all-adjacent collection whose corner union supports
no opposite-repeat target lies in one of the two support families below.

\hypertarget{the-common-corner-support}{%
\subsection*{1. The common-corner
support}\label{the-common-corner-support}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. The common-corner support}

For colours \(a,b,i,j,k\), the six cuts containing \(a\to b\) are
\(C(ai),C(aj),C(ak),C(aij),C(aik),C(ajk)\). Only this support
description is needed from the common-corner alternative here; its
numerical assembly lemmas are proved separately.

\hypertarget{butterfly-family-the-coarse-profile-is-forced}{%
\subsection*{2. Butterfly family: the coarse profile is
forced}\label{butterfly-family-the-coarse-profile-is-forced}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Butterfly family: the coarse
profile is forced}

Use pivot colour \texttt{0}, and partition the other four colours into
\texttt{P=\{1,2\}} and \texttt{Q=\{3,4\}}. Assume every class has at
most three copies. The six cuts are

\begin{verbatim}
C_1: {0,1} -> {2,3,4},   C_2: {0,2} -> {1,3,4},
C_3: {1,2,4} -> {0,3},   C_4: {1,2,3} -> {0,4},
U:   {1,2} -> {0,3,4},   V:   {0,1,2} -> {3,4}.
\end{verbatim}

Their counts are \texttt{x\_1,x\_2,x\_3,x\_4,u,v}. Define

\begin{verbatim}
L = x_1+x_2+v,       R = x_3+x_4+u,       L+R=14.
\end{verbatim}

The pole pair of \texttt{C\_i} is \texttt{\{0,i\}}. Those of
\texttt{U,V} are \texttt{P,Q}. Each block contains three classes of
capacity three. Thus \(L,R\ge5\).

\hypertarget{two-completion-criteria}{%
\subsubsection*{Two completion criteria}\label{two-completion-criteria}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Two completion criteria}

\textbf{Low cuts are robust.} For U, the arcs \(10,20\) have the common
source pool \(C_3\cup C_4\cup U\), of size \(R\ge5\). Each other arc's
pool omits at most two high classes and therefore has at least eight
sources. After any two reservations, a set confined to \(10,20\) has at
least three neighbours, while a set meeting any other arc has at least
six. Hall proves robustness of U; the argument for V is symmetric.

\textbf{High cuts have a completion when their block weight is at least
six.} For C\_1, after the two bicoloured reservations suppose at least
two C\_1 copies remain. Use two for its private arcs \(02,12\). The four
remaining arcs have pools

\begin{verbatim}
03,04: C_1,C_2,V;
13:    C_1,C_3,U,V;
14:    C_1,C_4,U,V.
\end{verbatim}

The first pool has size L; each singleton pool has at least \(14-3-3=8\)
sources. Removing the four reserved copies leaves at least two
neighbours for a subset of \(03,04\) when \(L\ge6\), and at least four
for any subset meeting \(13\) or \(14\). Hall completes the four
positions. The other high cuts are symmetric.

\hypertarget{the-low-cuts-are-robust}{%
\subsubsection*{The low cuts are robust}\label{the-low-cuts-are-robust}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{The low cuts are robust}

Each block contains three classes of capacity three. Since \(L+R=14\),
both block weights are at least five. The low-cut completion criterion
therefore makes both low cuts robust. In a failure, each uses every
repeated colour at most once.

\hypertarget{both-block-weights-are-at-least-six}{%
\subsubsection*{Both block weights are at least
six}\label{both-block-weights-are-at-least-six}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Both block weights are at least
six}

Suppose \(L=5\). Then \(R=9\), so \(x_3=x_4=u=3\). The robust low class
U uses repeats \(0,3,4\). Its repeat-restoration constraint forces every
C\_1 source to repeat 2 and every C\_2 source to repeat 1. The high cuts
C\_3,C\_4 have safe completion by their block weight nine and the
displayed high-cut criterion. No repeated colour can occur in both
\texttt{C\_3,C\_4}, nor in one of them and a positive \texttt{C\_1} or
\texttt{C\_2}: the two corresponding cubes would supply a high target's
bichromatic corners, leaving two private copies.

If one of \texttt{x\_1,x\_2} is at least two, any repeat \texttt{4} in
\texttt{C\_3} or repeat \texttt{3} in \texttt{C\_4} gives a solution in
that positive high class. Reserve that copy and the corresponding copy
of \texttt{U} for the bichromatic corners; use two copies of the chosen
high class for its private arcs; use two of the remaining \texttt{L-2=3}
sources in \texttt{C\_1,C\_2,V} for
\texttt{0-\textgreater{}3,0-\textgreater{}4}; and use the two remaining
\texttt{U} copies for its other two arcs. Avoiding this forces
\texttt{C\_3,C\_4} to use only repeats \texttt{1,2}; their repeat sets
are disjoint and nonempty, so one uses only \texttt{1} and the other
only \texttt{2}. Either positive class on the other side then shares a
repeat with one of them and gives a solution.

The only remaining possibility is \texttt{x\_1=x\_2=1,v=3}. Now
\texttt{V} has repeats \texttt{0,1,2}; its repeat-\texttt{1} copy and
the \texttt{C\_2} copy provide the bichromatic corners of the
\texttt{C\_3} target with repeat \texttt{1}, which has safe completion.
Thus \texttt{L=5} is impossible, and symmetrically so is \texttt{R=5}.

We have proved \texttt{L,R\textgreater{}=6}. Every high target therefore
has safe completion whenever two copies remain for its private arcs.

\hypertarget{repeated-colour-counting-forces-the-profile}{%
\subsubsection*{Repeated-colour counting forces the
profile}\label{repeated-colour-counting-forces-the-profile}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Repeated-colour counting forces the
profile}

If a nonpivot colour \texttt{q} occurs as a repeat in \texttt{U},
robustness of \texttt{U} forbids that repeat in \texttt{C\_1,C\_2}. Also
\texttt{x\_1+x\_2=L-v\textgreater{}=3}, so one of these high classes has
at least two copies. Any high-class copy repeating \texttt{q} could pair
with the \texttt{U} copy to provide that target's bichromatic corners,
with safe completion. Therefore \texttt{q} is absent from \textbf{all}
high classes. Symmetrically, any nonpivot repeat in \texttt{V} is absent
from all high classes.

Furthermore a repeated colour used by a high class of size three cannot
occur in another high class: one copy from each would supply the
size-three class's bichromatic corners, leaving two private copies.

Let \texttt{k} be the number of colours still available as high-class
repeats. If \texttt{1\textless{}=k\textless{}=3}, the total
\texttt{H=x\_1+x\_2+x\_3+x\_4} is at most \texttt{k+7}. Indeed, if
\texttt{t} high classes have size three, they use disjoint nonempty
repeat sets, so \texttt{t\textless{}=k}. If \texttt{t\textless{}=k-1},
then \texttt{H\textless{}=3t+2(4-t)\textless{}=k+7}. If \texttt{t=k},
every other high class is empty, so \texttt{H=3k\textless{}=k+7}. If
\texttt{k=0}, then \texttt{H=0}.

Suppose one low class has at least two copies. If both low classes are
nonempty, their distinct repeats exclude at least \texttt{u+v-2}
nonpivot colours, so \texttt{k\textless{}=6-u-v\textless{}=3}. The bound
gives

\begin{verbatim}
H <= 13-u-v < 14-u-v = H,
\end{verbatim}

a contradiction (and \texttt{k=0} is immediate). If one low class is
empty, say \texttt{v=0,u\textgreater{}=2}, then
\texttt{k\textless{}=5-u\textless{}=3}, giving the even stronger
\texttt{H\textless{}=12-u\textless{}14-u}. Thus
\texttt{u,v\textless{}=1}.

Finally \texttt{H\textless{}=12} and total fourteen force

\begin{verbatim}
(x_1,x_2,x_3,x_4,u,v) = (3,3,3,3,1,1).
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Butterfly profile lemma.} A failing fourteen-pool in this
family, with every class of multiplicity at most three, has profile
\(3,3,3,3,1,1\). No repeated-colour enumeration is needed for this
statement.

\hypertarget{arg-16}{%
\section{Argument 16: Three identical opposite cubes plus the reverse
six-cut family}\label{arg-16}}

\hypertarget{statement}{%
\subsection*{Statement}\label{statement}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Statement}

Let \texttt{O} be the opposite-repeat variety with repeated colour
\texttt{0} and opposite-pair partition
\texttt{\{1,2\}\textbar{}\{3,4\}}. A collection consisting of three
copies of \texttt{O} and eleven adjacent-repeat cubes from root classes
\texttt{0,3,10,12,15,17} always solves.

These six adjacent classes are exactly the directed cuts containing the
common arc \texttt{1\ -\textgreater{}\ 2}. Write them as follows, with
counts \texttt{a,b,c,d,e,f}:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Name & Outset & Poles & Root class \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{A\_0} & \texttt{\{0,1\}} & \texttt{\{0,1\}} & 0 \\
\texttt{A\_3} & \texttt{\{1,3\}} & \texttt{\{1,3\}} & 10 \\
\texttt{A\_4} & \texttt{\{1,4\}} & \texttt{\{1,4\}} & 12 \\
\texttt{B\_0} & \texttt{\{1,3,4\}} & \texttt{\{0,2\}} & 3 \\
\texttt{B\_3} & \texttt{\{0,1,4\}} & \texttt{\{2,3\}} & 15 \\
\texttt{B\_4} & \texttt{\{0,1,3\}} & \texttt{\{2,4\}} & 17 \\
\end{longtable}

We may assume every adjacent class count is at most five, by the
established six-class lemma. No shared-four pair bound is needed below.

\hypertarget{an-opposite-target-construction-forces-two-zero-classes}{%
\subsection*{1. An opposite-target construction forces two zero
classes}\label{an-opposite-target-construction-forces-two-zero-classes}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. An opposite-target construction
forces two zero classes}

Consider the opposite target with repeated colour \texttt{1} and
partition \texttt{\{0,2\}\textbar{}\{3,4\}} (root target 63). Four of
its arcs are
\texttt{0-\textgreater{}3,0-\textgreater{}4,3-\textgreater{}0,4-\textgreater{}0}.

The first pair can be matched from \texttt{A\_0,B\_3,B\_4} precisely
when

\begin{verbatim}
a+e >= 1,   a+f >= 1,   a+e+f >= 2.             (1)
\end{verbatim}

The second pair can be matched from the disjoint class set
\texttt{B\_0,A\_3,A\_4} precisely when

\begin{verbatim}
d+b >= 1,   d+c >= 1,   d+b+c >= 2.             (2)
\end{verbatim}

If both pairs can be matched, reserve these four adjacent cubes. Every
adjacent class except \texttt{A\_0} supplies at least one of the four
remaining target arcs
\texttt{2-\textgreater{}3,2-\textgreater{}4,3-\textgreater{}2,4-\textgreater{}2}.
Since \texttt{a\textless{}=5}, there are at least six non-\texttt{A\_0}
cubes; at least two remain after the four reservations. Use one for any
such arc, and use the three copies of \texttt{O} for the other three
arcs. This gives a solution.

Hence one of the two blocks fails its two-corner matching. The symmetry
interchanging colours \texttt{1,2} and reversing all arrows swaps the
blocks, so suppose (1) fails. Thus

\begin{verbatim}
a+e+f <= 1,   or   a=e=0,   or   a=f=0.         (3)
\end{verbatim}

The first possibility is impossible in a failure. It would give
\texttt{b+c+d\textgreater{}=10}, so one of \texttt{b,c,d} is at least
four. Any such class is robust in this situation, as the inequalities in
the next section show, and a robust class of size at least four supplies
a solution.

Swapping colours \texttt{3,4} interchanges the last two possibilities in
(3). We may therefore assume

\begin{verbatim}
a=e=0.                                        (4)
\end{verbatim}

\hypertarget{robustness-leaves-just-one-count-pattern}{%
\subsection*{2. Robustness leaves just one count
pattern}\label{robustness-leaves-just-one-count-pattern}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Robustness leaves just one count
pattern}

A cut is robust if its six tricoloured corners survive arbitrary removal
of two source cubes. The three copies of \texttt{O} are included in
these matching counts. The following are the nonautomatic Hall
conditions for the four classes needed here:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & Conditions for robustness \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{A\_3} & \texttt{b+d\textgreater{}=3},
\texttt{b+f\textgreater{}=3}, \texttt{b+c+d\textgreater{}=4},
\texttt{b+d+f\textgreater{}=4}, \texttt{b+c+d+f\textgreater{}=5} \\
\texttt{A\_4} & \texttt{c+d\textgreater{}=3},
\texttt{c+e\textgreater{}=3}, \texttt{b+c+d\textgreater{}=4},
\texttt{c+d+e\textgreater{}=4}, \texttt{b+c+d+e\textgreater{}=5} \\
\texttt{B\_0} & \texttt{b+d\textgreater{}=3},
\texttt{c+d\textgreater{}=3}, \texttt{b+c+d\textgreater{}=5} \\
\texttt{B\_4} & \texttt{a+f\textgreater{}=3},
\texttt{b+f\textgreater{}=3}, \texttt{a+b+f\textgreater{}=4},
\texttt{a+e+f\textgreater{}=4}, \texttt{a+b+e+f\textgreater{}=5} \\
\end{longtable}

These are obtained directly from the six arcs of the indicated cut: any
tested subset meeting \texttt{O} has three additional neighbours. The
total-count condition is automatic because there are eleven adjacent
sources. The table also justifies the preceding paragraph when
\texttt{a+e+f\textless{}=1}.

Under (4), put \texttt{Y=b+c+d=11-f\textgreater{}=6}. Any one of
\texttt{b,c,d} of size at least four satisfies its row of the table and
gives a solution. Thus each is at most three. If \texttt{f=5}, its class
is robust. If \texttt{f=4}, its class is robust unless \texttt{b=0}, but
then \texttt{c+d=7} contradicts \texttt{c,d\textless{}=3}. Therefore
\texttt{f\textless{}=3} as well.

The four counts \texttt{b,c,d,f} sum to eleven and are at most three.
Exactly one equals two and the other three equal three.

\hypertarget{three-placements-of-the-two-are-impossible-by-repeat-constraints}{%
\subsection*{3. Three placements of the two are impossible by repeat
constraints}\label{three-placements-of-the-two-are-impossible-by-repeat-constraints}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Three placements of the two are
impossible by repeat constraints}

A robust class of size three in a failure has one copy of each of its
three varieties. If a source's pole pair meets the robust pole pair in
one colour, its repeated colour is forced to be the unmatched pole of
the robust class; otherwise the two corresponding copies provide that
robust target's two bichromatic corners.

If \texttt{d=2} or \texttt{f=2}, both \texttt{A\_3,A\_4} have size three
and are robust. Their pole pairs \texttt{\{1,3\}} and \texttt{\{1,4\}}
meet. One robust class therefore forces the other to repeat a single
colour, contradicting its three distinct repeats.

If \texttt{b=2}, the classes \texttt{A\_4,B\_0} are robust of size
three, with disjoint pole pairs \texttt{\{1,4\}} and \texttt{\{0,2\}}.
Every \texttt{B\_4} copy, with poles \texttt{\{2,4\}}, would have to
repeat \texttt{1} because of \texttt{A\_4}, and repeat \texttt{0}
because of \texttt{B\_0}. This is impossible.

It remains that

\begin{verbatim}
(b,c,d,f) = (3,2,3,3).
\end{verbatim}

\hypertarget{the-final-forced-case-has-an-explicit-eight-cube-solution}{%
\subsection*{4. The final forced case has an explicit eight-cube
solution}\label{the-final-forced-case-has-an-explicit-eight-cube-solution}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. The final forced case has an
explicit eight-cube solution}

Now \texttt{A\_3,B\_0} are robust of size three. Their repeats are
respectively \texttt{\{0,2,4\}} and \texttt{\{1,3,4\}}. The
pole-intersection rule forces both \texttt{A\_4} copies to repeat
\texttt{3}, and all three \texttt{B\_4} copies to repeat \texttt{0}.

Build an adjacent target of class \texttt{A\_4} with repeated colour
\texttt{0}:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Target corner & Source \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
bichromatic \texttt{(0,0,1)} & the repeat-\texttt{0} copy of
\texttt{A\_3} \\
bichromatic \texttt{(0,0,4)} & one \texttt{B\_4} copy \\
\texttt{1\ -\textgreater{}\ 0} & another \texttt{A\_3} copy \\
\texttt{1\ -\textgreater{}\ 2}, \texttt{4\ -\textgreater{}\ 0},
\texttt{4\ -\textgreater{}\ 2} & the three \texttt{B\_0} copies \\
\texttt{1\ -\textgreater{}\ 3}, \texttt{4\ -\textgreater{}\ 3} & the two
\texttt{A\_4} copies \\
\end{longtable}

This uses eight distinct adjacent cubes; none of the three opposite
cubes is needed in the final construction. Every possible case has
therefore yielded a solution.

\hypertarget{arg-17}{%
\section{Argument 17: Butterfly repeats and concentrated-opposite
tools}\label{arg-17}}

\hypertarget{notation}{%
\subsection*{1. Notation}\label{notation}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Notation}

Let the colours be V=\{0,1,2,3,4\}. Write \texttt{ab} for the directed
tricoloured corner a→b and β(r,p) for a bichromatic corner whose
repeated colour is r and whose singleton colour is p.~An adjacent class
is the directed cut δ⁺(S)=\{ab:a∈S,b∉S\}, where \textbar S\textbar=2 or
3. Its pole pair is the two-element side, and it has three physical
varieties, according to the repeated colour outside the pole pair. A
variety with poles \{p,q\} and repeated r has bichromatic corners
β(r,p),β(r,q). Write O(r;P\textbar Q) for the opposite-repeat variety
whose eight corners are the two directions of the four edges between P
and Q.

A target is constructible precisely when its eight corner positions can
be assigned injectively to available cubes possessing those corners. All
constructions below explicitly give such assignments or use elementary
matching in small disjoint pools.

\hypertarget{a-concentrated-opposite-extension}{%
\subsection*{2. A concentrated-opposite
extension}\label{a-concentrated-opposite-extension}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. A concentrated-opposite extension}

Write \(O=O(0;12\mid34)\), \(A=C(01)\), and \(B=C(124)\).

\hypertarget{a-useful-extension-lemma}{%
\subsubsection*{A useful extension
lemma}\label{a-useful-extension-lemma}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{A useful extension lemma}

\textbf{Lemma.} Three O, five A, five B, and any additional variety Y≠O
are soluble.

The four A-private corners are P\_A=\{02,03,04,12\}; the four B-private
corners are P\_B=\{10,20,40,43\}. Every Y≠O contains a corner in
P\_A∪P\_B. For an adjacent cut avoiding P\_A, membership of 0 in its
outgoing side would force membership of 2,3,4, which is impossible. With
0 excluded, avoiding P\_B excludes 1,2,4 too, leaving an outgoing side
of size at most one. For an opposite variety, avoiding the union
requires omitting vertex 0 because every edge incident to 0 occurs in
the union in some direction. The omitted vertex is therefore 0; avoiding
edges12 and34 then forces precisely O.

If Y meets P\_A, choose two same-repeat A cubes for the two bichromatic
corners of their A target; such a pair exists among five A cubes and
three repeated colours. The remaining three A cubes cover the other
three A-private corners, Y covers the selected one, and two O cubes
cover 13,14. The B case is identical.

\hypertarget{the-pure-adjacent-family-nine-derangements}{%
\subsection*{3. The pure-adjacent family: nine
derangements}\label{the-pure-adjacent-family-nine-derangements}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. The pure-adjacent family: nine
derangements}

Partition V=P⊔\{z\}⊔Q, where \textbar P\textbar=\textbar Q\textbar=2.
Include one cube from each low class

\begin{verbatim}
L_P=δ⁺(P),  L_Q=δ⁺(P∪{z}),
\end{verbatim}

and three cubes from each of the four high classes C\_v, v≠z, where

\begin{verbatim}
C_v=δ⁺({z,v})         if v∈P,
C_v=δ⁺(V\{z,v})      if v∈Q.
\end{verbatim}

Every high class has pole pair \{z,v\}.

\textbf{Proposition.} If the collection fails, both low cubes repeat z,
and there is a derangement π of V\{z\} such that all three cubes in C\_v
repeat π(v).

For necessity, fix v∈P, write P=\{v,w\}, Q=\{q\_1,q\_2\}, and consider a
C\_v target. Its private tricoloured corners zw,vw require two C\_v
copies. Its other four tricoloured corners are zq\_1,zq\_2,vq\_1,vq\_2.
The disjoint pools C\_w∪L\_Q, C\_\{q\_1\},C\_\{q\_2\} have respectively
four, three, three copies and can supply respectively the first two,
third, fourth of these corners. Reserving any two cubes for bichromatic
corners leaves at least two, one, one in those pools, so they still
complete the target. The dual argument applies to v∈Q.

If repeated colour r occurs in two different high classes C\_v,C\_u, use
C\_v for β(r,v), C\_u for β(r,z), two remaining C\_v for its private
tricoloured corners, and the pool construction for the rest. Thus each
nonpivot colour can occur in at most one high class in a failure. Four
nonempty high classes use four nonpivot colours, so each class uses
exactly one repeated colour and these colours are distinct. The own pole
colour v cannot be repeated in C\_v; hence the resulting permutation is
a derangement.

If L\_P repeats r∈Q, choose v∈P and use L\_P for β(r,v), the unique high
class repeating r for β(r,z), two C\_v for the private tricoloured
corners, and the same pool completion. Therefore L\_P repeats z. By
duality L\_Q repeats z too.

\hypertarget{opposite-triangle-extension-lemmas}{%
\subsection*{5. Opposite-triangle extension
lemmas}\label{opposite-triangle-extension-lemmas}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. Opposite-triangle extension lemmas}

Use the following three opposite types:

\begin{verbatim}
X=O(0;12|34) [60],
Y=O(2;03|14) [67],
Z=O(4;01|23) [72].
\end{verbatim}

They share precisely the bidirected edge13 pairwise.

\textbf{Lemma A.} Three X, two Y, two Z, and any eighth cube T are
soluble.

For the target O(0;13\textbar24) {[}61{]}, two Y fill34,43; two Z
fill12,21; three X can fill any three of H\_1=\{14,41,23,32\}. Thus this
target works if T has any corner in H\_1. The target O(0;14\textbar23)
{[}62{]} gives the same assignment with H\_2=\{13,31,24,42\}. The sets
H\_1,H\_2 partition the eight corners of X, and every variety shares at
least two corners with X, proving the claim.

\textbf{Lemma B.} Three X, three Y, one Z, and an eighth cube T are
soluble unless T is X or Y.

The following three targets work whenever T supplies one of the
indicated two holes. For the other hole, interchange the two directions
in the final column. Entries are the corners assigned to each source
pool.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Target & Three X & Three Y & One Z & Hole supplied by T \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
O(0;14\textbar23) {[}62{]} & 13,42,24 & 43,31,34 & 21 & 12 (or21) \\
O(2;01\textbar34) {[}66{]} & 41,31,14 & 13,40,04 & 30 & 03 (or30) \\
O(4;03\textbar12) {[}74{]} & 32,31,23 & 13,10,01 & 20 & 02 (or20) \\
\end{longtable}

The six possible holes form the two directions of the path1--2--0--3.
Every adjacent cut crosses this path: avoiding it would put all four
path vertices on the same side, impossible for a two-three cut. An
opposite variety avoiding the path cannot omit the isolated colour4,
because the four remaining vertices would have to lie in one side of its
two-two partition. Nor can it omit a path endpoint, because the other
three path vertices would then have to lie in one side. Omitting the
internal vertex0 forces X; omitting the internal vertex2 forces Y. These
are the only exceptions.

\hypertarget{three-anchor-hall-covers-of-size-at-most-three}{%
\subsection*{6. Three-anchor Hall covers of size at most
three}\label{three-anchor-hall-covers-of-size-at-most-three}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{6. Three-anchor Hall covers of size at
most three}

Suppose a fourteen-cube failure contains three copies of X {[}60{]}.
Assuming the proved caps (each adjacent class≤5, each shared-four
pair≤7, each opposite variety≤3 unless the four-anchor branch applies),
a Hall obstruction for target X gives a cover of at most four of its
corners. For size c, at least 7+c nonanchor cubes have their entire
intersection with X in the cover. Covers of size two are impossible by
the caps. The five size-three cover orbits have representatives below,
using X corner order 13,14,23,24,31,32,41,42. Class labels are outgoing
sets, not numeric indices.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Cover & Permitted adjacent classes & Permitted opposite types \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
13,14,23 & 01,124 & none \\
13,14,31 & 01 & Y,Z \\
14,23,31 & none & none \\
13,24,31 & 023,23 & Y,Z \\
14,24,31 & 123,023,23 & none \\
\end{longtable}

The first row forces five copies in each class and one outside cube T≠X,
so the extension lemma of Section2 applies. The second row forces at
least two each of Y,Z, so Lemma A applies. The third is empty.

For the fourth row write u,v for the two class counts and p,q for Y,Z.
If u+v≥6 and one of u,v≥4, choose a repeated-colour pair in the larger
class for its two bichromatic corners. Two more cubes from that class
cover its two private tricoloured corners (01,04 for023, or20,30 for23).
The other class supplies21,34, using a fifth cube in the larger class if
necessary, while two X supply24,31. Hence failure requires u+v≤5 or
u=v=3. Since u+v+p+q≥10 and p,q≤3, the first alternative gives p,q≥2, so
Lemma A applies. In the second alternative p+q≥4. Again Lemma A applies
unless \{p,q\}=\{3,1\}. This final case has exactly ten cubes in the
cover and one cube T outside, and Lemma B applies because T is neither X
nor the opposite type of multiplicity three.

For the fifth row an equivalent normal form is classes U=01, V=13,
W=013, with counts u,v,w. We have u+v+w≥10, u+w≤7, v+w≤7, each≤5, so
u,v≥3 and at least one class has count≥4. Reserve two same-repeat cubes
in such a class for the two bichromatic positions. The following
tricoloured completions use only two X:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Case/target & Remaining U & Remaining V & Remaining W & X \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
u=5, U & 02,03,04 & 12 & --- & 13,14 \\
u=4, U & 02,03 & 12 & 04 & 13,14 \\
v=5, V & 12 & 10,30,34 & --- & 32,14 \\
v=4, V & 12 & 10,30 & 34 & 32,14 \\
w≥4, W & 02,04,12 & 34 & --- & 32,14 \\
\end{longtable}

In the last row the caps force w=4,u=v=3. Thus every size-three cover is
eliminated. Thus a failing collection with three identical opposite
cubes must have a size-four Hall cover. The arguments above do not use
the subsequent analysis of those four-corner covers.

\hypertarget{arg-18}{%
\section{Argument 18: Hand elimination of three-anchor cover
orbit27}\label{arg-18}}

Use three copies of X=O(0;12\textbar34). The allowed adjacent classes
and counts are

\begin{verbatim}
A=01 (a), B=123 (b), C=023 (c), D=23 (d),
\end{verbatim}

and the other opposite types are Y=O(2;03\textbar14) {[}67{]} and
Z=O(4;01\textbar23) {[}72{]}, with counts p,q. Thus

\begin{verbatim}
a+b+c+d+p+q=11,
a,b,c,d≤5,  p,q≤3,  b+d≤7,  c+d≤7.
\end{verbatim}

The opposite-triangle extension lemmas give p+q≤3 in a failure. If both
counts are at least two, the 3X+2Y+2Z core plus any extra cube solves.
If one count is three and the other positive, the 3,3,1 core plus a cube
different from its two large types solves; such an extra cube exists by
the caps and the fourteen-cube total. Consequently at least eight
adjacent cubes remain.

\hypertarget{a-four-position-hall-reduction}{%
\subsection*{1. A four-position Hall
reduction}\label{a-four-position-hall-reduction}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. A four-position Hall reduction}

Consider target T=O(3;04\textbar12) {[}71{]}. Corners41,42 are supplied
only by X among the allowed groups, so reserve two X for them. The third
X can fill14 or24. Every adjacent cube also fits14 or24, and at least
eight adjacent cubes exist. Therefore it suffices to match the four
positions

\begin{verbatim}
Q={01,02,10,20}
\end{verbatim}

to distinct nonanchor cubes. Once this is done, an unused adjacent cube
and the third X fill14,24. The intersections with Q are:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Source & Available positions \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
A & 02 \\
B & 10,20 \\
C & 01 \\
D & 20 \\
Y & 01,10 \\
Z & 02,20 \\
\end{longtable}

Every three-position subset has at least six neighbours (it excludes at
most one adjacent class of count≤5), and all four have eleven
neighbours. Among the six pairs, four are automatically safe: their
neighbour counts are a+c+p+q=11-b-d≥4; a+b+p+q=11-c-d≥4;
b+c+d+p+q=11-a≥6; and a+b+d+q=11-c-p≥3. The other two pairs have
neighbour counts b+c+p and b+d+p+q.

Thus if T cannot be assembled, at least one of the following conditions
holds:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  c=p=0 (position01 missing);
\item
  a=q=0 (position02 missing);
\item
  b=p=0 (position10 missing);
\item
  b=d=q=0 (position20 missing);
\item
  b=c=0,p=1 (01 and10 have only one common source);
\item
  b=1,d=p=q=0 (10 and20 have only one common source).
\end{enumerate}

For the last two cases, we have assumed all individual positions
supported; any other pair deficiency is already included among1--4. This
is a direct four-position Hall calculation, with no multiplicity
enumeration.

\hypertarget{all-cases-with-a0-are-soluble}{%
\subsection*{2. All cases with a\textgreater0 are
soluble}\label{all-cases-with-a0-are-soluble}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. All cases with a\textgreater0 are
soluble}

A useful second target is Z itself. Reserve two X for13,31. Its other
six positions split into L=\{02,03,12\} and R=\{20,21,30\}. A fits all
of L; B fits20,30; C fits21; D fits all of R; Y fits none; and Z fits
all six. Hall's condition for these two blocks is satisfied if

\begin{verbatim}
a+q≥3,
b+d+q≥2,
c+d+q≥1,
a+b+d+q≥5,
a+c+d+q≥4.                         (*)
\end{verbatim}

The two additional full-block inequalities are automatic: R has 11-a-p≥3
neighbours and all six positions have11-p≥8. The five displayed
inequalities are the strongest requirements for L, \{20,30\}, \{21\},
L∪\{20,30\}, and L∪\{21\}, respectively; all smaller subsets follow.

Assume a\textgreater0. Condition2 of the previous section is impossible.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  In condition1, c=p=0. The pair cap b+d≤7 gives a+q≥4. The total gives
  b+d+q=11-a≥6 and a+d+q=11-b≥6. Also d+q≥1, since otherwise a+b=11
  would contradict the class caps. All of(*) hold, so Z is assembled.
\item
  In condition3, b=p=0. Now c+d≤7 gives a+q≥4, while a+d+q=11-c≥6 and
  c+d+q=11-a≥6. Thus(*) hold unless d+q≤1. In that exception a+c≥10; the
  caps and total force a=c=5,d+q=1. A same-repeat pair among the five A
  cubes fills an A target's bichromatic positions; the remaining three A
  fill02,03,12, one C fills04, and two X fill13,14.
\item
  In condition4, b=d=q=0 and a+c+p=11. If p≤2, one of a,c is five. Five
  A with any C solve as just described. Five C with any A similarly
  solve: reserve a same-repeat C pair; use C:01,21,34, A:04, X:24,31. If
  p=3, then a,c≥3, and target Y is assembled by Y:10,40,43; X:13,31;
  C:01,34; A:04.
\item
  In condition5, b=c=0,p=1 and a+d+q=10. Since a,d≤5, both a+q and d+q
  are at least five, so every inequality(*) holds.
\item
  In condition6, the total forces a=c=5. The five-A construction above
  applies.
\end{itemize}

Hence every collection in this cover with a\textgreater0 is soluble.

\hypertarget{the-cases-with-a0}{%
\subsection*{3. The cases with a=0}\label{the-cases-with-a0}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. The cases with a=0}

Suppose first that q\textgreater0. Conditions2 and4 are impossible.
Condition1 would give b+d+q=11, contradicting b+d≤7 and q≤3. Condition3
gives the same contradiction with c+d.~Condition5 would give d+q=10,
contradicting d≤5, q≤3. Condition6 requires q=0. Therefore the
four-position matching succeeds and T is assembled. A failure must have
q=0.

Now a=q=0 and b+c+d+p=11. The pair caps imply

\begin{verbatim}
b+p≥4,  c+p≥4.
\end{verbatim}

If p=2, then b,c≥2. Assemble target Y by Y:40,43; X:13,31; C:01,04;
B:10; and any further B,C,D cube at34. If p=3, then b,c≥1; use
Y:40,43,01; X:13,31; C:04; B:10; and a further adjacent cube at34. The
extra cube exists because at least eight adjacent cubes are available.

It remains p≤1, so b+c+d≥10 and b,c≥3. Some class has count at least
four. Reserve two same-repeat copies in that class for its bichromatic
positions; the following table supplies its six tricoloured positions:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Case/target & Tricoloured placements \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
b=4, B & B:10,20; D:30; C:34; X:14,24 \\
b=5, B & B:10,20,30; C:34; X:14,24 \\
c=4, C & C:01,04; D:21; B:34; X:24,31 \\
c=5, C & C:01,04,21; B:34; X:24,31 \\
d≥4, D & D:21,34; B:20,30; X:24,31 \\
\end{longtable}

When b=4 or c=4, the other two counts sum at least six and neither
exceeds five, so d≥1 as required. In the final row at least two D remain
after the reserved pair, and b≥3 supplies the two B. All cases therefore
solve.

This closes cover orbit27 using only the established caps, two
elementary Hall calculations on four and six positions, and explicit
corner assignments.

\hypertarget{arg-19}{%
\section{Argument 19: Hand elimination of three-anchor cover orbits 23,
53, and 102}\label{arg-19}}

Use X=O(0;12\textbar34) three times. All adjacent-class counts are at
most five; each other opposite variety has count at most three. A
shared-four adjacent pair has total at most seven. The missing-pair
bound says that the total number of cubes omitting a fixed bichromatic
colour pair is at most seven.

\hypertarget{mask23}{%
\subsection*{Mask23}\label{mask23}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Mask23}

The allowed groups are A=01 (a), B=124 (b), Y=O(2;03\textbar14) (p), and
Z=O(4;01\textbar23) (q), with a+b+p+q=11.

The opposite-triangle extension lemmas show p+q≤3 in a failure. Indeed,
p,q≥2 gives the 3,2,2 core, while one count3 and the other positive
gives the 3,3,1 core plus an additional cube different from its two
large types. Such an additional cube must exist since every opposite
count is at most3.

Therefore a+b≥8. If a=5, any Y or Z meets an A-private corner; two
same-repeat A fill the bichromatic positions, three A fill three private
corners, Y/Z fills the fourth, and two X fill13,14. There is necessarily
a Y or Z because a+b≤10. The same argument handles b=5. Thus a=b=4 and
p+q=3. If p≥2, make a B target: reserve two same-repeat B for its
bichromatic positions; the two remaining B and two Y fill its four
private corners10,20,40,43 (Y fits10,40,43); two X fill13,23. If q≥2,
the symmetric A target works, because Z fits three of the four A-private
corners02,03,04,12. One of p,q is at least two, completing the proof.

\hypertarget{mask53}{%
\subsection*{Mask53}\label{mask53}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Mask53}

The adjacent classes are A=03 and B=124, with counts a,b. The allowed
opposite types and counts are

\begin{verbatim}
U=O(1;03|24) [64], u;
Y=O(2;03|14) [67], y;
Z=O(4;01|23) [72], z;
V=O(4;02|13) [73], v;
W=O(4;03|12) [74], w.
\end{verbatim}

All eleven nonanchor cubes lie in these groups. The adjacent varieties
with poles03 and the opposite varieties U,Y,W omit the bichromatic
pair03. Consequently

\begin{verbatim}
a+b+u+y+w≤7,
\end{verbatim}

and hence z+v≥4. Since z,v≤3, both are positive.

If z,v≥2, use three X, two Z, and two V. These seven cubes can fill any
seven corners of target W. To see this, W's corners split into three
pairs/blocks:

\begin{verbatim}
P={01,10}, Q={02,20}, R={13,31,23,32}.
\end{verbatim}

Two V cover P, two Z cover Q, and three X cover three corners of R. This
handles a hole in R. If the hole lies in P, the spare V fills23 or32 and
X fills the other three R corners. If it lies in Q, the spare Z fills13
or31 and X fills the other three R corners. Any eighth cube shares a
corner with W, so it fills one of the permissible holes and completes W.

The remaining count pattern is \{z,v\}=\{3,1\}. The colour permutation
(1 2) exchanges Z,V and U,Y while preserving X,A,B,W, so take z=3,v=1.
Three X fill23,31,32 of W; three Z fill13,02,20; one V fills one
of01,10. Any extra cube having01 or10 fills the other. Every cube of
A,B,Y,W has at least one of those corners. If none of those groups
occurs, the remaining seven nonanchor cubes must all be U, contradicting
u≤3. Thus W is constructible.

\hypertarget{mask102}{%
\subsection*{Mask102}\label{mask102}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Mask102}

The adjacent classes are

\begin{verbatim}
A=13 (a), B=024 (b), C=24 (c), D=013 (d),
\end{verbatim}

and the allowed opposite types are

\begin{verbatim}
E=O(0;13|24) [61], e;
U=O(1;03|24) [64], u;
V=O(2;04|13) [68], v;
Y=O(3;01|24) [69], y;
Z=O(4;02|13) [73], z.
\end{verbatim}

Their counts total eleven. Set F=a+d, R=b+c, K=u+v, L=y+z. The
shared-four bound gives F,R≤7 and the individual opposite bound gives
K,L≤6. This orbit does not require the missing-pair bound.

\hypertarget{halls-condition-for-e}{%
\subsubsection*{Hall's condition for E}\label{halls-condition-for-e}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Hall's condition for E}

Every allowed source intersects target E in at least four corners; E
itself intersects it in all eight. Thus a set of at least five target
corners has all fourteen cubes as neighbours. A set of at most three
corners meeting X has its three anchor cubes as neighbours. A set of
four corners meeting X also has a nonanchor neighbour: otherwise all
eleven nonanchor cubes would have exactly the same four-corner
intersection with E. The only groups with identical four-corner
intersections are A,D (total F≤7) and B,C (total R≤7); each of U,V,Y,Z
has its own distinct intersection and count≤3. Hence eleven such cubes
cannot exist.

It remains to inspect subsets of

\begin{verbatim}
Q=E\X={12,21,34,43}.
\end{verbatim}

The intersections of the nonanchor groups with Q are:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Group & Available corners of Q \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
F=A∪D & 12,34 \\
R=B∪C & 21,43 \\
K=U∪V & 34,43 \\
L=Y∪Z & 12,21 \\
E & all four \\
\end{longtable}

Any pair in Q has at least four neighbouring cubes: a same-row pair
omits only F or R (each at most seven of eleven), a same-column pair
omits only K or L (each at most six), and a diagonal pair meets every
group. Any set of three or four corners meets every group. Consequently
the only possible Hall failure for E is an entirely unsupported single
corner of Q:

\begin{verbatim}
F+L+e=0, F+K+e=0, R+L+e=0, or R+K+e=0.
\end{verbatim}

Global arc reversal exchanges F,R; the colour permutation (1 3)(2 4)
exchanges K,L while preserving F,R. We may therefore assume

\begin{verbatim}
F=L=e=0.
\end{verbatim}

Thus only B,C,U,V remain, with b+c+u+v=11. Since b+c≤7, u+v≥4. Since
u,v≤3, both are positive.

\hypertarget{three-explicit-completions}{%
\subsubsection*{Three explicit
completions}\label{three-explicit-completions}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Three explicit completions}

If u,v≥2, take any B or C cube T, which exists because b+c≥5. Make
target O(1;02\textbar34) {[}63{]} with X:24,32,42; U:04,40; V:03,30;
T:23.

If u=3,v=1, then b+c=7 and b,c≤5 give b,c≥2. Make the same target63 with
X:24,32,42; U:23,40,04; V:30; B:03.

If u=1,v=3, again b+c=7 gives b,c≥2. Make target O(2;01\textbar34)
{[}66{]} with X:41,31,13; U:04; V:14,30,03; C:40.

These cases exhaust u+v≥4 with u,v≤3, proving that mask102 has no
failure.

All three orbit proofs use only the stated caps and explicit assignments
or the elementary four-position Hall argument above. No multiplicity
search is needed.

\hypertarget{arg-20}{%
\section{Argument 20: Hand elimination of three-anchor cover orbits 30
and 51}\label{arg-20}}

Notation is as in \protect\hyperlink{arg-17}{Argument 17}: an adjacent
class is named by its outgoing set; X=O(0;12\textbar34) is available
three times. Adjacent counts are at most five, shared-four pairs have
combined count at most seven, and each other opposite type has count at
most three. Corner labels \texttt{ab} mean a→b.

\hypertarget{mask30-four-adjacent-classes}{%
\subsection*{Mask30: four adjacent
classes}\label{mask30-four-adjacent-classes}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Mask30: four adjacent classes}

The classes and counts are

\begin{verbatim}
A=02 (a), B=123 (b), C=023 (c), D=23 (d),
a+b+c+d=11,
a+c≤7, b+d≤7, c+d≤7.
\end{verbatim}

No other opposite type occurs.

\hypertarget{a-class-of-count-at-least-four-gives-a-target}{%
\subsubsection*{A class of count at least four gives a
target}\label{a-class-of-count-at-least-four-gives-a-target}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{A class of count at least four
gives a target}

Two cubes repeating the same colour exist in every class of at least
four; reserve them for that class's two bichromatic corners.

For a C target, two X fill24,31; the remaining four tricoloured corners
are 01,04,21,34. If c=5, the three remaining C cubes fill three of these
and any other adjacent cube fills a suitable fourth. If c=4, two
remaining C fill two positions. Since b≤5 and a+b+d=7, at least two
other cubes lie in A∪D. Each A fits three of the four positions, and
each D fits21,34, so these two cubes can fill distinct positions; the
two C fill the rest. Thus c≥4 solves. The dual argument gives d≥4: two X
fill24,31 and the residual four positions are20,21,30,34; B
fits20,30,34, C fits21,34, and a≤5 guarantees two B∪C cubes when d=4.

We may therefore suppose c,d≤3. For an A target, two X fill23,24. The
other four positions are01,03,04,21. Position03 requires A, while C
fits01,04,21 and D fits21. Consequently a=5 solves whenever c+d≥1; a=4
solves whenever c≥1 and c+d≥2. The former condition follows from b≤5 and
total11. In the latter case, c+d=1 with c≥1 would force b=6, impossible.
The sole exception is c=0; then b+d=7 with d≤3, so b≥4 and d≥2. For a B
target, two X fill14,24 and the remaining four positions10,20,30,34 are
filled by the remaining B and D cubes: b=4 leaves two B and at least two
D, while b=5 leaves three B and at least one D. Thus the exception also
solves. Interchanging A,B and C,D treats b≥4.

\hypertarget{the-remaining-counts-are-3332}{%
\subsubsection*{The remaining counts are
3,3,3,2}\label{the-remaining-counts-are-3332}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{The remaining counts are 3,3,3,2}

Every class of count three must use three distinct repeated colours.
Indeed, if a repeated pair occurs, reserve it for the two bichromatic
positions and complete the six tricoloured positions as follows (all
class counts are now at least two):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Target & Tricoloured placement \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
A & A:03; C:01,04; D:21; X:23,24 \\
B & B:10; D:20,30; C:34; X:14,24 \\
C & A:01,04; D:21; B:34; X:24,31 \\
D & B:20,30; C:21,34; X:24,31 \\
\end{longtable}

If c=d=3, both classes contain repeated colour0. Use C\_0 and D\_0 for
the bichromatic corners β(0,1),β(0,2) of the target with outgoing set12.
Its six tricoloured positions are filled by B:10,14; D:20,24; X:13,23.
Two D remain after reserving D\_0.

If c=2 or d=2, then a=b=3. Both classes contain repeated colour1. Use
A\_1 for β(1,2) and B\_1 for β(1,0), making an A target. Its tricoloured
positions are filled by A:03; C:01,04; D:21; X:23,24. Thus every case
solves.

\hypertarget{mask51-a-three-leaf-star-and-two-opposite-types}{%
\subsection*{Mask51: a three-leaf star and two opposite
types}\label{mask51-a-three-leaf-star-and-two-opposite-types}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Mask51: a three-leaf star and two
opposite types}

The classes and counts are

\begin{verbatim}
A=01 (a), B=03 (b), C=13 (c), D=013 (d),
Y=O(2;03|14) (p), Z=O(4;01|23) (q),
a+b+c+d+p+q=11,
a+d≤7, b+d≤7, c+d≤7.
\end{verbatim}

The permutation (1 3)(2 4) fixes X,C,D and exchanges A with B and Y with
Z.

\hypertarget{opposite-targets-reduce-the-possibilities}{%
\subsubsection*{Opposite targets reduce the
possibilities}\label{opposite-targets-reduce-the-possibilities}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Opposite targets reduce the
possibilities}

First, p and q cannot both be positive. The triangle extension lemmas
already exclude p,q≥2, or one count3 and the other positive. The
remaining possibilities have p+q≤3 and at least eight adjacent cubes.
Make target O(0;13\textbar24): reserve X for41,23, Y for43, Z for21. Its
remaining positions are12,14,32,34. The third X can fill14 or32. A
fits12,14; B fits32,34; C,D fit all four. Three adjacent cubes can
supply the other three positions unless every adjacent cube lies in A or
every one lies in B. Those exceptions violate the class cap five because
at least eight adjacent cubes are available. More explicitly, if both A
and B occur use one at12 and one at34, and the third at14 or32; if only
one of A,B occurs, a C or D supplies the position on the other side, and
two of the many remaining adjacent cubes complete. If neither occurs,
three C/D cubes work. Thus p,q\textgreater0 is impossible.

By symmetry assume q=0. We show that the only possibilities not already
soluble by an opposite target are:

\begin{verbatim}
p=0; or
p=1, (a=0 or c=0), b≥3; or
p=2, c=0, a,b≥2.
\end{verbatim}

If p≥1 and a,c≥1, use target O(1;02\textbar34). Three X fill23,24,42;
one Y fills40; one A fills03; one C fills30. The remaining
positions04,32 have adjacent support A∪B∪D and B∪C∪D, respectively.
After reserving the A and C cubes, each of those pools has at least two
available cubes: there are at least eight adjacent cubes altogether and
neither a nor c exceeds five. Hence two distinct remaining cubes
fill04,32. Therefore a=0 or c=0.

For p=1 the adjacent total is10. If a=0, the pair caps b+d,c+d≤7 give
b,c≥3; if c=0, a+d,b+d≤7 give a,b≥3. In either case b≥3.

For p=2, suppose c\textgreater0. Then a=0, b+c+d=9, and the pair caps
give b,c≥2. Make target Y: two Y fill40,43; two X fill13,31; two B
fill01,04; two C fill10,34. Thus c=0. With a+b+d=9, the pair caps imply
a,b≥2.

For p=3, if a,c\textgreater0 the earlier target works. If c=0, then
a+b+d=8 and the pair caps give a,b≥1. Make target Y: three Y
fill10,40,43; two X fill13,31; the other three positions01,04,34 are
supplied by B and A∪B∪D. There are at least two cubes in B∪D, since
otherwise a≥7; one B fills01, another B/D fills34, and one further
adjacent cube fills04. If instead a=0 and c\textgreater0, then b,c≥1.
Two Y fill40,43, two X fill13,31, one B fills01, and one C fills10. The
third Y fills one of04,34, and an additional B/C/D cube fills the other;
such a cube exists because b+c+d=8. Hence p=3 always solves.

\hypertarget{completion-after-a-repeated-pair-in-a-large-adjacent-class}{%
\subsubsection*{Completion after a repeated pair in a large adjacent
class}\label{completion-after-a-repeated-pair-in-a-large-adjacent-class}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Completion after a repeated pair in
a large adjacent class}

There are at least eight adjacent cubes in all remaining cases. Reserve
a same-repeat pair in a class of count≥4. The following pool
descriptions give the six tricoloured placements:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  A target: two X fill13,14; an A fills03; the three positions02,04,12
  remain. A fits all three, B fits02,04, C fits12, and D fits all three.
  For a=5 this is always possible. For a=4 it is possible whenever
  b+d\textgreater0: after using one further A, the other pools contain
  at least four cubes, and a B/D plus a suitable second cube fills two
  distinct remaining positions.
\item
  B target: the symmetric statement holds, with the sole potential
  exception b=4,a=d=0.
\item
  C target: two X fill14,32; two remaining C fill10,30. The other
  positions12,34 are supported by A∪D and B∪D, respectively. Thus c=4
  works when a+d,b+d\textgreater0; c=5 always works because its third
  remaining C fills one of12,34.
\item
  D target: two X fill14,32, leaving02,04,12,34. At least two D remain
  after reserving the pair. Every other adjacent class fits at least two
  of these positions; two such cubes can fill distinct positions and the
  D fill the rest. If d=5 only one other cube is needed. Thus d≥4 always
  works.
\end{itemize}

For p=0, total11 and the cap5 exclude every exception in this list. For
p=1, the adjacent total10 and either a=0 or c=0 again exclude all
exceptions: when a=0, b,c≥3 and any b=4 or c=4 forces d\textgreater0;
when c=0, a,b≥3. For p=2, c=0 and a,b≥2, so again every count≥4 is
soluble.

\hypertarget{collections-with-all-adjacent-counts-at-most-three}{%
\subsubsection*{Collections with all adjacent counts at most
three}\label{collections-with-all-adjacent-counts-at-most-three}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Collections with all adjacent
counts at most three}

For p=1 there would be only three nonzero adjacent classes, contributing
at most nine cubes, contradicting the adjacent total10. For p=2 the only
remaining profile is a=b=d=3,c=0. For p=0 the four counts are3,3,3,2.

The following useful core handles all cases with a,b≥3 and d≥1: three A,
three B, one D, and two X are always soluble. If a same-repeat pair
occurs among the A cubes, reserve it and use A:03; B:02,04; D:12;
X:13,14. A repeated pair among B is treated symmetrically. Otherwise A
and B each use all their three repeated colours and have a common
repeated colour r∈\{2,4\}. Reserve A\_r for β(r,1) and B\_r for β(r,0),
making an A target, and use the same six tricoloured placements. This
proves the core assertion.

It remains in the p=0 case that a=2,b=c=d=3, or its symmetric image. In
the first case a repeated pair in B would solve by B:01; A:02,04; D:34;
X:31,32; a repeated pair in D would solve by A:02,04; C:12; B:34;
X:14,32. Hence B and D each use all three permitted repeated colours and
both contain colour1. Use B\_1 and D\_1 for β(1,3),β(1,4), making the
adjacent target with outgoing set012. Its tricoloured positions are
A:03,13; B:04; D:14; X:23,24. All reserved and assigned cubes are
distinct. The symmetric case follows by (1 3)(2 4).

Thus mask51, like mask30, contains no fourteen-cube failure. No
multiplicity enumeration is used in these proofs; every case follows
from the stated caps, totals, and explicit corner assignments.

\hypertarget{arg-21}{%
\section{Argument 21: The O3 Hall-cover case 54}\label{arg-21}}

This note gives a hand proof for one cover case. The eight tiny matching
constructions below were found by computation; the deductions from them
are ordinary integer inequalities and do not use enumeration of count
vectors. Each construction can be checked directly against the
directed-corner model.

Use the root model's classes and opposite varieties. Let X=O60 have
three copies. Let A, B, C be adjacent classes 4, 10, 17, with counts
a,b,c, and let U=O64 and V=O73 have counts u,v. Thus

a+b+c+u+v=11,
