with a,b,c\textless=5, u,v\textless=3. The standard pair caps give

a+c\textless=7, b+c\textless=7, c+u\textless=7, b+v\textless=7.

Specifically, a+c and b+c are bounded by the shared-four adjacent-class
lemma. The bounds c+u\textless=7 and b+v\textless=7 are the
missing-colour-pair bounds for the unordered colour pairs \{2,4\} and
\{1,3\}, respectively, and follow from the eight-plus-three lemma. These
are independent inputs to the argument.

Geometrically, A is the cut \{0,3\}-\textgreater\{1,2,4\}, B the cut
\{1,3\}-\textgreater\{0,2,4\}, and C the cut
\{0,1,3\}-\textgreater\{2,4\}. The opposite sources X,U,V are the
bidirected complete bipartite graphs 12\textbar34, 03\textbar24,
and02\textbar13, respectively. Here pq\textbar rs means that every
directed edge between \{p,q\} and \{r,s\} is present, in both
directions.

The following eight-cube pools have an opposite-repeat assembly
regardless of the repeated-colour labels of A,B,C:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Core & Target \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
3X+2A+2B+U & O71 \\
3X+2C+U+2V & O61 \\
2X+A+2B+C+2V & O73 \\
3X+B+C+2U+V & O61 \\
3X+A+B+3U & O74 \\
3X+3A+U+V & O74 \\
X+A+B+2C+3V & O73 \\
X+2A+3B+2V & O73 \\
\end{longtable}

The target names denote bidirected graphs O71=04\textbar12,
O61=13\textbar24, O73=02\textbar13, O74=03\textbar12. The following
arc-to-source assignments verify the cores in the same order as the
table (each arrow consumes one cube):

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  01:A,02:A,10:B,14:B,20:U,24:X,41:X,42:X.
\item
  12:V,14:C,21:V,23:X,32:X,34:C,41:X,43:U.
\item
  01:A,03:V,10:B,12:C,21:V,23:X,30:B,32:X.
\item
  12:C,14:X,21:V,23:U,32:X,34:B,41:X,43:U.
\item
  01:A,02:U,10:B,13:X,20:U,23:U,31:X,32:X.
\item
  01:A,02:A,10:V,13:X,20:U,23:X,31:A,32:X.
\item
  01:A,03:V,10:V,12:C,21:V,23:X,30:B,32:C.
\item
  01:A,03:V,10:B,12:B,21:V,23:X,30:B,32:A.
\end{enumerate}

\hypertarget{a-failure-has-u0}{%
\subsection*{1. A failure has u=0}\label{a-failure-has-u0}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. A failure has u=0}

Suppose u\textgreater=1. The first core implies a\textless=1 or
b\textless=1.

If a\textless=1, then b+c\textless=7 and the total give
u+v\textgreater=3.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  If v\textgreater=2, the second core forces c\textless=1. Since
  b+v\textless=7, a+c+u\textgreater=4, so u\textgreater=2. If c=1, then
  b\textgreater=3 and the fourth core applies. If c=0, then
  a+u\textgreater=4 forces a=1,u=3, and the fifth core applies
  (b\textgreater=4).
\item
  If v=1, then u\textgreater=2. The total forces b,c\textgreater=1, so
  the fourth core applies.
\item
  If v=0, then u=3 and b+c=8-a\textless=7, so a=1 and b+c=7. The fifth
  core applies.
\end{itemize}

It remains to consider b\textless=1 and a\textgreater=2. Again
u+v\textgreater=3 follows from a+c\textless=7.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  If v=0, then u=3, b=1 and a+c=7; the fifth core applies.
\item
  If v\textgreater=1 and a\textgreater=3, the sixth core applies.
\item
  Otherwise a=2 and v\textgreater=1. If v\textgreater=2 and
  c\textgreater=2, the second core applies. If v=1, the total and
  c+u\textless=7 force b=1,c+u=7; hence c\textgreater=4,u\textgreater=2
  and the fourth core applies. The only other possibility c\textless=1
  is impossible because c+u+v=9-b\textgreater=8 but c+u+v\textless=7.
\end{itemize}

Thus u=0.

\hypertarget{a-failure-has-v1}{%
\subsection*{2. A failure has v\textless=1}\label{a-failure-has-v1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. A failure has v\textless=1}

If v=2, the total and a+c,b+c\textless=7 imply a,b\textgreater=2. For
c\textgreater=1 the third core applies; for c=0, a+b=9 implies
a,b\textgreater=4 and the eighth core applies.

If v=3, the same inequalities imply a,b\textgreater=1. For
c\textgreater=2 the seventh core applies. For c=1, a+b=7 and
a,b\textless=5 give a,b\textgreater=2, so the third core applies. For
c=0, a+b=8 gives a,b\textgreater=3, so the eighth core applies.

Consequently v\textless=1. Since a+c,b+c\textless=7,

a\textgreater=4-v\textgreater=3, b\textgreater=4-v\textgreater=3.

\hypertarget{a-class-of-size-at-least-four-supplies-an-adjacent-target}{%
\subsection*{3. A class of size at least four supplies an adjacent
target}\label{a-class-of-size-at-least-four-supplies-an-adjacent-target}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. A class of size at least four
supplies an adjacent target}

Now a+b+c=11-v\textgreater=10, so at least one class has size at least
four. Among its copies two have the same repeated colour; reserve them
for the two bichromatic corners of that class's target. The following
six-corner completions use only tricoloured corners and therefore ignore
every remaining repeated-colour label.

For A, the target corners are 01,02,04,31,32,34 (directed arcs). If
a\textgreater=4 and c\textgreater=1, use A for01,04; C for02; X
for31,32; B for34. If a\textgreater=4 and v\textgreater=1, instead use V
for01 and A for02,04. If a=5, use three A for 01,02,04, two X for31,32,
and B for34. When a=4, c+v\textgreater=1 follows from the total and
b\textless=5, so these cases suffice.

For B, the corners are10,12,14,30,32,34. If b\textgreater=4 and
c+v\textgreater=1, use B for10,30; C or V for12; X for14,32; A for34. If
b=5, use three B for10,12,30; two X for14,32; A for34. Again the total
forces c+v\textgreater=1 when b=4.

For C, the corners are02,04,12,14,32,34. If c\textgreater=4, use two A
for02,04; two remaining C for12,14; X for32; B for34. Here
a\textgreater=3 and b\textgreater=3 ensure the required supplies.

Thus every pool in cover case54 is soluble. The earlier computation
enumerated13 opposite-failing count vectors in this case, but that
number is not used anywhere in this proof.

\hypertarget{arg-22}{%
\section{Argument 22: Three identical opposite copies}\label{arg-22}}

Assume a fourteen-failure contains three copies of an opposite variety.
The four-copy theorem already handles any opposite variety occurring
four times; hence assume each opposite multiplicity is at most three.
Normalize the three copies to X=O(0;12\textbar34).

Hall's theorem supplies a cover C of at most four of X's eight corners,
with at least7+\textbar C\textbar{} nonanchor copies whose intersections
with X lie in C. The two-corner case is impossible by the established
caps. The five three-corner types are eliminated in Section6 of
\protect\hyperlink{arg-17}{Argument 17}. This uses the explicit
opposite-triangle extension lemmas from its preceding section.

If \textbar C\textbar=4, all eleven other sources belong to the
indicated cover family. The ten symmetry types are listed below. A bit
mask is only an index for the displayed corner subset, in
order13,14,23,24,31,32,41,42.

13,14,23,24 & 15 & 2 & \protect\hyperlink{arg-11}{Argument 11}: exactly
the5,3,3 family survives \\
13,14,23,31 & 23 & 8 & \protect\hyperlink{arg-19}{Argument 19} \\
13,14,24,31 & 27 & 16 & \protect\hyperlink{arg-18}{Argument 18} \\
14,23,24,31 & 30 & 8 & \protect\hyperlink{arg-20}{Argument 20} \\
13,14,31,32 & 51 & 8 & \protect\hyperlink{arg-20}{Argument 20} \\

The orbit sizes sum to70=binom(8,4). This is a geometric classification
of four directed arcs on a bidirected four-cycle, not an enumeration of
cube inventories. One can list it by the number of underlying edges
selected in both directions: zero, one, or two.

Only the first cover family can survive; its analysis forces three
identical opposite copies and adjacent-class sizes \(5,3,3\). Together
with the four-copy theorem, this proves the necessary coarse profiles
for failures with an opposite multiplicity at least three.

The arguments for covers53 and54 use the existing eight-plus-three
missing-pair lemma from the paper. All other inputs are established in
this bundle or are the elementary directed-corner geometry.

\hypertarget{arg-23}{%
\section{Argument 23: A common-corner 3,2,2,2,1 core always
assembles}\label{arg-23}}

\textbf{Lemma.} Suppose five distinct adjacent tricoloured classes
containing one common directed corner occur with multiplicities
\texttt{3,2,2,2,1}. These ten sources always admit an assembly,
irrespective of their repeated colours.

The proof consists of elementary six-corner completions and
repeated-colour pigeonhole arguments. It does not use a classification
of failed pools.

\hypertarget{notation-and-the-five-class-normal-form}{%
\subsection*{1. Notation and the five-class normal
form}\label{notation-and-the-five-class-normal-form}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Notation and the five-class normal
form}

Normalize the common corner to \texttt{01}, write
\texttt{Q=\{i,j,k\}=\{2,3,4\}}, and set

\[
P_i=C(0i),\qquad D_i=C(\{0\}\cup(Q\setminus\{i\})).
\]

Their pole pairs are \texttt{0i} and \texttt{1i}, respectively.
Reversing every cut and interchanging 0 and 1 exchanges the P and D
families. We may therefore suppose the absent class is \texttt{D\_i}.
The present classes and their corners are

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & Corners \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{P\_i} & \texttt{01,0j,0k,i1,ij,ik} \\
\texttt{P\_j} & \texttt{01,0i,0k,j1,ji,jk} \\
\texttt{P\_k} & \texttt{01,0i,0j,k1,ki,kj} \\
\texttt{D\_j} & \texttt{01,0j,i1,ij,k1,kj} \\
\texttt{D\_k} & \texttt{01,0k,i1,ik,j1,jk} \\
\end{longtable}

Write their multiplicities as \texttt{a,b,c,e,f}, respectively. Thus
these five positive numbers, in some order, are \texttt{3,2,2,2,1}.

An \textbf{own loop} means that, after reserving two copies of a class,
the other sources match all six corners of that class. In a failure, a
looped class must use a different repeated colour on every copy. Two
classes \textbf{conflict} if reserving one copy of each permits a
six-corner completion of a target whose two poles they can supply.
Conflicting classes must have disjoint repeated-colour sets.

We use the universal-restoration observation: a class with an own loop
conflicts with every present class whose pole pair meets its own.
Restore one reserved copy of the looped class and remove the other
class's reserved copy; the restored universal source replaces that
removed source if the six-corner matching had used it.

\hypertarget{four-useful-loop-completions}{%
\subsection*{2. Four useful loop
completions}\label{four-useful-loop-completions}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Four useful loop completions}

The following facts follow directly from the corner table.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{If \texttt{a\textgreater{}=2}, then \texttt{P\_i} has an own
  loop.} After reserving two \texttt{P\_i}, use \texttt{P\_j:0k},
  \texttt{P\_k:0j}, and an additional \texttt{P\_j} or \texttt{P\_k} at
  \texttt{01}. Such an additional copy exists because
  \texttt{b+c\textgreater{}=3}. Use \texttt{D\_j:ij}, \texttt{D\_k:ik},
  and one remaining source from \texttt{D\_j,D\_k,P\_i} at \texttt{i1}.
  There are enough because \texttt{e+f+a-2\textgreater{}=3}.
\item
  \textbf{If \texttt{b=3} and \texttt{f\textgreater{}=2}, then
  \texttt{P\_j} has an own loop.} After the reservation, match
  \texttt{P\_j:ji;\ D\_k:j1,jk;\ P\_i:0k;\ P\_k:0i;\ D\_j:01}.
\item
  \textbf{If \texttt{e=3}, then \texttt{D\_j} has an own loop.} After
  the reservation, use
  \texttt{D\_j:k1;\ P\_i:ij;\ P\_k:kj;\ D\_k:i1;\ P\_j:01}, and one
  additional \texttt{P\_i} or \texttt{P\_k} at \texttt{0j}. The last
  copy exists because \texttt{a+c\textgreater{}=3}.
\item
  \textbf{If \texttt{e=2} and \texttt{a=c=2}, then \texttt{D\_j} has an
  own loop.} Use \texttt{P\_i:0j,ij;\ P\_k:k1,kj;\ D\_k:i1;\ P\_j:01}
  after reserving the two \texttt{D\_j}.
\end{enumerate}

We now locate the unique triple. Interchanging \texttt{j,k} leaves three
possibilities: \texttt{P\_i}, \texttt{P\_j}, or \texttt{D\_j}.

\hypertarget{the-triple-is-p_i}{%
\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{3. The triple is
\texttt{P\_i}}{3. The triple is P\_i}}\label{the-triple-is-p_i}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. The triple is \texttt{P\_i}}

The four classes \texttt{P\_i,P\_j,D\_j,D\_k} form a conflict clique.

The edge \texttt{P\_i,P\_j} follows from the own loop of \texttt{P\_i}.
For the edge \texttt{P\_i,D\_j}, use target \texttt{P\_j}. After
reserving one of each, the two remaining \texttt{P\_i} fill
\texttt{01,0k}, and \texttt{P\_k} fills \texttt{0i}. The three other
target corners \texttt{j1,ji,jk} are filled by \texttt{P\_j,D\_k}: use a
\texttt{P\_j} at \texttt{ji}, then two remaining copies at
\texttt{j1,jk}. There are at least three copies altogether because
\texttt{b+f\textgreater{}=3}. The edge \texttt{P\_i,D\_k} is symmetric.

For any pair among \texttt{P\_j,D\_j,D\_k}, use target \texttt{D\_j}:
the reserved pair can supply its poles \texttt{1j}. The three
\texttt{P\_i} fill \texttt{0j,i1,ij}. Two sources from \texttt{P\_k} and
the unreserved \texttt{D\_j} fill \texttt{k1,kj}. At least two are
available since \texttt{c+e\textgreater{}=3} and at most one
\texttt{D\_j} was reserved. Any still unused source fills \texttt{01};
at this point only seven of the ten sources have been reserved or used.

The looped triple needs three distinct repeated colours, and each other
member of the four-class clique needs another colour. Six different
colours would be required, which is impossible.

\hypertarget{the-triple-is-p_j}{%
\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{4. The triple is
\texttt{P\_j}}{4. The triple is P\_j}}\label{the-triple-is-p_j}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. The triple is \texttt{P\_j}}

First suppose the singleton is \texttt{P\_k} or \texttt{D\_j}. Then
\texttt{a=f=2}, so \texttt{P\_i} and \texttt{P\_j} both have own loops.
They conflict because their pole pairs share 0. Their five required
repeated colours would all have to avoid 0, an impossibility.

If the singleton is \texttt{P\_i}, then \texttt{c=e=f=2}. The looped
triple \texttt{P\_j} conflicts with both \texttt{P\_i,P\_k}. These
latter two also conflict: reserve one of each and complete target
\texttt{P\_i} by

\[
D_j:i1,ij;\quad D_k:0k,ik;\quad
\text{remaining }P_k:0j;\quad P_j:01.
\]

The three classes need at least five different repeated colours, all
avoiding their common pole 0. This is impossible.

It remains that the singleton is \texttt{D\_k}, so \texttt{a=c=e=2}.
Both \texttt{P\_i} and \texttt{D\_j} have own loops. The four classes
\texttt{P\_i,P\_j,P\_k,D\_j} form a conflict clique. Own-loop
restoration gives the edges \texttt{P\_i,P\_j}, \texttt{P\_i,P\_k}, and
\texttt{D\_j,P\_j}. The other edges are witnessed by the following
completions, made after reserving the indicated pair.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Reserved pair
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Target
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Six-corner completion
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{P\_i,D\_j} & \texttt{P\_j} & remaining \texttt{P\_i:0k};
\texttt{P\_k:0i}; remaining \texttt{D\_j:01}; \texttt{P\_j:ji,j1};
\texttt{D\_k:jk} \\
\texttt{P\_k,D\_j} & \texttt{P\_j} & \texttt{P\_j:j1,ji,jk};
\texttt{P\_i:01,0k}; remaining \texttt{P\_k:0i} \\
\texttt{P\_j,P\_k} & \texttt{P\_j} & remaining \texttt{P\_j:ji,j1};
\texttt{D\_k:jk}; \texttt{P\_i:01,0k}; remaining \texttt{P\_k:0i} \\
\end{longtable}

The two looped doubles and the other two nonempty classes would require
\texttt{2+2+1+1=6} distinct repeated colours. This is impossible.

\hypertarget{the-triple-is-d_j}{%
\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{5. The triple is
\texttt{D\_j}}{5. The triple is D\_j}}\label{the-triple-is-d_j}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. The triple is \texttt{D\_j}}

If \texttt{P\_i} is not the singleton, then \texttt{a=2}. Thus
\texttt{P\_i} and \texttt{D\_j} are looped and both conflict with
\texttt{P\_j}. They also conflict with one another. To see this, reserve
one of each for target \texttt{P\_j}. Use remaining \texttt{P\_i:0k},
\texttt{P\_k:0i}, and remaining \texttt{D\_j:01}. The classes
\texttt{P\_j,D\_k} fill the other three corners \texttt{ji,j1,jk}, using
\texttt{P\_j} for \texttt{ji}; there are enough copies because
\texttt{b+f\textgreater{}=3}. This three-class clique would require
\texttt{2+3+1=6} distinct repeats.

Finally let \texttt{P\_i} be the singleton, so \texttt{b=c=f=2}. We show
that \texttt{D\_j,P\_i,P\_j,P\_k} form a clique. The edge
\texttt{D\_j,P\_j} follows from the own loop of \texttt{D\_j}. The other
five edges have these completions.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Reserved pair
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Target
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Six-corner completion
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{P\_i,P\_j} & \texttt{P\_i} & \texttt{D\_j:0j,i1,ij};
\texttt{D\_k:0k,ik}; \texttt{P\_k:01} \\
\texttt{P\_i,P\_k} & \texttt{P\_i} & \texttt{D\_j:0j,i1,ij};
\texttt{D\_k:0k,ik}; \texttt{P\_j:01} \\
\texttt{P\_j,P\_k} & \texttt{P\_j} & remaining \texttt{P\_j:ji};
\texttt{D\_k:j1,jk}; \texttt{P\_i:0k}; remaining \texttt{P\_k:0i};
\texttt{D\_j:01} \\
\texttt{P\_i,D\_j} & \texttt{P\_j} & \texttt{P\_j:ji,j1};
\texttt{D\_k:jk,0k}; \texttt{P\_k:0i}; remaining \texttt{D\_j:01} \\
\texttt{P\_k,D\_j} & \texttt{P\_j} & \texttt{P\_j:ji,j1};
\texttt{D\_k:jk}; \texttt{P\_i:0k}; remaining \texttt{P\_k:0i};
remaining \texttt{D\_j:01} \\
\end{longtable}

The looped triple and the three other nonempty classes require
\texttt{3+1+1+1=6} distinct repeated colours. This final contradiction
proves the lemma.

\hypertarget{arg-24}{%
\section{Argument 24: Nine cubes occupying all six classes through one
corner}\label{arg-24}}

\textbf{Theorem.} Suppose all six adjacent tricoloured classes
containing a fixed directed corner occur. Any nine cubes from those
classes have an assembly. Two useful eight-cube configurations also
always assemble.

This is a hand proof. It uses only repeated-colour pigeonhole and the
explicit six-corner matchings below.

Normalize the common directed corner to \texttt{01}. Put
\texttt{Q=\{i,j,k\}=\{2,3,4\}} and write

\[
P_i=C(0i),\qquad D_i=C(0,Q\setminus\{i\}).
\]

Their pole pairs are respectively \texttt{0i} and \texttt{1i}. These six
cuts all contain \texttt{01}. Simultaneous cut reversal and interchange
of 0 and 1 interchanges the \texttt{P} and \texttt{D} families.

Call a class looped when two copies with the same repeated colour can
provide bicoloured corners and the remaining sources match its six
tricoloured corners. Call two classes joined when copies with the same
repeated colour can do the analogous job. In a failure, a looped class
uses different repeated colours on all its copies; joined classes have
disjoint repeated-colour sets.

We use one elementary observation. If class \texttt{U} has an
own-orientation loop, it is joined to every present class \texttt{V}
whose pole pair meets its pole pair. Reserve one \texttt{U,V} for an
\texttt{U} target's bicoloured corners. Compared with the loop matching,
a \texttt{U} copy is restored and one \texttt{V} is removed; the
restored universal \texttt{U} replaces that \texttt{V} if it had been
used in the six-corner matching.

\hypertarget{a-triple-and-five-singletons-already-solve}{%
\subsection*{1. A triple and five singletons already
solve}\label{a-triple-and-five-singletons-already-solve}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. A triple and five singletons
already solve}

Take three \texttt{P\_i} and one copy of each other class. The class
\texttt{P\_i} has a loop, as witnessed after reserving two copies by

\[
P_i:i1,\quad P_j:0k,\quad P_k:0j,\quad
D_i:01,\quad D_j:ij,\quad D_k:ik.
\]

The four classes \texttt{P\_i,P\_j,D\_j,D\_k} are pairwise joined:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{P\_i,P\_j} are joined by the own-loop observation.
\item
  For \texttt{P\_i,D\_j}, use target \texttt{P\_j}: after reserving one
  of each, use remaining \texttt{P\_i:01,0k}, \texttt{P\_k:0i},
  \texttt{D\_i:ji}, \texttt{D\_k:jk}, and \texttt{P\_j:j1}. The
  \texttt{P\_i,D\_k} case is symmetric.
\item
  Any two of \texttt{P\_j,D\_j,D\_k} can supply the poles \texttt{1j} of
  target \texttt{D\_j}. The three \texttt{P\_i} fill \texttt{0j,i1,ij},
  while \texttt{P\_k} fills \texttt{kj}, \texttt{D\_i} fills
  \texttt{k1}, and the unused member of that three-class group fills
  \texttt{01}.
\end{itemize}

Thus \texttt{P\_i} requires three distinct repeats, and each of the
other three joined classes requires another repeat. Six different
colours would be needed. This proves the eight-cube statement with class
counts \texttt{3,1,1,1,1,1}. By symmetry a triple in any of the six
classes works.

\hypertarget{two-opposite-index-doubles-and-four-singletons-solve}{%
\subsection*{2. Two opposite-index doubles and four singletons
solve}\label{two-opposite-index-doubles-and-four-singletons-solve}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Two opposite-index doubles and four
singletons solve}

Take two \texttt{P\_i}, two \texttt{D\_j} with \texttt{i!=j}, and one of
each other class. Both doubled classes have own loops:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Reserved class & Six-corner completion \\
\midrule\noalign{}

\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
two \texttt{P\_i} & \texttt{P\_j:0k}; \texttt{P\_k:0j};
\texttt{D\_i:01}; \texttt{D\_j:i1,ij}; \texttt{D\_k:ik} \\
two \texttt{D\_j} & \texttt{P\_i:0j,ij}; \texttt{P\_k:kj};
\texttt{D\_i:k1}; \texttt{D\_k:i1}; \texttt{P\_j:01} \\
\end{longtable}

The classes \texttt{P\_i,D\_j,P\_j,D\_i} form a conflict clique. Four of
its edges follow from the own-loop observation: \texttt{P\_i} meets the
poles of \texttt{P\_j,D\_i}, and \texttt{D\_j} meets those of
\texttt{P\_j,D\_i}. The remaining two edges have these direct
completions after reserving the indicated pair:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}

  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}

\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Reserved pair

Target

Six-corner completion
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}

\bottomrule\noalign{}

\texttt{P\_i,D\_j} & \texttt{P\_j} & remaining \texttt{P\_i:0k};
\texttt{P\_k:0i}; \texttt{D\_i:ji}; \texttt{D\_k:jk}; remaining
\texttt{D\_j:01}; \texttt{P\_j:j1} \\
\texttt{P\_j,D\_i} & \texttt{P\_i} & \texttt{P\_i:01,0k};

\end{longtable}

The two looped classes require two repeats each, and the other two
classes require one each. Again six different colours would be needed.

\hypertarget{three-doubles-in-one-family-and-three-singletons-solve}{%
\subsection*{3. Three doubles in one family and three singletons
solve}\label{three-doubles-in-one-family-and-three-singletons-solve}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Three doubles in one family and
three singletons solve}

Take two of each \texttt{P\_i,P\_j,P\_k} and one of each
\texttt{D\_i,D\_j,D\_k}. All six classes are pairwise joined. Up to
permutations of \texttt{i,j,k}, there are four kinds of pair; these
completions cover all of them.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}

Reserved pair

Target

Six-corner completion

\texttt{P\_i,P\_j} & \texttt{P\_i} & remaining \texttt{P\_i:i1};
remaining \texttt{P\_j:0k}; \texttt{P\_k:0j}; \texttt{D\_i:01};
\texttt{D\_j:ij}; \texttt{D\_k:ik} \\

Six pairwise disjoint nonempty repeated-colour sets cannot fit into five
colours. Interchanging the \texttt{P} and \texttt{D} families gives the
symmetric case with three doubled \texttt{D} classes.

\hypertarget{deduction-for-any-nine-cubes}{%
\subsection*{4. Deduction for any nine
cubes}\label{deduction-for-any-nine-cubes}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Deduction for any nine cubes}

If any class has at least three copies, Section 1 applies. Otherwise
each class has one or two copies, and at least three classes are
doubled. If doubles occur in both families, there is an opposite-index
doubled pair \texttt{P\_i,D\_j} with \texttt{i!=j}: with at least three
doubled classes, not all cross-family pairs can share the same index.
Section 2 applies. If all doubles are in one family, all three classes
of that family are doubled, and Section 3 applies.

Thus nine cubes occupying all six common-corner classes always solve.

\hypertarget{arg-25}{%
\section{Argument 25: Pure fourteen-pools: the four-copy anchor, one-row
cover}\label{arg-25}}

\textbf{Proved by hand.} Suppose every adjacent class has at most four
copies, the pool is all-adjacent and has fourteen cubes, and A=C(01) has
four copies. Suppose every other source either is disjoint from A in
tricoloured corners or meets A only in the row 0→\{2,3,4\}. Then the
pool has an assembly.

Here C(S) is the cut S→Sᶜ. Put Q=\{2,3,4\}. Besides A the permitted
classes are

\begin{verbatim}
P_i=C(0i), D_i=C(0,Q\{i}), Z_i=C(Q\{i})  (i∈Q),
B=C(Q).
\end{verbatim}

Write p\_i,d\_i,z\_i,b for their counts and z=b+Σz\_i for the total of
the classes disjoint from A. There are ten nonanchor copies. Each
disjoint class has at most three copies: four A and four copies of any
such class build an opposite target. Also z≤5, since six disjoint copies
with individual counts≤3 match the six tricoloured corners of C(Q), and
two A with the same repeat supply its bichromatic corners.

\hypertarget{two-disjoint-from-a-sources-already-force-an-opposite-target}{%
\subsection*{1. Two disjoint-from-A sources already force an opposite
target}\label{two-disjoint-from-a-sources-already-force-an-opposite-target}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Two disjoint-from-A sources already
force an opposite target}

For q,s∈Q distinct, the opposite target with bipartition01\textbar qs is
supplied by four A in one direction and four other sources at

\begin{verbatim}
q→0, s→0, q→1, s→1.
\end{verbatim}

The first two positions see only the z zero-intersection sources. Let
n\_q count nonzero-intersection sources whose outset contains q. The
four-position Hall conditions reduce to

\begin{verbatim}
z−z_q≥1, z−z_s≥1,
z−z_q+n_q≥2, z−z_s+n_s≥2,
z+n_q≥3, z+n_s≥3.
\end{verbatim}

The union of all four neighbourhoods has size10−p\_r≥6, where
Q=\{q,s,r\}; every other required union follows from these inequalities.

If z≥3, choose q,s for which z−z\_q,z−z\_s≥2. At most one index can fail
this bound, because two counts z\_i≥z−1 would sum to more than z. The
displayed conditions hold, giving an assembly.

Suppose z=2. Let Z be the subset of Q appearing in the two zero-source
outsets, and N the subset appearing in the eight nonzero-source outsets.
Both have at least two elements: each zero outset has two or three
Q-elements, and eight nonzero sources cannot all be the single class
P\_i of capacity four. If \textbar Z∩N\textbar≥2, select q,s in the
intersection and again apply the displayed conditions.

The only remaining case is Z=\{q,s\}, N=\{s,r\}. The two zero sources
are both Z\_r, while all eight nonzero sources lie in P\_s,P\_r,D\_q.
The shared-four bounds p\_s+d\_q≤7 and p\_r+d\_q≤7 imply p\_s,p\_r≥1;
one of them is at least two. Assume p\_s≥2. Build the opposite target
with bipartition\{q,s\}\textbar\{1,r\}, omitting0:

\begin{verbatim}
two Z_r: q→1,q→r;
two P_s: s→1,s→r;
two A:   1→q,1→s;
one P_r: r→s;
another P_r or D_q: r→q.
\end{verbatim}

The last source exists because p\_r+d\_q=8−p\_s≥4. Thus every failure
would have z≤1.

\hypertarget{no-p-or-d-class-can-have-four-copies}{%
\subsection*{2. No P or D class can have four
copies}\label{no-p-or-d-class-can-have-four-copies}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. No P or D class can have four
copies}

First suppose d\_i=4, with Q=\{i,j,k\}. Four D\_i and two A fill six
corners of the opposite target1i\textbar jk; the remaining positions are
i→j and i→k. If two other cubes match these, we are done. Otherwise
p\_i≤1, and if p\_i=1 then d\_j=d\_k=0; if p\_i=0, at most one of
d\_j,d\_k is positive.

There are at least five nonzero sources outside D\_i. Hence
p\_j+p\_k\textgreater0: otherwise those sources are P\_i,D\_j,D\_k, and
the preceding restrictions give at most four of them. At least four
sources belong to P\_j,P\_k,D\_j,D\_k; each fits at least one of

\begin{verbatim}
j→1,j→i,k→1,k→i,
\end{verbatim}

and a source in P\_j or P\_k fits two. Two distinct sources therefore
match two distinct positions in this four-set. Reserve a same-repeat
pair from D\_i; its remaining two copies fill the other two positions.
One A fills0→i, and an unused nonzero source fills0→1. This completes
the D\_i target, a contradiction.

Now suppose p\_i=4. Reserve its same-repeat pair. Two A can fill0→j,0→k,
and the two remaining P\_i can fill two of0→1,i→1,i→j,i→k. Every other
nonzero source fits0→1; if d\_j+d\_k\textgreater0, one of them also fits
an i-row corner, so two other sources complete the four-set. The same
holds if the unique zero source contains i. Thus failure forces
d\_j=d\_k=0 and, when z=1, the zero source is Z\_i.

Use the opposite target1i\textbar jk again. Two A and two P\_i fill its
forward half. The reverse half has row capacities p\_j,p\_k and a
universal pool of size d\_i+z. These sum to six. Its four positions can
fail Hall only if p\_j+d\_i+z≤1 or p\_k+d\_i+z≤1, which would force the
other p-count to be at least five. This is impossible. Consequently
p\_i,d\_i≤3.

\hypertarget{the-remaining-opposite-target-hall-conditions}{%
\subsection*{3. The remaining opposite-target Hall
conditions}\label{the-remaining-opposite-target-hall-conditions}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. The remaining opposite-target Hall
conditions}

Ignore the zero source if present. There are N=10−z∈\{9,10\} nonzero
sources, in six classes of capacity three. For target1i\textbar jk, two
A fill 1→j,1→k. Label its six other positions

\begin{verbatim}
a=i→j, b=i→k, c=j→1, d=j→i, e=k→1, f=k→i.
\end{verbatim}

The six source neighbourhoods are

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & Positions \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
P\_i & a,b \\
P\_j & c,d \\
P\_k & e,f \\
D\_i & c,d,e,f \\
D\_j & a,e \\
D\_k & b,c \\
\end{longtable}

All Hall inequalities are automatic from N≥9 and the capacity-three
bounds except

\begin{verbatim}
p_i+d_j≥1, p_i+d_k≥1, p_j+d_i≥1, p_k+d_i≥1;
p_i+d_j+d_k≥2, p_j+p_k+d_i≥2;
p_j+d_i+d_k≥2, p_k+d_i+d_j≥2.                 (*)
\end{verbatim}

Classifying the zero coordinates in these six counts gives a short
description if all three opposite targets fail:

\textbf{Family I.} The main positive classes are P\_j,P\_k,D\_i, where
\{i,j,k\}=Q. Their counts are at least two and sum to at least eight.
There may be one additional main class of count one.

\textbf{Family II.} P\_i,D\_j,D\_k each have count three, every other
main count is zero, and z=1.

Here are details of that classification. With zero or one zero
coordinate, some choice of i satisfies(\emph{). With exactly two zeros,
the matched pair p\_i=d\_i=0 also allows target i, as do two zero
p-counts. If d\_j=d\_k=0, all targets can fail only when p\_i=1, giving
Family I. If p\_i=d\_j=0 with i≠j, the third target k succeeds unless
d\_k=1, again Family I. With at least three zeros, N≥9 and the
capacity-three bound force exactly three nonzero counts, all three.
Three P-counts, three D-counts, or a mixed triple whose minority index
matches one of the majority indices solve directly by(}). The two
unmatched mixed triples are exactly Families I,II.

\hypertarget{both-families-contradict-repeated-colour-pigeonhole}{%
\subsection*{4. Both families contradict repeated-colour
pigeonhole}\label{both-families-contradict-repeated-colour-pigeonhole}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Both families contradict
repeated-colour pigeonhole}

In Family I put U=P\_j,V=P\_k,W=D\_i. Their three counts are at least
two, and at least two counts equal three. Any class here of size three
must use three distinct repeats in a failure. To see this, reserve a
same-repeat pair and use these tricoloured completions:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  U target: remaining U at j→k; two W at j→1,j→i; one V at0→1; two A
  at0→i,0→k.
\item
  V target: the symmetric assignment.
\item
  W target: remaining W at0→1; two U at j→1,j→i; two V at k→1,k→i; one A
  at0→i.
\end{itemize}

Moreover any two of U,V,W must use disjoint repeat sets. For U,V, one
cube from each repeating r supplies the bichromatic corners of the U
target; its tricoloured completion uses one further U, two W, one
further V and two A as above. For U,W, use their poles0 and1 to make an
A target: three A fill1→i,1→j,1→k, while one further W, one further U
and one V fill0→i,0→k,0→j. The V,W case is symmetric. Thus the two
size-three classes demand six different repeated colours, impossible in
a five-colour palette.

In Family II let U=P\_i,V=D\_j,W=D\_k. U must use three distinct
repeats: after reserving a repeated pair, two A fill0→j,0→k, one V fills
i→j, one W fills i→k, another V or W fills i→1, and the remaining U
fills0→1.

The zero source contains at least one of j,k; assume it contains k. Then
V also must use three distinct repeats. After reserving a V pair, the
remaining V fills k→j, the zero source fills k→1, three U fill
0→1,i→1,i→j, and one A fills0→j. Finally U and V cannot share a repeat:
reserve one from each for an A target's bichromatic corners; four A fill
the three1-row corners and0→i, and the remaining two U fill0→j,0→k.
Again six different repeats would be required.

Every case gives an assembly, proving the one-row-cover lemma.

\hypertarget{arg-26}{%
\section{Argument 26: Four copies of an adjacent class: the other two
three-corner covers}\label{arg-26}}

This supplements \protect\hyperlink{arg-25}{Argument 25}. All assertions
below are hand matching arguments in the directed-cut model; the
computation was used only to find the cases. Let \texttt{C(S)} denote
the cut from \texttt{S} to its complement. Fix

\[
A=C(01),\qquad a=4,
\]

and assume every class has at most four copies. A failure cannot have
four copies each of disjoint cuts, or more than seven copies in two cuts
sharing four arcs. In particular, the latter bound will be used for
\texttt{P=C(04)} with each of \texttt{D=C(034)} and \texttt{F=C(024)}.

The four cuts disjoint from \texttt{A} are

\[
B=C(234),\quad Z_2=C(34),\quad Z_3=C(24),\quad Z_4=C(23).
\]

Write \texttt{z} for their combined multiplicity. We use the established
half-array lemma: four \texttt{A}, one cut meeting \texttt{A} in a
single arc, and any three cuts disjoint from \texttt{A} build an
opposite target. Thus \texttt{z\textless{}=2} whenever a singleton
intersection class is present.

An opposite target written \texttt{ab\textbar{}cd} asks for all eight
arcs between its two pairs, in both directions. A statement assigning
several arcs to one class always uses that many distinct copies.

\hypertarget{hook-cover-020312}{%
\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{1. Hook cover
\texttt{\{02,03,12\}}}{1. Hook cover \{02,03,12\}}}\label{hook-cover-020312}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Hook cover \texttt{\{02,03,12\}}}

Apart from zero-intersection cuts the allowed outside classes are

\[
P=C(04),\quad D=C(034),\quad F=C(024),\quad E=C(134).
\]

Their counts are \texttt{p,d,f,e}; all are at most four. Assume
\texttt{e\textgreater{}0}, since otherwise the one-row-cover lemma
applies. Then

\[
e+p+d+f+z=10,\qquad p+d\le7,\quad p+f\le7.
\]

\hypertarget{two-zero-cuts}{%
\subsubsection*{Two zero cuts}\label{two-zero-cuts}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Two zero cuts}

If one of the two zeros contains colour 3, target \texttt{01∣34} works.
Use four \texttt{A} for the forward half. Use a \texttt{P,D,F} copy for
\texttt{41}, an \texttt{E} for one of \texttt{30,40}, and the two zeros
for \texttt{31} and the other of \texttt{30,40}. The two zeros can be
assigned this way: if the zero used for \texttt{31} contains 4, swap
their roles if necessary; a zero missing 3 is exactly \texttt{C(24)} and
supplies \texttt{40}. Every zero contains at least one of 3 and 4.

If neither zero contains 3, both are \texttt{C(24)}. Target
\texttt{01∣24} uses four \texttt{A} for the forward half and the two
zeros, \texttt{E}, and a \texttt{P,D,F} copy for \texttt{20,21,40,41},
respectively. A \texttt{P,D,F} copy exists because
\texttt{e\textless{}=4}. Consequently \texttt{z\textless{}=1} in a
failure.

\hypertarget{one-zero-cut}{%
\subsubsection*{One zero cut}\label{one-zero-cut}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{One zero cut}

If \texttt{d\textgreater{}0}, then \texttt{p+f\textgreater{}0}, because
otherwise \texttt{e+d=9\textgreater{}8}. Target \texttt{01∣34} uses four
\texttt{A}, then \texttt{D} for \texttt{31}, a \texttt{P,F} for
\texttt{41}, and \texttt{E} and the zero for \texttt{30,40}. Hence
\texttt{d=0}.

Now \texttt{e+p+f=9} and \texttt{p+f\textless{}=7}, so
\texttt{e\textgreater{}=2} and \texttt{p,f\textgreater{}=1}. If the zero
is \texttt{Z\_3=C(24)}, target \texttt{01∣24} uses four \texttt{A}, then
zero:\texttt{20}, \texttt{F:21}, \texttt{E:40}, and \texttt{P:41}.

Every other zero contains 3 and supplies \texttt{30}. If
\texttt{f\textgreater{}=2}, target \texttt{02∣13} is given by

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & Arcs \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{A} & \texttt{03,12} \\
\texttt{E} & \texttt{10,32} \\
zero & \texttt{30} \\
\texttt{F} & \texttt{21,23} \\
\texttt{P} & \texttt{01} \\
\end{longtable}

If \texttt{f=1}, necessarily \texttt{p=e=4}. Then \texttt{P} has a loop:
reserve two same-repeat \texttt{P} cubes for its two bicoloured corners,
and fill its six tricoloured corners by remaining \texttt{P:01,43},
\texttt{A:02,03}, \texttt{E:42}, \texttt{F:41}. Four copies of a class
force a repeated repeat colour, so this solves.

\hypertarget{no-zero-cuts}{%
\subsubsection*{No zero cuts}\label{no-zero-cuts}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{No zero cuts}

If \texttt{e\textgreater{}=2} and \texttt{d\textgreater{}=1}, target
\texttt{01∣34} uses four \texttt{A}, two \texttt{E} at \texttt{30,40},
one \texttt{D} at \texttt{31}, and another \texttt{P,D,F} at
\texttt{41}. Thus \texttt{e\textgreater{}=2} implies \texttt{d=0} in a
failure. Then \texttt{e+p+f=10}, so \texttt{e\textgreater{}=3} and
\texttt{p,f\textgreater{}=2}. Target \texttt{02∣13} uses
\texttt{A:03,12}, \texttt{E:10,30,32}, \texttt{F:21,23}, \texttt{P:01}.

The only remaining possibility has \texttt{e=1} and \texttt{p+d+f=9}.
The bounds \texttt{p+d,p+f\textless{}=7} imply
\texttt{d,f\textgreater{}=2}. A class of size four has a loop, as
follows; the class in the first column has two copies reserved for the
bicoloured corners.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Reserved class & Its six tricoloured corners \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{P} & \texttt{A:02,03}; \texttt{D:42}; \texttt{F:43}; remaining
\texttt{P:01,41} \\
\texttt{D} & remaining \texttt{D:31,01}; \texttt{A:02}; \texttt{E:32};
\texttt{P:42}; \texttt{F:41} \\
\texttt{F} & remaining \texttt{F:21,23}; \texttt{A:03}; \texttt{P:43};
two further \texttt{P,D:01,41} \\
\end{longtable}

In the last row \texttt{p+d=5}, so the further copies exist. The other
rows have all displayed classes present by the preceding count bounds.
Thus a failure would require \texttt{p=d=f=3}.

For this last case use the thirteen-cube-core lemma in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-25}{Argument 25}:
\texttt{A\^{}4,P\^{}3,D\^{}3,F\^{}3} plus any cut containing one of
\texttt{21,31,23,32} solves. Here \texttt{E=C(134)} contains
\texttt{32}.

This proves the hook-cover case.

\hypertarget{disjoint-cover-020314}{%
\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{2. Disjoint cover
\texttt{\{02,03,14\}}}{2. Disjoint cover \{02,03,14\}}}\label{disjoint-cover-020314}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Disjoint cover
\texttt{\{02,03,14\}}}

Use the same \texttt{P,D,F}, but now

\[
E=C(123),
\]

with \texttt{e\textgreater{}0}; again \texttt{e+p+d+f+z=10} and
\texttt{z\textless{}=2}. In addition to \texttt{p+d,p+f\textless{}=7} we
may assume \texttt{p+e\textless{}=7}, since \texttt{P} and \texttt{E}
are reverse cuts and four of each solve an opposite target.

The following small assemblies will be used:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{2A+2P+2D+F+E} builds \texttt{12∣34}: \texttt{A:13,14},
  \texttt{E:24}, \texttt{F:23}, \texttt{D:31,32}, \texttt{P:41,42}.
\item
  Interchanging 2 and 3 gives \texttt{2A+2P+D+2F+E} building
  \texttt{13∣24}.
\item
  \texttt{2A+4D+F+E} builds \texttt{12∣34}: \texttt{A:13,14},
  \texttt{E:24}, \texttt{F:23}, and \texttt{D} fills all four reverse
  arcs. There is a symmetric version with \texttt{4F+D}.
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{a-zero-containing-4}{%
\subsubsection*{A zero containing 4}\label{a-zero-containing-4}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{A zero containing 4}

If \texttt{d\textgreater{}0} and a zero contains 4, target
\texttt{01∣34} uses four \texttt{A}, \texttt{E:30}, zero:\texttt{40},
\texttt{D:31}, and one further \texttt{P,D,F:41}. If
\texttt{f\textgreater{}0}, use the colour-swapped target \texttt{01∣24}.
There is always a further non-\texttt{E} copy because
\texttt{p+d+f=10-z-e\textgreater{}=4}. Also \texttt{d=f=0} would force
\texttt{p+e\textgreater{}=8}, forbidden.

Therefore any zero in a failure is \texttt{Z\_4=C(23)}.

\hypertarget{two-copies-of-z_4}{%
\subsubsection*{\texorpdfstring{Two copies of
\texttt{Z\_4}}{Two copies of Z\_4}}\label{two-copies-of-z_4}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Two copies of \texttt{Z\_4}}

If \texttt{e\textgreater{}=2}, use target \texttt{14∣23}. Two \texttt{A}
fill \texttt{12,13}, two \texttt{E} fill \texttt{24,34}, the two zeros
fill \texttt{21,31}, and two \texttt{P,D,F} fill \texttt{42,43}. These
last two arcs can be matched unless \texttt{p+d=0} or \texttt{p+f=0};
there are at least four copies in \texttt{P,D,F}. In either exceptional
case the entire non-\texttt{E} multiplicity is four in \texttt{D} or
\texttt{F}, and \texttt{e=4}. These two cuts are disjoint, so their
eight cubes already solve.

If \texttt{e=1}, then \texttt{p+d+f=7}, whence
\texttt{p+d,p+f\textgreater{}=3}. At least one of \texttt{d,f} is
positive. Suppose \texttt{d\textgreater{}0}; the other case is
symmetric. Target \texttt{14∣23} uses \texttt{A:12,13}, \texttt{E:24},
zeros:\texttt{34,21}, \texttt{D:31}; the remaining sources match
\texttt{42,43} because their degrees are at least two and three,
respectively. Thus this case also solves.

\hypertarget{one-copy-of-z_4}{%
\subsubsection*{\texorpdfstring{One copy of
\texttt{Z\_4}}{One copy of Z\_4}}\label{one-copy-of-z_4}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{One copy of \texttt{Z\_4}}

If \texttt{e\textgreater{}=3}, target \texttt{04∣13} uses
\texttt{E:10,30,34}, \texttt{A:14,03}, a \texttt{P,F} copy at
\texttt{43}, and two further \texttt{P,D,F} copies at \texttt{01,41}.
Here \texttt{p+d+f=9-e\textgreater{}=5}, and \texttt{p+f\textgreater{}0}
since \texttt{d\textless{}=4}.

If \texttt{e=2}, then \texttt{p+d+f=7} and
\texttt{p+d,p+f\textgreater{}=3}. As in the previous case, choose
\texttt{D} if \texttt{d\textgreater{}0} (otherwise interchange 2 and 3).
Target \texttt{14∣23} uses \texttt{A:12,13}, \texttt{E:24,34},
zero:\texttt{21}, \texttt{D:31}, and two remaining \texttt{P,D,F} copies
for \texttt{42,43}.

If \texttt{e=1}, then \texttt{p+d+f=8}. Both \texttt{d,f} are positive:
otherwise a shared-four-arcs pair has total eight. If
\texttt{p\textgreater{}=2}, at least one of \texttt{d,f} is at least
two, and the corresponding first or second small assembly above applies.
If \texttt{p\textless{}=1}, at least one of \texttt{d,f} equals four,
and the third small assembly or its symmetric version applies.

\hypertarget{no-zero-cuts-1}{%
\subsubsection*{No zero cuts}\label{no-zero-cuts-1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{No zero cuts}

If \texttt{e=1}, then \texttt{p+d+f=9}; the same small assemblies apply
(both \texttt{d,f} are positive, and if \texttt{p\textless{}=1} one is
four).

Suppose \texttt{e\textgreater{}=2} and \texttt{d,f\textgreater{}0}.
Target \texttt{14∣23} uses \texttt{A:12,13}, \texttt{E:24,34},
\texttt{D:31}, \texttt{F:21}, and two remaining \texttt{P,D,F} at
\texttt{42,43}. Both last degrees are positive and their union has at
least four copies: indeed \texttt{p+d+f=10-e\textgreater{}=6}, so
\texttt{p+d,p+f\textgreater{}=2} before removing \texttt{D,F}.

Finally suppose, say, \texttt{d=0}. Then \texttt{e+p+f=10} and
\texttt{p+e,p+f\textless{}=7} give \texttt{e,f\textgreater{}=3}; also
\texttt{p\textgreater{}0}. Target \texttt{04∣13} uses
\texttt{E:10,30,34}, \texttt{A:14}, and four \texttt{P,F} copies for its
forward half. Since \texttt{p+f=10-e\textgreater{}=6}, this is possible.

This proves the disjoint-cover case. Together with the one-row lemma,
all three-corner covers without source exceptions are now settled.

\hypertarget{arg-27}{%
\section{Argument 27: Four copies of an adjacent class: covers with
source exceptions}\label{arg-27}}

This completes the pure fourteen-cube case with a tricoloured class of
size four, assuming the established bound that no class has more than
four copies. The three-corner covers are handled in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-25}{Argument 25} and
\protect\hyperlink{arg-26}{Argument 26}.

\hypertarget{normal-form-and-a-useful-zero-cut-lemma}{%
\subsection*{Normal form and a useful zero-cut
lemma}\label{normal-form-and-a-useful-zero-cut-lemma}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Normal form and a useful zero-cut
lemma}

Write \texttt{C(S)} for the directed cut from \texttt{S} to its
complement in \texttt{\{0,1,2,3,4\}}. Fix \texttt{A=C(01)} with four
copies. Its six arcs form the two-row, three-column array

\[
\{02,03,04;12,13,14\}.
\]

The cuts disjoint from \texttt{A} are \texttt{B=C(234)} and
\texttt{Z\_i=C(\{2,3,4\}\textbackslash{}setminus\{i\})} for
\texttt{i=2,3,4}. Call their combined count \texttt{z}.

\textbf{Four-zero lemma.} Four \texttt{A} and any four zero cuts build
an opposite target. Choose two colours \texttt{q,s} in
\texttt{\{2,3,4\}} each contained in at least two of the four zero
outsets. Such a choice exists because at most one of the three counts of
\texttt{Z\_i} exceeds two. Every zero contains \texttt{q} or \texttt{s}.
Hence their four copies match \texttt{q0,q1,s0,s1}: each row has degree
at least two and the union of the rows sees all four copies. Four
\texttt{A} fill the forward half of target \texttt{01\textbar{}qs}.

Thus \texttt{z\textless{}=3} in a failure. We also use the established
half-array lemma that four \texttt{A}, a cut meeting \texttt{A} in one
arc, and any three zero cuts build an opposite target. If a
singleton-intersection class is present, then \texttt{z\textless{}=2} in
a failure.

Two of the four \texttt{A} copies have the same repeated colour. Reserve
them for the bicoloured corners of their own target. If the ten
outsiders have matching rank at least four on the six arcs of
\texttt{A}, four of them and the remaining two \texttt{A} complete the
target. Therefore their rank is at most three. By Konig's theorem, they
admit a cover by \texttt{c} corner vertices and at most \texttt{3-c}
individual source copies.

For \texttt{c=0} or \texttt{1}, removing at most three or two source
copies leaves at least seven zeros, or at least eight copies in one
singleton class and the zero classes. The former contradicts
\texttt{z\textless{}=3}; the latter contradicts the sum of the bounds
four and three. Thus only \texttt{c=2,3} remain.

\hypertarget{two-corner-covers-in-different-rows}{%
\subsection*{Two-corner covers in different
rows}\label{two-corner-covers-in-different-rows}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Two-corner covers in different rows}

If the two corners lie in a column, normalize them to
\texttt{\{02,12\}}. Apart from one exceptional source, the outsiders are
zero cuts and the two singleton classes \texttt{D=C(034),\ E=C(134)}.
Their counts satisfy \texttt{d+e+z=9}. There is a singleton class, so
\texttt{z\textless{}=2}; thus \texttt{d,e\textgreater{}=3}. But target
\texttt{01∣34} is filled by four \texttt{A}, \texttt{D:31,41}, and
\texttt{E:30,40}.

If the two corners are in distinct columns, normalize them to
\texttt{\{02,13\}}. The two singleton classes are now
\texttt{D=C(034),\ E=C(124)}, again with \texttt{d,e\textgreater{}=3}.
Target \texttt{03∣12} is filled by \texttt{A:02,13},
\texttt{D:01,31,32}, and \texttt{E:10,20,23}.

Consequently a cover with a source exception must have its two corners
in the same row.

\hypertarget{two-corners-in-one-row}{%
\subsection*{Two corners in one row}\label{two-corners-in-one-row}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Two corners in one row}

Normalize the corners to \texttt{\{02,03\}}. The nonzero normal classes
are

\[
P=C(04),\quad D=C(034),\quad F=C(024),
\]

with counts \texttt{p,d,f}. There is one exceptional cube \texttt{E},
and

\[
p+d+f+z=9,\quad p,d,f\le4,\quad p+d,p+f\le7.
\]

The last inequalities follow from the shared-four-arcs bound. There is
at least one \texttt{D} or \texttt{F}: otherwise \texttt{p+z=9}
contradicts \texttt{p\textless{}=4,z\textless{}=3}. Thus
\texttt{z\textless{}=2}.

If \texttt{E} contributes at most one corner outside \texttt{\{02,03\}},
all outsiders have a three-corner cover, already settled. Otherwise, up
to swapping 2 and 3, \texttt{E} is one of

\[
C(14),\quad C(12),\quad C(014),\quad C(013).
\]

These are respectively an aligned opposite-row pair, a skew opposite-row
pair, an aligned four-corner rectangle, and a skew rectangle. In
particular, every remaining \texttt{E} contains an arc of the second row
of \texttt{A}.

\hypertarget{completion-and-conflict-lemmas}{%
\subsubsection*{Completion and conflict
lemmas}\label{completion-and-conflict-lemmas}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Completion and conflict lemmas}

The following statements make the subsequent counting short. A loop on a
class means that any two copies of that class with the same repeated
colour build a target after adding six further cubes. An edge between
two classes means the analogous statement for one reserved cube from
each class. Thus looped classes use distinct repeated colours in a
failure, and joined classes use disjoint sets of repeated colours.

\textbf{Heavy-class completions.} Suppose \texttt{p,d,f\textgreater{}0}
and one count is four. It has a loop. Reserve two of that class; the
following table gives the six tricoloured corners, using remaining
copies.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 2\tabcolsep) * \real{0.5000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Class of size four
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Completion
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{P} & \texttt{A:02,03}; \texttt{D:42}; \texttt{F:43}; remaining
\texttt{P:01,41} \\
\texttt{D} & remaining \texttt{D:31,32}; \texttt{A:02}; \texttt{P:42};
\texttt{F:41}; one further \texttt{P,F:01} \\
\texttt{F} & remaining \texttt{F:21,23}; \texttt{A:03}; \texttt{P:43};
\texttt{D:41}; one further \texttt{P,D:01} \\
\end{longtable}

The further copy in the last two rows exists since the other two counts
sum to \texttt{5-z\textgreater{}=3}. A size-four loop solves by the
pigeonhole principle.

\textbf{Small conflict clique.} Suppose \texttt{p,d,f\textgreater{}=2},
at least one of \texttt{d,f} is at least three, and \texttt{E} has a
second-row arc of \texttt{A}. Then \texttt{A,P,D,F} are pairwise joined,
and \texttt{P} has a loop.

For the loop on \texttt{P}, reserve two \texttt{P} and use
\texttt{A:02,03}, \texttt{D:01,42}, \texttt{F:41,43}. To join \texttt{A}
to any one of \texttt{P,D,F}, use target \texttt{A}: after reserving one
copy of each endpoint, three \texttt{A} remain; two normal copies fill
\texttt{02,03} and \texttt{E} fills a second-row arc, while the three
\texttt{A} fill the other three positions. The endpoint pole pairs
permit its two bicoloured corners: \texttt{P} supplies pole 0, and
\texttt{D,F} supply pole 1.

To join \texttt{P,D}, use target \texttt{A}, with bicoloured corners
supplied at poles 0 and 1. Four \texttt{A} remain, and a remaining
\texttt{P} and an \texttt{F} supply \texttt{02,03}. The \texttt{P,F}
edge is symmetric. Finally, if \texttt{f\textgreater{}=3}, use target
\texttt{F} for the \texttt{D,F} edge: after reserving one of each, fill
its tricoloured corners by remaining \texttt{F:21,23}, \texttt{P:01,43},
remaining \texttt{D:41}, and \texttt{A:03}. If
\texttt{d\textgreater{}=3} instead, interchange 2 and 3.

In particular, if \texttt{p=3} this clique requires at least
\texttt{1+3+1+1=6} different repeated colours, impossible with five
colours.

If \texttt{p=2,d=f=3} and a zero cube is also available, that zero
contains 2 or 3. If it contains 3, \texttt{D} has a loop: reserve two
\texttt{D} and fill by remaining \texttt{D:32}, zero:\texttt{31},
\texttt{A:02}, \texttt{P:41,42}, \texttt{F:01}. If it contains 2, the
symmetric construction loops \texttt{F}. The clique \texttt{A,P,D} or
\texttt{A,P,F} then requires \texttt{1+2+3=6} different repeated
colours.

\hypertarget{no-zero-cubes}{%
\subsubsection*{No zero cubes}\label{no-zero-cubes}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{No zero cubes}

Here \texttt{p+d+f=9}. The shared-four-arcs bounds imply
\texttt{d,f\textgreater{}=2}, and \texttt{p\textgreater{}=1}. If any
count is four, the heavy-class completion solves. Otherwise all three
counts equal three, and the small conflict clique solves.

\hypertarget{one-zero-cube}{%
\subsubsection*{One zero cube}\label{one-zero-cube}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{One zero cube}

Here \texttt{p+d+f=8}, and \texttt{d,f\textgreater{}=1}. If
\texttt{p\textgreater{}0}, a size-four count solves by the heavy-class
completion. Otherwise the counts are a permutation of \texttt{2,3,3},
covered by the small conflict clique and its zero-cube extension.

If \texttt{p=0}, then \texttt{d=f=4}. There are only the four
exceptional source types listed above:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  If \texttt{E=C(14)} or \texttt{C(014)}, it contains \texttt{42}.
  Reserve two \texttt{D}, then use remaining \texttt{D:31,32},
  \texttt{A:02}, \texttt{F:01,41}, \texttt{E:42}. This is a loop on
  \texttt{D}.
\item
  If \texttt{E=C(12)}, target \texttt{12∣34} uses \texttt{A:13,14},
  \texttt{E:24}, \texttt{F:23}, and four \texttt{D} for all four reverse
  arcs.
\item
  If \texttt{E=C(013)}, target \texttt{13∣24} uses \texttt{A:12,14},
  \texttt{E:34}, \texttt{D:32}, and four \texttt{F} for all four reverse
  arcs.
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{two-zero-cubes}{%
\subsubsection*{Two zero cubes}\label{two-zero-cubes}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Two zero cubes}

Four \texttt{A} fill the forward half of any target
\texttt{01\textbar{}qs}, with \texttt{q,s} in \texttt{\{2,3,4\}}. The
two zero cubes fill \texttt{q0,s0} unless both are the same
\texttt{Z\_i} with \texttt{i} equal to \texttt{q} or \texttt{s}. Once
these are filled:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  pair \texttt{\{3,4\}} works if \texttt{d\textgreater{}0}, using
  \texttt{D:31} and any further \texttt{P,D,F:41};
\item
  pair \texttt{\{2,4\}} works if \texttt{f\textgreater{}0},
  symmetrically;
\item
  pair \texttt{\{2,3\}} works if \texttt{d,f\textgreater{}0}, using
  \texttt{F:21,D:31}.
\end{itemize}

Since \texttt{p+d+f=7}, at least one of \texttt{d,f} is positive. If the
zeros differ, or one is \texttt{B}, the first or second construction
therefore solves. If both zeros are \texttt{Z\_2}, failure forces
\texttt{d=0}; if both are \texttt{Z\_3}, it forces \texttt{f=0}; if both
are \texttt{Z\_4}, it forces at least one of \texttt{d,f} to vanish. Up
to swapping 2 and 3, the only remaining possibility is \texttt{d=0},
\texttt{p+f=7}, hence \texttt{(p,f)=(4,3)} or \texttt{(3,4)}.

For \texttt{(4,3)}, \texttt{P} has a loop: reserve two \texttt{P} and
use remaining \texttt{P:01,42}, \texttt{A:02,03}, \texttt{F:41,43}. For
\texttt{(3,4)}, \texttt{F} has a loop: reserve two \texttt{F} and use
remaining \texttt{F:21,23}, \texttt{A:03}, and \texttt{P:01,41,43}.

All source-exception covers are now excluded. Together with the three
corner-cover lemmas, this proves that any pure fourteen-cube pool with
maximum tricoloured-class count four has an assembly.

\hypertarget{arg-28}{%
\section{Argument 28: Six doubled adjacent classes}\label{arg-28}}

\textbf{Elementary theorem.} Take two cubes from each of six distinct
adjacent tricoloured-corner classes. If their tricoloured-corner union
supports an opposite-repeat target, these twelve cubes assemble an
opposite target. The repeated colours within the six classes are
irrelevant.

Write C(S) for the directed cut S→Sᶜ on five colours, where
\textbar S\textbar{} is two or three. Opposite targets are the eight
directed edges of a bidirected four-cycle. Only tricoloured corners are
used in this proof.

\hypertarget{a-failed-supported-target-has-three-private-corners}{%
\subsection*{1. A failed supported target has three private
corners}\label{a-failed-supported-target-has-three-private-corners}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. A failed supported target has three
private corners}

Fix an opposite target T with halves P→Q and Q→P, where
\textbar P\textbar=\textbar Q\textbar=2; the fifth colour is omitted.
The intersections of adjacent classes with T have the following
elementary description.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Two classes contain the whole half P→Q, and two the whole half Q→P.
\item
  Eight classes give a two-corner row or column in one of the two
  halves, one class for each such row or column.
\item
  The other eight classes give four pairs crossing between the halves,
  two classes per pair. Each corner belongs to exactly one such pair.
\end{itemize}

The row/column pairs form a four-cycle in each half; the four doubled
pairs form a matching between the halves. It follows that at most three
classes have their entire intersection with T inside any prescribed
three corners, and at most two inside any prescribed two corners. For
the three-corner assertion, three corners in one half contain at most
two row/column pairs. If the corners lie in both halves, they contain at
most one row/column pair and one doubled pair.

Now each of our six classes supplies two sources. A set of one or two
target corners has enough neighbours because T is supported. A set of
five corners sees at least three classes, hence six sources: its
complement contains at most three complete source intersections. A set
of six corners similarly sees at least four classes, and a set of seven
or eight sees all six classes. Thus Hall can fail only on three or four
corners, all supplied by one class A.

Such a class must contain one whole target half, since all other
intersections have size two. In particular A has three target corners
that no other selected class contains.

\hypertarget{three-private-corners-force-a-different-opposite-assembly}{%
\subsection*{2. Three private corners force a different opposite
assembly}\label{three-private-corners-force-a-different-opposite-assembly}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Three private corners force a
different opposite assembly}

Reverse all cuts if necessary, and relabel colours, to put

\begin{verbatim}
A=C(01),   H={03,04,13}.
\end{verbatim}

Here ab denotes the directed corner a→b. The three corners H are private
to A. The classes avoiding H are exactly the following eight:

\begin{verbatim}
U = { C234, C134, C123, C034 },
V = { C13, C23, C24, C34 }.
\end{verbatim}

This list follows directly from the implications 0∈S⇒3,4∈S and 1∈S⇒3∈S
for an outset S of size two or three.

Our five other distinct classes therefore include three from U or three
from V. Two copies of A and two copies of any such three classes already
build an opposite target, as follows.

\hypertarget{three-classes-from-u}{%
\subsubsection*{Three classes from U}\label{three-classes-from-u}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Three classes from U}

If C234 is present, use the target with bipartition 01∣24. C234 supplies
any two of the reverse corners \{20,21,40,41\}. The other three members
of U have these two-corner intersections:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & Forward corner & Reverse corner \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
C134 & 12 & 40 \\
C123 & 14 & 20 \\
C034 & 02 & 41 \\
\end{longtable}

For either two of these classes, use one copy at its listed forward
corner and the other at its listed reverse corner. The two A copies fill
the two remaining forward corners, and the two C234 copies fill the two
remaining reverse corners.

If C234 is absent, the classes are C134,C123,C034. Use target 04∣12:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Sources & Assigned corners \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
2 C01 & 02,14 \\
2 C134 & 10,42 \\
2 C123 & 20,24 \\
2 C034 & 01,41 \\
\end{longtable}

\hypertarget{three-classes-from-v}{%
\subsubsection*{Three classes from V}\label{three-classes-from-v}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Three classes from V}

If C13 is present, use target 01∣24. The two A copies fill 02,04, and
the two C13 copies fill 12,14. The other two classes are selected from
C23,C24,C34. C23 supplies 20,21; C34 supplies 40,41; and C24 can supply
either pair. Thus the reverse half can always be filled.

If C13 is absent, use target 12∣34:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Sources & Assigned corners \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
2 C01 & 13,14 \\
2 C23 & 24,31 \\
2 C24 & 23,41 \\
2 C34 & 32,42 \\
\end{longtable}

Every case supplies a different opposite assembly. This contradicts the
assumed failure of all opposite targets, and proves the theorem.

\hypertarget{corollary-six-repeated-classes-suffice-in-a-pure-fourteen-pool}{%
\subsection*{Corollary: six repeated classes suffice in a pure
fourteen-pool}\label{corollary-six-repeated-classes-suffice-in-a-pure-fourteen-pool}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Corollary: six repeated classes
suffice in a pure fourteen-pool}

\textbf{Theorem.} Fourteen adjacent-repeat cubes are soluble whenever at
least six distinct tricoloured-corner classes each occur at least twice.

In a hypothetical failure, pure concentration first bounds every class
by three: use \protect\hyperlink{arg-13}{Argument 13},
\protect\hyperlink{arg-12}{Argument 12}, and the four-copy proofs
\protect\hyperlink{arg-25}{Argument 25},
\protect\hyperlink{arg-26}{Argument 26},
\protect\hyperlink{arg-27}{Argument 27}. None uses this corollary.
Select two copies from six such classes. The theorem above solves if
their union supports an opposite target. Otherwise the support-poset
theorem in \protect\hyperlink{arg-15}{Argument 15} places the six
distinct classes in one of the two six-class families below; thus they
fill that family exactly.

\hypertarget{common-corner-family}{%
\subsubsection*{Common-corner family}\label{common-corner-family}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Common-corner family}

Write the common corner as a→b, and the other colours as i,j,k. The six
classes are A\_i=C(ai), A\_j=C(aj), A\_k=C(ak), and B\_i=C(ajk),
B\_j=C(aik), B\_k=C(aij), two copies of each.

Each A\_i has a loop: after its two copies have been reserved for the
bicoloured corners, fill its six tricoloured corners by

\begin{verbatim}
B_j: ij, ib;   B_k: ik, ak;   A_k: aj, ab.
\end{verbatim}

Consequently, in a failure the two A\_i copies must have different
repeated colours. Also A\_i and A\_j cannot share a repeated colour.
Indeed reserve one of each for the bicoloured corners of an A\_i target,
using their common pole a. Relative to the displayed loop completion,
one universal A\_i copy has been restored and one A\_j copy removed; the
restored A\_i can replace the removed source if it was used. Thus the
three A classes would require six different repeated colours, which is
impossible. The twelve selected cubes already solve.

\hypertarget{butterfly-family}{%
\subsubsection*{Butterfly family}\label{butterfly-family}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Butterfly family}

Normalize the six classes to

\begin{verbatim}
C01, C02, C124, C123, C12, C012.
\end{verbatim}

Their union misses exactly 31,32,41,42. Any adjacent class outside these
six contains one of these four corners: avoiding all four means being an
upset of the two-below-two order, whose size-two and size-three upsets
are exactly the six listed sets.

By symmetry suppose an outside source Y contains 31. The target 03∣14 is
assembled using Y and seven of the eight high-class copies:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Sources & Corners \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Y & 31 \\
2 C01 & 04,13 \\
C02 & 01 \\
2 C124 & 40,43 \\
2 C123 & 10,34 \\
\end{longtable}

Thus any outside source gives an opposite assembly. If all fourteen
sources lie within the butterfly family, the cap-three butterfly profile
lemma in the same reference says that a failure has class counts
3,3,3,3,1,1. That is incompatible with all six classes occurring at
least twice. This proves the corollary.

\hypertarget{arg-29}{%
\section{Argument 29: Nine distinct adjacent classes: an explicit
Hall-table proof}\label{arg-29}}

\textbf{Status.} This is a finite Hall-table proof, with the complete
small certificate displayed below. A computer was used to find and audit
the table. Unlike the six-doubled-classes lemma, this has not yet been
reduced to a conceptual geometric argument. Its only finite calculations
concern five fixed sources and at most four of one target's eight
corners; no fourteen-cube inventory enumeration is assumed. The Hall
reduction and table dominance were independently audited from the
directed-cut model.

\textbf{Theorem.} One cube from each of any nine distinct adjacent
tricoloured-corner classes has an opposite-target assembly.

Write \texttt{S} for the class C(S)=S→Sᶜ, where S has size two or three.
Write \texttt{ab} for the directed corner a→b. A target
\texttt{ab\textbar{}cd} consists of all eight directed corners between
its two pairs. Repeated colours do not enter this lemma.

\hypertarget{six-possible-cores}{%
\subsection*{1. Six possible cores}\label{six-possible-cores}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Six possible cores}

Call a class positive when its outset has size two. Simultaneous
reversal interchanges the two orientations, so at least five of our nine
distinct classes may be assumed positive. Regard any five of these as
five edges on the five colours. Up to relabelling, a five-edge simple
graph on five vertices has the following six forms: a triangle with two
leaves at one vertex; a diamond and an isolated vertex; a triangle with
leaves at two vertices; a triangle with an attached two-edge path; a
four-cycle with a leaf; or a five-cycle. These are the six cores in the
tables below.

This graph list can be checked from its cycles. A triangle-free
five-edge graph is the five-cycle or a four-cycle with a leaf. With a
triangle, the two remaining edges either complete a diamond, attach a
path, or attach the two extra vertices to the same or different triangle
vertices.

Let H be the four remaining, distinct helper classes. None belongs to
its chosen five-class core.

\hypertarget{the-hall-calculation-used-by-every-table-row}{%
\subsection*{2. The Hall calculation used by every table
row}\label{the-hall-calculation-used-by-every-table-row}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. The Hall calculation used by every
table row}

Fix a target T and a set S of its corner positions. Put

\begin{verbatim}
a(S) = number of the five core classes meeting S,
D(S) = helper classes disjoint from S,
k(S) = |S|−1−a(S).
\end{verbatim}

The Hall inequality for S fails exactly when

\begin{verbatim}
|H \ D(S)| ≤ k(S).                              (1)
\end{verbatim}

Every source meets T in either two or four positions. At most three
classes have their entire intersection inside any three positions; at
most two have it inside any two positions. These facts follow from the
two square halves and the four doubled cross-pairs described in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-28}{Argument 28}. For nine distinct sources,
therefore, Hall is automatic for sets of at least five positions. Only
\textbar S\textbar≤4 needs to be considered in (1).

If a(S)≥\textbar S\textbar, there is no obstruction. If
a(S)=\textbar S\textbar−1, condition (1) says H⊆D(S); it is impossible
when \textbar D(S)\textbar\textless4. Such a set S can be discarded when
it contains a smaller core-deficient set, since that smaller set has a
larger D-set. The only higher-deficiency sets of size at most four occur
for the first core; they are recorded in Section 4. This gives a short
way to check completeness of the tables.

A useful general dominance rule is the following. An obstruction
\texttt{\textbar{}H\ \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}\ D\textbar{}≤k}
implies a listed obstruction
\texttt{\textbar{}H\ \textbackslash{}\textbackslash{}\ E\textbar{}≤l}
whenever

\begin{verbatim}
|D \\ E| + k ≤ l.
\end{verbatim}

Indeed at most \textbar D\textbackslash E\textbar{} helpers can lie in D
outside E, and at most k helpers lie outside D. This checks the table
reductions without enumerating possible helper collections.

\hypertarget{possible-helper-sets-when-a-target-fails}{%
\subsection*{3. Possible helper sets when a target
fails}\label{possible-helper-sets-when-a-target-fails}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Possible helper sets when a target
fails}

In the following tables, a normal row means all four helpers must lie in
the displayed D-set. A row with k=1 instead means at most one helper
lies outside D. For k=1 the displayed D has three members, so all three
must be present. The corner set S is supplied to make each obstruction
checkable directly from the cut definition.

12\}}\label{core-1-01-02-03-04-12}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Core 1: \{01, 02, 03, 04, 12\}}

\endlastfoot
14∣23 & \{31,34\} & \{134, 124, 024, 14, 014, 24, 012\} & 0 \\

The first target forces H into one of its two seven-element sets,

The intersection of a first-target set with both remaining k=0 sets has

\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Core 5: \{01, 02, 03, 14, 24\}}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllr@{}}

02∣14 & \{10,42\} & \{234, 024, 023, 23, 013, 012\} & 0 \\
\end{longtable}

Every D-set for the first target meets every D-set for the second in at
most 3 classes. Hence four distinct helpers cannot make both targets
fail.

\hypertarget{core-6-01-02-13-24-34}{%
\subsubsection*{Core 6: \{01, 02, 13, 24,
34\}}\label{core-6-01-02-13-24-34}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Core 6: \{01, 02, 13, 24, 34\}}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Target & Corner set S & D(S) & k \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
01∣23 & \{02,13\} & \{234, 134, 123, 024, 023, 23\} & 0 \\
01∣23 & \{03,13,20,21\} & \{134, 03, 034, 013\} & 0 \\
01∣23 & \{02,12,30,31\} & \{124, 12, 024, 012\} & 0 \\

Every D-set for the first target meets every D-set for the second in at
most 2 classes. Hence four distinct helpers cannot make both targets
fail.

\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Completeness details for the small

contains one listed here, and its D-set is a subset of that listed set's
D-set.

The only deficit-two sets of size at most four are, for Core 1:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  target \texttt{14∣23}: S=\{31,34,42,43\}, with only one avoiding
  helper class, so four helpers cannot satisfy k=1;
\item
  target \texttt{02∣14}: S=\{10,21,24,40\}, giving its k=1 row above;
\item

There are no larger deficits for \textbar S\textbar≤4. For the first
core, the deficit-one triple in either of the last two targets is
already covered by its displayed deficit-two four-set: its four-element
avoiding set consists of the three-element D-set plus one additional
class. This explains why no separate row is needed.

This completes the Hall reduction and the six-case proof.

\hypertarget{consequence-for-fourteen-cubes}{%
\subsection*{Consequence for fourteen
cubes}\label{consequence-for-fourteen-cubes}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Consequence for fourteen cubes}

An opposite-unsoluble all-adjacent pool has at most eight occupied
tricoloured-corner classes. If a fourteen-pool had every class count at
most two, at least six of those classes would have count two. The
corollary in \protect\hyperlink{arg-28}{Argument 28} would then solve
it. Thus a pure fourteen-cube failure has a class of size at least
three.

Together with the separate four- and five-class proofs and the older
six-class bound, every pure fourteen-cube failure has maximum class
count exactly three. This does not yet prove that its support is a
butterfly, or that its class-size profile is 3,3,3,3,1,1.

\hypertarget{sharpness}{%
\subsection*{Sharpness}\label{sharpness}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Sharpness}

The bound nine is sharp even when adjacent targets are allowed. An
explicit eight-cube counterexample, with a direct Hall proof, is given
in the main article's direct fifteen-source argument.

\hypertarget{arg-30}{%
\section{Argument 30: Five opposite cubes and nine adjacent
cubes}\label{arg-30}}

\hypertarget{result}{%
\subsection*{Result}\label{result}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Result}

\textbf{Theorem.} Five opposite-repeat five-colour cubes, with no
variety occurring more than twice, together with nine adjacent-repeat
cubes, with no tricoloured-corner class occurring more than three times,
contain eight cubes that assemble an opposite-repeat target.

This is an elementary proof. Its ingredients are a replacement lemma,
the previously written five-opposite grid constructions, and the
nine-distinct-class theorem. No exhaustive inventory list is used.

The essential new observation is that the third copy of an adjacent
class in the older proofs can be introduced temporarily. All the
subsequent constructions use only two actual copies of that class.

\hypertarget{replacing-the-third-copy}{%
\subsection*{1. Replacing the third
copy}\label{replacing-the-third-copy}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Replacing the third copy}

\textbf{Replacement lemma.} Let O be any five opposite sources and let A
be an adjacent tricoloured-corner class. If O together with three copies
of A assemble an opposite target, then O together with two copies of A
and any adjacent source B also assemble an opposite target.

\textbf{Proof.} In the given eight-cube assembly, the three A copies
occupy corners on three distinct undirected target edges: an adjacent
cut contains at most one direction of each edge. Every adjacent source
meets an opposite target on at least two underlying edges. Therefore B
has a compatible corner on one of the three edges occupied by A.

If its direction agrees with the A corner, replace that A copy by B.
Otherwise swap the two directed corner positions on that one target
edge. Its other position was occupied by an opposite source, since the
three A positions lie on distinct edges. That opposite source supports
both directions, so it remains compatible. Put B at the reversed A
position and leave the other two A assignments untouched.

This is an operation on the corner assignment. Each chosen source can
then be properly rotated to its assigned corner. It does not assert a
reflection of any physical cube. The repeated colours within adjacent
classes do not affect compatibility with an opposite target. ∎

\hypertarget{the-geometric-constructions-that-remain-after-replacement}{%
\subsection*{2. The geometric constructions that remain after
replacement}\label{the-geometric-constructions-that-remain-after-replacement}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. The geometric constructions that
remain after replacement}

Fix A=C(01), with its six corners in a two-by-three grid. Colour
permutation and, if necessary, reversal of all directed corners reduce
any adjacent class to this form.

Each opposite variety meets this grid in two positions in different
columns, or in a complete two-column rectangle. Each such pattern
determines its variety uniquely.

If the five opposite sources match four positions of a two-column
rectangle, they assemble with three copies of A: four fill the reverse
rectangle, and the fifth plus the three A copies fill the forward
rectangle. The replacement lemma then gives an assembly with two actual
A copies and any adjacent helper.

Otherwise all three rectangle ranks are at most three. Any five grid
positions contain two complete columns, so a matching to five positions
would give a matching of a complete rectangle. Thus the full grid rank
is at most four. Hall's theorem gives a set H of three or four grid
positions satisfying

\begin{verbatim}
|H| + number of opposite copies with pattern not contained in H ≤ 4.
\end{verbatim}

The elementary consequences of this cover have already been proved in
the five-opposite notes. The relevant conclusions, including the number
of A copies they actually use, are as follows.

\hypertarget{at-most-one-doubled-opposite-variety}{%
\subsubsection*{At most one doubled opposite
variety}\label{at-most-one-doubled-opposite-variety}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{At most one doubled opposite
variety}

The opposite profile is 1,1,1,1,1 or 2,1,1,1. Either all five patterns
lie in a four-position shape with column sizes 2,1,1, or four source
copies lie on three positions in three different columns, with a fifth
outside. In the latter case the four inside copies are 2P+Q+R, where
P,Q,R form a line of neighbouring varieties.

In either case there is a central target T such that:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  A meets T in exactly two directed corners a,b;
\item
  after a,b are removed, the five opposite sources can fill any five of
  the remaining six positions.
\end{enumerate}

The proof is the Hall calculation in Sections 1--3 of
\protect\hyperlink{arg-6}{Argument 6}. It uses only the grid cover, not
a third physical A cube.

At most two adjacent classes have their entire T-intersection inside
\{a,b\}. One of those classes is A. Any adjacent helper outside these at
most two classes supplies a third corner c.~Use two A copies at a,b, the
helper at c, and the five opposite sources at the remaining positions.

\hypertarget{two-doubled-neighbouring-opposite-varieties}{%
\subsubsection*{Two doubled neighbouring opposite
varieties}\label{two-doubled-neighbouring-opposite-varieties}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Two doubled neighbouring opposite
varieties}

The opposite profile is 2P+2Q+R, with P,Q sharing two undirected edges.
The explicit neighbouring-pairs construction assembles with two A copies
and any helper from a different class. See
\protect\hyperlink{arg-2}{Argument 2}.

\hypertarget{two-doubled-nonneighbouring-opposite-varieties}{%
\subsubsection*{Two doubled nonneighbouring opposite
varieties}\label{two-doubled-nonneighbouring-opposite-varieties}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Two doubled nonneighbouring
opposite varieties}

Here P,Q share one undirected edge. The grid cover leaves one
overlapping pattern shape or three disjoint pattern shapes. Their
explicit targets and allocations are in Sections 2--3 of
\protect\hyperlink{arg-5}{Argument 5}.

Every final construction uses exactly two A copies, all five opposite
