sources, and one adjacent helper meeting a specified rescue set E. In
each case at most two adjacent classes avoid E, and A is one of them.
The third A copy occurs only in the initial rectangle construction
already covered by the replacement lemma.

For the overlapping shapes, E consists of five remaining positions after
two A copies and the single opposite source are assigned. The two
opposite pairs fill any four of these five. For the disjoint shapes, E
is the union of the helper positions in two explicit completions; it
consists of two bidirected edges and a further directed edge joining
their components. Keeping both endpoint pairs together and respecting
the remaining direction permits at most two adjacent cuts. These include
A, which has no corner in E.

\hypertarget{a-useful-nine-cube-strengthening}{%
\subsection*{3. A useful nine-cube
strengthening}\label{a-useful-nine-cube-strengthening}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. A useful nine-cube strengthening}

The preceding argument proves the following more general statement.

\textbf{Helper lemma.} Let A,B,C be three different adjacent
tricoloured-corner classes. Five capped opposite sources, two copies of
A, one source in B, and one in C contain an opposite assembly. Indeed,
at least one of the eight-cube subpools O+2A+B and O+2A+C assembles.

If O+3A assembles, the replacement lemma applies to either helper.
Otherwise use the grid-cover constructions above. In the low-repetition
and nonneighbouring cases, at most two classes avoid the rescue set, one
of which is A. Thus B and C cannot both avoid it. The neighbouring case
accepts either helper directly. Every construction uses the two actual
copies of A and only one helper. ∎

\hypertarget{proof-of-the-requested-fourteen-cube-theorem}{%
\subsection*{4. Proof of the requested fourteen-cube
theorem}\label{proof-of-the-requested-fourteen-cube-theorem}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Proof of the requested
fourteen-cube theorem}

If the nine adjacent cubes belong to nine different classes, the
elementary nine-distinct-class theorem gives an opposite assembly; see
\protect\hyperlink{arg-29}{Argument 29}.

Otherwise some class A occurs at least twice. Since the nine adjacent
sources have class cap three, at least three classes occur. Choose
sources from two other distinct classes B,C. The helper lemma gives an
assembly using the five opposite sources, the two A copies, and one of
those helpers. ∎

\hypertarget{arg-31}{%
\section{Argument 31: A nonlooped three-copy class has two private
corners}\label{arg-31}}

\textbf{Theorem.} In a failing pure fourteen-cube pool whose tricoloured
classes have multiplicity at most three, every three-copy class without
a loop has at least two tricoloured corners absent from all other
classes. In fact, it suffices that the class has no loop using its own
cut orientation.

This proof uses the explicit matching lemmas in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-25}{Argument 25},
\protect\hyperlink{arg-26}{Argument 26}, and
\protect\hyperlink{arg-27}{Argument 27}. It does not use the exhaustive
lists of fourteen-cube failures.

\hypertarget{hall-covers-and-four-zero-cuts}{%
\subsection*{1. Hall covers and four zero
cuts}\label{hall-covers-and-four-zero-cuts}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Hall covers and four zero cuts}

Normalize the three-copy class to \texttt{A=C(01)}, and suppose at most
one of its six corners is private. Thus its eleven outsiders meet at
least five corners of the two-by-three array \texttt{02,03,04;12,13,14}.

Call a cut disjoint from \texttt{A} a zero cut. The zero classes are
\texttt{B=C(234)} and
\texttt{Z\_i=C(\{2,3,4\}\textbackslash{}setminus\{i\})}, for
\texttt{i=2,3,4}.

If four zero cubes are present, choose two colours \texttt{q,s} each
contained in at least two of their outsets. This is always possible: at
most one of the three \texttt{Z\_i} multiplicities exceeds two. Their
four cubes match \texttt{q0,q1,s0,s1}, since each row has degree at
least two and their union sees all four sources. Some outsider meets the
forward half \texttt{\{0q,0s,1q,1s\}}: otherwise all outsider
intersections with \texttt{A} would lie in the other column,
contradicting the five-corner hypothesis. Use that outsider once and
three \texttt{A} copies for the forward half. This gives an opposite
target. Consequently the zero count satisfies

\[
z\le3.
\]

After removing two \texttt{A} cubes for bicoloured corners, one
\texttt{A} remains. If the outsiders match five \texttt{A} corners, that
last \texttt{A} completes the sixth and gives a loop. Their matching
rank is therefore at most four. By Konig's theorem they admit a cover by
\texttt{c} corner vertices and at most \texttt{4-c} source copies.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{c=4} with no source exceptions would make at least two corners
  private, contrary to the hypothesis.
\item
  \texttt{c=0} leaves at least seven zeros, impossible.
\item
  \texttt{c=1} leaves at least eight sources in one
  singleton-intersection class and the zero classes; these have total
  capacity at most \texttt{3+3=6}.
\end{itemize}

Thus it remains to examine three corners plus one exceptional source, or
two corners plus two exceptional sources.

\hypertarget{the-fourth-anchor-substitution}{%
\subsection*{2. The fourth-anchor
substitution}\label{the-fourth-anchor-substitution}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. The fourth-anchor substitution}

The following observation allows the four-copy proofs to be reused. If a
cut \texttt{R} meets \texttt{A} in at least two columns, then for every
pair \texttt{q,s} from \texttt{\{2,3,4\}}, it meets the forward half of
target \texttt{01\textbar{}qs}. Hence three \texttt{A} and \texttt{R}
can replace four \texttt{A} whenever those four copies were used to fill
that forward half.

The intersections of a cut with \texttt{A} are empty, a single cell, a
pair in one row, a two-by-two rectangle, or the full array. Therefore
any exceptional cut contributing at least two new cells has the required
two-column property.

\hypertarget{three-corner-cover-one-exceptional-source}{%
\subsection*{3. Three-corner cover, one exceptional
source}\label{three-corner-cover-one-exceptional-source}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Three-corner cover, one exceptional
source}

The other ten outsiders have their intersections within three cells. The
exceptional source \texttt{R} contributes at least two additional cells,
by the five-corner hypothesis. Up to symmetry the three-cell cover is a
row, a hook, or a disjoint pair-and-singleton.

\hypertarget{row-cover}{%
\subsubsection*{Row cover}\label{row-cover}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Row cover}

Normalize the row to \texttt{02,03,04}. Then \texttt{R} contains two
corners of the other row. The ten normal outsiders are exactly the
\texttt{P\_i,D\_i} and zero classes of
\protect\hyperlink{arg-25}{Argument 25}, now all of capacity three.

The opposite-target arguments in Section 1 of that note still work: each
use of four \texttt{A} was a forward half of \texttt{01\textbar{}qs}, so
use three \texttt{A} and \texttt{R}. Thus \texttt{z\textless{}=1}.
Section 2 is unnecessary, since the class cap three is already assumed.
Section 3 uses only two \texttt{A} and reduces the normal counts to the
same Families I and II.

Every completion and conflict in Family I uses at most three \texttt{A},
so its repeated-colour contradiction applies unchanged. In Family II,
the only use of four \texttt{A} is to fill the three second-row corners
and one first-row corner in an \texttt{A} target. \texttt{R} fills one
of its second-row corners and the three \texttt{A} fill the other three
positions. The same two looped three-copy classes are therefore joined
and require six different repeated colours. This settles the row cover.

\hypertarget{hook-cover}{%
\subsubsection*{Hook cover}\label{hook-cover}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Hook cover}

Normalize the cover to \texttt{\{02,03,12\}}. Its nonzero normal classes
are

\[
P=C(04),\quad D=C(034),\quad F=C(024),\quad E=C(134).
\]

If \texttt{e=0}, the row argument just proved applies. Otherwise
\texttt{p+d+f+e+z=10}, with all four counts at most three. The possible
exceptional cuts adding at least two cells outside the hook are

\[
R=C(12),\quad C(013),\quad C(012).
\]

The two-zero and one-zero arguments of the hook proof in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-26}{Argument 26} transfer directly whenever four
\texttt{A} fill a target \texttt{01\textbar{}qs}: replace one by
\texttt{R}. For completeness, the count reduction is particularly short
here. Two zeros already solve by that matching argument. With one zero,
\texttt{d\textgreater{}0} gives target \texttt{01∣34}; if \texttt{d=0},
then \texttt{e=p=f=3}. For zero \texttt{Z\_3}, target \texttt{01∣24}
solves; every other zero supplies \texttt{30} in target \texttt{02∣13},
whose other seven positions are \texttt{A:03,12}, \texttt{E:10,32},
\texttt{F:21,23}, \texttt{P:01}.

With no zeros, \texttt{e\textgreater{}=2} implies
\texttt{d\textgreater{}0} because the other three counts could otherwise
sum to at most nine. Target \texttt{01∣34} then uses the four-anchor
substitution and \texttt{E:30,40}, \texttt{D:31}, and another
\texttt{P,D,F:41}. The only remaining case is \texttt{e=1,p=d=f=3}. Its
three possible exceptional sources give these opposite assemblies:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Exceptional \texttt{R}
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Target
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Assignment
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{C(12)} & \texttt{01∣24} & \texttt{A:04,12,14}; \texttt{P:02};
\texttt{R:20}; \texttt{F:21}; \texttt{E:40}; \texttt{D:41} \\
\texttt{C(013)} & \texttt{13∣24} & \texttt{A:12,14}; \texttt{D:32};
\texttt{R:34}; \texttt{F:21,23,41}; \texttt{P:43} \\
\texttt{C(012)} & \texttt{12∣34} & \texttt{A:13,14}; \texttt{F:23};
\texttt{R:24}; \texttt{D:31,32,41}; \texttt{P:42} \\
\end{longtable}

Thus the hook cover is impossible.

\hypertarget{disjoint-cover}{%
\subsubsection*{Disjoint cover}\label{disjoint-cover}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Disjoint cover}

Normalize the cover to \texttt{\{02,03,14\}}. Use the same
\texttt{P,D,F} but now \texttt{E=C(123)}. If \texttt{e=0}, use the row
case. Otherwise the disjoint-cover proof in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-26}{Argument 26} applies with the fourth-anchor
substitution. Every use of four \texttt{A} in that proof fills the
forward half of a target \texttt{01\textbar{}qs}; all other assemblies
use at most three \texttt{A}. Its initial half-array lemma also has that
forward-half form. All count bounds remain valid, since the present cap
three is stronger than its cap four. Thus the disjoint cover also gives
an assembly.

\hypertarget{two-corner-cover-two-exceptional-sources}{%
\subsection*{4. Two-corner cover, two exceptional
sources}\label{two-corner-cover-two-exceptional-sources}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Two-corner cover, two exceptional
sources}

There are nine normal outsiders. If either exception adds at most one
cell outside the two-corner cover, add that cell to the corner cover;
there is then a three-corner cover with only one source exception,
already settled. We can therefore suppose both add at least two cells.

\hypertarget{corners-in-different-rows}{%
\subsubsection*{Corners in different
rows}\label{corners-in-different-rows}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Corners in different rows}

For a column, normalize to \texttt{\{02,12\}}. The only nonzero normal
classes are \texttt{D=C(034),\ E=C(134)}. Since \texttt{d+e+z=9} and
each of these three terms is at most three, all are three. Target
\texttt{01∣34} needs four forward sources and \texttt{D:31,41},
\texttt{E:30,40}. The five-corner hypothesis guarantees that at least
one exception has a forward corner outside column 2; use it with three
\texttt{A} to complete the forward half.

For a diagonal, normalize to \texttt{\{02,13\}}. The singleton classes
\texttt{D=C(034),\ E=C(124)} again each occur three times. Target
\texttt{03∣12} is filled by \texttt{A:02,13}, \texttt{D:01,31,32},
\texttt{E:10,20,23}.

\hypertarget{corners-in-the-same-row}{%
\subsubsection*{Corners in the same row}\label{corners-in-the-same-row}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Corners in the same row}

Normalize to \texttt{\{02,03\}}. The normal nonzero classes are
\texttt{P=C(04),D=C(034),F=C(024)}, with

\[
p+d+f+z=9,\qquad p,d,f\le3.
\]

Both exceptions \texttt{R,S} contain a pair of second-row corners,
because they each add at least two cells outside \texttt{\{02,03\}}. At
least one of \texttt{D,F} is present, since otherwise
\texttt{p+z\textless{}=6}. The half-array lemma with three \texttt{A},
the fourth-anchor substitute \texttt{R}, a singleton source, and three
zero sources shows that \texttt{z\textless{}=2}.

The proof now follows the same small conflict clique as in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-27}{Argument 27}, with these explicit
adjustments:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{P} has a loop whenever \texttt{p,d,f\textgreater{}=2}: reserve
  two \texttt{P}, then use \texttt{A:02,03}, \texttt{D:01,42},
  \texttt{F:41,43}.
\item
  To join \texttt{A} to \texttt{P,D}, or \texttt{F}, use an \texttt{A}
  target. After reserving one cube from each endpoint, two \texttt{A}
  remain. Normal sources fill \texttt{02,03}; the exceptions fill two
  different second-row corners, possible because each sees two such
  corners; two \texttt{A} finish.
\item
  To join \texttt{P,D} or \texttt{P,F}, use an \texttt{A} target again.
  Two normal sources fill \texttt{02,03}; one exception fills a
  second-row corner and the three \texttt{A} finish.
\item
  If one of \texttt{D,F} occurs three times, their edge is witnessed
  exactly as before: for \texttt{f\textgreater{}=3}, reserve one
  \texttt{D,F} and use remaining \texttt{F:21,23}, \texttt{P:01,43},
  remaining \texttt{D:41}, and \texttt{A:03}.
\end{itemize}

If \texttt{z=0}, then \texttt{p=d=f=3}; the clique \texttt{A,P,D,F},
with \texttt{P} looped, requires \texttt{1+3+1+1=6} different repeats.
If \texttt{z=1}, the counts are a permutation of \texttt{2,3,3}. The
same contradiction applies when \texttt{p=3}. When \texttt{p=2}, the
zero contains 2 or 3, so it loops \texttt{F} or \texttt{D} as in the
earlier note, and the clique \texttt{A,P,F} or \texttt{A,P,D} requires
\texttt{1+2+3=6} repeats.

If \texttt{z=2}, the forward-half argument from that note gives an
opposite assembly unless the two zeros are identical and one of
\texttt{d,f} is zero. But then \texttt{p+d+f=7} would have only two
nonzero counts, contradicting the cap three. So this final case solves
as well.

All possible Hall covers have led to an assembly. The assumed three-copy
nonlooped class must therefore have at least two private tricoloured
corners.

\hypertarget{arg-32}{%
\section{Argument 32: A source meeting a private corner promotes a
triple to an own loop}\label{arg-32}}

\textbf{Matching lemma.} In a failing pure fourteen-cube pool with class
multiplicities at most three, suppose a triple class has exactly two
private tricoloured corners. Its other four tricoloured corners can be
matched by four distinct cubes from other classes.

\textbf{Promotion corollary.} Adding any cube whose tricoloured corners
include either private corner makes this triple class completable in its
own orientation after two copies are reserved. In particular, an added
opposite-repeat cube meeting a private corner creates an own loop.

\hypertarget{the-possible-hall-cover}{%
\subsection*{1. The possible Hall cover}\label{the-possible-hall-cover}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. The possible Hall cover}

Normalize the triple to \texttt{A=C(01)}. Its eleven outsiders meet
exactly four of its six tricoloured corners. Call a source disjoint from
\texttt{A} a zero source, and write their number as \texttt{z}.

There are at most three zero sources. Indeed any four zero cuts have a
matching on two full reverse rows \texttt{q0,q1,s0,s1}: choose two rows
each seen by at least two of the sources. Their union sees all four
sources. The corresponding four-corner forward half cannot consist
entirely of private corners, since \texttt{A} has only two private
corners. An outsider therefore supplies one forward corner, and three
\texttt{A} supply the other three, giving an opposite assembly.

Thus at least eight outsiders meet \texttt{A}. Suppose they cannot match
its four nonprivate corners. Their matching rank is at most three, so by
Konig's theorem their incidences have a cover consisting of \texttt{c}
corner vertices and at most \texttt{3-c} source vertices.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Three corner vertices and no source exceptions cannot cover four
  occupied corners.
\item
  One corner and two source exceptions cover at most five nonzero
  sources: the unique singleton-intersection class has capacity three.
  Zero corners cover at most three sources.
\item
  Two corners in different rows allow only their two singleton classes,
  with combined capacity six, plus one exception. This is again fewer
  than eight sources.
\end{itemize}

The only possibility is two corners in one row and one exceptional
source. Normalize these corners to \texttt{02,03}. The ordinary nonzero
classes are

\[
P=C(04),\quad D=C(034),\quad F=C(024).
\]

If their counts are \texttt{p,d,f}, and the exceptional source is
\texttt{E}, then

\[
p+d+f+z=10,\qquad p,d,f\le3,\qquad z\le3.
\]

Consequently \texttt{1\textless{}=z\textless{}=3}, and all three
ordinary classes are present. The exceptional source supplies exactly
two new \texttt{A} corners. It is therefore one of
\texttt{C(12),C(13),C(14),C(014)}. In particular, it meets two
second-row columns of \texttt{A}, and meets the forward half of every
target \texttt{01\textbar{}qs} with \texttt{q,s} in \texttt{\{2,3,4\}}.
Thus three \texttt{A} and \texttt{E} can fill any such forward half.

\hypertarget{two-or-three-zero-sources-give-an-opposite-assembly}{%
\subsection*{2. Two or three zero sources give an opposite
assembly}\label{two-or-three-zero-sources-give-an-opposite-assembly}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Two or three zero sources give an
opposite assembly}

Choose any two zeros. If they can fill \texttt{20,30}, use
\texttt{F:21,D:31} to complete the reverse half of target
\texttt{01\textbar{}23}.

Otherwise the two zeros must both omit row 2 or both omit row 3: each
zero sees at least one of these rows, and two nonempty neighbour sets
fail a two-position matching only when they are the same singleton.

If both zeros are \texttt{C(34)}, let them fill \texttt{30,40}, and use
\texttt{D:31,P:41} for target \texttt{01\textbar{}34}. If both are
\texttt{C(24)}, use them at \texttt{20,40} and use \texttt{F:21,P:41}
for target \texttt{01\textbar{}24}.

In every case three \texttt{A} and \texttt{E} fill the forward half.
Hence \texttt{z\textgreater{}=2} is impossible in a failure.

\hypertarget{one-zero-source-gives-a-repeated-colour-contradiction}{%
\subsection*{3. One zero source gives a repeated-colour
contradiction}\label{one-zero-source-gives-a-repeated-colour-contradiction}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. One zero source gives a
repeated-colour contradiction}

Now \texttt{z=1}, so necessarily \texttt{p=d=f=3}.

The class \texttt{P} has an own loop. After reserving two \texttt{P},
match its tricoloured corners by

\[
A:02,03;\quad D:01,42;\quad F:41,43.
\]

The zero source contains 2 or 3. If it contains 3, \texttt{D} also has
an own loop: after reserving two \texttt{D}, use

\[
\text{remaining }D:32;\quad \text{zero}:31;\quad A:02;
\quad P:41,42;\quad F:01.
\]

If the zero contains 2, interchange 2 and 3 to obtain a loop on
\texttt{F}.

The looped classes \texttt{P,D} are joined. If they share a repeated
colour, reserve one of each for an \texttt{A} target's bicoloured
corners, using pole 0 from \texttt{P} and pole 1 from \texttt{D}. A
remaining \texttt{P} fills \texttt{02}, an \texttt{F} fills \texttt{03},
and \texttt{E} fills one second-row corner; three \texttt{A} fill the
other positions. The same argument joins \texttt{P,F}.

Each looped triple needs three different repeated colours, while the
joined triples must have disjoint repeat sets. Six colours would be
required. This is impossible, completing the matching lemma.

\hypertarget{promotion-by-an-added-source}{%
\subsection*{4. Promotion by an added
source}\label{promotion-by-an-added-source}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Promotion by an added source}

Let the private corners be \texttt{u,v}. Reserve two cubes of the
triple. If an added source supplies \texttt{u}, use it there, use the
remaining cube of the triple at \texttt{v}, and use the four outside
matches just proved for the other corners. This is an own-orientation
loop.

If another own-loop triple is already present and its pole pair meets
that of the promoted triple, an assembly follows immediately: both
triples must otherwise use all three possible repeated colours, their
repeat sets overlap, and the universal-restoration argument joins them.
This last conclusion remains valid when the added source is opposite-
repeat; it is used only for a tricoloured position.

\hypertarget{arg-33}{%
\section{Argument 33: Two own-loop triple classes cannot occur in a
failure}\label{arg-33}}

\textbf{Theorem.} A failing pure fourteen-cube pool with all
tricoloured-class counts at most three has at most one three-copy class
admitting an own-orientation loop.

An own-orientation loop on a class means that, after removing two copies
of that class, the remaining cubes match its six tricoloured corners.
This proof is elementary and independent of the exploratory own-loop
profile enumeration.

\hypertarget{repeated-colours-and-the-available-pole-pairs}{%
\subsection*{1. Repeated colours and the available pole
pairs}\label{repeated-colours-and-the-available-pole-pairs}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Repeated colours and the available
pole pairs}

An own-loop triple must use all three possible repeated colours;
otherwise a same-repeat pair completes its own target.

If \texttt{U} has an own loop, it conflicts with every present class
\texttt{V} whose pole pair meets that of \texttt{U}. Indeed, to reserve
one \texttt{U,V} for an \texttt{U} target, start with the loop matching
after two \texttt{U} reservations, restore one universal \texttt{U}, and
remove the chosen \texttt{V}. If the removed \texttt{V} had been used,
the restored \texttt{U} replaces it.

Consequently two own-loop triples must have disjoint pole pairs. Their
three-element repeat sets overlap, so intersecting pole pairs would
already give a solution. A reverse-orientation class with the same pole
pair as an own-loop triple is absent for the same reason.

Normalize the two pole pairs to \texttt{01} and \texttt{23}, and the
first class to

\[
A=C(01).
\]

The second class is either \texttt{C(23)} or \texttt{C(014)}. Both
triples use all three repeats: \texttt{A} uses \texttt{2,3,4}, while the
other class uses \texttt{0,1,4}. Every other present pole pair must meet
at most one of \texttt{01,23}. If it met both, its repeat would have to
lie in both disjoint pairs. Thus all eight remaining cubes have one of
the four pole pairs

\[
04,\quad14,\quad24,\quad34.
\]

Their repeated colours are forced, respectively, to be

\[
1,\quad0,\quad3,\quad2.
\]

\hypertarget{the-second-class-is-c23}{%
\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{2. The second class is
\texttt{C(23)}}{2. The second class is C(23)}}\label{the-second-class-is-c23}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. The second class is \texttt{C(23)}}

Consider opposite target \texttt{01\textbar{}23}. Each of the eight
possible remaining classes meets exactly one of its two four-corner
halves. If a source of each kind occurs, use those two sources, three
\texttt{A}, and three \texttt{C(23)} to fill the target.

Otherwise all remaining sources meet the same half. If they meet the
forward half, their only possible corners in the \texttt{C(23)} target
are \texttt{24,34}; if they meet the reverse half, their only possible
corners in the \texttt{A} target are \texttt{04,14}. After reserving two
copies of the relevant anchor, its one remaining universal copy and
these two corner positions cannot supply six corners. One of the
asserted own loops is impossible.

Thus this orientation always solves.

\hypertarget{the-nested-orientation-cc014}{%
\subsection*{\texorpdfstring{3. The nested orientation
\texttt{C=C(014)}}{3. The nested orientation C=C(014)}}\label{the-nested-orientation-cc014}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. The nested orientation
\texttt{C=C(014)}}

Name the eight other classes as follows. A bar denotes the reverse cut.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllr@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Pole pair & Class & Reverse class & Forced repeat \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{04} & \texttt{P=C(04)} & \texttt{Pbar=C(123)} & 1 \\
\texttt{14} & \texttt{Q=C(14)} & \texttt{Qbar=C(023)} & 0 \\
\texttt{24} & \texttt{R=C(24)} & \texttt{Rbar=C(013)} & 3 \\
\texttt{34} & \texttt{S=C(34)} & \texttt{Sbar=C(012)} & 2 \\
\end{longtable}

Call \texttt{P,Q,Rbar,Sbar} the main classes and the other four the
outer classes. Put

\[
a=g(Pbar)+g(Qbar),\qquad b=g(R)+g(S).
\]

\hypertarget{three-preliminary-observations}{%
\subsubsection*{Three preliminary
observations}\label{three-preliminary-observations}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Three preliminary observations}

\textbf{First, \texttt{a,b\textless{}=3}.} An own-loop triple has at
least five tricoloured corners met by outsiders. Four sources disjoint
from it would give an opposite assembly: choose two reverse rows each
met by at least two of the four zero sources; they fill that reverse
rectangle, and some outsider meets its forward rectangle because the
outside support has at least five of the anchor's six corners. That
outsider and three anchor copies fill the forward rectangle.

The only permitted classes disjoint from \texttt{A} are \texttt{R,S},
and the only permitted classes disjoint from \texttt{C} are
\texttt{Pbar,Qbar}. Hence \texttt{a,b\textless{}=3}.

\textbf{Second, a main class of size three excludes every outer source.}
By symmetry it suffices to consider \texttt{P\^{}3}. Its two corners
outside the four-corner intersection with \texttt{C} are \texttt{01,41}.
A \texttt{Qbar}, \texttt{R}, or \texttt{S} supplies one of these.
Reserve a same-repeat \texttt{P} pair, use its remaining copy for the
other corner, and use three \texttt{C} and one \texttt{A} for
\texttt{02,03,42,43}. This assembles a \texttt{P} target.

If a \texttt{Pbar} instead occurs, it has the same forced repeat as
\texttt{P} and the same poles. Reserve one \texttt{P} and one
\texttt{Pbar}; the two remaining \texttt{P} fill \texttt{01,41}, and the
same four-corner completion works.

The needed symmetries are interchange of 0 and 1, interchange of 2 and
3, and reversal followed by \texttt{(0\ 2)(1\ 3)}. The last symmetry
exchanges \texttt{A,C}, exchanges the blocks \texttt{\{P,Q\}} and
\texttt{\{Rbar,Sbar\}}, and exchanges the two outer blocks.

\textbf{Third, if both main blocks are present, every main count is at
most one.} For example, if \texttt{P} occurs twice and \texttt{Rbar}
occurs, reserve an \texttt{A} cube repeating 3 and that \texttt{Rbar}
for the poles \texttt{04} of a \texttt{P} target. Two \texttt{P} fill
\texttt{01,41}; three \texttt{C} and one remaining \texttt{A} fill
\texttt{02,03,42,43}. The same construction works with \texttt{Q}, or
with \texttt{Sbar} instead of \texttt{Rbar}.

Conversely, if \texttt{Rbar} occurs twice and \texttt{P} occurs, reserve
the \texttt{C} cube repeating 1 and \texttt{P} for an \texttt{Rbar}
target's poles \texttt{24}. Two \texttt{Rbar} fill \texttt{32,34}; three
\texttt{A} and one remaining \texttt{C} fill \texttt{02,04,12,14}. Again
the other choices are symmetric.

\hypertarget{the-outer-sources-have-only-two-types}{%
\subsubsection*{The outer sources have only two
types}\label{the-outer-sources-have-only-two-types}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{The outer sources have only two
types}

There must be an outer source. If there were none, the own loop on
\texttt{A} would require some \texttt{Rbar,Sbar}, since its corners
\texttt{04,14} cannot both be filled by its one remaining copy.
Similarly the own loop on \texttt{C} would require some \texttt{P,Q} to
supply \texttt{42,43}. Both main blocks would then be present, forcing
at most one of each main class, whereas eight sources are required.

Since an outer source is present, the second observation bounds every
main class by two. If both main blocks occur, the third observation
improves this to one. Either way, the main total is at most four. Thus

\[
a+b\ge4,\qquad a,b\le3,
\]

so both outer blocks are nonempty.

For target \texttt{01\textbar{}23}, the reverse intersections of the
outer classes are

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & Reverse positions \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{Pbar} & \texttt{20,30} \\
\texttt{Qbar} & \texttt{21,31} \\
\texttt{R} & \texttt{20,21} \\
\texttt{S} & \texttt{30,31} \\
\end{longtable}

Any three of these four types, with at least four copies in total, match
the four positions. This follows directly from Hall: each position has a
neighbour, any two positions see at least two sources, and any three
positions see every source. Three \texttt{A} and one \texttt{C} fill the
forward half. Therefore at most two outer types can occur. Since both
outer blocks are nonempty, exactly one occurs in each. By interchanging
0,1 and 2,3, normalize them to

\[
Pbar^a,\quad R^b,\qquad 1\le a,b\le3,\quad a+b\ge4.
\]

\hypertarget{the-remaining-numerical-cases}{%
\subsubsection*{The remaining numerical
cases}\label{the-remaining-numerical-cases}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{The remaining numerical cases}

Write \texttt{p,q,rbar,sbar} for the main counts. Their sum is
\texttt{8-a-b}, between two and four. By the exchange symmetry above,
assume the block \texttt{\{Rbar,Sbar\}} is present.

If \texttt{a\textgreater{}=2} and \texttt{sbar\textgreater{}0}, target
\texttt{04\textbar{}23} is filled by

\[
A:02;\quad C:03,42,43;\quad Pbar:30,34;\quad
R:20;\quad Sbar:24.
\]

Otherwise consider target \texttt{13\textbar{}24}. Its forward half is
filled by

\[
A:12,14;\quad Rbar:32;\quad Pbar:34.
\]

A \texttt{C} fills \texttt{43}; three \texttt{R} can fill its other
reverse positions \texttt{21,23,41}. Two \texttt{R} also suffice if a
\texttt{P} supplies \texttt{41}.

If \texttt{a=1}, then \texttt{b=3} and the main total is four. Here
\texttt{Rbar} must be present: without it, a single main block has
capacity two, while two present blocks have total capacity at most
three. Thus the displayed target works with three \texttt{R}.

The only remaining case has \texttt{a\textgreater{}=2} and
\texttt{sbar=0}, hence \texttt{rbar\textgreater{}0}. If \texttt{P,Q} are
both absent, the main total is at most two, forcing \texttt{a=b=3}, and
three \texttt{R} finish. If the block \texttt{\{P,Q\}} is present, every
main count is at most one; therefore \texttt{a+b\textgreater{}=5} and
\texttt{b\textgreater{}=2}. When \texttt{b=3} we again finish with three
\texttt{R}. When \texttt{b=2}, necessarily \texttt{a=3} and
\texttt{p=q=rbar=1}; use two \texttt{R} at \texttt{21,23} and the
\texttt{P} at \texttt{41}.

All cases have an opposite or adjacent assembly. Two own-loop triple
classes are therefore impossible in a failure.

\hypertarget{the-same-exclusion-for-fifteen-cubes}{%
\subsection*{4. The same exclusion for fifteen
cubes}\label{the-same-exclusion-for-fifteen-cubes}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. The same exclusion for fifteen
cubes}

The argument also excludes two own-loop triple classes in a failing pure
fifteen-cube pool with class counts at most three. The disjoint- outset
orientation is handled exactly as in Section 2.

For the nested orientation there are now nine star-class helpers. Every
preliminary observation remains valid: \texttt{a,b\textless{}=3}, an
outer source exists, each main count is at most two, and the main total
is at most four. Consequently \texttt{a+b\textgreater{}=5}. The
reverse-square argument again reduces the outer sources to
\texttt{Pbar\^{}a,R\^{}b}. Now

\[
2\le a,b\le3,\qquad
m=p+q+rbar+sbar=9-a-b\in\{3,4\}.
\]

By the exchange symmetry assume the \texttt{Rbar,Sbar} block is present.
If \texttt{sbar\textgreater{}0}, the displayed \texttt{04\textbar{}23}
assembly applies, since \texttt{a\textgreater{}=2}. Otherwise
\texttt{rbar\textgreater{}0}. The \texttt{P,Q} block cannot be empty,
since the main total is at least three but \texttt{rbar\textless{}=2}.
Both blocks therefore occur, so each main count is at most one. With
\texttt{sbar=0}, this forces

\[
m=3,\quad a=b=3,\quad p=q=rbar=1.
\]

The displayed \texttt{13\textbar{}24} assembly, using three \texttt{R},
now solves. This proves the fifteen-cube extension independently of any
classification of fourteen-pools with a unique own-loop triple.

\hypertarget{arg-34}{%
\section{Argument 34: Adding an opposite source to a butterfly
failure}\label{arg-34}}

\textbf{Theorem.} Every pool obtained by adding one opposite-repeat cube
to a fourteen-cube butterfly failure has an assembly.

This is an elementary fifteen-cube result. It uses the already proved
butterfly classification and repeated-colour refinement, and does not
require a classification of failures having an own-loop triple.

Normalize the pivot to0 and the two blocks to P=\{1,2\}, Q=\{3,4\}. The
four high classes are

\begin{verbatim}
C1=C(01), C2=C(02), C3=C(124), C4=C(123),
\end{verbatim}

with three cubes in each. The low classes C(12), C(012) occur once. By
the elementary refinement theorem, all three cubes in each high class
have the same repeated colour; the four high repeats form a derangement
of \{1,2,3,4\}. Both low cubes repeat0.

The private-corner pairs of the high classes are

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & Private corners \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
C1 & 02,12 \\
C2 & 01,21 \\
C3 & 40,43 \\
C4 & 30,34 \\
\end{longtable}

If the added opposite source meets one of these pairs, reserve two
same-repeat copies of that high class for its bicoloured corners. Its
third copy fills the other private corner, and the opposite source fills
the chosen private corner. The standard butterfly pools fill the four
remaining positions. For example, for C1, two C2 fill03,04, while C3 and
C4 fill13,14. The other high classes are symmetric.

It remains to consider an opposite source avoiding all eight private
corners. Because an opposite source is symmetric under arc reversal, it
must avoid every undirected edge in the two triangles012 and034. The
only remaining edges are the four between P and Q. Therefore the unique
avoiding opposite variety is O=opposite12\textbar34, omitting0.

For this source assemble the opposite target02\textbar13, omitting4:

\begin{verbatim}
O:32;
C2:01,21,23;
C1:03,12;
C4:10,30.
\end{verbatim}

These are its eight distinct corner types. Hence every opposite source
completes the butterfly failure.

\hypertarget{arg-35}{%
\section{Argument 35: A common-corner 3,3,2,2 core with a looped
triple}\label{arg-35}}

\textbf{Lemma.} Suppose four distinct adjacent tricolour classes have a
common directed corner. They occur with multiplicities \texttt{3,3,2,2}.
If either of the triple classes admits an own-orientation loop in the
full pool, the full pool is soluble.

An own loop may use any extra sources in the full pool. All other
matchings in this proof use just the ten sources in the stated core.

\hypertarget{notation-and-the-three-possible-supports}{%
\subsection*{1. Notation and the three possible
supports}\label{notation-and-the-three-possible-supports}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Notation and the three possible
supports}

Normalize the common corner to \texttt{01}, and put
\texttt{Q=\{2,3,4\}}. The six classes containing that corner are

\[
P_i=C(0i),\qquad D_i=C(\{0\}\cup(Q\setminus\{i\})),
\quad i\in Q.
\]

Their pole pairs are \texttt{0i} and \texttt{1i}, respectively. The
symmetries are arbitrary permutations of \texttt{Q}, and interchange of
all \texttt{P\_i,D\_i} (reverse every cut and then interchange colours 0
and 1).

Four present classes leave two absent. Up to these symmetries, the
absent classes are either two \texttt{P} classes, \texttt{P\_i,D\_i}, or
\texttt{P\_i,D\_j} with \texttt{i!=j}. Consequently it suffices to treat
the supports

\[
\{P_4,D_2,D_3,D_4\},\qquad
\{P_3,P_4,D_3,D_4\},\qquad
\{P_3,P_4,D_2,D_4\}.
\]

Recall that a looped triple in a failure must use all three allowed
repeated colours. A looped double uses two distinct repeated colours.
Two classes are called conflicting when a shared repeated colour would
produce an assembly: reserve one cube of each for the two bicoloured
target corners and match the six tricoloured corners.

\hypertarget{three-classes-of-one-kind-and-one-of-the-other}{%
\subsection*{2. Three classes of one kind and one of the
other}\label{three-classes-of-one-kind-and-one-of-the-other}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Three classes of one kind and one
of the other}

For support \texttt{P4,D2,D3,D4}, every pair of classes conflicts
already when all four counts are just two. Here is a complete table. Its
assignments are made after one source of each reserved class is removed;
a class name in the target column specifies its six tricoloured corners.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Reserved pair & Target & Tricoloured assignment \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{P4,D2} & \texttt{P2} &
\texttt{P4:03;\ D2:01;\ D3:21,23;\ D4:04,24} \\
\texttt{P4,D3} & \texttt{P3} &
\texttt{P4:02;\ D2:31,32;\ D3:01;\ D4:04,34} \\
\texttt{P4,D4} & \texttt{P4} &
\texttt{P4:43;\ D2:02,42;\ D3:03,41;\ D4:01} \\
\texttt{D2,D3} & \texttt{D2} &
\texttt{P4:02,42;\ D2:32;\ D3:41;\ D4:01,31} \\
\texttt{D2,D4} & \texttt{D2} &
\texttt{P4:02,42;\ D2:32;\ D3:01,41;\ D4:31} \\
\texttt{D3,D4} & \texttt{D3} &
\texttt{P4:03,43;\ D2:01,41;\ D3:23;\ D4:21} \\
\end{longtable}

In each row the two reserved pole pairs can supply the two poles of the
stated target. Hence these four classes form a conflict clique. The
asserted looped triple needs three repeated colours; each of the other
three classes needs at least one; and all those colours must be
distinct. Six colours would be required, a contradiction.

\hypertarget{two-matched-pairs-of-classes}{%
\subsection*{3. Two matched pairs of
classes}\label{two-matched-pairs-of-classes}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Two matched pairs of classes}

Now the support is \texttt{P3,P4,D3,D4}, with all counts at least two
and total ten. We show that these four classes again form a conflict
clique.

The cross pair \texttt{P3,D4} conflicts already at counts
\texttt{2,2,2,2}: reserve one of each for a \texttt{P4} target, and
match

\[
P_3:02;\quad D_4:01;\quad P_4:42,43;\quad D_3:03,41.
\]

Interchanging 3 and 4 gives the edge \texttt{P4,D3}.

Since the counts total ten, one of

\[
g(P_3)+g(D_4),\qquad g(P_4)+g(D_3)
\]

is at least five. Interchange 3 and 4 if needed, so that the first sum
is at least five. To prove the edges \texttt{P3,P4} and \texttt{P3,D3},
reserve one cube of each relevant pair and use target \texttt{P3}.
Assign

\[
P_3:32;\qquad P_4:02;\qquad D_3:01.
\]

The remaining target positions are \texttt{04,31,34}. All remaining
\texttt{P3} and \texttt{D4} sources contain them, and their number is

\[
g(P_3)-2+g(D_4)\ge3.
\]

Thus they fill the last three positions. The displayed \texttt{P4,D3}
sources remain available in either reservation because their original
counts are at least two.

Similarly, to prove \texttt{D4,D3} and \texttt{D4,P4}, use target
\texttt{D4}, assigning

\[
D_4:24;\qquad D_3:21;\qquad P_4:01.
\]

Its remaining positions are again \texttt{04,31,34}, filled by the
remaining \texttt{D4,P3} sources. This gives all six edges. As in
Section 2, one looped triple and the three other nonempty classes would
require six distinct repeated colours. This is impossible.

\hypertarget{two-unmatched-absent-classes}{%
\subsection*{4. Two unmatched absent
classes}\label{two-unmatched-absent-classes}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Two unmatched absent classes}

The remaining support is \texttt{P3,P4,D2,D4}.

\hypertarget{two-intrinsic-loops-and-four-edges}{%
\subsubsection*{Two intrinsic loops and four
edges}\label{two-intrinsic-loops-and-four-edges}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Two intrinsic loops and four edges}

At counts \texttt{2,2,2,2}, the classes \texttt{P3} and \texttt{D2}
already have own loops. After reserving two \texttt{P3}, match a
\texttt{P3} target by

\[
P_4:01,02;\quad D_2:31,32;\quad D_4:04,34.
\]

After reserving two \texttt{D2}, match a \texttt{D2} target by

\[
P_3:02,32;\quad P_4:41,42;\quad D_4:01,31.
\]

The usual universal-restoration argument therefore gives the edges
\texttt{P3,P4} and \texttt{D2,D4}, since the corresponding pole pairs
intersect. Two more edges have the following explicit matchings:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Reserved pair & Target & Tricoloured assignment \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{P3,D4} & \texttt{P4} &
\texttt{D4:01;\ P3:02;\ D2:41,42;\ P4:03,43} \\
\texttt{P4,D2} & \texttt{D4} &
\texttt{P4:01;\ D2:31;\ P3:04,34;\ D4:21,24} \\
\end{longtable}

If \texttt{P4} has count three, then \texttt{P4,D4} also conflict:
reserve one of each for target \texttt{P4}, and assign

\[
P_4:03,43;\quad D_2:41,42;\quad P_3:02;\quad D_4:01.
\]

If \texttt{D4} has count three instead, use target \texttt{D4}, with

\[
D_4:21,24;\quad P_3:04,34;\quad D_2:31;\quad P_4:01.
\]

\hypertarget{neither-intrinsic-loop-class-can-be-a-triple}{%
\subsubsection*{Neither intrinsic-loop class can be a
triple}\label{neither-intrinsic-loop-class-can-be-a-triple}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Neither intrinsic-loop class can be
a triple}

Suppose \texttt{P3} is a triple. Its loop forces its repeated-colour set
to be \texttt{\{1,2,4\}}. Its conflict with \texttt{P4}, whose poles are
\texttt{04}, then forces every \texttt{P4} to repeat 3.

The looped class \texttt{D2} has allowed repeats \texttt{\{0,3,4\}} and
conflicts with \texttt{P4}. It therefore cannot be a triple, and its two
copies must repeat 0 and 4. A \texttt{D4} must avoid the repeat sets of
both \texttt{P3} and \texttt{D2}, by the two edges above. Since its
allowed repeats are \texttt{\{0,2,3\}}, it too must repeat 3.

The second triple is now either \texttt{P4} or \texttt{D4}. The
corresponding displayed matching gives the edge between them. Both
repeat 3, a contradiction.

The symmetry interchanging all \texttt{P,D} classes and then
interchanging 2 and 3 exchanges \texttt{P3} and \texttt{D2}, while
preserving this support. It excludes a \texttt{D2} triple in the same
way.

\hypertarget{the-remaining-two-triples}{%
\subsubsection*{The remaining two
triples}\label{the-remaining-two-triples}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{The remaining two triples}

Consequently \texttt{P3,D2} are the doubled classes and \texttt{P4,D4}
are the triples. At least one of these latter two is assumed to have an
own loop in the full pool.

If \texttt{P4} is looped, it uses all repeats \texttt{\{1,2,3\}}. Its
conflict with the looped double \texttt{P3}, whose repeats must be two
distinct members of \texttt{\{1,2,4\}}, leaves only 4 available to
\texttt{P3}. This is impossible.

If \texttt{D4} is looped, it uses all repeats \texttt{\{0,2,3\}}. Its
conflict with the looped double \texttt{D2}, whose repeats must be two
distinct members of \texttt{\{0,3,4\}}, again leaves only 4. This is
equally impossible.

All three support cases have been exhausted by the two-absence
classification in Section 1. The lemma follows.

\hypertarget{arg-36}{%
\section{Argument 36: No triple in a failing pure fourteen-pool has a
private row pair}\label{arg-36}}

\textbf{Lemma.} Consider a failing pool of fourteen adjacent-repeat
cubes, with every tricoloured class of multiplicity at most three. No
triple class has two private corners in one row of its two-by-three cut
array. Consequently every triple without an own-orientation loop has
exactly two private corners.

The dependencies are the nonprivate-triple lemma
\protect\hyperlink{arg-31}{Argument 31}, the two-own-loop exclusion
\protect\hyperlink{arg-33}{Argument 33}, the six-repeated-class bound
\protect\hyperlink{arg-28}{Argument 28}, and the common-corner lemmas
cited below. None uses this row-pair exclusion.

\hypertarget{row-geometry}{%
\subsection*{1. Row geometry}\label{row-geometry}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Row geometry}

Normalize the distinguished class to A=C(01), with private corners 02
and03, and put Q=\{2,3,4\}. Every outsider avoids both private corners.
The possible outsider classes are exactly

\begin{verbatim}
P_i=C(1i), N_i=C(1,Q\{i})  (i∈Q),
H=C(023), B=C(234), Z_i=C(Q\{i})  (i∈Q).
\end{verbatim}

The six P/N classes have the common corner 10. Write v for their total
multiplicity, h for the H multiplicity, and z for the total B/Z
multiplicity. Thus v+h+z=11, with every individual class count at most3.

In the reverse-A array, whose rows are q0,q1 for q∈Q, the P/N sources
meet only the 0-column:

\begin{verbatim}
P_i sees i0;       N_i sees j0,k0.
\end{verbatim}

Their intersections with A are complementary sets of columns in the
1-row: P\_i sees 1j,1k, while N\_i sees1i. A B/Z source sees both
positions in at least two reverse rows. H sees21,31 and no 0-column
position. Thus every source outside the P/N family that meets the
reverse 1-column meets at least two rows there.

\hypertarget{at-most-three-zero-intersection-sources}{%
\subsubsection*{At most three zero-intersection
sources}\label{at-most-three-zero-intersection-sources}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{At most three zero-intersection
sources}

Suppose z≥4 and some nonzero-intersection outsider is present. If z≥5,
every reverse row sees at least z−3≥2 zero sources. Choose a two-row
rectangle containing a forward column met by a nonzero outsider X. Four
zero sources fill the reverse half: each row has degree at least2, and
every zero source meets their union. X and three A fill the forward
half.

If z=4, choose two reverse rows each having zero-source degree at
least2. At most one row can fail this bound. The seven nonzero outsiders
cannot all meet A in the remaining column: only the two
singleton-intersection classes for that column could do so, with total
capacity6. Thus an X meeting the chosen rectangle exists, and the same
construction works.

If every outsider is zero, z=11. The three Z classes have capacity9, so
B is present; their combined count is at least8 because B≤3. The Z
sources alone match all six corners of reverse A: every row has at least
five neighbours and any two rows see all Z sources. A repeated colour
among the three A copies would therefore solve the reverse-A target.
Otherwise A uses all three possible repeat colours, and any B copy
shares one of them; one A and that B supply the bicoloured corners,
while the Z sources fill the tricoloured corners. This also solves.

Consequently z≤3.

\hypertarget{reducing-to-two-exceptional-supports}{%
\subsubsection*{Reducing to two exceptional
supports}\label{reducing-to-two-exceptional-supports}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Reducing to two exceptional
supports}

Put u=h+z. Suppose u≥2. Then u≤6 and v=11−u≥5. Let R⊆Q be the rows met
by the P/N sources in the reverse 0-column. Since only P\_i is confined
to a single row and its count is at most3, \textbar R\textbar≥2.

We first record a small completion fact. If i,j∈R, three distinct P/N
sources can fill i0,j0 and one forward corner of the rectangle on i,j.
To apply Hall, regard the last position as a flexible slot accepting any
source with either1i or1j. Its degree is v−n\_k≥2, where k is the third
row. The first two positions have positive degree, and their union has
degree v−p\_k≥2. The flexible slot together with either 0-column
position sees every P/N source, by the complementary pattern in
Section1. Thus Hall holds. Three A fill the other forward positions.

If the u sources can match two distinct reverse 1-column positions whose
rows lie in R, the preceding completion gives an opposite target. For
\textbar R\textbar=3 they always can. For R=\{i,j\}, every such source
meets at least one of these rows, and u≥2, so failure forces all of them
to miss one row, say j.

All zero sources are then Z\_j, and H can occur only if j=4. Every one
of these u sources meets rows i,k, where k is outside R. If
z\textgreater0 and u≥3, use a Z\_j at k0 and two other u-sources at
i1,k1. Two distinct P/N sources supply i0 and one forward corner of the
i,k rectangle: the former has positive degree, the latter has degree
v−n\_j≥2, and their union contains all v sources. Three A complete the
forward half.

There remain just two possibilities.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  u=2. Then v=9. Because the 0-column support is R=\{i,j\}, the only
  possible P/N classes are P\_i,P\_j,N\_k, so each has count3.
\item
  z=0 and h≥2. Then j=4, v=11−h≥8, and the same three classes
  P\_i,P\_j,N\_k are the only possibilities. All are present, and at
  least one of P\_i,P\_j has count3.
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{the-two-exceptions-need-no-all-private-assumption}{%
\subsection*{2. The two exceptions need no all-private
assumption}\label{the-two-exceptions-need-no-all-private-assumption}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. The two exceptions need no
all-private assumption}

Whenever \texttt{A,P\_i,P\_j,N\_k} are present, both \texttt{P\_i} and
\texttt{P\_j} have at most one private corner. For \texttt{P\_i}, A
covers its 1-row corners, \texttt{P\_j} covers \texttt{10}, and
\texttt{N\_k} covers \texttt{i0,ik}, leaving at most \texttt{ij}. The
statement for \texttt{P\_j} is symmetric. The nonprivate-triple lemma
therefore makes either of these classes an own-loop class if its count
is three.

The first exception has two such triples. It contradicts the
two-own-loop-triple exclusion.

In the second exception, \texttt{h=2} likewise makes all three common
classes triples, giving the same contradiction. Thus \texttt{h=3} and
their counts sum to eight. If both \texttt{P\_i,P\_4} are triples, again
there are two own-loop triples. Otherwise \texttt{N\_k} is a triple and
exactly one of \texttt{P\_i,P\_4} is a triple, with the other doubled.

If \texttt{P\_4} is that triple, its own loop conflicts with the present
class \texttt{H=C(023)}, which has the same pole pair \texttt{14}. The
looped triple uses all three allowed repeated colours; H has no other
allowed repeated colour. This is impossible.

If \texttt{P\_i} is the triple, its own loop uses all three repeated
colours outside its poles \texttt{1i}. Both A and H have pole pairs
meeting \texttt{1i}, so own-loop restoration makes them conflict with
\texttt{P\_i}. Each A and each H must consequently repeat \texttt{i}.
Reserve one A and one H for an A target's bicoloured corners. Its six
tricoloured corners have the completion

\[
 A:02,03;\qquad H:04;\qquad P_4:1i;\qquad
 N_k:1k;\qquad P_i:14.
\]

All six sources remain available: A and H originally have three copies
each, and the other three classes are present. This is a solution,
another contradiction.

Thus \texttt{u\textless{}=1}, and hence \texttt{v\textgreater{}=10}.

\hypertarget{ten-common-corner-sources-are-now-impossible}{%
\subsection*{3. Ten common-corner sources are now
impossible}\label{ten-common-corner-sources-are-now-impossible}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Ten common-corner sources are now
impossible}

If all six P/N classes occur, the common-corner nine-cube theorem
applies. At most three present classes have capacity at most nine. It
remains to consider four or five present common-corner classes.

With five classes present, every one has at most one private corner even
within this family. Thus every triple among them has an own loop, and at
most one can be a triple. Their total is at most eleven.

If the total is eleven, the counts are \texttt{3,2,2,2,2}; these five
repeated classes, together with A, violate the six-repeated-class bound.
If the total is ten and there is no triple, all five are doubled, giving
the same contradiction. The only remaining profile is
\texttt{3,2,2,2,1}, which always solves by
\protect\hyperlink{arg-23}{Argument 23}.

With exactly four common-corner classes present, at most one has two
private corners after A is included. Here is the two-absence check. In
the common-corner family, P\_i has private arcs \(i\to j,i\to k\) only
when \(N_j,N_k\) are absent; if only \(N_j\) is absent, a second private
arc \(1\to j\) also requires \(P_k\) absent. The dual statements hold
for N\_i. The additional class A supplies every \(1\to q\), removing
that second P-type arc. If both absent classes are N-types, only the
complementary P-type can retain two private corners; the dual holds when
both are P-types. With one absent type of each kind, at most one N-type
retains two. Hence at most one triple can lack an own loop. Since there
is at most one own-loop triple as well, the total is at most
\texttt{3+3+2+2=10}.

Equality is required by \texttt{v\textgreater{}=10}. The profile is
therefore \texttt{3,3,2,2}, and at least one of its two triples has an
own loop. The lemma \protect\hyperlink{arg-35}{Argument 35} excludes
exactly this case.

All possibilities have been excluded, proving the row-pair lemma.

\hypertarget{consequence-for-every-non-own-loop-triple}{%
\subsection*{4. Consequence for every non-own-loop
triple}\label{consequence-for-every-non-own-loop-triple}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Consequence for every non-own-loop
triple}

The nonprivate-triple lemma gives at least two private corners to every
triple without an own loop. Three private corners in a two-row array
would put two in one row, which has just been excluded. Thus such a
triple has exactly two private corners.

In particular \protect\hyperlink{arg-32}{Argument 32} now applies to
every non-own-loop triple in a failing pure fourteen-pool: its four
nonprivate corners have an outsider matching, and adjoining any source
meeting a private corner creates an own loop.

\hypertarget{arg-37}{%
\section{Argument 37: Every non-own-loop triple has a private
column}\label{arg-37}}

\textbf{Lemma.} In a failing pool of fourteen adjacent-repeat cubes,
with all tricoloured class counts at most three, every triple without an
own-orientation loop has exactly two private corners, in one column of
its two-by-three cut array.

The unconditional row lemma already gives exactly two private corners.
Here we exclude a diagonal pair even when the pool has one own-loop
triple. The proof uses \protect\hyperlink{arg-36}{Argument 36}, the
nonprivate-triple lemma, and the two-own-loop-triple exclusion.

\textbf{Orientation convention.} For a positive class \texttt{C(pq)}, a
row pair has the same tail \texttt{p} or \texttt{q}. For a negative
class \texttt{C(V\textbackslash{}\{p,q\})}, a row pair has the same head
\texttt{p} or \texttt{q}. In particular the pairs \texttt{40,42} in
\texttt{C(134)} and \texttt{20,24} in \texttt{C(123)} are column pairs;
we make no row-based exclusion of them.

\hypertarget{the-diagonal-normal-form-and-reverse-rectangles}{%
\subsection*{1. The diagonal normal form and reverse
rectangles}\label{the-diagonal-normal-form-and-reverse-rectangles}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. The diagonal normal form and
reverse rectangles}

Normalize the distinguished triple to \texttt{A=C(01)} with private
corners \texttt{02,13}. Its eleven outsiders avoid these corners, and
have exactly the following possible classes:

\[
\begin{array}{lll}
X_0=C(13),&X_1=C(134),&X_2=C(123),\\
Y_0=C(02),&Y_1=C(024),&Y_2=C(023),
\end{array}
\]

together with \texttt{B=C(234)}, \texttt{Z\_2=C(34)},
\texttt{Z\_3=C(24)}, and \texttt{Z\_4=C(23)}. Let \texttt{x\_i,y\_i} be
their counts, \texttt{x=Σx\_i}, \texttt{y=Σy\_i}, and let \texttt{z}
count the B/Z sources. Thus \texttt{x+y+z=11}.

A has exactly two private corners, so its other four corners are covered
by outsiders; in particular \texttt{x,y\textgreater{}0}. The reverse-A
intersections are as follows.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & Reverse-A corners \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{X\_0} & \texttt{30} \\
\texttt{X\_1} & \texttt{30,40} \\
\texttt{X\_2} & \texttt{20,30} \\
\texttt{Y\_0} & \texttt{21} \\
\texttt{Y\_1} & \texttt{21,41} \\
\texttt{Y\_2} & \texttt{21,31} \\
\end{longtable}

Each B/Z source sees both reverse corners in at least two rows.

A matching of four outsiders to any reverse rectangle
\texttt{q0,q1,s0,s1} implies an opposite assembly. To verify Hall, a set
of at most three target corners meeting A has its three copies as
neighbours. A four-set meeting A gains an outsider from the reverse
matching if it meets the reverse half; otherwise A has only two private
corners, so the forward four-set also gains an outsider. Reverse-only
sets are covered by the assumed matching. For five- and six-sets, their
complements contain complete intersections of at most three and two
adjacent classes, respectively. The class cap gives at least \(14-9=5\)
and \(14-6=8\) neighbours. A complement of at most one corner contains
no whole source intersection. These are all Hall inequalities.

It follows that \texttt{z\textless{}=3}. At \texttt{z\textgreater{}=5}
each reverse row has at least \texttt{z-3\textgreater{}=2} zero
neighbours; at \texttt{z=4} some two rows each have degree at least two.
In either case four zeros match a reverse rectangle.

The permutation \texttt{(0\ 1)(2\ 3)} exchanges X and Y and exchanges
\texttt{Z\_2,Z\_3}. We will use this symmetry.

\hypertarget{preliminary-restrictions-if-the-base-class-is-a-triple}{%
\subsection*{2. Preliminary restrictions if the base class is a
triple}\label{preliminary-restrictions-if-the-base-class-is-a-triple}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Preliminary restrictions if the
base class is a triple}

Suppose \texttt{x\_0=3} and at least one of \texttt{x\_1,x\_2} is
positive. A and that branch cover all but at most one corner of
\texttt{X\_0}, so the nonprivate-triple lemma gives \texttt{X\_0} an own
loop.

Its three cubes must use all three repeated colours outside its pole
pair \texttt{13}. Its reverse class \texttt{Y\_1=C(024)} is therefore
absent: own-loop restoration makes classes with the same poles conflict,
and \texttt{Y\_1} has the same three available repeated colours. Thus

\[
y_1=0.
\]

If \texttt{Y\_0,Y\_2} both occur, neither can be a triple. A and
\texttt{Y\_2} cover all but corner \texttt{23} of \texttt{Y\_0}, while
\texttt{Y\_0} and \texttt{X\_0} cover all but corner \texttt{31} of
\texttt{Y\_2}. A triple in either class would have another own loop.
Since at most one own-loop triple is possible, both counts are at most
two. If only one class is present, its count is at most three.
Consequently

\[
y\le4.
\]

Also \texttt{x\_2\textless{}=2}. Indeed \texttt{X\_0} covers four
corners of \texttt{X\_2}, leaving only \texttt{20,24}. At least one of
\texttt{Y\_0,Y\_2} occurs because \texttt{y\textgreater{}0} and
\texttt{y\_1=0}; either covers \texttt{24}. Thus a triple \texttt{X\_2}
would have at most one private corner and give a second own loop. We
have proved

\[
x_0=3,\quad x_2\le2,\quad x\le8,\quad
 y_1=0,\quad y\le4.
\]

\hypertarget{the-base-triple-exception-is-impossible}{%
\subsection*{3. The base-triple exception is
impossible}\label{the-base-triple-exception-is-impossible}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. The base-triple exception is
impossible}

Continue under the assumptions of Section 2.

\hypertarget{no-zero-sources}{%
\subsubsection*{No zero sources}\label{no-zero-sources}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{No zero sources}

Now \texttt{x+y=11}, so \texttt{x\textgreater{}=7} and
\texttt{y\textgreater{}=3}. In particular \texttt{x\_2\textgreater{}0}.
If \texttt{y\_2\textgreater{}0}, match rectangle 2,3 by

\[
X_2:20;\quad X_0:30;\quad Y_2:31;\quad
\text{another Y}:21.
\]

Thus \texttt{y\_2=0}, giving \texttt{y=y\_0\textless{}=3}. Equality is
forced, as is \texttt{x=8}, and therefore

\[
x_1=3,\qquad x_2=2,\qquad y_0=3.
\]

But \texttt{Y\_0=C(02)} then has private corners \texttt{21,23}: none of
A or the X classes contains either. They form a private row pair in this
positive class, contradicting the unconditional row lemma.

\hypertarget{one-zero-source}{%
\subsubsection*{One zero source}\label{one-zero-source}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{One zero source}

Here \texttt{x+y=10}, so \texttt{x\textgreater{}=6} and
\texttt{y\textgreater{}=2}.

If the zero is B or \texttt{Z\_4}, an \texttt{X\_2} gives rectangle 2,3
by \texttt{X\_2:20,\ X\_0:30,\ Y:21,\ zero:31}; a \texttt{Y\_2} gives
the analogous rectangle using zero \texttt{20}, \texttt{X\_0:30}, and
another Y at \texttt{21}. Thus \texttt{x\_2=y\_2=0}. But then
\texttt{x\textless{}=6} and \texttt{y=y\_0\textless{}=3}, contradicting
their sum ten.

If the zero is \texttt{Z\_2}, the first of those constructions gives
\texttt{x\_2=0}. Hence \texttt{x\textless{}=6} and
\texttt{y\textgreater{}=4}; equality is forced, so \texttt{x\_1=3} and
\texttt{y\_0=y\_2=2}. Rectangle 3,4 is filled by
\texttt{X\_1:40,\ X\_0:30,\ Y\_2:31,\ zero:41}.

If the zero is \texttt{Z\_3}, a \texttt{Y\_2} gives rectangle 2,3 by
\texttt{zero:20,\ X\_0:30,\ Y\_2:31}, and another Y at \texttt{21}. Thus
\texttt{y\_2=0}, so \texttt{y\textless{}=3} and
\texttt{x\textgreater{}=7}. Both X branches occur. Rectangle 2,4 is
filled by \texttt{X\_2:20,\ X\_1:40,\ Y\_0:21,\ zero:41}.

\hypertarget{two-or-three-zero-sources}{%
\subsubsection*{Two or three zero
sources}\label{two-or-three-zero-sources}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Two or three zero sources}

If two zeros match \texttt{20,31}, an X at \texttt{30} and a Y at
\texttt{21} finish rectangle 2,3. Every zero sees at least one of
\texttt{20,31}; with at least two copies, failure of this two-position
matching means that they all miss row 2 or all miss row 3. Thus they are
all \texttt{Z\_2}, or all \texttt{Z\_3}.

Both types see \texttt{40,41}. Two zeros at those positions, together
with \texttt{X\_2:20} and a Y at \texttt{21}, exclude
\texttt{x\_2\textgreater{}0}. Similarly using \texttt{X\_0:30,Y\_2:31}
excludes \texttt{y\_2\textgreater{}0}. Hence \texttt{x\_2=y\_2=0}. The
equation \texttt{3+x\_1+y\_0=11-z\textgreater{}=8} forces
\texttt{x\_1\textgreater{}=2}.

For zeros all \texttt{Z\_2}, match rectangle 3,4 by
\texttt{X\_1:40,\ X\_0:30}, and two zeros at \texttt{31,41}. For zeros
all \texttt{Z\_3}, match rectangle 2,4 by \texttt{X\_1:40,\ Y\_0:21},
and two zeros at \texttt{20,41}. Both give a contradiction.

This proves that \texttt{x\_0=3} is impossible whenever either X branch
is present. By symmetry, \texttt{y\_0=3} is impossible whenever either Y
branch is present.

\hypertarget{the-original-count-bounds-are-restored}{%
\subsection*{4. The original count bounds are
restored}\label{the-original-count-bounds-are-restored}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. The original count bounds are
restored}

We now have

\[
x_0\le2\text{ if }x_1+x_2>0,
\qquad y_0\le2\text{ if }y_1+y_2>0.
\]

Therefore \texttt{x,y\textless{}=8}; if either branch of a group is
absent, that group's total is at most five. These are the count bounds
used in the original diagonal proof, now established without an
all-private hypothesis. We finish its three zero-count cases, replacing
its final private-corner objections where necessary.

\hypertarget{no-zero-sources-1}{%
\subsection*{5. No zero sources}\label{no-zero-sources-1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. No zero sources}

Here \texttt{x+y=11}, so \texttt{x,y\textgreater{}=3}. If
\texttt{x\_2,y\_2\textgreater{}0}, match rectangle 2,3 by
\texttt{X\_2:20}, another X at \texttt{30}, \texttt{Y\_2:31}, and
another Y at \texttt{21}. By symmetry suppose \texttt{x\_2=0}.

Then \texttt{x\textless{}=5} and \texttt{y\textgreater{}=6}, forcing
\texttt{y\_1,y\_2\textgreater{}0}. An \texttt{X\_1} would give rectangle
3,4 using \texttt{X\_1:40}, another X at \texttt{30},
\texttt{Y\_2:31,Y\_1:41}. Thus \texttt{x\_1=0}. The counts now force

\[
x_0=3,\qquad y_0=2,\qquad y_1=y_2=3.
\]

The positive class \texttt{X\_0=C(13)} has private corners
\texttt{30,32}: A and all three Y classes miss both. This is a forbidden
private row pair. Hence there are no zero-free failures of this form.

\hypertarget{two-or-three-zero-sources-1}{%
\subsection*{6. Two or three zero
sources}\label{two-or-three-zero-sources-1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{6. Two or three zero sources}

As in Section 3, the \texttt{20,31} test forces all zeros to be one of
\texttt{Z\_2,Z\_3}. By symmetry choose \texttt{Z\_2}.

Two zeros at \texttt{40,41} exclude \texttt{x\_2\textgreater{}0} using
rectangle 2,4, and exclude \texttt{y\_2\textgreater{}0} using rectangle
3,4. Thus \texttt{x\_2=y\_2=0}. Now \texttt{x,y\textless{}=5} and
\texttt{x+y=11-z} is eight or nine, so \texttt{x,y\textgreater{}=3}. If
\texttt{x\_1,y\_1\textgreater{}0}, rectangle 3,4 is supplied by
\texttt{X\_1:40}, another X at \texttt{30}, \texttt{Y\_1:41}, and a zero
at \texttt{31}.

If \texttt{x\_1=0}, the counts force \texttt{z=3}, \texttt{x\_0=3}, and
\texttt{y=5}. But the positive class \texttt{Z\_2=C(34)} has private
corners \texttt{40,42}, since A, \texttt{X\_0,Y\_0,Y\_1} all miss both.
Its three copies have a forbidden private row pair.

If \texttt{y\_1=0}, the counts force \texttt{z=3}, \texttt{y\_0=3}, and
\texttt{x=5}. Now the positive class \texttt{Y\_0=C(02)} has private
corners \texttt{21,23,24}, all missed by A, \texttt{X\_0,X\_1,Z\_2}.
Again a triple has a forbidden private row pair.

\hypertarget{one-zero-source-1}{%
\subsection*{7. One zero source}\label{one-zero-source-1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{7. One zero source}

Now \texttt{x+y=10}, so \texttt{x,y\textgreater{}=2}.

If the zero is B or \texttt{Z\_4}, it sees both rows 2,3. An
\texttt{X\_2} gives rectangle 2,3 with another X, a Y, and zero
\texttt{31}; a \texttt{Y\_2} gives the symmetric construction. Thus
\texttt{x\_2=y\_2=0}, forcing \texttt{x=y=5}. In particular both
\texttt{X\_1,Y\_1} occur, and rectangle 3,4 is matched by
\texttt{X\_1:40}, another X at \texttt{30}, \texttt{Y\_1:41}, and zero
\texttt{31}.

Otherwise symmetry makes the zero \texttt{Z\_2}. An \texttt{X\_2} again
gives rectangle 2,3, so \texttt{x\_2=0}, \texttt{x\textless{}=5}, and
\texttt{y\textgreater{}=5}. If \texttt{y\_1,y\_2} are both positive,
match rectangle 3,4 by \texttt{X:30}, zero \texttt{40},
\texttt{Y\_2:31,Y\_1:41}. Therefore one Y branch is absent, and the
bounds give

\[
x_0=2,\quad x_1=3,\quad y_0=2,
\]

with exactly one of \texttt{y\_1,y\_2} equal to three.

If \texttt{y\_1=3}, use \texttt{X\_1:40}, another X at \texttt{30},
\texttt{Y\_1:41}, and zero \texttt{31} for rectangle 3,4.

If \texttt{y\_2=3}, neither \texttt{X\_1} nor \texttt{Y\_2} has any
private corner. For \texttt{X\_1}, \texttt{X\_0} covers
\texttt{10,12,30,32} and \texttt{Z\_2} covers \texttt{40,42}. For
\texttt{Y\_2}, \texttt{Y\_0} covers \texttt{01,04,21,24}, \texttt{Z\_2}
covers \texttt{31}, and \texttt{X\_0} covers \texttt{34}. The
nonprivate-triple lemma gives both triples own loops, contradicting the
at-most-one-own-loop theorem.

All cases have now been excluded. A non-own-loop triple cannot have a
diagonal private pair. Its two private corners therefore form a column,
as claimed.

\hypertarget{arg-38}{%
\section{Argument 38: Private columns force a butterfly}\label{arg-38}}

\textbf{Theorem.} Let a failing pool consist of fourteen adjacent-repeat
cubes. Suppose no class of multiplicity three is completable in its own
orientation after two copies have been reserved. Then the pool is
contained in a butterfly support family. Consequently the earlier
butterfly classification gives the class-size profile
\texttt{3,3,3,3,1,1}.

This is a hand argument, using the already proved facts that a pure
fourteen-failure has maximum class size three, has at most five repeated
classes, and that, under the present hypothesis, each triple class has
exactly two private corners in a column. The stronger private-column
lemma in \protect\hyperlink{arg-37}{Argument 37} proves this without
assuming that the other triples lack own completions.

Here \texttt{ab} means the directed corner a→b, and C(S) is the directed
cut S→Sᶜ on the five colours. A private corner of a class belongs to no
other occupied class. A butterfly support family has no supported
opposite target; the elementary classification of such families is in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-15}{Argument 15}.

\hypertarget{normalize-one-triple-class}{%
\subsection*{1. Normalize one triple
class}\label{normalize-one-triple-class}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Normalize one triple class}

Choose a triple class and, reversing all cuts if necessary, normalize it
to A=C(01), with private column \{02,12\}. Every other outset must
satisfy

\begin{verbatim}
0∈S ⇒ 2∈S,        1∈S ⇒ 2∈S.
\end{verbatim}

The complete list of possible other classes, and their intersections
with the reverse-A array, is:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Name & Class & Reverse-A corners \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
P & C02 & 21 \\
Q & C12 & 20 \\
L3 & C123 & 20,30 \\
L4 & C124 & 20,40 \\
R3 & C023 & 21,31 \\
R4 & C024 & 21,41 \\
H & C012 & none \\
B & C234 & 20,21,30,31,40,41 \\
Z23 & C23 & 20,21,30,31 \\
Z24 & C24 & 20,21,40,41 \\
Z34 & C34 & 30,31,40,41 \\
\end{longtable}

The reverse array has rows \{20,21\}, \{30,31\}, \{40,41\}. Call B and
the three Z classes the zero classes: they have no tricoloured corner in
A. Lower-case letters denote the corresponding multiplicities.

We use the following completion observation throughout.

\textbf{Reverse-rectangle criterion.} If four outsiders match both
reverse corners in each of two rows, the pool has an opposite assembly.

Indeed use the opposite target whose forward half is the corresponding
four corners of A. Hall holds for a set of at most three corners meeting
the forward half, because the three A copies meet it. For a four-set
meeting both halves, the A copies and a reverse neighbour suffice. For
the whole forward half, an outsider neighbour exists because A has only
two private corners. Sets contained in the reverse half are covered by
the assumed four-corner matching. Finally a complement of three corners
contains entire intersections with at most three adjacent classes, and a
complement of two contains at most two. The class cap three therefore
gives at least 14−9=5 neighbours to a five-set and at least 14−6=8 to a
six-set. Every adjacent class meets an opposite target in at least two
corners, so seven- and eight-sets see all fourteen sources. These are
all Hall inequalities.

\hypertarget{three-classes-cannot-be-triples}{%
\subsection*{2. Three classes cannot be
triples}\label{three-classes-cannot-be-triples}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Three classes cannot be triples}

\hypertarget{h-has-at-most-two-copies}{%
\subsubsection*{H has at most two
copies}\label{h-has-at-most-two-copies}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{H has at most two copies}

A covers four of H's six corners, leaving only \{23,24\} as possible
private corners. If H were a triple, both would be private. Avoiding
\{02,12,23,24\} leaves only A,B,Z34 besides H. Four classes with cap
three contain at most twelve cubes. Thus h≤2.

\hypertarget{b-has-at-most-two-copies}{%
\subsubsection*{B has at most two
copies}\label{b-has-at-most-two-copies}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{B has at most two copies}

Suppose A and B each have three copies. For any opposite target with
parts 01 and two of \{2,3,4\}, they supply three universal forward and
three universal reverse sources. Two additional sources, one at a
forward corner and one at a reverse corner, complete the target.

If a helper has a corner in both halves, any second helper allows this
completion: every adjacent class meets the target, and the first helper
can take the opposite half from the second. Each of P,Q,L3,L4, R3,R4 has
such a mixed intersection for some target through A. For P,Q use rows
2,3; for L3,R3 use rows 2,4; for L4,R4 use rows 2,3. Thus none of these
classes can occur.

The eight helpers must therefore lie in H and the Z classes. H meets
only the forward half of every target through A, and every Z meets only
the reverse half. Both kinds occurring would complete a target. If all
helpers are H, B has all six corners private; if all are Z classes, A
has all six private. Both contradict the private-column hypothesis.
Therefore b≤2.

\hypertarget{z34-has-at-most-two-copies}{%
\subsubsection*{Z34 has at most two
copies}\label{z34-has-at-most-two-copies}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Z34 has at most two copies}

Suppose A and Z34 are triples, and use target 01∣34. They supply the two
universal halves. Each L/R helper meets both halves and would complete
the target with any second helper. The remaining helpers split into
forward-only classes P,Q,H and reverse-only classes B,Z23,Z24. Both
kinds occurring again complete the target. If all helpers are
forward-only, every corner of Z34 is private. If all are reverse-only,
every corner of A is private. Thus z34≤2.

\hypertarget{a-second-triple-must-exist}{%
\subsection*{3. A second triple must
exist}\label{a-second-triple-must-exist}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. A second triple must exist}

Suppose every class other than A has multiplicity at most two. Since
h≤2, at least nine outsiders have a nonempty reverse-A intersection.

Their matching rank on the six reverse corners is at most four. A
matching of size five would occupy two full rows, and the reverse-
rectangle criterion would solve the pool.

By König's theorem, cover these incidences by at most four vertices, of
which c are corner vertices and at most 4−c are physical-source
vertices. Every nonexceptional source has its whole reverse pattern
inside those c corners.

For c≤3, at most c distinct nonempty patterns fit inside the chosen
corners. To see this without a case table, the six patterns of size at
most two form two disjoint rooted stars, each with its root singleton:
root20 with leaves30,40, and root21 with leaves31,41. Within each star,
the number of contained patterns is at most the number of selected
vertices. Every other nonempty pattern has size at least four. Each
pattern has multiplicity at most two, so at most 2c+(4−c)=4+c≤7 nonempty
sources can occur, a contradiction.

For c=4 there are no source exceptions. At least five distinct patterns
must fit inside the four corners, since at least nine sources occur. The
only four-corner sets containing five listed patterns are rows 2,3 or
rows 2,4 in full. Their five patterns are P,Q,Lq,Rq,Z2q, respectively.
Every one must occur, since four patterns have capacity only eight. The
four classes P,Q,Lq,Rq match 21,20,q0,q1. This is a reverse rectangle,
another contradiction.

Thus some class besides A has multiplicity three.

\hypertarget{a-triple-p-or-q-forces-butterfly-support}{%
\subsection*{4. A triple P or Q forces butterfly
support}\label{a-triple-p-or-q-forces-butterfly-support}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. A triple P or Q forces butterfly
support}

By symmetry it suffices to treat P. Since A supplies P's corners 03,04,
the only possible private column of P is \{01,21\}. Avoiding this pair
as well as \{02,12\} leaves only

\begin{verbatim}
A, P, Q, L3, L4, Z34, H.
\end{verbatim}

If Q were also a triple, its private column would be \{10,20\},
excluding L3,L4. Then the pool has at most 3+3+3+2+2=13 cubes, using the
bounds on Z34,H. Thus q≤2.

Suppose z34\textgreater0. Neither L3 nor L4 can be a triple: A rules out
its column indexed 1, P rules out its column indexed 2, and Z34 rules
out its remaining column. Therefore q,l3,l4,z34,h≤2 and

\begin{verbatim}
q+l3+l4+z34+h=8.
\end{verbatim}

At least one of L3,L4 occurs; by symmetry take L3. Since q+l3+l4≥4,
match reverse corners by

\begin{verbatim}
L3:30; Z34:31; P:21;
another source from Q,L3,L4:20.
\end{verbatim}

The reverse-rectangle criterion gives a contradiction. Consequently
z34=0, and all sources lie in

\begin{verbatim}
{C01,C02,C12,C123,C124,C012}.                 (F)
\end{verbatim}

This is a butterfly family: its union misses exactly 31,32,41,42.

The same argument with colours 0,1 exchanged handles Q and gives the
reflected butterfly family with R3,R4 in place of L3,L4.

\hypertarget{any-triple-lr-class-forces-butterfly-support}{%
\subsection*{5. Any triple L/R class forces butterfly
support}\label{any-triple-lr-class-forces-butterfly-support}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. Any triple L/R class forces
butterfly support}

The symmetries 0↔1 and 3↔4 reduce this to L3=C123.

A rules out L3's column indexed 1. Its column indexed 2 is \{20,24\}; if
this were private, the only other allowed classes would be A,R4,Z34,
whose combined capacity with L3 is at most twelve. Thus L3's private
column is \{30,34\}. The possible sources are now exactly

\begin{verbatim}
A, L3, P, Q, L4, R4, Z24, H.
\end{verbatim}

If P is a triple, Section 4 already gives (F). If L4 is a triple, the
same private-column argument forces its pair \{40,43\}; this excludes
R4,Z24 and directly gives (F).

R4 cannot be a triple, since the following eight-source assignment
assembles target 04∣13:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Sources & Corners \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
2 A & 03,14 \\
3 L3 & 10,30,34 \\
3 R4 & 01,41,43 \\
\end{longtable}

Q cannot be a triple: its only possible private column is \{10,20\},
already supplied by L3. We may therefore assume

\begin{verbatim}
p,q,l4,r4,h≤2.
\end{verbatim}

We next show z24≤2. If Z24 were a triple, its column indexed 0 is
blocked by L3 at 20. Its column indexed 3 is \{23,43\}; making this
private leaves only A,L3,Z24, far too few sources. Therefore its private
pair would be \{21,41\}, excluding P,R4. This forces

\begin{verbatim}
q+l4+h=5,
\end{verbatim}

so l4\textgreater0. But then L3:20, L4:40 and two Z24 at21,41 form a
reverse rectangle. Thus z24≤2 as claimed.

All six counts p,q,l4,r4,z24,h are now at most two and total eight. Put

\begin{verbatim}
R=p+l4+r4+z24=8−q−h≥4.
\end{verbatim}

To fill reverse rows 2,4, use an L3 at20 and seek a matching on
\{21,40,41\}. The relevant neighbour patterns are

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & Positions \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
P & 21 \\
L4 & 40 \\
R4 & 21,41 \\
Z24 & 21,40,41 \\
\end{longtable}

Every pair has at least two neighbours: for \{40,41\}, subtract p≤2 from
R≥4; for \{21,41\}, subtract l4≤2; the other pair sees all R. The
three-set sees R≥4. Also 21 has at least R−l4≥2 neighbours. Thus Hall
can fail only if 40 or 41 is unsupported, namely

\begin{verbatim}
l4+z24=0  or  r4+z24=0.
\end{verbatim}

Hence z24=0. If r4=0, all sources lie in (F). Otherwise l4=0, and
R=p+r4≥4 forces p=r4=2. The total then gives q=h=2. Together with A,L3,
these are six repeated classes, forbidden by the six-repeated- classes
theorem. This proves the claim for every L/R triple.

In particular the conclusion of Sections 4 and 5 excludes every zero
