class whenever one of P,Q,L3,L4,R3,R4 is a triple.

\hypertarget{the-remaining-zero-classes-cannot-be-triples}{%
\subsection*{6. The remaining zero classes cannot be
triples}\label{the-remaining-zero-classes-cannot-be-triples}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{6. The remaining zero classes cannot
be triples}

It remains to consider Z23,Z24; exchange 3,4 to treat Z23.

If Z23's private column is indexed 4, its pair is \{24,34\}. The only
other allowed classes are A,B,L4,R4,Z24,Z34. None supplies 04 or14.
These would be additional private corners of A, contradicting that A has
exactly the pair \{02,12\}.

If Z23's private column is indexed 0, its pair is \{20,30\}. The only
other allowed classes are A,P,R3,R4,H. A triple among P,R3,R4 would
force butterfly support by Sections 4 or 5, excluding Z23. Thus all four
of P,R3,R4,H have counts at most two. Their total must be eight, so all
are doubled. Together with A,Z23, this is again six repeated classes,
impossible.

The column indexed 1 is the same argument with 0,1 exchanged. Therefore
Z23,Z24 are not triples.

\hypertarget{conclusion}{%
\subsection*{7. Conclusion}\label{conclusion}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{7. Conclusion}

Section 3 forces a second triple. Sections 2 and 6 exclude H,B and all Z
classes as that triple. It must therefore be P,Q or an L/R class, and
Sections 4 and 5 force a butterfly support family.

The earlier elementary butterfly classification now gives exactly four
triple classes and two singleton classes. In particular, for this entire
branch,

\begin{verbatim}
A_stat = 4 binom(3,2)=12,    B_stat=0,    3A_stat−4B_stat=36.
\end{verbatim}

This closes the all-private pure branch. It does not settle a pool with
an own-orientation looped triple; that is a separate remaining branch.

\hypertarget{arg-39}{%
\section{Argument 39: An elementary sharp threshold for adjacent-repeat
cubes}\label{arg-39}}

\textbf{Theorem.} Any fifteen adjacent-repeat five-colour cubes contain
eight that assemble a five-colour 2×2×2 cube with monochromatic faces.
Fourteen do not suffice.

This proof does not use the exhaustive fourteen-cube failure list. It
also does not require finishing the classification of fourteen-failures
with exactly one own-orientation looped triple. It is a synthesis of the
written elementary lemmas indexed below; the full proof chain is longer
than this final deletion argument.

\hypertarget{three-established-facts}{%
\subsection*{1. Three established facts}\label{three-established-facts}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Three established facts}

A tricoloured-corner class consists of three adjacent-repeat varieties
with the same six oriented tricoloured corners. Call a class
\emph{own-looped} if, after two cubes from it are reserved, the
remaining pool matches its six tricoloured corners. The property is
monotone under adding cubes.

The following results have elementary proofs.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Concentration and diversity bounds.} A failing pure
  fourteen-pool has maximum class size three; a pool occupying nine
  distinct classes already has an opposite assembly; a pure
  fourteen-pool with six repeated classes assembles.
\item
  \textbf{No own-looped triple.} A pure fourteen-failure with no
  own-looped triple lies in a butterfly family and has class
  counts3,3,3,3,1,1.
\item
  \textbf{Two own-looped triples.} A failing pure pool of fifteen cubes,
  all class counts at most three, cannot contain two own-looped triples.
\end{enumerate}

For (1), see \protect\hyperlink{arg-12}{Argument 12}, the three
four-copy proofs in \texttt{../pure\_supported/},
\protect\hyperlink{arg-29}{Argument 29}, and
\protect\hyperlink{arg-28}{Argument 28}. For (2), see
\protect\hyperlink{arg-38}{Argument 38} and its explicit dependencies.
For (3), see Section4 of \protect\hyperlink{arg-33}{Argument 33}.

\hypertarget{the-deletion-argument}{%
\subsection*{2. The deletion argument}\label{the-deletion-argument}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. The deletion argument}

Suppose a pure fifteen-cube failure exists. Every fourteen-cube subpool
is also a failure. Therefore every class has at most three copies: any
four copies could be retained in a fourteen-subpool, contradicting the
concentration bound.

At most eight classes are occupied, by the nine-distinct-class theorem.
Some class has size three. Otherwise fifteen cubes in at most eight
classes, each of size at most two, force seven doubled classes. Select
two copies from six of those classes and any two additional sources; the
resulting fourteen-pool has six repeated classes and solves.

Now take any triple class T and delete one of its cubes. The resulting
fourteen-pool still fails and has an occupied class of size two. It
cannot be a failure with no own-looped triple, since such a failure has
only triple and singleton classes. Thus it contains an own-looped triple
U≠T. The same U is own-looped in the original fifteen-pool, by
monotonicity.

Apply this argument again, now taking T=U. It produces a second
own-looped triple different from U. This contradicts fact (3). Therefore
every pure fifteen-pool assembles.

\hypertarget{arg-40}{%
\section{Argument 40: Five copies in one class, with diffuse opposite
sources}\label{arg-40}}

\textbf{Theorem.} A fourteen-pool containing five copies from an
adjacent tricolour class and at least one opposite-repeat source is
soluble, provided no opposite variety occurs more than twice.

Together with the separate pure-adjacent five-class theorem, this
removes every adjacent class of size five from the remaining diffuse
branch.

\hypertarget{notation-and-two-elementary-bounds}{%
\subsection*{1. Notation and two elementary
bounds}\label{notation-and-two-elementary-bounds}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Notation and two elementary bounds}

Colours are 0,1,2,3,4, and \texttt{ab} denotes the directed tricoloured
corner a→b. Write C(S) for the cut S→Sᶜ. Its pole pair is its
two-element side. Its three varieties are indexed by the repeated colour
outside that pair. Thus four copies of a class contain a same-repeat
pair, supplying both bicoloured corners of a target of that class.
Opposite varieties are bidirected K₂,₂ graphs on four colours.

Normalize the distinguished class to A=C(01). Its six corners form a
two-by-three array with rows 0,1 and columns 2,3,4. The adjacent classes
disjoint from A are precisely

\begin{verbatim}
B=C(234), Z23=C(23), Z24=C(24), Z34=C(34).
\end{verbatim}

The reverse of A has three two-corner columns q→\{0,1\}. A B source sees
all three, and a Z source sees two entire columns. Every opposite source
meets A, and also its reverse, in either two corners in different
columns or four corners forming a two-column rectangle. Moreover a
two-corner intersection determines the opposite variety uniquely: its
two undirected edges determine the omitted colour and the bipartition.

We also use the shared-four bound: two adjacent classes with four common
corners have at most seven copies in a failure. Otherwise choose a class
with at least four copies, reserve a same-repeat pair for its bicoloured
corners and two further copies for its two private tricoloured corners,
and fill the four common corners from the other four sources.

\hypertarget{three-useful-eight-cube-constructions}{%
\subsection*{2. Three useful eight-cube
constructions}\label{three-useful-eight-cube-constructions}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Three useful eight-cube
constructions}

Call an adjacent source \textbf{disjoint} if it has no tricoloured
corner of A.

\hypertarget{construction-i-four-a-three-disjoint-sources-one-opposite-source}{%
\subsubsection*{Construction I: four A, three disjoint sources, one
opposite
source}\label{construction-i-four-a-three-disjoint-sources-one-opposite-source}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Construction I: four A, three
disjoint sources, one opposite source}

Choose two reverse-A columns met by the opposite source. On these four
positions, every disjoint source sees one whole column or both. If the
three disjoint sources do not all see just the same single column, the
four sources have a perfect matching: the three disjoint sources jointly
see all four positions, and any two of them together with the opposite
source see at least three.

In the exceptional case all three disjoint sources have the same class:
each sees the common column and the third column. Retarget to those two
columns. The three sources are now universal on the four reverse
positions, and the opposite source still sees at least one. In either
case the four A copies fill the four forward positions of the resulting
opposite target.

Consequently, if a failing pool contains at least four A and one
opposite source, it contains at most two sources disjoint from A.

\hypertarget{construction-ii-three-a-three-disjoint-sources-two-opposite-sources}{%
\subsubsection*{Construction II: three A, three disjoint sources, two
opposite
sources}\label{construction-ii-three-a-three-disjoint-sources-two-opposite-sources}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Construction II: three A, three
disjoint sources, two opposite sources}

Apply Construction I's reverse-half argument using the first opposite
source. The second opposite source meets the selected forward half,
because it meets at least two of the three A columns. It and three A
copies fill that half.

Consequently, if a failure contains at least three A and two opposite
sources, at most two adjacent sources are disjoint from A.

\hypertarget{construction-iii-three-a-four-disjoint-sources-one-opposite-source}{%
\subsubsection*{Construction III: three A, four disjoint sources, one
opposite
source}\label{construction-iii-three-a-four-disjoint-sources-one-opposite-source}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Construction III: three A, four
disjoint sources, one opposite source}

The four disjoint sources match the reverse positions of some two-column
rectangle. If three of them miss the same column, select the other two;
otherwise every missing-column class occurs at most twice, and any two
columns work. This is Hall's theorem for two position pairs: at most two
sources are confined to either pair. The opposite source and three A
copies fill the forward half.

Consequently, if a failure contains at least three A and one opposite
source, at most three adjacent sources are disjoint from A.

\hypertarget{five-a-and-two-distinct-opposite-varieties}{%
\subsection*{3. Five A and two distinct opposite
varieties}\label{five-a-and-two-distinct-opposite-varieties}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Five A and two distinct opposite
varieties}

First we record a stronger two-disjoint-source construction:

\begin{quote}
Four A, two disjoint adjacent sources, and two distinct opposite
varieties always assemble an opposite target.
\end{quote}

Here is an elementary three-column matching argument. Write P,Q for the
reverse-A intersections of the opposite sources. Each meets at least two
columns, and \textbar P∪Q\textbar≥3. Write Z,W for the two disjoint
sources' reverse-A intersections; each contains at least two complete
columns.

If Z,W have two whole columns in common, select them. Both are universal
on the selected rectangle. If P,Q can fill two different positions
there, we are done. Otherwise both restrict to the same singleton. Each
also meets the third column, and their union on that column has at least
two positions. Retarget to the third column and the singleton's column:
P,Q fill the third column, and Z,W fill the other column.

The remaining case has Z and W missing distinct columns x,y. On the
rectangle x,y, they fill one position in each column, while P,Q fill the
other positions provided P and Q can be assigned different columns. If
this fails, both P,Q miss the same one of x,y, say y. They therefore
both meet x and the third column z. Retarget to x,z. The source W is
universal, while P,Q and Z match three positions: each has at least two
positions there, and their union has at least three. This completes the
reverse half; four A fill the forward half.

Replacing one A in this construction by a third opposite source gives
another useful eight-cube core: three A, two disjoint sources, and three
opposite sources with maximum variety multiplicity two always solve.
Indeed choose two distinct opposite varieties for the reverse-half
construction; the remaining opposite meets the selected forward half and
completes it with three A.

Consequently a failure with at least four A and two distinct opposite
varieties has at most one disjoint adjacent source. A failure with at
least three A and at least three diffuse opposite sources also has at
most one disjoint adjacent source.

We can now show that nine pieces suffice for the five-copy assertion:
five A, two distinct opposite varieties, and two arbitrary additional
sources.

Reserve a same-repeat A pair for the bicoloured positions of an A
target. If either of the two additional sources meets A, it and the two
opposite sources match three A corners. Each opposite sees at least two
corners, and their union has at least three, since a two-corner pattern
uniquely determines an opposite variety. Hall therefore gives the
three-corner matching; the three remaining A fill the other corners.

Otherwise both additional sources are adjacent and disjoint from A. The
stronger construction just proved solves using four A, those two sources
and the two opposite varieties.

We may therefore assume that the fourteen-pool contains just one
opposite variety O, with multiplicity t=1 or2.

\hypertarget{a-two-corner-hall-cover}{%
\subsection*{4. A two-corner Hall cover}\label{a-two-corner-hall-cover}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. A two-corner Hall cover}

Let z count adjacent sources disjoint from A. Construction I gives z≤2.
After a same-repeat A pair is reserved for its bicoloured corners, three
A remain. Therefore the nine outside sources cannot match three A
corners in a failure.

By König's theorem their incidence graph has a vertex cover of size at
most two. Two source vertices cannot cover it, because at least seven
outside sources meet A. A source vertex and a corner vertex cannot cover
it either. For t=2, one opposite copy remains uncovered and sees at
least two corners. For t=1, the exceptional source must be O; all eight
other outside sources are then either disjoint or in the unique adjacent
class meeting A in that single corner. Since z≤2, that class would
contain at least six copies.

Hence all outside intersections are contained in two A corners H. The
intersection O∩A is exactly H. It is a pair in different columns, so up
to symmetry there are only two forms: a row pair or a diagonal pair.

\hypertarget{row-pair-h1314}{%
\subsection*{5. Row pair: H=\{13,14\}}\label{row-pair-h1314}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. Row pair: H=\{13,14\}}

Here O has corners ±\{13,14,23,24\}. The allowed outside adjacent
classes and their counts are

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llrl@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & Symbol & Count & Intersection with A \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
C(234) & B & b & empty \\
C(124) & D & d & 13 \\
C(123) & E & e & 14 \\
C(12) & F & f & 13,14 \\
C(23) & Z23 & c & empty \\
C(24) & Z24 & g & empty \\
C(34) & Z34 & h & empty \\
\end{longtable}

Their total is 9−t. Each count is at most five, and the shared-four
bound gives d+f≤7 and e+f≤7. Write z=b+c+g+h.

The reverse-A position pools are as follows:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Position & Adjacent classes containing it & O contains it? \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
20 & B,D,E,F,Z23,Z24 & no \\
21 & B,Z23,Z24 & no \\
30 & B,E,Z23,Z34 & no \\
31 & B,Z23,Z34 & yes \\
40 & B,D,Z24,Z34 & no \\
41 & B,Z24,Z34 & yes \\
\end{longtable}

Four A can supply the forward positions of any rectangle target with
parts \{0,1\} and two of \{2,3,4\}.

\hypertarget{two-o-copies}{%
\subsubsection*{5.1. Two O copies}\label{two-o-copies}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{5.1. Two O copies}

Use the two O at31,41 in the rectangle on columns3,4. Positions30 and40
have at least two adjacent neighbours in their union, since their union
has size7−f≥2. Thus failure forces one position pool to be empty. By
interchanging3 and4, suppose b=e=c=h=0. Only D,F,Z24 remain, totalling7.

If g\textgreater0, use the rectangle on columns2,4:

\begin{verbatim}
four A forward; O at41; Z24 at21;
another D or Z24 at40; another D,F or Z24 at20.
\end{verbatim}

This is possible since d+g=7−f≥2. Therefore g=0 and d+f=7. Both d,f are
at least two, and one is at least four.

If d≥4, reserve a same-repeat D pair and fill a D target by

\begin{verbatim}
D:40,43; F:10,20; A:13; O:23.
\end{verbatim}

If f≥4, reserve a same-repeat F pair and fill an F target by

\begin{verbatim}
F:10,20; A:13,14; D:23; O:24.
\end{verbatim}

Both constructions are possible, a contradiction.

\hypertarget{one-o-copy}{%
\subsubsection*{5.2. One O copy}\label{one-o-copy}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{5.2. One O copy}

There are eight outside adjacent sources. Consider the rectangle on
columns3,4. Hall's theorem on its reverse positions30,31,40,41 says that
it solves unless at least one of the following holds:

\begin{verbatim}
b+e+c+h=0;  b+d+g+h=0;  z=0;  d+f=7;  e+f=7.
\end{verbatim}

For completeness: the first two alternatives are empty singleton pools;
the pair31,41 has only z+1 neighbours; the triples30,31,41 and31,40,41
have respectively9−d−f and9−e−f neighbours. All other Hall inequalities
follow from f≤5, d+f≤7 and e+f≤7.

If b+e+c+h=0, only D,F,Z24 remain. Now d+f≤7 forces g≥1, and d+g=8−f≥3,
so the columns2,4 construction above works. The second empty-pool
alternative is symmetric.

If z=0, only D,E,F remain, with total8. The shared-four bounds force
d,e≥1, and at least one is at least two. Assume d≥2. Assemble the
opposite target with parts \{0,3\} and \{2,4\}:

\begin{verbatim}
A:02,04; O:32; E:34; D:40,43;
two further D,E,F sources:20,23.
\end{verbatim}

Every source in the last pool fits20. A remaining D or F fits23: failure
of that assertion would mean d=2,f=0,e=6, contrary to the class bound.

Finally suppose d+f=7; the case e+f=7 is symmetric. There is one other
outside adjacent source. If it is B,Z23 orZ24, use it at21 in the
columns2,4 rectangle, with D at40, F at20 and O at41. If it is E, the
z=0 case just proved applies. If it is Z34, one of d,f is at least four,
and the corresponding same-repeat construction of Section5.1 works.

Thus the row-pair case is always soluble.

\hypertarget{diagonal-pair-h0214}{%
\subsection*{6. Diagonal pair: H=\{02,14\}}\label{diagonal-pair-h0214}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{6. Diagonal pair: H=\{02,14\}}

Here O is the opposite target with parts \{0,4\} and \{1,2\}. The
allowed outside adjacent classes are

\begin{verbatim}
B=C(234), D=C(123), E=C(034), Z23, Z24, Z34,
\end{verbatim}

with counts b,d,e,c,g,h, respectively. Their total is9−t, and again
z=b+c+g+h≤2. Every count is at most five.

In O itself, A supplies02,14. The remaining positions split into the
private triples

\begin{verbatim}
L={10,20,24}, supplied by D;
R={01,41,42}, supplied by E.
\end{verbatim}

The disjoint sources B,Z24 meet L at20 and R at41; Z23 meets L at20,24;
Z34 meets R at41,42. The permutation (0 1)(2 4) interchanges D and E,
Z23 and Z34, and preserves A and O.

\hypertarget{two-o-copies-1}{%
\subsubsection*{6.1. Two O copies}\label{two-o-copies-1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{6.1. Two O copies}

If z≤1 then d+e≥6. Since neither exceeds five, min(d,3)+min(e,3)≥4. Use
two A at02,14, four D/E sources in their private triples, and the two O
at the remaining two positions.

If z=2, try the rectangle on columns2,4, using four A forward and the
two O at20,41. The two disjoint sources can fill21,40 unless both are
Z23 or both are Z34. By symmetry take both to be Z34. If e\textgreater0,
assemble the rectangle on columns3,4 with

\begin{verbatim}
four A forward; Z34:30,40; E:31; O:41.
\end{verbatim}

If e=0, then d\textgreater0, and instead use Z34 at31,40, D at30 and O
at41.

\hypertarget{one-o-copy-1}{%
\subsubsection*{6.2. One O copy}\label{one-o-copy-1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{6.2. One O copy}

Now d+e≥6. If d,e≥2, then min(d,3)+min(e,3)≥5. Use two A at02,14, five
D/E in their private triples, and O at the remaining position.

Otherwise symmetry gives d=1,e=5,z=2. If a disjoint source is B,Z23
orZ24, use

\begin{verbatim}
A:02,14; E:01,41,42; D:10; disjoint source:20; O:24.
\end{verbatim}

If both disjoint sources are Z34, use the columns3,4 construction of
Section6.1 with Z34 at30,40, E at31 and O at41.

This closes the diagonal case and proves the theorem.

\hypertarget{a-thirteen-cube-corollary-of-the-constructions}{%
\subsection*{7. A thirteen-cube corollary of the
constructions}\label{a-thirteen-cube-corollary-of-the-constructions}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{7. A thirteen-cube corollary of the
constructions}

Sections 5 and 6 use at most four A copies in every assembly. Therefore
they also prove the following more local statement, without an initial
five-copy A class:

\begin{quote}
Take four A, t copies of a single opposite variety O (t=1 or2), and 9−t
adjacent sources, every one of whose A intersections is contained in the
two-corner set O∩A. Suppose adjacent class counts are at most five and
the shared-four bound holds. Then these thirteen cubes solve.
\end{quote}

Construction I still supplies the required bound z≤2 on sources disjoint
from A. This corollary is useful when a Hall cover has one exceptional
source, which can simply be discarded.

\hypertarget{what-remains}{%
\subsection*{8. What remains}\label{what-remains}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{8. What remains}

This theorem does not classify all diffuse fourteen-pools. It reduces a
putative mixed diffuse failure to adjacent class counts at most four.
The three constructions give additional necessary conditions: a class of
size four has disjoint-adjacent mass at most two; a class of size three
has disjoint mass at most two when there are at least two opposite
sources, and at most three when there is just one.

The separate computer verification of the diffuse eight-cube lemma, if
used, further limits the total number of opposite sources to1,2,3 or4.
That last restriction has not been proved by the hand arguments here.

\hypertarget{arg-41}{%
\section{Argument 41: Four adjacent copies and four diffuse opposite
sources}\label{arg-41}}

\textbf{Theorem.} A fourteen-pool containing at least four copies of one
adjacent tricolour class and at least four opposite-repeat sources, with
no opposite variety occurring more than twice, is soluble.

Indeed a stronger local assertion suffices: after the four opposite
sources fail to match four corners of that adjacent class, any two
additional adjacent sources complete a solution, unless one already
completes the adjacent target directly.

Use the notation of \protect\hyperlink{arg-40}{Argument 40}: \texttt{ab}
is a directed corner, C(S)=S→Sᶜ, and an opposite target is the
bidirected K₂,₂ between two disjoint pairs. Normalize the four-copy
class to A=C(01).

\hypertarget{reduction-to-seven-tiny-configurations}{%
\subsection*{1. Reduction to seven tiny
configurations}\label{reduction-to-seven-tiny-configurations}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Reduction to seven tiny
configurations}

Reserve a same-repeat pair from the four A copies for an A target's
bicoloured positions. If the four opposite sources match four of its six
tricoloured corners, the remaining two A complete the target.

Every opposite source meets A in two corners from different columns, or
in a four-corner rectangle. A two-corner intersection determines its
opposite variety uniquely. Hall's theorem consequently shows that the
four opposite sources fail to match four A corners only if all four
intersections lie in a common three-corner set H. Indeed a deficient
triple of sources would have the same two-corner intersection, giving
three copies of one opposite variety, which is excluded.

The four sources have matching rank three on H. Thus any additional
source having an A corner outside H completes the adjacent target. We
may assume that every additional source meets A only in H. This also
shows that the pool has exactly four copies of A. At most three opposite
varieties have their A intersections in H, so at most six opposite
sources occur in total. There are therefore at least four other adjacent
sources; we may select any two as helpers.

In A's two-by-three corner array, H has one of three shapes:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Three corners of one row: normalize H=\{02,03,04\}.
\item
  Two corners of one column and one of another: H=\{02,03,12\}.
\item
  One corner in each column, split between the rows: H=\{02,03,14\}.
\end{enumerate}

The eligible opposite varieties correspond to pairs of H in different
columns. In the first and third shapes these are the three edges of a
triangle; in the second shape they form a two-edge path. Four sources
with each variety used at most twice give respectively two, one and four
possibilities up to symmetry. We handle these seven cases below.

\hypertarget{two-matching-observations}{%
\subsection*{2. Two matching
observations}\label{two-matching-observations}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Two matching observations}

Suppose an opposite target T has exactly two A corners X. Put
Y=T\textbackslash X, so \textbar Y\textbar=6, and let P′,Q′ be the
intersections with Y of two helper sources.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  If there are two copies of T and two copies of another opposite U,
  with C=(U∩T)\textbackslash X of size three, then the target solves
  whenever P′,Q′ are nonempty, \textbar P′∪Q′\textbar≥2 and P′∪Q′ is not
  contained in C. Two A fill X; the two U and the helpers match four Y
  positions; the two T fill the rest. Hall for these four sources has
  only two possible failures: the helpers jointly see one position, or
  all four sources jointly see only the three positions C.
\item
  If there are two T and one each of U,V, and their residual
  intersections C\_U,C\_V partition Y into triples, the same assignment
  works whenever P′,Q′ are nonempty and \textbar P′∪Q′\textbar≥2. All
  other Hall inequalities follow from the two disjoint triples.
\end{itemize}

All helpers in the following tables are adjacent sources. The tables are
obtained simply by intersecting directed cuts with the named target and
deleting its two A corners.

\hypertarget{three-corners-in-one-row}{%
\subsection*{3. Three corners in one
row}\label{three-corners-in-one-row}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Three corners in one row}

Set

\begin{verbatim}
R = opposite 02∣34, omitting1;
S = opposite 03∣24, omitting1;
T = opposite 04∣23, omitting1.
\end{verbatim}

These are precisely the eligible opposite varieties. By symmetry the
four opposite sources are either 2S+2T or R+S+2T.

The helper classes and their residual intersections are:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Helper & S minus its A corners02,04 & T minus its A corners02,03 \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
C(234) & 20,40 & 20,30 \\
C(02) & 23 & 24 \\
C(03) & 32,34 & 34 \\
C(04) & 43 & 42,43 \\
C(034) & 32 & 42 \\
C(024) & 23,43 & 43 \\
C(023) & 34 & 24,34 \\
C(23) & 20,34 & 20,24,30,34 \\
C(24) & 20,23,40,43 & 20,43 \\
C(34) & 32,40 & 30,42 \\
\end{longtable}

\hypertarget{two-s-and-two-t}{%
\subsubsection*{3.1. Two S and two T}\label{two-s-and-two-t}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{3.1. Two S and two T}

In either target S or T, the other opposite variety's residual triple is
\{20,34,43\}. The first matching observation and the table show:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  S can fail only when both helpers belong to \{C(04),C(023),C(23)\}, or
  they are two C(02), or two C(034).
\item
  T can fail only when both helpers belong to \{C(03),C(024),C(24)\}, or
  they are two C(02), or two C(034).
\end{itemize}

The two displayed three-class families are disjoint. Only the two
doubled-helper cases remain. They both assemble R:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Helpers & A & Helpers' positions & S positions & T positions \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
two C(02) & 03,04 & 23,24 & 32,40 & 30,42 \\
two C(034) & 03,04 & 32,42 & 23,40 & 24,30 \\
\end{longtable}

\hypertarget{one-r-one-s-two-t}{%
\subsubsection*{3.2. One R, one S, two T}\label{one-r-one-s-two-t}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{3.2. One R, one S, two T}

In T, the residual intersections of R and S are the disjoint triples
\{24,30,42\} and \{20,34,43\}. By the second matching observation the
only exceptions are two helpers from the same singleton row of the T
column: two C(02), C(03), C(034), or C(024).

The symmetry interchanging2 and3 exchanges the first two and the last
two cases. For two C(02), assemble R using A at03,04, the helpers
at23,24, R at40, S at32, and T at30,42. For two C(034), use A at03,04,
the helpers at32,42, R at40, S at23, and T at24,30.

This closes the row shape.

\hypertarget{two-corners-in-a-column}{%
\subsection*{4. Two corners in a column}\label{two-corners-in-a-column}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Two corners in a column}

Here H=\{02,03,12\}. The opposite sources are

\begin{verbatim}
two T = opposite04∣23, omitting1;
two U = opposite02∣13, omitting4.
\end{verbatim}

The helper classes are C(234), C(134), C(04), C(034), C(024), C(23),
C(24), C(34).

If either helper is C(234), C(034), C(23) or C(34), it contains31.
Assemble the opposite target01∣23 using four A for02,03,12,13, that
helper for31, T for20, U for21, and another T or U for30.

Thus both helpers belong to \{C(134),C(04),C(024),C(24)\}.

If one is C(134), assemble the target02∣34. Use two A at03,04 and that
helper at40. The other helper has at least one corner in
\{23,24,30,32,42\}. The two T meet this five-set in \{24,30,42\}, and
the two U meet it in \{23,30,32\}. After assigning the other helper
anywhere in the five-set, the two triples, with common point30, match
its four remaining positions using two T and two U.

If neither helper is C(134), both belong to \{C(04),C(024),C(24)\}. All
three classes contain41,43. Assemble the target04∣13 by

\begin{verbatim}
A:03,14; helpers:41,43; T:30,34; U:01,10.
\end{verbatim}

This closes the column shape.

\hypertarget{three-columns-split-between-the-rows}{%
\subsection*{5. Three columns, split between the
rows}\label{three-columns-split-between-the-rows}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. Three columns, split between the
rows}

Here H=\{02,03,14\}. Put

\begin{verbatim}
T = opposite04∣23, omitting1;
U = opposite04∣13, omitting2;
V = opposite04∣12, omitting3.
\end{verbatim}

These are the eligible opposite varieties. The eligible helpers have the
following residual intersections:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Helper & T minus02,03 & U minus03,14 & V minus02,14 \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
C(234) & 20,30 & 30,41 & 20,41 \\
C(04) & 42,43 & 01,41,43 & 01,41,42 \\
C(123) & 20,24,30,34 & 10,30,34 & 10,20,24 \\
C(034) & 42 & 01,41 & 01,41,42 \\
C(024) & 43 & 01,41,43 & 01,41 \\
C(23) & 20,24,30,34 & 30,34 & 20,24 \\
C(24) & 20,43 & 41,43 & 20,41 \\
C(34) & 30,42 & 30,41 & 41,42 \\
\end{longtable}

Interchanging2 and3 exchanges U and V. There are four opposite-source
multisets up to this symmetry.

\hypertarget{two-u-and-two-v}{%
\subsubsection*{5.1. Two U and two V}\label{two-u-and-two-v}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{5.1. Two U and two V}

For either target the other variety's residual triple is \{01,10,41\}.
By the first matching observation, the U construction can fail only if
both helpers are C(034), and the V construction can fail only if both
are C(024). They cannot both fail.

\hypertarget{two-t-and-two-v}{%
\subsubsection*{5.2. Two T and two V}\label{two-t-and-two-v}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{5.2. Two T and two V}

The common residual triple is \{20,24,42\}. The T construction can fail
only for two C(034) or two C(024); the V construction can fail only for
two C(23). Again one construction works.

\hypertarget{one-t-one-u-two-v}{%
\subsubsection*{5.3. One T, one U, two V}\label{one-t-one-u-two-v}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{5.3. One T, one U, two V}

In V, the residual intersections of T and U are the disjoint triples
\{20,24,42\} and \{01,10,41\}. Every helper sees at least two positions
of V outside A, so the second matching observation always applies.

\hypertarget{two-t-one-u-one-v}{%
\subsubsection*{5.4. Two T, one U, one V}\label{two-t-one-u-one-v}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{5.4. Two T, one U, one V}

In T, the residual intersections of U and V are the disjoint triples
\{30,34,43\} and \{20,24,42\}. The second observation works unless the
helpers are both C(034) or both C(024). By interchanging2 and3 it is
enough to treat two C(034). Assemble V using

\begin{verbatim}
A:02,14; helpers:01,41; T:20,24; U:10; V:42.
\end{verbatim}

This closes the last shape and proves the theorem.

\hypertarget{arg-42}{%
\section{Argument 42: Four adjacent copies and three diffuse opposite
sources}\label{arg-42}}

\textbf{Theorem.} A fourteen-pool containing at least four copies of an
adjacent tricolour class and exactly three opposite sources, with no
opposite variety occurring three times, is soluble.

Use the directed-corner notation of \protect\hyperlink{arg-40}{Argument
40}. The nine-piece lemma there handles any adjacent class of size at
least five, since the three opposite sources include two distinct
varieties. We may therefore suppose that every adjacent class has at
most four copies. Normalize the distinguished four-copy class to
A=C(01).

We will use the following already proved eight-cube construction:

\begin{verbatim}
3B + two adjacent sources disjoint from B + three diffuse O solve.
\end{verbatim}

Thus any class of size at least three in a putative failure has at most
one adjacent source disjoint from it.

\hypertarget{reduction-to-three-corner-covers}{%
\subsection*{1. Reduction to three-corner
covers}\label{reduction-to-three-corner-covers}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Reduction to three-corner covers}

Reserve a same-repeat A pair for its bicoloured positions, leaving two
A. The three opposite sources have matching rank three on A: each sees
at least two corners, and three sources with only two corners between
them would be three copies of one opposite variety.

Suppose their union on A has at least four corners. A nonempty adjacent
source fails to extend them to a four-matching only if two of the
opposite sources are identical, have a two-corner A intersection P, and
the adjacent source's intersection is contained in P. This follows
directly from Hall: a deficient triple is the only possible obstruction,
since the four-source union already has size at least four.

There are seven other adjacent sources, and at most one is disjoint from
A. Consequently either an adjacent target solves at once, or all seven
adjacent sources have their A intersections in the same pair P. The
thirteen-cube corollary in the five-class note then solves using four A,
the two identical opposite sources and those seven adjacent sources; the
third opposite is unnecessary.

It remains that the three opposite sources' union on A is a set H of
size three. Any further source meeting A outside H extends their
three-matching and solves A. We may therefore assume all seven other
adjacent sources meet A only in H.

Up to the stabilizer of A there are three shapes for H, treated below.

\hypertarget{a-row-triple-h020304}{%
\subsection*{2. A row triple:
H=\{02,03,04\}}\label{a-row-triple-h020304}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. A row triple: H=\{02,03,04\}}

The eligible opposite varieties are

\begin{verbatim}
opposite02∣34, opposite03∣24, opposite04∣23,
\end{verbatim}

all omitting colour1. Two distinct such varieties can match q0,r0 for
any two different columns q,r in \{2,3,4\}: their sets of available
columns are different two-element subsets of a three-element set.

Every eligible adjacent helper has at least one reverse-A position q1.
It has only one such position exactly when its class is C(0q); all other
eligible classes have at least two. Hence two helpers match q1,r1 for
some distinct columns unless both are the same class C(0q).

The seven helpers cannot all belong to one class, since class counts are
at most four. Choose two with a matching to q1,r1, and choose two
distinct opposite sources at q0,r0. Four A fill0q,0r,1q,1r, completing
the rectangle target with parts \{0,1\} and \{q,r\}.

\hypertarget{a-column-pair-h020312}{%
\subsection*{3. A column pair:
H=\{02,03,12\}}\label{a-column-pair-h020312}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. A column pair: H=\{02,03,12\}}

The opposite sources use both varieties

\begin{verbatim}
T = opposite04∣23, omitting1;
U = opposite02∣13, omitting4,
\end{verbatim}

one twice and the other once. The three sources match20,21,30: use T
at20, U at21 and the repeated variety at30. Thus any helper at31
completes the rectangle01∣23 with four A.

The eligible helpers containing31 are C(234), C(034), C(23), C(34). If
none occurs, the remaining helper classes are

\begin{verbatim}
D=C(134), R=C(04), S=C(024), Z=C(24).
\end{verbatim}

If D occurs with any of R,S,Z, assemble the rectangle01∣24 using

\begin{verbatim}
four A forward; T:20; U:21; D:40; R,S orZ:41.
\end{verbatim}

Seven helpers cannot all be D, so D is absent in a failure. Likewise, if
Z occurs, some R or S occurs, and the same construction uses Z at40 and
that other source at41. Thus Z is absent too.

The seven helpers are therefore R and S. One class has four copies, and
the other has three. If R has four, reserve a same-repeat R pair. The
remaining two R fill two of its six tricoloured positions; the three
opposite sources and one A match four more. Explicitly:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Opposite counts & A & T copies & U copies \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
two T, one U & 02 & 42,43 & 01 \\
one T, two U & 02 & 42 & 01,03 \\
\end{longtable}

If S has four, reserve a same-repeat S pair and use one R with the three
opposite sources for four tricoloured positions:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Opposite counts & R & T copies & U copies \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
two T, one U & 01 & 03,43 & 21 \\
one T, two U & 01 & 43 & 21,23 \\
\end{longtable}

The two remaining S fill the other two positions. This closes the
column-pair shape.

\hypertarget{three-columns-split-between-the-rows-h020314}{%
\subsection*{4. Three columns, split between the rows:
H=\{02,03,14\}}\label{three-columns-split-between-the-rows-h020314}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Three columns, split between the
rows: H=\{02,03,14\}}

Write

\begin{verbatim}
T = opposite04∣23, omitting1;
U = opposite04∣13, omitting2;
V = opposite04∣12, omitting3.
\end{verbatim}

These are the eligible opposite varieties. Their reverse-A intersections
are respectively \{20,30\}, \{30,41\}, \{20,41\}. Thus any two distinct
opposite sources match any prescribed pair of positions from
P=\{20,30,41\}.

Let K=\{21,31,40\}. If two helpers match two K positions, select the
corresponding rectangle:21,31 uses columns2,3;21,40 uses2,4;31,40
uses3,4. Two distinct opposite sources fill its other two reverse
positions from P, and four A fill the forward half.

We may therefore suppose the helpers have matching rank at most one on
K. Their K intersections are:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Helper class & K intersection \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
R=C(04) & empty \\
D=C(123) & empty \\
E=C(034) & 31 \\
F=C(024) & 21 \\
C(234), C(23), C(24), C(34) & at least two positions \\
\end{longtable}

If one of the last four classes occurs, all six other helpers must be R
or D; any other nonempty K intersection would give a two-matching. But R
and D are reverse cuts and hence disjoint. Two counts totalling six,
each at most four, have one at least three and the other at least two,
contradicting the disjoint-source bound of the introduction.

Thus none of the last four classes occurs. Also E and F cannot both
occur. Interchanging2 and3 if necessary, assume F is absent. Write r,d,e
for the counts of R,D,E, with r+d+e=7.

Every eligible opposite source meets each of R and D in four corners.
Since A meets each of them, three opposite sources and one A match four
corners of either class. Four copies of R or D would therefore solve
after reserving a same-repeat pair. Hence r,d≤3.

Suppose e=4. Since D and E are disjoint, the disjoint-source bound gives
d≤1, so r≥2. In E the opposite intersections lie in

\begin{verbatim}
M={01,02,41,42}:
T sees02,42; U sees01,41; V sees all four.
\end{verbatim}

The three opposite sources match three M positions, since no variety
occurs three times. An R source sees all M and fills the fourth. After
reserving a same-repeat E pair, the two other E fill31,32. Thus e=4 also
solves, and e≤3.

The class D is disjoint from both R and E. If d=3, its disjoint mass
would be r+e=4. If d=2, then r+e=5 forces r or e to be three, and that
class has two disjoint D sources. Both violate the disjoint-source
bound. Therefore d≤1; since r+d+e=7 and r,e≤3, necessarily

\begin{verbatim}
r=3, d=1, e=3.
\end{verbatim}

This last form assembles V directly. Four of the six R/E sources fill
their common positions M. An A fills14. Of the three remaining target
positions \{10,20,24\}, two can be filled by opposite sources: T
sees20,24, U sees10, and V sees all three; failure to find a
two-matching would require three U copies. The D source fills the last
position.

All three cover shapes are soluble. This proves the theorem.

\hypertarget{consequence-2}{%
\subsection*{Consequence}\label{consequence-2}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Consequence}

Combining this theorem with \protect\hyperlink{arg-41}{Argument 41}
shows, entirely elementarily, that a mixed fourteen-cube failure with at
least three opposite sources, each variety used at most twice, has every
adjacent class count at most three. The one- and two-opposite four-class
branches are treated in their respective companion notes.

\hypertarget{arg-43}{%
\section{Argument 43: Four adjacent copies and two distinct opposite
sources}\label{arg-43}}

\textbf{Theorem.} A fourteen-pool containing four copies of an adjacent
tricolour class and exactly two opposite sources of different varieties
is soluble.

The earlier nine-piece construction excludes any adjacent class of size
at least five. Suppose therefore that all adjacent classes have size at
most four, and normalize the distinguished class to A=C(01). The
notation and elementary constructions are those of the other notes in
this directory.

\hypertarget{the-hall-cover-reduction}{%
\subsection*{1. The Hall-cover
reduction}\label{the-hall-cover-reduction}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. The Hall-cover reduction}

Four A contain a same-repeat pair for the bicoloured positions of an A
target. The other two A fill two arbitrary tricoloured positions, so the
ten outside sources must have matching rank at most three on A in a
failure.

The eight-cube construction 4A+two disjoint adjacent sources+two
distinct opposite sources shows that at most one outside adjacent source
is disjoint from A.

A vertex cover of the outside/A incidence graph therefore cannot have at
most one corner vertex. With no corner vertices it would leave at least
seven disjoint sources. With one corner vertex, both opposite sources
must be exceptional source vertices, leaving at least seven adjacent
copies in the unique class meeting A in that single corner.

If the cover consists of two corners and one source vertex, the
exceptional source must be opposite: two different opposite varieties
cannot both have their A intersections in a two-corner set. The other
opposite source and all eight adjacent sources are then contained in
that pair. The thirteen-cube corollary of the five-class note solves
using four A, that one opposite source and the eight adjacent sources.

It remains that all outside incidences lie in three A corners H. The two
different opposite sources occupy different two-corner subsets of H, so
their union is H. Again H has three possible shapes.

\hypertarget{row-triple-h020304}{%
\subsection*{2. Row triple: H=\{02,03,04\}}\label{row-triple-h020304}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Row triple: H=\{02,03,04\}}

The opposite sources omit colour1. Their reverse-A row0 intersections
are different two-element subsets of \{20,30,40\}, and hence they match
q0,r0 for any two distinct columns q,r.

Every eligible adjacent helper has at least one position q1. It has
exactly one precisely for the three classes C(02),C(03),C(04). Two
helpers therefore match q1,r1 unless both are the same one of these
classes. The eight helpers cannot all be one class, since its count
would exceed four. Choose a suitable pair of helpers and assemble the
rectangle01\textbar qr with four A forward and the opposite sources and
helpers on its reverse half.

\hypertarget{column-pair-h020312}{%
\subsection*{3. Column pair: H=\{02,03,12\}}\label{column-pair-h020312}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Column pair: H=\{02,03,12\}}

The opposite sources are

\begin{verbatim}
T=opposite04∣23, omitting1;
U=opposite02∣13, omitting4.
\end{verbatim}

They can fill any prescribed two of \{20,21,30\}. Thus the rectangle
01∣23 solves if two helpers can fill31 and a position in \{20,21,30\}.

The eligible helper classes, restricted to these four reverse positions,
are

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Helper & Available reverse positions \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
C(234) & 20,21,30,31 \\
C(134) & 30 \\
C(04) & empty \\
C(034) & 31 \\
C(024) & 21 \\
C(23) & 20,21,30,31 \\
C(24) & 20,21 \\
C(34) & 30,31 \\
\end{longtable}

Suppose some helper contains31, but the required helper pair cannot be
chosen. Hall on the two categories \{31\} and \{20,21,30\} says either
no helper meets the second category, or only one helper meets their
union. The first alternative puts all eight helpers in C(04),C(034),
which share four corners and thus have combined count at most seven. The
second leaves at least seven copies of C(04), contradicting its class
bound. Hence in a failure no helper contains31.

The helpers are therefore among

\begin{verbatim}
D=C(134), R=C(04), S=C(024), Z=C(24).
\end{verbatim}

Now use the rectangle01∣24: T fills20 and U fills21. D sees40, R and S
see41, and Z sees both. If both positions40,41 occur among the helpers,
two different helpers can fill them, since every helper sees at least
one and there are eight helpers. Otherwise all helpers are D, violating
the class bound, or all are R,S, violating their shared-four bound
seven.

This closes the column shape.

\hypertarget{split-triple-h020314}{%
\subsection*{4. Split triple:
H=\{02,03,14\}}\label{split-triple-h020314}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Split triple: H=\{02,03,14\}}

The eligible opposite varieties are

\begin{verbatim}
T=opposite04∣23, omitting1;
U=opposite04∣13, omitting2;
V=opposite04∣12, omitting3.
\end{verbatim}

Any two distinct varieties match any prescribed pair from
P=\{20,30,41\}. As in the three-opposite proof, if two helpers match a
pair from K=\{21,31,40\}, those two helper positions and the
corresponding two P positions form the reverse half of an A rectangle,
which solves.

The K intersections are:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Helper & K intersection \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
R=C(04) & empty \\
D=C(123) & empty \\
E=C(034) & 31 \\
F=C(024) & 21 \\
C(234),C(23),C(24),C(34) & at least two \\
\end{longtable}

If a helper from the last four classes occurs and the helpers have
K-rank at most one, all seven other helpers must be R or D. Their counts
are three and four. They are reverse cuts, so four of one, three of the
other and one opposite source solve by the elementary
4A+three-disjoint+O construction.

Thus no helper from the last four classes occurs. Nor can E and F both
occur. Interchanging2 and3 if necessary, take F absent, and write r,d,e
for the counts of R,D,E. Then r+d+e=8. Since R,E share four corners,
r+e≤7 and therefore d≥1. Since d≤4, we also have r+e≥4.

Assemble V. Four R/E sources fill their common positions
\{01,02,41,42\}. One A fills14. Of the remaining positions \{10,20,24\},
the two opposite sources fill two different ones: T sees20,24, U sees10,
and V sees all three, so any two different varieties have a
two-matching. One D fills the last position.

This proves the theorem.

\hypertarget{consequence-3}{%
\subsection*{Consequence}\label{consequence-3}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Consequence}

Together with the preceding notes, every mixed fourteen-pool with an
adjacent class of size at least four is soluble whenever at least two
different opposite varieties occur. The homogeneous two-opposite and
one-opposite cases are proved in their respective companion notes.

\hypertarget{arg-44}{%
\section{Argument 44: Four adjacent copies and two identical opposite
sources}\label{arg-44}}

\textbf{Theorem.} A fourteen-pool containing four copies of an adjacent
tricolour class and exactly two opposite sources, both of the same
variety, is soluble.

We use the notation and proved constructions of the five-class note. Its
theorem already excludes adjacent class counts at least five, so all
such counts may be assumed at most four. Normalize the chosen four-copy
class to A=C(01), and write O for the opposite variety.

The following bounds hold in a failure:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  At most two adjacent sources are disjoint from A, by
  4A+three-disjoint+O.
\item
  A class of size at least three has disjoint-adjacent mass at most two,
  by 3B+three-disjoint+two-O.
\item
  Two adjacent classes sharing four corners have combined count≤7.
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{hall-reduction}{%
\subsection*{1. Hall reduction}\label{hall-reduction}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Hall reduction}

Reserve a same-repeat A pair, leaving two A for tricoloured positions.
The ten outside sources must have matching rank at most three on A. A
vertex cover with no corner vertices would leave at least seven disjoint
sources. A cover with one corner and two source vertices must use both
source vertices for the opposite copies, leaving at least six adjacent
sources in one singleton-intersection class. Both are impossible.

For a cover with two corners and one exceptional source, use the
thirteen-cube corollary in the five-class note: if an adjacent source is
exceptional, retain the two O and seven covered adjacent sources; if an
O is exceptional, retain the other O and all eight adjacent sources.
Therefore all remaining cases have a three-corner cover H.

In particular O∩A has size two. If O met A in four corners, neither of
the possible covers could contain even one of its copies, and the pool
would already solve. The two corners form either a row pair or a
diagonal pair in A's two-by-three array.

\hypertarget{row-pair-with-h-itself-a-row-h020304}{%
\subsection*{2. Row pair, with H itself a row:
H=\{02,03,04\}}\label{row-pair-with-h-itself-a-row-h020304}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Row pair, with H itself a row:
H=\{02,03,04\}}

Normalize O to opposite04∣23, omitting1, so O∩A=\{02,03\}. Its two
copies fill20,30 in the reverse half of rectangle01∣23. Thus helpers
matching21,31 complete the rectangle with four A.

If no such helper pair exists, one of21,31 is absent: otherwise only one
helper could meet either position, leaving at least seven helpers in
C(04), the unique eligible class missing both. Interchanging2 and3,
suppose21 is absent. The eight helpers then belong to

\begin{verbatim}
X=C(03), Y=C(04), Z=C(034), W=C(34).
\end{verbatim}

If W occurs, use one W at40 in rectangle01∣34 and one O at30. The seven
remaining helpers match31,41: each sees at least one, and neither
position can be absent without forcing seven copies of X or Y. Thus W is
absent.

Let x,y,z be the remaining class counts, with x+y+z=8. Four X solve:
reserve a same-repeat pair, use two X at31,32, A at04, O at34, and two
Y/Z sources at01,02. Four Y solve using two remaining Y at01,41, two A
at02,03 and the two O at42,43. Thus x,y≤3.

If z=4, then x+y=4 and both x,y are positive. Reserve a same-repeat Z
pair; two Z fill01,32, while X fills31, Y fills41, O fills42 and A
fills02. Hence z≤3. The counts are therefore a permutation of3,3,2.

\hypertarget{repeated-colour-contradiction}{%
\subsubsection*{Repeated-colour
contradiction}\label{repeated-colour-contradiction}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Repeated-colour contradiction}

Each class has a loop: two copies with the same repeated colour solve.
Here are the tricoloured assignments after reserving the indicated pair:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Target and reserved pair & Six tricoloured positions \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
X, two X & Z:31,32; A:04; O:34; Y:01,02 \\
Y, two Y & Z:01,41; A:02,03; O:42,43 \\
Z, two Z & X:31,32; Y:41,42; A:02; one further X/Y:01 \\
\end{longtable}

The last extra source exists because x+y≥5.

The three classes also conflict pairwise whenever they use the same
repeated colour:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{X,Y:} reserve one of each for an X target's bicoloured
  corners. Use Z at31,32, A at04, O at34, and two remaining X/Y/Z
  at01,02.
\item
  \textbf{X,Z:} their only possible common repeat is4. They supply an A
  target's poles0,1. Four A fill12,13,14,02; a remaining X fills04 and a
  Y fills03.
\item
  \textbf{Y,Z:} their only possible common repeat is3. They again supply
  an A target's poles0,1. Four A fill12,13,14,04; a remaining Y fills03
  and an X fills02.
\end{itemize}

Consequently all eight X/Y/Z copies would have different repeated
colours, impossible with five colours. This closes the row cover.

\hypertarget{row-pair-with-h020312}{%
\subsection*{3. Row pair, with
H=\{02,03,12\}}\label{row-pair-with-h020312}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Row pair, with H=\{02,03,12\}}

Again O is opposite04∣23. The helper classes are

\begin{verbatim}
B=C(234), D=C(134), R=C(04), E=C(034), F=C(024),
Z23=C(23), Z24=C(24), Z34=C(34).
\end{verbatim}

Only D has an A corner outside O∩A. If d≤1, discard that source and
apply the thirteen-cube corollary with the other seven helpers. Thus
d≥2. Also R cannot occur four times: after reserving an R pair, use the
other two R at01,41, two A at02,03 and the two O at42,43. Hence r≤3.

Try rectangle01∣34, using O at30 and three helpers at31,40,41. The
helper pools are

\begin{verbatim}
31: B,E,Z23,Z34;
40: B,D,Z24,Z34;
41: B,R,E,F,Z24,Z34.
\end{verbatim}

All pair and triple Hall bounds hold: the first two pools have union
8−r−f≥2 since d≥2; the first and third have union8−d≥4; the last two
have union8−z23≥6. The40 pool is nonempty, and the41 pool has at
least8−d−z23≥2 sources. Thus failure forces31 to be absent.

Only D,R,F,Z24 remain. In rectangle01∣24, use O at20 and ask the helpers
to fill21,40,41. The pools are F+Z24, D+Z24 and R+F+Z24. Their pair
unions have sizes at least5,4 and8 respectively. The last two pools are
nonempty; the first is nonempty as well, since otherwise d+r=8
contradicts d≤4,r≤3. Hall gives the required three-matching.

\hypertarget{row-pair-with-h020314}{%
\subsection*{4. Row pair, with
H=\{02,03,14\}}\label{row-pair-with-h020314}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Row pair, with H=\{02,03,14\}}

Write

\begin{verbatim}
R=C(04), D=C(123), E=C(034), F=C(024),
\end{verbatim}

and let z count the other four, disjoint-from-A classes. Only D meets A
outside O∩A, so d≥2 by the thirteen-cube corollary. As above r≤3.

D is disjoint from R,E,F. If d≥3, the disjoint-mass bound gives r+e+f≤2.
Since d+r+e+f+z=8 and z≤2, this forces d=4,z=2. But every
disjoint-from-A source meets D in a corner of O. A meets D at14, outside
O. Therefore two O, one such source and one A match four D corners;
after reserving a D pair, the two remaining D complete the target. Hence
d=2 and r+e+f=6−z≥4.

If z\textgreater0 and a disjoint source sees40, use it there. Another
helper sees21 or31: either another disjoint source exists, or
e+f\textgreater0, since z=1,e=f=0 would give r=5. A further R/E/F source
fills41. Together with O at20 or30 these helpers complete rectangle01∣24
or01∣34.

If no disjoint source sees40, all z sources are C(23). They can fill
21,31 with each other or with E/F; again e+f\textgreater0 when z=1. The
two O fill20,30 and rectangle01∣23 solves. Thus z=0.

If E and F both occur, they fill31,21 with the two O at20,30, solving
the same rectangle. Otherwise interchange2 and3 so F is absent. Now
r+e=6. Assemble opposite04∣12: four R/E sources fill their common
positions01,02,41,42; A fills14; D fills10; the two O fill20,24.

Sections 2--4 prove the theorem whenever O has a row-pair intersection
with the chosen four-copy class. We may use this conclusion for any
other four-copy class as well.

\hypertarget{diagonal-pair-with-h020312}{%
\subsection*{5. Diagonal pair, with
H=\{02,03,12\}}\label{diagonal-pair-with-h020312}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. Diagonal pair, with H=\{02,03,12\}}

Now O is opposite02∣13, omitting4. The helper classes are the same as in
Section 3. Every class among D=C(134), R=C(04), F=C(024) has either four
O corners or a row-pair O intersection. By Section 1 or Sections 2--4,
its count is at most three.

Also E=C(034) has count at most three. If it had four, reserve a
same-repeat pair. O fills01,32 and A fills02. Every other eligible
helper class has a corner in \{31,41,42\}; use one there and the two
remaining E at the other two positions. There is such a helper since the
total helper count is eight.

Let d,r,e,f≤3 be these counts and z≤2 the disjoint-from-A mass. Try
rectangle01∣34, with O at30 and helpers at31,40,41. As in Section 3,
their pair unions have sizes8−r−f≥2,8−d≥5,8−z23≥6. The41 pool cannot be
empty. Thus failure forces either31 or40 to be absent.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  If31 is absent, the only helpers are D,R,F,Z24. If Z24 occurs,
  rectangle01∣24 solves with O at21 and helpers at20,40,41: use Z24
  at20, and the remaining helpers match40,41. Indeed d+z24≥2, since
  d=0,z24=1 would force r+f=7\textgreater6. If Z24 is absent, then
  d+r+f=8, so each count is at least two. Assemble O itself using
  A:03,12; D:10,32; F:21,23; R:01; O:30.
\item
  If40 is absent, the only helpers are R,E,F,Z23. If Z23 occurs,
  rectangle01∣23 solves with the two O at21,30 and helpers at20,31: Z23
  supplies20, and another Z23 or E supplies31. Such another source
  exists, since e=0,z23=1 would force r+f=7\textgreater6. If Z23 is
  absent, then r+e+f=8, with each count at least two. Assemble O using
  A:03,12; O:10,30; F:21,23; E:32; another helper:01.
\end{itemize}

This closes the first diagonal shape.

\hypertarget{diagonal-pair-with-h020314}{%
\subsection*{6. Diagonal pair, with
H=\{02,03,14\}}\label{diagonal-pair-with-h020314}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{6. Diagonal pair, with H=\{02,03,14\}}

Now O is opposite04∣12, omitting3. Let

\begin{verbatim}
R=C(04), D=C(123), E=C(034), F=C(024).
\end{verbatim}

Each has either four O corners or a row-pair intersection, so all four
counts are at most three by the established cases.

Try O itself. Two A fill02,14. For its six other positions

\begin{verbatim}
Y={01,10,20,24,41,42},
\end{verbatim}

the helper intersections are

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Helper & Intersection with Y \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
R,E & 01,41,42 \\
D & 10,20,24 \\
F & 01,41 \\
C(234),C(24) & 20,41 \\
C(23) & 20,24 \\
C(34) & 41,42 \\
\end{longtable}

If the helpers match four Y positions, the two O fill the rest. Suppose
their rank is at most three. Every helper has at least two Y positions.
A cover with two Y vertices and one source vertex would put at least
seven helpers into one identical two-position pattern; the table and the
bounds f≤3,z≤2 make that impossible. Therefore all helper intersections
lie in three Y positions.

Unless those positions are \{01,41,42\}, the table permits at most five
helpers: at most three from D or F and at most two disjoint-from-A
sources. Consequently all helpers belong to R,E,F,Z34=C(34).

Write z for the Z34 count. We have r+e+f+z=8, with r,e,f≤3,z≤2. If
f\textgreater0, assemble opposite12∣34, omitting0. Use A at13,14, F
at23, O at24, and fill31,32,41,42 from R,E,Z34. This four-matching
exists: E and Z34 see all four positions, R sees41,42, and

\begin{verbatim}
e+z≥8−r−f≥2,     r+e+z≥5.
\end{verbatim}

If f=0, necessarily r=e=3,z=2. Assemble rectangle01∣34 using four A
forward, O at41, E at31, and the two Z34 at30,40.

All cases are soluble, proving the theorem.

\hypertarget{consequence-and-remaining-gap}{%
\subsection*{Consequence and remaining
gap}\label{consequence-and-remaining-gap}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Consequence and remaining gap}

All mixed four-class branches with at least two opposite sources are
elementary-closed, whether the opposite varieties are distinct or
identical. The companion one-opposite proof extends the conclusion to
every mixed diffuse fourteen-cube failure: all adjacent class counts are
at most three.

\hypertarget{arg-45}{%
\section{Argument 45: One opposite source: the row-triple Hall
cover}\label{arg-45}}

We use directed corners \texttt{ab}, adjacent cuts C(S)=S→Sᶜ, and
opposite varieties given by bidirected complete bipartite graphs on two
pairs. Suppose a putative fourteen-cube failure contains four A=C(01),
one O=opposite04\textbar23, and nine adjacent helpers. Suppose every
helper meets A only in H=\{02,03,04\}. Adjacent class counts are at most
four, and classes sharing four tricoloured corners have combined count
at most seven. Both bounds have already been proved for a failure.

Write Q=\{2,3,4\}. The eligible helper classes are

\begin{verbatim}
Fi=C(0i),  Gi=C({0}∪(Q\{i})),  Zi=C(Q\{i})  (i∈Q),
B=C(Q).
\end{verbatim}

The Zi and B are precisely the helpers disjoint from A. We will show
that this covered configuration always solves.

\hypertarget{a-disjoint-helper-gives-an-opposite-assembly}{%
\subsection*{1. A disjoint helper gives an opposite
assembly}\label{a-disjoint-helper-gives-an-opposite-assembly}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. A disjoint helper gives an opposite
assembly}

For any helper X, let L(X) be the set of columns q for which X has the
reverse-A corner q1. Then

\begin{verbatim}
L(Fi)={i};  L(Gi)=L(Zi)=Q\{i};  L(B)=Q.
\end{verbatim}

Choose a disjoint helper Z. It has q0 and q1 for every q in a set M of
at least two columns. O has the reverse-A positions20,30, so write
P=\{2,3\} for its columns. Let U be the union of the L sets of the other
eight helpers.

We have \textbar U\textbar≥2. For any two columns in U, two of these
eight helpers can fill their two q1 positions: otherwise at most one
helper meets either column, leaving at least seven helpers in the unique
singleton class Fi of the third column, contrary to the class bound
four.

If distinct columns q∈M∩U and r∈P∩U can be chosen, assemble the
rectangle01\textbar qr: Z supplies q0, O supplies r0, two other helpers
supply q1,r1, and four A supply the forward half.

The only way those distinct columns cannot be chosen is M=P=\{2,3\} and
U=\{2,4\} or \{3,4\}. Indeed all three of M,P,U have size at least two
in a three-element set; if both intersections are the same singleton, M
and P have the same missing column.

Thus Z=C(23), and by interchanging2 and3 we may take U=\{3,4\}. The
other helpers are then F3,F4,G2 or C(34). If C(34) occurs, choose it as
Z instead: its column pair differs from P, and the preceding rectangle
construction works. Therefore the sole disjoint helper is C(23), and the
other eight helpers are F3,F4,G2.

Let their counts be x,y,z. Since F3,G2 and F4,G2 share four corners,
x+z≤7 and y+z≤7. Their total is eight, so x,y≥1. Assemble the opposite
target14\textbar23, omitting0, by

\begin{verbatim}
A:12,13;  C(23):21;  O:24,
\end{verbatim}

and fill31,34,42,43 from F3,F4,G2. These helpers see respectively
\{31,34\}, \{42,43\}, \{31,42\}. Assign F3 to34 and F4 to43. The
remaining helpers match31,42: each position still has at least three
neighbours, because x+z=8−y≥4 and y+z=8−x≥4.

Consequently a failure in this row cover has no disjoint helpers.

\hypertarget{the-six-position-path}{%
\subsection*{2. The six-position path}\label{the-six-position-path}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. The six-position path}

Write a,b,c,d,e,f for the counts of F2,F3,F4,G2,G3,G4, respectively.
Their sum is nine, and each is at most four.

Consider the opposite target14\textbar23. Two A supply12,13. Its
remaining six positions, in the order

\begin{verbatim}
24,21,43,42,31,34,
\end{verbatim}

have the following helper neighbourhoods:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  F2,G3,F4,G2,F3 are the five consecutive edges of this six-vertex path;
\item
  G4 sees the outer four positions24,21,31,34;
\item
  O sees the two ends and the two central positions24,43,42,34.
\end{itemize}

The four pairs of consecutive path-edge classes share four corners. Thus
their weight sums a+e, e+c, c+d, d+b are at most seven.

We now check Hall's theorem for these six positions.

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  A set of five or six positions meets every helper, so is safe.
\item
  A set of four positions can miss at most one path-edge class; it
  cannot miss G4. Hence it has at least9−4=5 helper neighbours.
\item
  The complement of a three-position set contains at most two path
  edges. If it contains two, they are consecutive and have combined
  weight at most seven. G4 cannot be missed, and O must meet the chosen
  three positions. There are therefore at least9−7+1=3 neighbours.
\end{itemize}

Only singleton and pair obstructions remain. The singleton positions not
already supplied by O are21 and31. Their failure conditions are

\begin{verbatim}
a+e+f=0,              b+d+f=0.
\end{verbatim}

For a pair, if O misses it then the pair is \{21,31\}, which has 9−c≥5
helper neighbours. Otherwise a deficient pair must have no helper
neighbour at all. For the central pair \{43,42\}, this means c+d+e=0.
For any other pair, G4 meets it, so f=0. Nine remaining copies, each
path-edge class of weight at most four, require at least three positive
path edges in the four-position complement. Those three edges must form
four consecutive vertices. The resulting conditions are the two
singleton conditions already listed, or a+b+f=0.

Therefore failure of this opposite target forces one of

\begin{verbatim}
a+e+f=0;   b+d+f=0;   c+d+e=0;   a+b+f=0.
\end{verbatim}

The first three put all helpers in one of the families

\begin{verbatim}
{F3,F4,G2},  {F2,F4,G3},  {F2,F3,G4}.
\end{verbatim}

The fourth puts them in \{F4,G2,G3\}. This last family actually solves
another opposite target. Its counts c+d+e=9 obey c+d≤7,c+e≤7, so d,e≥2.
Assemble opposite12\textbar34 by

\begin{verbatim}
A:13,14;  O:24;  G3:23;  G2:31,32.
\end{verbatim}

Two of the six remaining helpers fill41,42: every one sees41, and at
least c+d−2≥3 also see42.

Thus only the first three support families need to be considered.

\hypertarget{the-three-support-families}{%
\subsection*{3. The three support
families}\label{the-three-support-families}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. The three support families}

Interchanging2 and3 exchanges the first two families and preserves O. It
suffices to treat these two forms:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Case & X & Y & Z \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
I & F3=C(03) & F4=C(04) & G2=C(034) \\
II & F2=C(02) & F3=C(03) & G4=C(023) \\
\end{longtable}

Write x+y+z=9, with each count at most four.

Four copies of X or Y solve by reserving a same-repeat pair and using
the following six tricoloured positions. The two positions in the last
column are supplied by two sources from the other two classes; at least
five such sources are available.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 8\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Case and target
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Two remaining target-class copies
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
A
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
O
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Two sources from the other classes
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
I, X & 31,32 & 04 & 34 & 01,02 \\
I, Y & 41,43 & 03 & 42 & 01,02 \\
II, X & 21,23 & 03 & 24 & 01,04 \\
II, Y & 31,32 & 02 & 34 & 01,04 \\
\end{longtable}

Hence x,y≤3. If z=4, then x+y=5 and x,y≥2. Reserve a same-repeat Z pair.
In Case I use X at31,32, Y at41,42, A at02 and a remaining Z at01. In
Case II use X at21,24, Y at31,34, A at04 and a remaining Z at01. Thus
z=4 also solves. Necessarily x=y=z=3.

\hypertarget{each-class-has-a-loop}{%
\subsubsection*{Each class has a loop}\label{each-class-has-a-loop}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Each class has a loop}

The same Z-target construction works after reserving two of its three
copies. For X and Y, the following assignments work after reserving
their same-repeat pair:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1667}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1667}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1667}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1667}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1667}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 10\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1667}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Case and target
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Remaining target copy
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Z
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
A
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
O
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Two copies of the other class
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
I, X & 31 & 32 & 04 & 34 & Y:01,02 \\
I, Y & 43 & 41 & 03 & 42 & X:01,02 \\
II, X & 23 & 21 & 03 & 24 & Y:01,04 \\
II, Y & 32 & 31 & 02 & 34 & X:01,04 \\
\end{longtable}

Thus all three copies of each class must have different repeated
colours.

\hypertarget{the-three-classes-conflict-pairwise}{%
\subsubsection*{The three classes conflict
pairwise}\label{the-three-classes-conflict-pairwise}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{The three classes conflict
pairwise}

If X and Y share a repeated colour, reserve one of each for an X
target's bicoloured positions. There are now two X copies left, so the X
row of the preceding four-copy table supplies the six tricoloured
positions. Two sources from Y,Z remain available for its common pair.

If X and Z share a repeated colour, they supply the bicoloured corners
of an A target: X has pole0, Z has pole1, and their common repeat lies
outside \{0,1\}. The same holds for Y and Z. In either case, one
remaining X and one remaining Y match two different A corners, and four
A fill the other four. Their A intersections are different two-element
subsets of \{02,03,04\}, so this two-matching always exists.

The three repeated-colour sets must therefore be pairwise disjoint. Each
has size three, requiring nine colours where only five exist. This
contradiction closes the row-triple cover.

\hypertarget{arg-46}{%
\section{Argument 46: Four adjacent copies and one opposite
source}\label{arg-46}}

\textbf{Theorem.} Every fourteen-pool containing exactly one
opposite-repeat source and at least four copies from one adjacent
tricolour class is soluble.

Use directed corners \texttt{ab}, adjacent classes C(S)=S→Sᶜ, and
opposite varieties given by bidirected complete bipartite graphs on two
pairs. The mixed five-class theorem already handles a class of size at
least five. In a putative failure, therefore, every adjacent class has
count at most four. Normalize a four-copy class to A=C(01).

We use the established shared-four bound and two eight-cube
constructions:

\begin{verbatim}
4B + three adjacent sources disjoint from B + O solve;
3B + four adjacent sources disjoint from B + O solve.
\end{verbatim}

Thus a four-copy class has disjoint-adjacent mass at most two, and a
three-copy class has disjoint-adjacent mass at most three.

\hypertarget{a-two-corner-transfer-lemma}{%
\subsection*{1. A two-corner transfer
lemma}\label{a-two-corner-transfer-lemma}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. A two-corner transfer lemma}

Suppose the nine adjacent sources outside A meet A only in a two-corner
set P. Then four A together with those helpers and O solve.

If O∩A⊆P, retain any eight helpers and use the thirteen-cube corollary
of \protect\hyperlink{arg-40}{Argument 40}.

Otherwise O has an A corner outside P. Apply the explicit two-corner
cover constructions in Sections 3--5 of
\protect\hyperlink{arg-12}{Argument 12}. Their numerical assumptions
hold here: our class bound four is stronger than the bound five there,
the shared-four bound is unchanged, and the disjoint mass is at most
two. All of those constructions use at most four A except the two
conflicts between A and a singleton-intersection class in the final
row-cover case. In either such conflict one A has been reserved for a
bicoloured position, and four A were used at the four corners outside P.
Here use O at one of those corners and the three remaining A at the
other three. Every other displayed construction transfers without
change. This proves the lemma.

This use of the pure five-class proof concerns its displayed post-cover
constructions. The initial five-A matching-rank reduction is not needed.

\hypertarget{hall-reduction-and-the-rectangle-exclusion}{%
\subsection*{2. Hall reduction and the rectangle
exclusion}\label{hall-reduction-and-the-rectangle-exclusion}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Hall reduction and the rectangle
exclusion}

Reserve a same-repeat A pair for its bicoloured positions, leaving two
A. The ten outside sources must have matching rank at most three on A's
six tricoloured positions. König's theorem gives a cover with at most
three source/corner vertices.

A cover without a corner vertex leaves at least seven adjacent sources
disjoint from A, impossible. A cover with one corner vertex must use an
exceptional source vertex for O. Even if another adjacent source is
exceptional, at least eight adjacent sources remain covered by that
single corner. At most two are disjoint, so at least six belong to the
unique singleton-intersection class, again impossible.

For a cover with two corners and one exceptional source, use Section 1
if O is exceptional. If an adjacent source is exceptional, the other
eight helpers and O lie in the pair O∩A, and the thirteen-cube corollary
applies directly.

Consequently every remaining case has a three-corner cover H for all
outside sources. O∩A has exactly two corners, in different columns. In
particular, \textbf{a four-copy class meeting O in four corners already
solves}. We may use this rectangle exclusion for any other class.

Up to the stabilizer of A, there are five covered configurations: three
with O∩A a row pair, and two with O∩A a diagonal pair.

\hypertarget{row-pair-with-h020304}{%
\subsection*{3. Row pair, with
H=\{02,03,04\}}\label{row-pair-with-h020304}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Row pair, with H=\{02,03,04\}}

Normalize O to opposite04\textbar23, omitting1. This is precisely the
case proved in \protect\hyperlink{arg-45}{Argument 45}.

That proof first solves whenever a helper is disjoint from A. With no
disjoint helpers it reduces one opposite target to a six-position path
matching problem. Hall sets of size at least three are controlled by the
shared-four bound. The remaining singleton and pair conditions reduce to
three support families, each of which forces three looped, pairwise
conflicting classes with counts3,3,3. The resulting demand for nine
different repeated colours is impossible.

\hypertarget{row-pair-with-h020312-1}{%
\subsection*{4. Row pair, with
H=\{02,03,12\}}\label{row-pair-with-h020312-1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Row pair, with H=\{02,03,12\}}

Again O is opposite04\textbar23. The nine eligible helpers belong to

\begin{verbatim}
B=C(234), D=C(134), R=C(04), E=C(034), F=C(024),
Z23=C(23), Z24=C(24), Z34=C(34).
\end{verbatim}

Only D meets A outside O∩A. If d≤1, discard that source and apply the
thirteen-cube corollary to eight helpers contained in O∩A. Hence d≥2.
Also r≤3 by the rectangle exclusion: R has four O corners.

Try rectangle01\textbar34, with O at30 and helpers at31,40,41. Their
pools are

\begin{verbatim}
31: B,E,Z23,Z34;
40: B,D,Z24,Z34;
41: B,R,E,F,Z24,Z34.
\end{verbatim}

The pair unions have sizes9−r−f≥2,9−d≥5,9−z23≥7. The40 pool is nonempty
since d≥2, and the41 pool has at least9−d−z23≥3 sources. Thus Hall can
fail only when31 is absent.

Only D,R,F,Z24 then remain. Use rectangle01\textbar24, with O at20 and
helpers at21,40,41. These pools are F+Z24, D+Z24 and R+F+Z24. Their pair
unions have sizes at least6,5 and9. The last two pools are nonempty. The
first is also nonempty, since otherwise d+r=9 would contradict d≤4,r≤3.
The required three-matching exists.

\hypertarget{row-pair-with-h020314-1}{%
\subsection*{5. Row pair, with
H=\{02,03,14\}}\label{row-pair-with-h020314-1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. Row pair, with H=\{02,03,14\}}

O is still opposite04\textbar23. The nonzero-intersection helper classes
are

\begin{verbatim}
R=C(04), D=C(123), E=C(034), F=C(024).
\end{verbatim}

Write z≤2 for the other helpers, which are disjoint from A. Only D meets
A outside O∩A, so d≥2 as above. Both R and D have four O corners, so
r,d≤3 by the rectangle exclusion.

D is disjoint from R,E,F. If d=3, its disjoint mass would be
r+e+f=9−d−z≥4, contrary to the three-copy disjoint-mass bound. Therefore
d=2, and r+e+f+z=7.

One D and O fill20,30 in rectangle01\textbar23. If two other helpers
match 21,31, the rectangle solves with four A. At least e+f+z=7−r≥4
helpers meet one of21,31. Hence absence of such a two-matching forces
one position to be absent. Interchanging2 and3, assume21 is absent. Then
F,B,Z23,Z24 are absent, and the disjoint helpers are all Z34. Thus
r+e+z=7.

If z\textgreater0, use Z34 at40 and O at30 in rectangle01\textbar34. Two
remaining R/E/Z34 helpers match31,41: after reserving that Z34, the31
pool has e+z−1≥3 sources, and all six remaining helpers see31 or41.

Thus z=0. The bounds r≤3,e≤4 and r+e=7 force r=3,e=4. Reserve a
same-repeat E pair. The two other E fill31,32. R sees all four common
corners01,02,41,42, and O sees02,42, so the three R and O fill those
four positions. This assembles E.

Sections 3--5 prove that a four-copy class with a row-pair O
