intersection solves. We may now use that conclusion for any helper class
as well.

\hypertarget{diagonal-pair-with-h020312-1}{%
\subsection*{6. Diagonal pair, with
H=\{02,03,12\}}\label{diagonal-pair-with-h020312-1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{6. Diagonal pair, with H=\{02,03,12\}}

Now O is opposite02\textbar13, omitting4. The eligible helper classes
are the same as in Section 4. D=C(134) and F=C(024) each have four O
corners; R=C(04) has a row-pair O intersection. Their counts d,r,f are
therefore at most three.

E=C(034) also has count at most three. If e=4, reserve a same-repeat E
pair, put O at32 and A at02, and let two other helpers fill two
different positions in \{01,31,41,42\}; the two remaining E fill the
other two. Such a helper pair exists. Each of the five other helpers
meets this set. Those with only one position are D at42, Z23 at31, and
Z24 at41; each such class has count at most three (indeed the disjoint
classes have total at most two). Every other eligible helper sees at
least two positions. Five helpers therefore cannot all be confined to
one common position.

Now d,r,e,f≤3 and z≤2. Try rectangle01\textbar34 with O at30 and helpers
at31,40,41. Its pair unions have sizes9−r−f≥3,9−d≥6,9−z23≥7, and the41
pool is nonempty. Failure forces31 or40 to be absent.

If31 is absent, only D,R,F,Z24 remain. When Z24 occurs,
rectangle01\textbar24 solves with O at21, Z24 at20 and two helpers
at40,41. After reserving the Z24, the40 pool has d+z24−1≥2 sources and
the41 pool has at least five. When Z24 is absent, d+r+f=9 forces
d=r=f=3. Assemble O using A:03,12; D:10,32; F:21,23; R:01; O:30.

If40 is absent, only R,E,F,Z23 remain. If Z23 occurs, both E and F
occur, because otherwise the total helper count is at most3+3+2=8. Use
rectangle01\textbar23 with O:30, Z23:20, E:31, F:21 and four A forward.
Thus only r=e=f=3, with no disjoint helpers, remains.

\hypertarget{the-remaining-repeated-colour-triangle}{%
\subsubsection*{The remaining repeated-colour
triangle}\label{the-remaining-repeated-colour-triangle}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{The remaining repeated-colour
triangle}

The three classes R,E,F all have loops. After reserving a same-repeat
pair from the named target class, use:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Target & Six tricoloured positions \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
R & A:02,03; E:41,42; F:01,43 \\
E & remaining E:31; O:32; A:02; R:41,42; F:01 \\
F & remaining F:21; O:23; A:03; R:41,43; E:01 \\
\end{longtable}

They also conflict pairwise. R and E can share only repeat3, and R and F
only repeat2. In either case their poles supply0,1 for an A target. Four
A fill04,12,13,14; a remaining E fills02 and a remaining F fills03.

If E and F share a repeat, reserve one of each for an E target's poles
1,2. The two remaining E fill31,32, A fills02, two R fill41,42, and O
fills01. Thus E and F also conflict.

Three pairwise disjoint repeated-colour sets of size three would be
required, a contradiction.

\hypertarget{diagonal-pair-with-h020314-1}{%
\subsection*{7. Diagonal pair, with
H=\{02,03,14\}}\label{diagonal-pair-with-h020314-1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{7. Diagonal pair, with H=\{02,03,14\}}

Here O is opposite04\textbar12, omitting3. Let

\begin{verbatim}
R=C(04), D=C(123), E=C(034), F=C(024).
\end{verbatim}

R,D,E each have four O corners, and F has a row-pair O intersection.
Consequently r,d,e,f≤3. The disjoint-from-A count is z≤2, and
r+d+e+f+z=9.

Try O itself. Two A fill02,14, and the other six positions split into

\begin{verbatim}
C={01,41,42},       L={10,20,24}.
\end{verbatim}

R and E see all C; F sees01,41. There are at least four R/E/F sources,
and r+e≥1, since otherwise the total count is at most3+3+2=8. Therefore
three R/E/F sources match all C.

D sees all L. The disjoint helpers have L intersections

\begin{verbatim}
B=C(234), Z24: {20};   Z23: {20,24};   Z34: empty.
\end{verbatim}

If two sources from D and the disjoint classes match two L positions, O
fills the third and the target solves. Otherwise d≤1; and if d=1, all
disjoint sources must be Z34.

Write z34 for the Z34 count. We now try opposite12\textbar34, omitting0:

\begin{verbatim}
A:13,14;  F:23;  O:24;
\end{verbatim}

then fill31,32,41,42 from R,E,Z34. E and Z34 see all four positions,
while R sees41,42. This works whenever

\begin{verbatim}
f>0,        e+z34≥2,        r+e+z34≥4.
\end{verbatim}

The last inequality always holds under the remaining assumptions. If
d=1, all disjoint sources are Z34, giving r+e+z34=8−f≥5. If d=0, it is
at least9−f−z≥4.

If f=0, the count bounds force d=1,r=e=3,z=2, with both disjoint sources
Z34. Assemble rectangle01\textbar34 using four A forward, O at41, E
at31, and the two Z34 at30,40.

Finally suppose e+z34\textless2. This cannot happen when d=1, since then
e+z34=8−r−f≥2. When d=0, we have e≥1, and the bounds force
e=1,z34=0,r=f=3,z=2. The two disjoint sources cannot match two L
positions, so both are B or Z24; a Z23 together with either would give a
two-matching. Both B and Z24 contain21,40,41. Assemble
rectangle01\textbar24 with four A forward, O at20, the two disjoint
sources at21,40, and one R at41.

All cases are soluble. This proves the theorem.

\hypertarget{cumulative-consequence}{%
\subsection*{Cumulative consequence}\label{cumulative-consequence}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Cumulative consequence}

Together with the preceding mixed four-class proofs, this establishes:

\begin{quote}
A mixed diffuse fourteen-cube failure has every adjacent
tricoloured-corner class count at most three.
\end{quote}

The result is elementary and independent of the finite eight-cube lemma
for high opposite-source counts. It does not close the remaining mixed
cases in which all adjacent class counts are at most three.

\hypertarget{arg-47}{%
\section{Argument 47: Two own-loop triples with one opposite
source}\label{arg-47}}

\textbf{Theorem.} A failing pool of fourteen adjacent sources and one
opposite source, with adjacent-class multiplicities at most three, has
at most one triple admitting an own completion. The opposite source may
participate in either asserted completion.

We adapt \protect\hyperlink{arg-33}{Argument 33}, retaining its notation
and its explicit final matchings. Only the following changes are needed;
none assumes a completion avoiding the opposite source.

\hypertarget{the-two-anchors}{%
\subsection*{1. The two anchors}\label{the-two-anchors}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. The two anchors}

The repeat-restoration argument in Section 1 of that proof still
applies: restore an anchor copy when removing the other reserved source
from an own completion. It follows that two own-completed triples have
disjoint pole pairs. Normalize them to \[
A=C(01),\qquad B=C(23)\quad\hbox{or}\quad C=C(014).
\] The eight other adjacent sources have poles \(04,14,24,34\), with
forced repeats \(1,0,3,2\), respectively. Denote the opposite source by
\(O\).

For the disjoint orientation \(A,B\), use target \(01\mid23\). An
adjacent helper meets one of its directed halves. The source \(O\) meets
both: its intersection with \(A\) meets at least two of the three
columns, hence meets the rectangle on columns \(2,3\); its corners are
invariant under reversal. Put the helper in one half and \(O\) in the
other, and fill the remaining three positions in each half with the two
anchor triples.

\hypertarget{the-nested-orientation}{%
\subsection*{2. The nested orientation}\label{the-nested-orientation}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. The nested orientation}

For \(A,C\), retain the names \(P,\bar P,Q,\bar Q,R,\bar R,S,\bar S\)
and the main/outer blocks from Section 3 of the pure proof. Put \[
a=g(\bar P)+g(\bar Q),\qquad b=g(R)+g(S).
\] Its three restrictions remain valid:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \(a,b\le3\). Here use Construction III of
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-40}{Argument 40}: three copies of an adjacent
  class, four sources disjoint from it and one opposite source assemble.
  The first outer block is disjoint from \(C\), and the second from
  \(A\).
\item
  A main class of size three excludes every outer source. The displayed
  repeated-colour constructions in the pure proof use only the anchors,
  that main class and the outer source; they are unchanged.
\item
  If both main blocks occur, every main count is at most one. Again the
  displayed constructions use only adjacent sources and remain
  unchanged.
\end{enumerate}

There is an outer source: otherwise eight adjacent helpers would lie in
the main classes. One occupied main block has capacity at most six,
while two occupied blocks have total capacity at most four by
restriction 3. Consequently every main count is at most two. With one
main block the main total is at most four, and with two the same bound
follows from restriction 3. Hence \(a+b\ge4\) and both outer blocks
occur.

The reverse-square argument from Section 3 of the pure proof now applies
without alteration. If three outer types occur, their at least four
copies fill the reverse square of \(01\mid23\), while three \(A\) and
one \(C\) fill its forward square. Thus the outer classes can be
normalized to \[
\bar P^a,R^b,\qquad 1\le a,b\le3,\qquad a+b\ge4.
\] There are exactly eight adjacent helpers, so the main total is
\(8-a-b\). These are precisely the hypotheses of the pure proof's
``remaining numerical cases.'' Its assignments to \(04\mid23\) and
\(13\mid24\) use only adjacent sources and therefore apply verbatim.
Each case assembles, contradicting failure.

The two alterations from the pure proof are the use of Construction III
to bound the outer blocks, and the capacity argument establishing an
outer source. In particular, neither argument requires the asserted own
completions to avoid \(O\).

\hypertarget{arg-48}{%
\section{Argument 48: Five repeated adjacent classes and two further
classes}\label{arg-48}}

\hypertarget{theorem}{%
\subsection*{Theorem}\label{theorem}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Theorem}

Take adjacent-repeat sources from seven different tricoloured-corner
classes, with multiplicities

\begin{verbatim}
3,2,2,2,2,1,1.
\end{verbatim}

These thirteen sources contain an eight-source five-colour assembly. In
particular a pure fourteen-cube failure cannot have exactly one triple
class.

The proof classifies five fixed doubled classes by a small geometric
Hall table, then gives explicit helper constructions. It does not
enumerate source inventories or repeated-colour refinements.

Throughout C(S) means the directed cut S→Sᶜ. A label such as01 denotes
its outset, and a target written ab/cd has all eight directed corners
between the pairs ab andcd. Its two four-corner halves are ab→cd
andcd→ab. All sources may be properly rotated independently.

\hypertarget{the-hall-criterion-for-five-doubled-classes}{%
\subsection*{1. The Hall criterion for five doubled
classes}\label{the-hall-criterion-for-five-doubled-classes}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. The Hall criterion for five doubled
classes}

Take two sources from each of five distinct adjacent classes, and fix an
opposite target T that their union supports. These ten sources match T
if and only if no three target corners are private to one of the five
classes.

Necessity is immediate: two source copies cannot fill three corners
available only in their class. For sufficiency, an adjacent cut meets T
in either two positions or one of its full four-position halves. At most
two classes can have their whole intersection in two given positions.
Therefore sets of six positions see at least three of the five classes,
hence six sources. Sets of seven or eight positions see all five
classes. Supported singletons and pairs have at least two source
neighbours. A three- or four-set can fail Hall only if all its corners
have neighbours in just one class, giving a private triple.

Suppose a five-set S fails Hall. It then meets at most two classes.
Those two class intersections must cover all five positions because T is
supported. Two two-position intersections cannot do so. If both
intersections have size four, they must be opposite target halves: equal
halves would cover only four positions. They are disjoint, so one class
supplies at least three private positions of S. If one intersection has
size four and the other size two, at least three positions of S are
outside the latter intersection and are private to the former. Thus
every Hall failure again gives a private triple. This proves the
criterion.

\hypertarget{classifying-the-five-doubled-classes}{%
\subsection*{2. Classifying the five doubled
classes}\label{classifying-the-five-doubled-classes}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Classifying the five doubled
classes}

If their tricoloured union supports no opposite target, the elementary
support-poset theorem puts them inside a common-corner family or a
butterfly family. See Section0 of \protect\hyperlink{arg-15}{Argument
15}; the theorem there concerns arbitrary cut collections, not just a
fixed source count. Five distinct classes in a butterfly are, up to its
symmetries, one of the two sets

\begin{verbatim}
F1={01,02,124,123,12},
F2={01,02,124,12,012}.                            (F)
\end{verbatim}

These omit a low class or a high class from the six-class butterfly. Its
four high classes are interchanged by the permutations1↔2, 3↔4, and(1
3)(2 4) followed by reversal of all cuts. The last operation also
exchanges its two low classes. This verifies the two possible omissions
directly. The common-corner alternative will be handled directly in
Section3.

Now suppose a target is supported but none assembles. By Section1 some
class has three private target positions. An adjacent cut meeting an
opposite target in at least three positions contains an entire
four-position directed half, since its only possible intersection sizes
and shapes are two positions or a full half. Its private triple
therefore consists of three positions in that half. Normalize this class
to A=C(01) and those positions to

\begin{verbatim}
03,04,13.
\end{verbatim}

The other four classes must avoid those three corners. The implications
0∈S⇒3,4∈S and1∈S⇒3∈S give exactly these eight possible outsets:

\begin{verbatim}
U={234,134,123,034},     V={13,23,24,34}.
\end{verbatim}

If three of the four classes belong to U, or three belong to V, two
copies each of those three classes and A already assemble. The direct
constructions are Section2 of \protect\hyperlink{arg-28}{Argument 28};
they use only those eight sources. Thus exactly two classes lie in each
of U,V.

The following table treats all six choices from U against all six
choices from V. An ordinary entry is an opposite target supported by the
five classes and having no three positions private to one class.
Section1 therefore gives an assembly. Each entry is checked by
intersecting the five directed cuts with its eight positions. The
exceptional entries I,II,III are identified immediately below.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
U pair / V pair & 13,23 & 13,24 & 13,34 & 23,24 & 23,34 & 24,34 \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
234,134 & 01/24 & 13/24 & 01/24 & 12/34 & I & I \\
234,123 & 01/24 & 13/24 & 01/24 & I & I & 12/34 \\
234,034 & 01/24 & 13/24 & 01/24 & 12/34 & I & I \\
134,123 & III & 02/34 & III & 12/34 & 01/24 & 12/34 \\
134,034 & 01/24 & 03/14 & II & 12/34 & 01/24 & 01/24 \\
123,034 & 01/23 & 12/34 & 04/12 & 12/34 & 01/24 & 12/34 \\
\end{longtable}

The three exceptional representatives are

\begin{verbatim}
I   ={01,234,02,014,012},
II  ={01,02,124,024,24},
III ={01,02,124,023,012}.                         (E)
\end{verbatim}

Here are explicit relabellings for all nine exceptional cells, so their
identification requires no orbit enumeration. The tuple lists the new
labels of0,1,2,3,4. A final reversal reverses every directed cut
simultaneously.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
U pair & V pair & Representative & Relabelling tuple & Reverse cuts? \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
234,134 & 23,34 & I & (0,1,2,3,4) & yes \\
234,134 & 24,34 & I & (0,1,2,4,3) & yes \\
234,123 & 23,24 & I & (0,1,3,4,2) & yes \\
234,123 & 23,34 & I & (0,1,4,3,2) & yes \\
234,034 & 23,34 & I & (1,0,2,3,4) & yes \\
234,034 & 24,34 & I & (1,0,2,4,3) & yes \\
134,123 & 13,23 & III & (0,3,1,4,2) & yes \\
134,123 & 13,34 & III & (0,3,2,4,1) & yes \\
134,034 & 13,34 & II & (1,0,3,2,4) & no \\
\end{longtable}

This proves the required classification: unless the ten doubled sources
already assemble, the five classes either have one common corner, form
one of(F), or form one of(E).

\hypertarget{the-common-corner-case}{%
\subsection*{3. The common-corner case}\label{the-common-corner-case}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. The common-corner case}

One of the five repeated classes has a third actual source. The five
classes therefore have counts3,2,2,2,2 through a common corner. They
contain a3,2,2,2,1 subpool, which assembles by
\protect\hyperlink{arg-23}{Argument 23}.

The two singleton helpers are unnecessary in this case. This is the only
point of the proof that uses the third source in a repeated class.

\hypertarget{the-butterfly-case-f1}{%
\subsection*{4. The butterfly case F1}\label{the-butterfly-case-f1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. The butterfly case F1}

The four high classes01,02,124,123 are all doubled. Their union with the
two low classes12,012 misses precisely31,32,41,42. Any class outside the
six-class butterfly contains at least one of these four corners. At most
one of the two distinct helper classes is the remaining low class012, so
an outside helper exists.

If it has31, target03/14 is assembled by

\begin{verbatim}
helper:31; C01:04,13; C02:01;
C124:40,43; C123:10,34.
\end{verbatim}

Interchanging1,2 and interchanging3,4 preserve the doubled high classes
and act transitively on31,32,41,42. Thus any outside helper works. This
is also the explicit butterfly construction in the six-doubled-classes
note.

\hypertarget{the-butterfly-case-f2}{%
\subsection*{5. The butterfly case F2}\label{the-butterfly-case-f2}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. The butterfly case F2}

Write A=C01,P=C02,L=C124,Q=C12,H=C012, all doubled.

If a helper has41, use target01/24:

\begin{verbatim}
helper:41; A:02,12; P:04,21; Q:20,14; L:40.
\end{verbatim}

If a helper has42, use target02/14:

\begin{verbatim}
helper:42; A:04,12; P:01,21; Q:10,24; L:40.
\end{verbatim}

Otherwise both helpers avoid41,42. Outside the five core classes, the
only allowed helper classes are

\begin{verbatim}
C03,C123,C13,C023,C23,C013.
\end{verbatim}

Every one contains34; all except C123 contain31 or32. Since the helpers
have distinct classes, one can supply31 or32 and the other can supply34.
If the first supplies32, use target03/24:

\begin{verbatim}
helpers:32,34; A:02,04; Q:20,23; L:40,43.
\end{verbatim}

If it supplies31, use target03/14:

\begin{verbatim}
helpers:31,34; P:01,04; Q:10,13; L:40,43.
\end{verbatim}

This closes F2 using only two copies of every core class.

\hypertarget{exceptional-type-i}{%
\subsection*{6. Exceptional type I}\label{exceptional-type-i}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{6. Exceptional type I}

Write A=C01,B=C234,P=C02,D=C014,H=C012, all doubled.

A helper at32 gives target12/34:

\begin{verbatim}
helper:32; A:13,14; B:31,41; P:23,24; D:42.
\end{verbatim}

A helper at any corner c in \{20,40,41\} gives target01/24:

\begin{verbatim}
helper:c; B:the other two corners of{20,40,41};
P:04,21; D:02,12; H:14.
\end{verbatim}

The only helper class outside the core avoiding both32 and all of
20,40,41 is C023. Two distinct helper classes cannot both be this class,
so one of the displayed constructions applies.

\hypertarget{exceptional-type-ii}{%
\subsection*{7. Exceptional type II}\label{exceptional-type-ii}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{7. Exceptional type II}

Write A=C01,P=C02,L=C124,R=C024,Z=C24, all doubled.

A helper at any corner c in \{02,12,14\} gives target01/24:

\begin{verbatim}
helper:c; A:the other two corners of{02,12,14};
P:04,21; L:20,40; R:41.
\end{verbatim}

If neither helper meets that set, their only possible classes outside
the core are

\begin{verbatim}
C234,C023,C23,C34.
\end{verbatim}

Every one contains31, and all except C023 contain30. Distinct helpers
can therefore fill30,31 in target01/34. Its other six positions are
supplied by

\begin{verbatim}
A:04,14; P:03; L:13,40; R:41.
\end{verbatim}

This closes type II.

\hypertarget{exceptional-type-iii}{%
\subsection*{8. Exceptional type III}\label{exceptional-type-iii}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{8. Exceptional type III}

Write A=C01,P=C02,L=C124,R=C023,H=C012, all doubled.

A helper at any corner c in \{10,40,43\} gives target03/14:

\begin{verbatim}
helper:c; L:the other two corners of{10,40,43};
R:31,34; P:01,04; A:13.
\end{verbatim}

If neither helper meets that set, their only possible classes outside
the core are

\begin{verbatim}
C03,C034,C23,C013.
\end{verbatim}

All except C23 contain32, so one of two distinct helpers contains 32.
Use target03/12:

\begin{verbatim}
helper:32; A:02,13; P:01,23; L:10,20; R:31.
\end{verbatim}

Thus type III also assembles. All cases of the core classification are
now closed, proving the theorem.

\hypertarget{consequence-for-a-pure-fourteen-pool-with-one-triple}{%
\subsection*{9. Consequence for a pure fourteen-pool with one
triple}\label{consequence-for-a-pure-fourteen-pool-with-one-triple}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{9. Consequence for a pure
fourteen-pool with one triple}

In a hypothetical pure fourteen-failure, at most eight classes are
occupied and at most five have multiplicity at least two. Every class
has multiplicity at most three. If precisely one is a triple, let d be
the number of doubled classes and s the number of singletons. Then

\begin{verbatim}
3+2d+s=14,   1+d+s≤8,   1+d≤5.
\end{verbatim}

These inequalities force d=4,s=3. Retain the triple, the four doubled
classes and two of the three singleton classes. The thirteen-source
theorem above gives an assembly, a contradiction.

Therefore every pure fourteen-failure has at least two triple classes.
This deduction uses the existing pure concentration, diversity and
six-repeated-class bounds; none depends on the one-opposite fifteen-cube
theorem.

\hypertarget{arg-49}{%
\section{Argument 49: A private adjacent column and a high opposite
source}\label{arg-49}}

\hypertarget{theorem-1}{%
\subsection*{Theorem}\label{theorem-1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Theorem}

Let A=C(01), and take three sources of class A and eleven other adjacent
sources, each tricoloured-corner class having multiplicity at most
three. Suppose none of the eleven outsiders contains either corner 02 or
12. Adjoin the opposite source

\begin{verbatim}
O=01|34={03,04,13,14},
\end{verbatim}

where each undirected edge supplies both corner directions. These
fifteen sources contain an eight-source five-colour assembly.

No assumptions about own loops, private corners of other classes, or the
number of occupied classes are required. In particular, this settles the
high-intersection type of an opposite cube avoiding a private column of
an adjacent triple.

\hypertarget{the-eleven-possible-outsider-classes}{%
\subsection*{1. The eleven possible outsider
classes}\label{the-eleven-possible-outsider-classes}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. The eleven possible outsider
classes}

The forbidden corners 02,12 say that an outset S must satisfy 0∈S⇒2∈S
and 1∈S⇒2∈S. Its size is two or three. The possible classes besides A
are exactly:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Name & Class & Reverse-A corners \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
P & C(02) & 21 \\
Q & C(12) & 20 \\
L3 & C(123) & 20,30 \\
L4 & C(124) & 20,40 \\
R3 & C(023) & 21,31 \\
R4 & C(024) & 21,41 \\
H & C(012) & none \\
B & C(234) & 20,21,30,31,40,41 \\
Z23 & C(23) & 20,21,30,31 \\
Z24 & C(24) & 20,21,40,41 \\
Z34 & C(34) & 30,31,40,41 \\
\end{longtable}

Lower-case letters denote source counts. Their sum is eleven and each
count is at most three.

We use the elementary cut-containment facts already proved in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-1}{Argument 1}, Section 1: on an opposite target
every adjacent cut meets two or four directed positions; at most three
classes are confined to three specified positions, and at most two to
two specified positions.

\hypertarget{three-high-targets-give-a-short-count-reduction}{%
\subsection*{2. Three high targets give a short count
reduction}\label{three-high-targets-give-a-short-count-reduction}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Three high targets give a short
count reduction}

First use target 01\textbar23. The three A sources cover its four
forward positions, while O covers both directions of 03 and 13. This
target assembles if and only if the outsiders satisfy all three
conditions:

\begin{verbatim}
corner 20 is supported;
corner 21 is supported;
at least one of 30,31 is supported.                 (1)
\end{verbatim}

Necessity follows from the single corners 20,21 and the pair 30,31, the
latter having only O as a core neighbour. For sufficiency, check Hall. A
set of reverse positions containing both 20,21 has at least five
outsider neighbours: the only classes avoiding both are H and Z34, with
combined capacity six. A reverse triple containing just one of 20,21 has
at least two outsider neighbours: the avoiders of \{20,30,31\} are
P,R4,H, and those of \{21,30,31\} are Q,L4,H. Either list accommodates
at most nine sources. All four reverse positions have at least eight
outsider neighbours, because only H avoids them. The smaller reverse
sets are handled by (1) and the one O source.

A set meeting the forward half and meeting O has four core neighbours,
so sets of size at most four are automatic. If it avoids O it lies in
\{02,12,20,21\}; its only potentially deficient case is the full
four-set, which has outsider neighbours at 20,21. For sets of five or
more positions, the cut-containment bounds and eleven outsiders give
more than the required neighbours: a five-set has at least two outsider
neighbours, a six-set at least five, and seven- and eight-sets see all
eleven. This proves (1).

By exchanging 3 and 4, target 01\textbar24 assembles under the
corresponding conditions with the last line of (1) replaced by support
of 40 or41.

Suppose neither target assembles. If both 20 and 21 were supported, no
outsider could have any of 30,31,40,41. The table would leave only
P,Q,H, with capacity nine, fewer than eleven. Hence 20 or21 is
unsupported. Exchange 0 and1 if necessary to assume 21 is absent. The
table then leaves only

\begin{verbatim}
Q, L3, L4, H, Z34,
\end{verbatim}

so

\begin{verbatim}
q+l3+l4+h+z34=11.                                  (2)
\end{verbatim}

Now use target T=01\textbar34, which is O itself. On its reverse half R,
O is universal and the three A sources have no neighbours. On its
forward half the four core sources A,A,A,O are universal. The same Hall
bounds show that T assembles precisely when the outsiders have matching
rank at least three on R.

On R the remaining outsider patterns are

\begin{verbatim}
Q,H: empty;  L3:{30};  L4:{40};  Z34:R.
\end{verbatim}

Equation (2), together with q,h≤3, gives l3+l4+z34≥5. If z34≥3, three
universal Z34 sources give rank three. If z34=2, some L3 or L4 is
present and also gives rank three. If z34=1, then l3+l4≥4; both are
positive since each is at most three, again giving rank three. Therefore
failure forces z34=0, and (2) becomes

\begin{verbatim}
q+l3+l4+h=11.
\end{verbatim}

Each of these four counts is two or three, and exactly one is two. This
is the entire residual: it was derived from three target tests, without
enumerating inventories.

\hypertarget{explicit-own-loops-close-the-residual}{%
\subsection*{3. Explicit own loops close the
residual}\label{explicit-own-loops-close-the-residual}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Explicit own loops close the
residual}

The class H has an own loop whenever it is a triple: after reserving two
H sources, its six corners have this matching:

\begin{verbatim}
A:03,04;  L3:14,24;  L4:13,23.
\end{verbatim}

All required classes have at least two copies. A triple L3 also has an
own loop in the pool containing O. Reserve two L3 sources and use

\begin{verbatim}
remaining L3:34;  O:30;  A:14;  L4:20;  Q:10,24.
\end{verbatim}

Similarly a triple L4 has the loop

\begin{verbatim}
remaining L4:43;  O:40;  A:13;  L3:20;  Q:10,23.
\end{verbatim}

If h=3, at least one of L3,L4 is a triple, and its pole pair meets H's
pole pair 34. If h=2, both L3 and L4 are triples, and their pole pairs
04 and03 meet. Thus there are two own-loop triples with intersecting
pole pairs in every residual case.

For completeness, such a pair always assembles. An own-loop triple in a
failure must use all three possible repeated colours, since otherwise a
same-repeat pair supplies its two bicoloured target corners and its loop
finishes. The two triples' three-element repeat sets therefore
intersect. Choose a common repeated colour, reserve one source from each
class for the first class's two bicoloured corners, using the pole
shared by the two classes for the source from the second class. Start
with the first class's own-loop matching after two of its sources were
removed. Restore one of those universal sources, and remove the chosen
source from the second class; if it was used in the matching, the
restored universal source replaces it. This completes the first adjacent
target.

The contradiction proves the theorem. This final argument is valid even
when the opposite source participates in the loop matching.

\hypertarget{arg-50}{%
\section{Argument 50: A private adjacent column and a diagonal opposite
source}\label{arg-50}}

\hypertarget{theorem-2}{%
\subsection*{Theorem}\label{theorem-2}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Theorem}

Take three sources of class A=C(01) and eleven other adjacent sources,
each class occurring at most three times. Suppose every outsider avoids
02 and12. Adjoin

\begin{verbatim}
O={01,04,13,34}=03|14.
\end{verbatim}

Every undirected edge denotes both directed corners. These fifteen
sources contain an eight-source five-colour assembly.

This is the diagonal-intersection type of an opposite source avoiding a
private column of A. Exchanging colours3,4 gives the other labelled
variety of the same type. No assumptions about other private columns,
own loops, or occupied-class counts are used.

\hypertarget{outsiders-and-two-high-target-tests}{%
\subsection*{1. Outsiders and two high-target
tests}\label{outsiders-and-two-high-target-tests}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Outsiders and two high-target
tests}

Use the eleven names from \protect\hyperlink{arg-49}{Argument 49}:

\begin{verbatim}
P=C02, Q=C12, L3=C123, L4=C124,
R3=C023, R4=C024, H=C012,
B=C234, Z23=C23, Z24=C24, Z34=C34.
\end{verbatim}

Their reverse-A intersections are displayed in that note. Write x=z23,
y=z24, w=z34 and use lower-case letters for all other counts. Every
count is at most three and their sum is eleven. Put

\begin{verbatim}
L=b+l3+l4+q+x+y,       R=b+p+r3+r4+x+y,
a=b+l3+x+w,            d=b+r4+y+w.
\end{verbatim}

These are respectively the outsider neighbour counts of20,21,30,41.

Target01\textbar23 assembles exactly when

\begin{verbatim}
L≥1, R≥1, a≥1, L+w≥2.                           (I)
\end{verbatim}

Target01\textbar24 assembles exactly when

\begin{verbatim}
L≥1, R≥1, d≥1, R+w≥2.                           (II)
\end{verbatim}

Here is a direct Hall verification of(I). The core A,A,A,O supplies the
four forward positions through A and the two positions13,31 through O.
The potentially deficient reverse sets are the singleton corners20,21,30
and the pair\{20,30\}; their outsider neighbour counts are precisely the
four expressions in(I).

Every other reverse set is automatic under(I). The pair\{20,21\} has at
least five outsider neighbours, since only H,Z34 avoid it. The
pair\{21,30\} has at least two, since its only avoiding classes are
Q,L4,H. Pairs containing31 also have O and an outsider from their other
corner. A reverse triple containing both20,21 inherits five outsider
neighbours. The triple\{20,30,31\} inherits L+w≥2 neighbours; the
triple\{21,30,31\} has at least two by the same Q,L4,H bound. The full
reverse half sees all outsiders except H, hence at least eight.

A set meeting A and O has four core neighbours. If it meets A but avoids
O, a four-position set must contain at least one of20,21,30, which is
supported by(I). For larger sets, use the elementary cut bounds: at most
three classes fit inside three specified target positions and at most
two fit inside two. Thus a five-set has at least two outsider
neighbours, a six-set at least five, and a seven- or eight-set all
eleven. These counts cover every remaining Hall inequality. Necessity
of(I) follows from its listed singleton and pair sets. The argument
for(II) follows by the symmetry (0 1)(3 4), which preserves O and
exchanges L,R and a,d.

\hypertarget{if-20-or21-is-absent}{%
\subsection*{2. If 20 or21 is absent}\label{if-20-or21-is-absent}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. If 20 or21 is absent}

The same symmetry lets us assume R=0. All outsiders then belong to

\begin{verbatim}
Q,L3,L4,H,Z34,
\end{verbatim}

and

\begin{verbatim}
q+l3+l4+h+w=11.                                (2)
\end{verbatim}

Suppose w\textgreater0. Use target01\textbar34 and put O at31. Its other
three reverse positions are30,40,41. If w≥3, three Z34 sources fill
them. If w=2, equation(2) and q,h≤3 give l3+l4≥3; choose one L3 or L4 at
its corner30 or40 and let the two Z34 sources fill the other positions.
If w=1, then l3+l4≥4, so both are positive; use L3:30,L4:40,Z34:41.

There is a remaining forward helper from Q or H: their combined count is
at least two because l3+l4+w≤9. It and three A sources fill the forward
half. Thus w\textgreater0 always gives an assembly.

Failure therefore forces w=0. The four counts q,l3,l4,h sum to eleven,
so they are 3,3,3,2 in some order. Section4 closes this family.

\hypertarget{if-both-20-and21-are-present}{%
\subsection*{3. If both 20 and21 are
present}\label{if-both-20-and21-are-present}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. If both 20 and21 are present}

Assume L,R≥1. If w\textgreater0, both(I) and(II) hold, so suppose w=0.
To fail both targets we need

\begin{verbatim}
(a=0 or L=1) and (d=0 or R=1).                  (3)
\end{verbatim}

The alternatives involving L=1 or R=1 are incompatible with eleven
outsiders. If L=R=1, the total is

\begin{verbatim}
h+L+R-(b+x+y)≤5.
\end{verbatim}

If L=1 and d=0, then b=r4=y=0 and the total is

\begin{verbatim}
h+L+p+r3≤3+1+3+3=10.
\end{verbatim}

The remaining mixed alternative is symmetric. Thus(3) forces a=d=0.
Consequently b=l3=x=r4=y=w=0, and only

\begin{verbatim}
P,Q,L4,R3,H
\end{verbatim}

remain, with

\begin{verbatim}
p+q+l4+r3+h=11.                                (4)
\end{verbatim}

Use O itself as a target, with positions

\begin{verbatim}
01,10,04,40,13,31,34,43.
\end{verbatim}

The two L4-only outer positions are40,43; the two R3-only outer
positions are31,34. If l4,r3≥1 and l4+r3≥3, these four positions can be
filled by L4,R3,O, using O to assist whichever class has only one
source, if necessary.

When p,q\textgreater0, finish with P:01,Q:10 and A:04,13. If p=0,
equation(4) forces q,l4,r3,h each to be at least two. Use

\begin{verbatim}
L4:40,43; R3:01,34; O:31; Q:10; A:04,13.
\end{verbatim}

If q=0, the symmetric explicit matching is

\begin{verbatim}
R3:31,34; L4:10,43; O:40; P:01; A:04,13.
\end{verbatim}

The possibility p=q=0 is excluded by total capacity. Hence this target
solves whenever l4,r3≥1 and l4+r3≥3.

Only three count forms remain:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  l4=0 and p,q,r3,h are 3,3,3,2;
\item
  r3=0 and p,q,l4,h are 3,3,3,2;
\item
  l4=r3=1 and p=q=h=3.
\end{enumerate}

These are direct consequences of(4), not an inventory list used as a
premise. All are closed in Section4.

\hypertarget{explicit-loops-finish-all-count-forms}{%
\subsection*{4. Explicit loops finish all count
forms}\label{explicit-loops-finish-all-count-forms}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Explicit loops finish all count
forms}

Two own-loop triples whose pole pairs intersect always assemble:
otherwise each triple must use all three allowed repeated colours;
choose a common repeat and use one cube from each at the bicoloured
corners of the first target. Restoring one universal cube in the first
class's loop matching replaces the reserved cube of the second class if
it was used. This argument is detailed in the high-type note and remains
valid when O is used in a loop.

\hypertarget{the-family-ql3l4h3332}{%
\subsubsection*{The family
q,l3,l4,h=3,3,3,2}\label{the-family-ql3l4h3332}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{The family q,l3,l4,h=3,3,3,2}

A triple H has the loop

\begin{verbatim}
A:03,04; L3:14,24; L4:13,23.
\end{verbatim}

A triple L3 has the loop

\begin{verbatim}
remaining L3:30; O:34; A:14; L4:20; Q:10,24.
\end{verbatim}

A triple L4 has the loop

\begin{verbatim}
remaining L4:43; O:40; A:13; L3:20; Q:10,23.
\end{verbatim}

If h=3, one of L3,L4 is a triple and its poles meet H's poles34. If h=2,
both L3,L4 are triples and their poles04,03 meet. Thus the family solves
in every case.

\hypertarget{the-families-involving-pql4r3h}{%
\subsubsection*{The families involving
P,Q,L4,R3,H}\label{the-families-involving-pql4r3h}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{The families involving P,Q,L4,R3,H}

Whenever p=3 and q≥2, P has the loop

\begin{verbatim}
remaining P:21; O:01; A:03,04; Q:23,24.
\end{verbatim}

Whenever q=3 and p≥2, Q has the loop

\begin{verbatim}
remaining Q:20; O:10; A:13,14; P:23,24.
\end{verbatim}

If p=q=3, these two triples have intersecting poles02,12 and solve
immediately. This includes the third count form above.

In the first count form, if p and q are not both three, then r3=h=3 and
p,q≥2. The H loop is

\begin{verbatim}
A:03,04; P:23,24; Q:13,14,
\end{verbatim}

and the R3 loop is

\begin{verbatim}
remaining R3:34; O:31; A:04; P:01,21; Q:24.
\end{verbatim}

Their pole pairs34 and14 intersect. In the second count form the same
reasoning gives l4=h=3 and p,q≥2. Use the same H loop and

\begin{verbatim}
remaining L4:43; O:40; A:13; Q:10,20; P:23.
\end{verbatim}

The pole pairs34 and03 intersect. Every matching uses distinct physical
sources and respects the minimum counts in its case.

This completes the theorem.

\hypertarget{arg-51}{%
\section{Argument 51: A private adjacent column and a row opposite
source}\label{arg-51}}

\hypertarget{theorem-3}{%
\subsection*{Theorem}\label{theorem-3}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Theorem}

Take three sources of class A=C(01) and eleven other adjacent sources,
each class having multiplicity at most three. Suppose every outsider
avoids02 and12. Adjoin the opposite source

\begin{verbatim}
O={13,14,23,24}=12|34.
\end{verbatim}

The resulting fifteen sources contain an opposite-target assembly.
Exchanging0,1 gives the other labelled row-intersection type.

Together with the high and diagonal notes, this proves the following
uniform statement: three sources of an adjacent class, eleven capped
adjacent outsiders avoiding one of its columns, and any opposite source
avoiding that column always assemble a five-colour target.

\hypertarget{notation-and-the-two-high-target-tests}{%
\subsection*{1. Notation and the two high-target
tests}\label{notation-and-the-two-high-target-tests}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Notation and the two high-target
tests}

Use the eleven outsider classes of \protect\hyperlink{arg-49}{Argument
49}:

\begin{verbatim}
P=C02, Q=C12, L3=C123, L4=C124,
R3=C023, R4=C024, H=C012,
B=C234, Z23=C23, Z24=C24, Z34=C34.
\end{verbatim}

Put x=z23,y=z24,w=z34 and

\begin{verbatim}
L=b+l3+l4+q+x+y,       R=b+p+r3+r4+x+y,
a=b+l3+x+w,            c=b+l4+y+w.
\end{verbatim}

These count the outsider neighbours of20,21,30,40 respectively. All
outsider counts are at most three, and sum to eleven.

The target01\textbar23 assembles exactly when

\begin{verbatim}
L≥1, R≥1, a≥1, L+w≥2.                           (I)
\end{verbatim}

This is the Hall test proved in Section1 of
\protect\hyperlink{arg-50}{Argument 50}: on this target the present O
has exactly the same intersection \{13,31\} as the opposite source in
that note. The proof therefore transfers unchanged.

Exchanging3,4 preserves the present O, and gives the second test:
target01\textbar24 assembles exactly when

\begin{verbatim}
L≥1, R≥1, c≥1, L+w≥2.                           (II)
\end{verbatim}

We will also use the following explicit completion on target

\begin{verbatim}
T={12,14,23,34}.
\end{verbatim}

If r3≥1,r4≥2 and p+r3+r4≥4, fill

\begin{verbatim}
A:12,14; O:32; R3:34; R4:41,43;
another source from P,R3,R4:21;
one Q or H source:23.                            (C)
\end{verbatim}

The unused source at21 exists by the total p+r3+r4≥4, since only three
of those sources have been used. Each of those three classes has
corner21. A Q or H supplies23 independently. If instead r4≥1, r3≥2,
interchange3,4 throughout(C); its last position becomes24, also supplied
by Q or H. All sources in either matching are distinct.

\hypertarget{no-outsider-sees21}{%
\subsection*{2. No outsider sees21}\label{no-outsider-sees21}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. No outsider sees21}

Suppose R=0. Only Q,L3,L4,H,Z34 remain, so

\begin{verbatim}
q+l3+l4+h+w=11.                                (2)
\end{verbatim}

If w\textgreater0, use target01\textbar34, put O at31, and fill the
other reverse positions30,40,41. For w≥3 use three Z34 sources. For w=2,
the inequality l3+l4+w≥5 gives an L3 or L4 source at30 or40; the two Z34
sources fill the other positions. For w=1, it gives l3+l4≥4, so both
classes occur; use L3:30,L4:40,Z34:41. There is an unused forward helper
from Q or H because q+h≥2. It and three A sources fill the forward half.

It remains that w=0. Then q,l3,l4,h sum to eleven, so each is at least
two. The target \{02,04,23,34\} has the explicit assembly

\begin{verbatim}
A:02,04; Q:20,23; L3:34; L4:40,43; O:32.
\end{verbatim}

Thus R=0 always solves.

\hypertarget{no-outsider-sees20}{%
\subsection*{3. No outsider sees20}\label{no-outsider-sees20}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. No outsider sees20}

Suppose L=0. Only P,R3,R4,H,Z34 remain, and

\begin{verbatim}
p+r3+r4+h+w=11.                                (3)
\end{verbatim}

If w≥2, again use target01\textbar34. With w≥3, put O at31 and use three
Z34 sources at30,40,41. If w=2, there is an R3 or R4 source because
r3+r4+w≥5. Use that source at31 or41, O at the other of those two
positions, and two Z34 sources at30,40. In either case p+h≥2 supplies a
forward helper from P or H, which completes the forward half together
with three A sources.

If w≤1, equation(3) implies

\begin{verbatim}
p+r3+r4+h≥10.
\end{verbatim}

All four counts are positive; otherwise the other three have total
capacity nine. Moreover r3+r4≥4 and p+r3+r4≥7. Thus one of r3,r4 is at
least two, and completion(C), or its image under3↔4, works using an H
source at23 or24. This closes L=0.

\hypertarget{both20-and21-are-supported}{%
\subsection*{4. Both20 and21 are
supported}\label{both20-and21-are-supported}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Both20 and21 are supported}

Now L,R≥1. If w\textgreater0, both high-target tests hold. Assume w=0.

\hypertarget{l1}{%
\subsubsection*{L=1}\label{l1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{L=1}

The equation

\begin{verbatim}
b+l3+l4+q+x+y=1
\end{verbatim}

says that exactly one outsider occurs among those six classes. The other
ten outsiders are in P,R3,R4,H. Thus

\begin{verbatim}
p+r3+r4+h=10.
\end{verbatim}

Each of these four counts is positive, r3+r4≥4, and p+r3+r4≥7.
Completion(C), or its3↔4 image, works with an H source. The exceptional
outsider is not needed.

\hypertarget{l2}{%
\subsubsection*{L≥2}\label{l2}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{L≥2}

If both high targets fail, (I) and(II) force a=c=0. Hence

\begin{verbatim}
b=l3=l4=x=y=w=0.
\end{verbatim}

The only outsiders are P,Q,R3,R4,H, with

\begin{verbatim}
p+q+r3+r4+h=11,     q=L≥2.                     (4)
\end{verbatim}

If p≥2, at least one of R3,R4 occurs: otherwise p+q+h≤9. An R4 source
gives target \{01,02,14,24\} by

\begin{verbatim}
Q:10,20; A:02,14; P:01,24; R4:41; O:42.
\end{verbatim}

An R3 source gives the3↔4 image of this matching. Thus p≥2 solves.

If p≤1, equation(4) gives

\begin{verbatim}
r3+r4≥4,     p+r3+r4≥5.
\end{verbatim}

Both R3,R4 are positive, since each count is at most three, and at least
one is at least two. Use completion(C), or its3↔4 image, with one of the
Q sources at23 or24. This solves the last case.

All possible values of L,R,w have been covered, proving the theorem.

\hypertarget{uniform-private-column-consequence}{%
\subsection*{Uniform private-column
consequence}\label{uniform-private-column-consequence}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Uniform private-column consequence}

An opposite source avoiding02 and12 has exactly five possible varieties.
It either omits colour2, giving the three pairings of 0,1,3,4, or omits0
or1, giving one pairing in each case. Their three stabilizer types are:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Type & Representative & Proof \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
High rectangle & \{03,04,13,14\} & \protect\hyperlink{arg-49}{Argument
49} \\
Diagonal pair & \{01,04,13,34\} & \protect\hyperlink{arg-50}{Argument
50} \\
Row pair & \{13,14,23,24\} & This note \\
\end{longtable}

If all of0,1,2 occurred in an opposite four-cycle avoiding both
edges02,12, its two-element bipartition part containing2 would also need
to contain0,1, impossible. This proves the five-variety list directly.

\hypertarget{arg-52}{%
\section{Argument 52: One opposite cube and fourteen adjacent
cubes}\label{arg-52}}

\hypertarget{theorem-4}{%
\subsection*{Theorem}\label{theorem-4}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Theorem}

Any pool consisting of one opposite-repeat five-colour cube and fourteen
adjacent-repeat five-colour cubes contains eight cubes assembling a
five-colour 2×2×2 cube with monochromatic faces.

There is no multiplicity assumption in the statement. The necessary
class bounds arise from the already proved pure fourteen-cube theory.
The proof does not assume the fourteen-failure catalogue or the general
N5=15 theorem.

\hypertarget{elementary-facts-about-a-pure-fourteen-failure}{%
\subsection*{1. Elementary facts about a pure
fourteen-failure}\label{elementary-facts-about-a-pure-fourteen-failure}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Elementary facts about a pure
fourteen-failure}

The previous structural work proves that every failing pool of fourteen
adjacent cubes has the following properties:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Every adjacent tricoloured-corner class has count at most three.
\item
  At most eight classes occur, and at most five classes occur twice or
  more.
\item
  At most one triple class has an own-orientation loop: a matching of
  its six tricoloured corners after two of its cubes are reserved.
\item
  Every triple without such a loop has exactly two private corners,
  forming one column of its two-by-three corner array. Its other four
  corners have an outsider matching.
\item
  If no triple has an own loop, the pool has butterfly support.
\end{enumerate}

The dependency references are \protect\hyperlink{arg-39}{Argument 39},
\protect\hyperlink{arg-33}{Argument 33},
\protect\hyperlink{arg-36}{Argument 36},
\protect\hyperlink{arg-37}{Argument 37},
\protect\hyperlink{arg-32}{Argument 32}, and
\protect\hyperlink{arg-38}{Argument 38}.

The thirteen-source theorem \protect\hyperlink{arg-48}{Argument 48} now
adds:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\setcounter{enumi}{5}
\tightlist
\item
  A pure fourteen-failure has at least two triple classes.
\end{enumerate}

Indeed a pure failure has at least one triple by the previous
concentration and diversity results. If it has exactly one, properties 1
and2 force its counts to be3,2,2,2,2,1,1,1. The new theorem solves the
subpool retaining two of its singleton classes.

\hypertarget{add-the-opposite-cube}{%
\subsection*{2. Add the opposite cube}\label{add-the-opposite-cube}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Add the opposite cube}

Let P be the fourteen adjacent cubes and O the opposite cube. Suppose
P+O fails. Then P is itself a pure fourteen-failure.

If P has no own-loop triple, property5 places it in the butterfly
family. The earlier elementary lemma \protect\hyperlink{arg-34}{Argument
34} says that any opposite cube added to such a failure gives an
assembly. This case is therefore impossible.

Otherwise P has precisely one own-loop triple U. By property6 there is
another triple A, which has no own loop. Property4 gives A a private
column and an outsider matching of its other four corners.

If O contains one of A's two private corners, reserve two A cubes, put O
at that private corner, use the remaining A at the other one, and use
the four outsider matches at the other four corners. Thus A gains an own
loop in P+O. The loop on U persists. The elementary mixed two-own-loop
theorem \protect\hyperlink{arg-47}{Argument 47} excludes two such
triples in a failing fifteen-pool with one opposite source. Hence this
case also assembles.

It remains that O avoids A's whole private column. Normalize A to C(01)
and the column to02,12. The eleven adjacent outsiders all avoid those
two corners by privacy. They have class cap three by property1. An
opposite variety avoiding02,12 has one of exactly five forms, falling
into three geometric types:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Type & Representative edge set & Elementary proof \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
High & \{03,04,13,14\} & \protect\hyperlink{arg-49}{Argument 49} \\
Diagonal & \{01,04,13,34\} & \protect\hyperlink{arg-50}{Argument 50} \\
Row & \{13,14,23,24\} & \protect\hyperlink{arg-51}{Argument 51} \\
\end{longtable}

Those three notes prove that three A sources, the eleven capped adjacent
outsiders and O always assemble. Their proofs use small Hall tests and
explicit source allocations; the high and diagonal types finish with
intersecting own-loop triples.

This excludes the last possibility. Therefore every pool of one opposite
and fourteen adjacent cubes assembles, as claimed.

\hypertarget{arg-53}{%
\section{Argument 53: Eleven adjacent cubes through one directed
corner}\label{arg-53}}

\textbf{Theorem.} Eleven adjacent-repeat cubes whose tricoloured-corner
classes all contain one fixed directed corner assemble a five-colour
target, provided every class has multiplicity at most three.

No opposite sources are required. The argument uses only small
six-corner matchings and the previously proved common-corner lemmas. The
opposite-target failure catalogue is not a premise.

\hypertarget{reduction-to-two-multiplicity-patterns}{%
\subsection*{1. Reduction to two multiplicity
patterns}\label{reduction-to-two-multiplicity-patterns}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Reduction to two multiplicity
patterns}

There are six classes containing a given directed corner. If all six
occur, select nine cubes preserving all six classes and apply
\protect\hyperlink{arg-24}{Argument 24}.

If five classes occur, their positive counts, each at most three and
totalling eleven, are exactly

\begin{verbatim}
3,3,3,1,1;  3,3,2,2,1;  3,2,2,2,2.
\end{verbatim}

The latter two contain a 3,2,2,2,1 subpool and are solved by
\protect\hyperlink{arg-23}{Argument 23}.

If four classes occur, the counts are 3,3,3,2. At most three classes
cannot accommodate eleven cubes. Thus only 3,3,3,2 and 3,3,3,1,1 remain.

We use an \textbf{own loop} to mean a matching of all six tricoloured
corners of a class after reserving two copies of that class. In a
failure, an own-looped triple must use all three of its allowed repeated
colours. Classes \textbf{conflict} when reserving one cube of each with
a common repeated colour permits the six-corner completion of a target
whose two poles the reserved cubes supply. Conflicting classes must have
disjoint repeated-colour sets.

An own-loop class conflicts with every present class whose pole pair
intersects its own. To prove this, restore one of the reserved own class
copies, remove the other class's reserved source, and replace that
source in the matching by the restored universal source if necessary.
This is the usual universal-restoration argument.

\hypertarget{four-classes-of-sizes-3332}{%
\subsection*{2. Four classes of sizes
3,3,3,2}\label{four-classes-of-sizes-3332}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Four classes of sizes 3,3,3,2}

Normalize the common corner to 01. Write Q=\{2,3,4\}, and put

\begin{verbatim}
P_i=C(0i),    D_i=C({0}∪(Q\{i})).
\end{verbatim}

Arbitrary permutations of Q and interchange of the P and D families give
exactly three forms for a four-class support:

\begin{verbatim}
{P4,D2,D3,D4},
{P3,P4,D3,D4},
{P3,P4,D2,D4}.
\end{verbatim}

These correspond to the two missing classes being of one kind, matched
across kinds, or unmatched across kinds.

In each form at least one of the three triples has an own loop.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  For \{P4,D2,D3,D4\}, at least one of D2,D3 is a triple. If D2 is a
  triple, reserve two copies and match

\begin{verbatim}
D2:32; P4:02,42; D4:31; D3:41,01.
\end{verbatim}

  The case of a D3 triple follows by interchanging 2 and 3.
\item
  For \{P3,P4,D3,D4\}, at least one of the pairs \{P3,D4\} and \{P4,D3\}
  consists of two triples. In the former case, reserve two P3 and match

\begin{verbatim}
P3:32; D4:04,31,34; P4:02; D3:01.
\end{verbatim}

  The other pair follows by interchanging 3 and 4.
\item
  For \{P3,P4,D2,D4\}, both P3 and D2 have own loops already when each
  class has only two copies. The explicit completions are in Section 4
  of \protect\hyperlink{arg-35}{Argument 35}:

\begin{verbatim}
reserve two P3: P4:01,02; D2:31,32; D4:04,34;
reserve two D2: P3:02,32; P4:41,42; D4:01,31.
\end{verbatim}

  Since only one class is doubled, at least one of P3,D2 is a triple.
\end{itemize}

Now retain a 3,3,2,2 subpool in which this own-looped class remains a
triple. The common-corner 3,3,2,2 lemma in the same note applies: it
allows the asserted own loop to use any sources of the full pool. Thus
every 3,3,3,2 configuration assembles.

\hypertarget{five-classes-of-sizes-33311}{%
\subsection*{3. Five classes of sizes
3,3,3,1,1}\label{five-classes-of-sizes-33311}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Five classes of sizes 3,3,3,1,1}

Normalize the absent class to D\_i. The present classes are

\begin{verbatim}
A=P_i, B=P_j, C=P_k, E=D_j, F=D_k,
\end{verbatim}

where \{i,j,k\}=\{2,3,4\}. Their poles are 0i,0j,0k,1j,1k, and their
tricoloured corners are:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & Corners \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
A & 01,0j,0k,i1,ij,ik \\
B & 01,0i,0k,j1,ji,jk \\
C & 01,0i,0j,k1,ki,kj \\
E & 01,0j,i1,ij,k1,kj \\
F & 01,0k,i1,ik,j1,jk \\
\end{longtable}

Interchanging j,k leaves exactly six choices of the three triples:

\begin{verbatim}
ABC, ABE, ABF, AEF, BCE, BEF.
\end{verbatim}

The following small matching facts close these six cases.

\hypertarget{own-loop-matchings}{%
\subsubsection*{Own-loop matchings}\label{own-loop-matchings}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Own-loop matchings}

Every row is used after two copies of its target class have been
reserved. The other source multiplicities are as in the specified triple
patterns.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\columnwidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3333}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Looped class
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
When used
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Six-corner completion
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
A & Any pattern containing A as a triple & A:i1; E:ij; F:ik; B:0k; C:0j;
any unused helper:01 \\
B & ABF or BEF & B:ji; F:j1,jk; A:0k; C:0i; E:01 \\
C & BCE & C:ki; E:k1,kj; A:0j; B:0i; F:01 \\
E & ABE or AEF & E:k1; C:kj; A:0j,ij; F:i1; B:01 \\
E & BCE & E:i1; A:ij; C:0j,k1,kj; B:01 \\
F & BEF & F:i1; A:ik; B:0k,j1,jk; C:01 \\
\end{longtable}

The unused helper in the first row exists: the four helper classes
contain eight cubes in total, and only one from each has been used.
Every helper contains 01. All other rows use at most the stated counts
after the two target copies have been reserved.

\hypertarget{the-six-cases}{%
\subsubsection*{The six cases}\label{the-six-cases}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{The six cases}

\textbf{ABC.} The class A has an own loop. It therefore conflicts with B
and C, since all three pole pairs contain 0. The classes B,C also
conflict: after reserving one of each, complete a B target by

\begin{verbatim}
B:ji,j1; F:jk; A:0k,01; C:0i.
\end{verbatim}

Thus A requires three repeated colours and B,C require at least one
each, all distinct and all different from 0. Five colours would have to
fit into the four-element set \{1,2,3,4\}, a contradiction.

\textbf{ABE or AEF.} Both A and E have own loops. They conflict via a B
target. After reserving one A and one E, assign

\begin{verbatim}
A:01,0k; C:0i; B:ji,
\end{verbatim}

and fill j1,jk with two remaining B/F sources. This is possible because
B and F have combined multiplicity four, and only one B has been used.
Each remaining B/F source contains j1,jk. The reserved classes can
supply poles 0,j of B. The two triples would require six different
repeated colours, impossible.

\textbf{ABF.} Both A and B have own loops. Their poles intersect at 0,
so they conflict by restoration. Their two three-element repeated-
colour sets cannot be disjoint.

\textbf{BCE.} Both C and E have own loops. They conflict using a B
target: after reserving one C and one E, match

\begin{verbatim}
B:ji,j1,jk; C:01,0i; A:0k.
\end{verbatim}

The reserved C,E sources supply poles 0,j. Again the two triples would
need six different repeated colours.

\textbf{BEF.} Both B and F have own loops. They conflict using a C
target: after reserving one B and one F, match

\begin{verbatim}
E:0j,k1,kj; B:01,0i; C:ki.
\end{verbatim}

The reserved B,F sources supply poles 0,k. The same six-colour
contradiction applies.

All six triple placements are now covered. This proves the 3,3,3,1,1
case and hence the theorem.

\hypertarget{arg-54}{%
\section{Argument 54: Two opposites and thirteen adjacent cubes in a
butterfly}\label{arg-54}}

\hypertarget{theorem-5}{%
\subsection*{Theorem}\label{theorem-5}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Theorem}

Take thirteen adjacent sources, with at most three in any one
tricoloured-corner class, supported in the butterfly

\begin{verbatim}
A=C01, P=C02, L=C124, M=C123, U=C12, V=C012.
\end{verbatim}

Adding any two opposite sources gives an eight-source five-colour
assembly. The proof uses only the common-corner-eleven lemma and the
explicit assignments below; it does not enumerate inventories.

Write their counts a,p,l,m,u,v. Each is at most three and their sum is
thirteen. An opposite source ab/cd contains both directions of the four
edges between the displayed pairs.

\hypertarget{four-common-corner-counts}{%
\subsection*{1. Four common-corner
counts}\label{four-common-corner-counts}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Four common-corner counts}

The classes A,L,U,V all contain13; A,M,U,V all contain14; P,L,U,V all
contain23; and P,M,U,V all contain24. If one of these four subpools has
eleven sources, it assembles by \protect\hyperlink{arg-53}{Argument 53}.

We may therefore assume

\begin{verbatim}
a+l ≥3,  a+m ≥3,  p+l ≥3,  p+m ≥3.                (1)
\end{verbatim}

For example, a+l≤2 would leave the common-corner24 subpool P,M,U,V with
at least eleven sources. The other three inequalities follow in the same
way.

The butterfly union misses exactly31,32,41,42. Every opposite source
contains at least one of these four corners. Indeed, if a four-cycle
avoided all four undirected edges between\{1,2\} and\{3,4\}, it would
lie in the union of the triangles012 and034. A simple cycle in this
graph must lie in one of its triangle blocks, which cannot contain a
four-cycle.

\hypertarget{all-four-high-classes-present}{%
\subsection*{2. All four high classes
present}\label{all-four-high-classes-present}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. All four high classes present}

Suppose a,p,l,m are all positive. By(1), either a,p≥2 or l,m≥2:
otherwise one of a,p and one of l,m would both equal one, contradicting
the corresponding inequality. Also u+v≥1, since the four high classes
can contain at most twelve sources.

The permutation(1 3)(2 4), followed by reversing all directed cuts,
exchanges the two high pairs\{A,P\} and\{L,M\} and interchanges U,V.
Thus assume l,m≥2. Select one source K from U or V. If an opposite O
contains31, assemble target03/14 by

\begin{verbatim}
O:31;  P:01;  L:40,43;  M:10,34;  A:04;  K:13.   (2)
\end{verbatim}

Both low classes contain13. Interchanging1,2 and interchanging3,4
preserve the count hypotheses and act transitively on the four missing
corners. Consequently(2) covers every opposite source. In this case even
one of the two opposite sources suffices.

\hypertarget{a-high-class-absent}{%
\subsection*{3. A high class absent}\label{a-high-class-absent}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. A high class absent}

The butterfly symmetries act transitively on the four high classes, so
assume a=0. Equations(1) force l=m=3. The count equation gives

\begin{verbatim}
p+u+v=7,  with 1≤p,u,v≤3.                        (3)
\end{verbatim}

If either opposite O contains31, target03/14 has the assignment

\begin{verbatim}
O:31;  P:01;  L:40,43;  M:10,34;  V:04;  U:13.  (4)
\end{verbatim}

If an opposite contains41, interchange3,4. Thus we may suppose both
opposite sources avoid31 and41, equivalently avoid the undirected
edges13 and14.

There are exactly five such opposite varieties:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Name & Opposite variety & Missing butterfly corners it supplies \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
X & 02/34 & 32,42 \\
Y & 03/24 & 32 \\
Z & 04/23 & 42 \\
R & 02/14 & 42 \\
S & 02/13 & 32 \\
\end{longtable}

To see completeness, if colour1 is omitted, the other four colours have
exactly their three pair partitions X,Y,Z. If colour1 is present, its
two neighbours must be0 and2. The other vertex in its bipartition class
is3 or4, giving S or R.

If the two opposite sources can supply32 and42 using different sources,
use target02/34. Assign those two corners to the opposite sources,30 to
M,40 to L, and03,04,23,24 to four P or V sources. This is possible
because p+v=7-u≥4, and both P and V contain all four of these forward
corners.

Consequently the only pairs left are two sources from\{Y,S\}, or two
from\{Z,R\}. Interchanging3,4 exchanges these alternatives. Consider a
pair from\{Y,S\}.

If at least one source is Y, assign it02, assign the other32, and use
target03/24 with

\begin{verbatim}
Y:02;  other opposite:32;
P:04;  M:34;  L:40,43,20;  U:23.                 (5)
\end{verbatim}

Thus only two copies of S remain. If p≥2, target02/13 is

\begin{verbatim}
S:12,32;  P:01,21;  M:10,30;  V:03;  U:23,      (6)
\end{verbatim}

where the two S corners use the two distinct opposite sources. Therefore
the sole residual, up to the stated symmetries, is

\begin{verbatim}
p=1; l=m=u=v=3; a=0; two copies of S=02/13.      (7)
\end{verbatim}

Every reduction to(7) has followed from a count inequality, the five
geometrically determined opposite varieties, or an explicit assembly. No
search result is used.

\hypertarget{repeated-colours-close-the-sole-residual}{%
\subsection*{4. Repeated colours close the sole
residual}\label{repeated-colours-close-the-sole-residual}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. Repeated colours close the sole
residual}

Recall that the pole pairs of M=C123 and V=C012 are04 and34,
respectively. A source of pole pair ab repeating colour r has the two
bicoloured corners(r,a) and(r,b).

The class M has an own loop: after reserving any two of its three
sources, match its six tricoloured corners by

\begin{verbatim}
remaining M:34;  S:30;  P:24;  L:10,20;  U:14.  (8)
\end{verbatim}

Likewise V has an own loop, with

\begin{verbatim}
remaining V:04;  S:03;  P:24;  L:13,23;  M:14.  (9)
\end{verbatim}

These assignments use only one opposite source each. If two M sources
repeated the same colour, reserve them for M's two bicoloured target
corners and use(8). The analogous construction using(9) applies to V.
Therefore, in a failing pool, the M triple must use each repeat
colour1,2,3 once, and the V triple each repeat colour0,1,2 once.

Now choose the M source repeating1 for the bicoloured corner(1,0) and
the V source repeating1 for(1,4). Complete the M target by(8), using an
available M source at34. None of its other assignments uses V, so all
eight assigned sources are distinct. This is an assembly, contradicting
failure.

The theorem follows.

\hypertarget{arg-55}{%
\section{Argument 55: The alternating missing path for
2D+P}\label{arg-55}}

Let

\begin{verbatim}
D=O60={13,14,23,24}, P=O67={01,04,13,34}.
\end{verbatim}

Take two D sources, one P, and eleven adjacent sources of class cap
three, all avoiding the corners32,41,42. The latter directions alternate
along the three-edge path3--2--4--1 in D. We prove assembly.

The eight allowed adjacent classes and their counts are

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Count & Class \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
a & A=C(01) \\
b & B=C(02) \\
c & C=C(124) \\
d & E=C(123) \\
e & F=C(12) \\
f & G=C(023) \\
g & H=C(23) \\
h & J=C(012) \\
\end{longtable}

All counts lie between zero and three and sum to eleven. The class names
are used for physical sources; lower-case letters denote their counts. A
triple class has at most one source disjoint from it in a failure, by
the earlier three-opposite/disjoint-source construction. We also use
\protect\hyperlink{arg-53}{Argument 53}.

Split according to c, the number of C(124) sources.

\hypertarget{the-case-c0}{%
\subsection*{1. The case c=0}\label{the-case-c0}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. The case c=0}

On target O63=\{03,04,23,24\}, put D,D at32,42 and P at40. The five
remaining positions are03,04,23,24,30. The adjacent intersections are
described by the groups

\begin{verbatim}
A:03,04;
B,J:03,04,23,24;
E,H:24,30;
F:23,24;
G:04,24.
\end{verbatim}

These five positions can be matched whenever

\begin{verbatim}
d+g>0, a+b+h>0, b+h+e>0.                 (1)
\end{verbatim}

Here is the Hall verification. Choose one E/H at30. Put X=b+h and Y=d+g.
On the remaining four positions,03 and23 have neighbour counts a+X and
X+e. The pair03,04 has at least a+X+f≥2 neighbours because its
complementary source groups Y,e have capacity at most6+3=9. Similarly
the pair03,23 has at least a+X+e≥2 neighbours because Y,f have capacity
at most nine. All other two-position inequalities follow either from
these or from at least ten minus one class neighbours. The only triple
not seeing all ten remaining sources is03,04,23; it sees11−Y≥5. Thus (1)
suffices.

If (1) holds, we have an opposite assembly. Otherwise consider the three
possible zero sums.

\hypertarget{abh0}{%
\subsubsection*{1.1 a=b=h=0}\label{abh0}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{1.1 a=b=h=0}

Only E,F,G,H remain, with eleven sources. All four classes contain
corner24. The common-corner eleven theorem gives an assembly.

\hypertarget{bhe0}{%
\subsubsection*{1.2 b=h=e=0}\label{bhe0}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{1.2 b=h=e=0}

Only A,E,G,H remain. Their eleven counts have profile3,3,3,2, so each is
at least two. Target O71=\{01,02,14,24\} has the assignment

\begin{verbatim}
D:41,42; P:01; A:02,14; E:10,20; G:24.
\end{verbatim}

\hypertarget{dg0}{%
\subsubsection*{1.3 d=g=0}\label{dg0}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{1.3 d=g=0}

Only A,B,F,G,J remain. If a=0, all present classes contain24. If e=0,
all present classes contain04. Either case is covered by the
common-corner theorem.

Assume a,e\textgreater0. The counts give

\begin{verbatim}
b+f≥2, a+e+h≥5.
\end{verbatim}

Use target O71 with

\begin{verbatim}
D:41,42; P:10; A:02; F:20;
two B/G sources:01,24;
one further A/F/J source:14.
\end{verbatim}

The last source exists after the A,F reservations because at least five
sources lie in those three classes. This completes c=0.

\hypertarget{the-case-c1}{%
\subsection*{2. The case c=1}\label{the-case-c1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. The case c=1}

On O63, D,D again fill32,42. The single C source sees23,40 and P
sees04,40. Thus one of these two sources can fill40 and the other can be
used at either04 or23, according to which source fills40. For matching
purposes the ten remaining adjacent sources therefore have one extra
flexible source on \{04,23\}.

This gives an assembly whenever

\begin{verbatim}
d+g>0, a+b+h>0.                          (2)
\end{verbatim}

Indeed put one E/H at30. On03,04,23,24, the extra source supplies 04
or23. Corner03 has a+b+h\textgreater0 neighbours. Corner24 has at least
nine minus a≥6 adjacent neighbours. The pairs03,04 and03,23 have at
least two neighbours, using the extra source. The only potentially small
triple03,04,23 has at least10−(d+g)≥4 neighbours. All other Hall
inequalities are automatic from these counts. If the extra source is
assigned04, use P there and C at40; if it is assigned23, use C there and
P at40. If it is unused, put either eligible source at40 and discard the
other.

\hypertarget{dg0-1}{%
\subsubsection*{2.1 d=g=0}\label{dg0-1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{2.1 d=g=0}

The other ten adjacent sources are A,B,F,G,J. If a\textgreater0, then
b+f\textgreater0 because the other three classes have capacity nine. On
O71, assign

\begin{verbatim}
D:41,42; P:10; C:20; A:02;
one B/G source:01.
\end{verbatim}

The remaining positions14,24 have respectively at least a+e+h−1≥3 and
b+f+e+h−1≥6 unused neighbours, so distinct helpers fill them.

If a=0, the four counts b,e,f,h sum to ten, so all are positive. Use P
itself as target, with

\begin{verbatim}
D:13,31; P:43; C:40; G:34; F:10; B:01; J:04.
\end{verbatim}

\hypertarget{abh0-1}{%
\subsubsection*{2.2 a=b=h=0}\label{abh0-1}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{2.2 a=b=h=0}

The four counts d,e,f,g sum to ten and are all positive. If f≥2, again
use target P:

\begin{verbatim}
D:13,31; P:43; C:40; G:01,04; F:10; H:34.
\end{verbatim}

If f=1, then d=e=g=3. The pool contains the smaller core

\begin{verbatim}
one D +3C(12)+3C(23)+2C(123).
\end{verbatim}

This core assembles by the nine-source union-class lemma: C(12) and
C(23) have the common pole2, their union class is C(123), and D contains
the edge13 joining their other two poles. The lemma's proof is the main
article's nine-source construction. The singleton C(124) and C(023)
sources are unnecessary.

Thus c=1 is closed.

\hypertarget{the-cases-c2-or3}{%
\subsection*{3. The cases c=2 or3}\label{the-cases-c2-or3}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. The cases c=2 or3}

Use P as target. Put D,D at13,31 and two C sources at40,43. If the other
adjacent sources can match any three positions of

\begin{verbatim}
K={01,04,10,34},
\end{verbatim}

P fills the fourth and the target is complete. Their K patterns are

\begin{verbatim}
A,J:{04}; B:{01,04}; E:{10,34};
F:{10}; G:{01,04,34}; H:{34}.
\end{verbatim}

There are11−c≥8 such sources. If their matching rank is at most two,
König's theorem confines all their patterns to two K positions. A cover
using one corner and one exceptional source would require at least seven
sources at one corner; at most six can be confined to04, and at most
three to any other corner. A cover with only source vertices is also
impossible.

The only two-position sets accommodating eight sources are:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Positions & Eligible classes besides C \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
01,04 & A,B,J \\
04,10 & A,F,J \\
04,34 & A,H,J \\
10,34 & E,F,H \\
\end{longtable}

The other two pairs admit at most one class. In each displayed row all
three classes occur at least twice, so with C the full adjacent profile
is3,3,3,2.

For the second row, all eleven adjacent sources contain13; for the
fourth, all contain20. The common-corner theorem solves both.

The third row is impossible in a failure: A and H are disjoint, both
have at least two copies, and at least one is a triple. If both had only
two, even three each of C,J would give total only ten.

For the first row, use target O61:

\begin{verbatim}
D:32,41; P:34; A:12,14; B:21; C:43; J:23.
\end{verbatim}

All assignments respect the lower bound of two per class. This completes
c≥2 and hence the alternating-path case.

\hypertarget{symmetry-and-scope}{%
\subsection*{Symmetry and scope}\label{symmetry-and-scope}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Symmetry and scope}

The other possible single opposite variety in this normalized
alternating five-position cover is O72=\{02,03,12,13\}. Reversing the
path by (1 3)(2 4) and reversing all directed corners preserves the
absent alternating directions and exchanges O67,O72. It also preserves
D. Thus the proof covers both singles, with source class names permuted
accordingly.

The cut list follows directly from the three forbidden directions. All
count reductions and matching criteria above are proved by Hall and the
class caps; no inventory catalogue is used.

\hypertarget{arg-56}{%
\section{Argument 56: Eliminating four-position Hall covers for
2D+P}\label{arg-56}}

Let the pool contain 2D+P, where D and P are distinct opposite
varieties, and eleven adjacent sources of class multiplicity at most
three. Suppose, for contradiction, that the pool has no assembly.

This note proves that all twelve sources other than D,D have their
D-intersections inside a five-position set H. In particular there is no
four-position cover with one exceptional source. The proof does not
enumerate source inventories.

We use two earlier facts:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  A triple adjacent class has at most one adjacent source disjoint from
  it. Otherwise three copies of the class, two disjoint adjacent sources
  and the three diffuse opposite sources assemble; see Section 3 of
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-40}{Argument 40}.
\item
  Nine adjacent cubes cannot all avoid two underlying edges in a failure
  with an opposite source and class cap three. The short cut proof is in
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-9}{Argument 9}, Section 1, and does not require
  four opposite sources.
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{the-initial-hall-cover}{%
\subsection*{1. The initial Hall cover}\label{the-initial-hall-cover}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. The initial Hall cover}

Use D as target. Its two copies are universal. Hence the other twelve
sources have matching rank at most five on its eight positions. König's
theorem gives a set H of corner vertices and some exceptional source
vertices, with total size at most five, covering all incidences.

Each adjacent class meets D in two or four positions. At most two
classes fit in a two-position set, and at most three fit in a
three-position set. These facts are proved in Section 1 of
\protect\hyperlink{arg-1}{Argument 1}.

Thus \textbar H\textbar≤2 is impossible: at least five of the eleven
adjacent sources would remain exceptional when \textbar H\textbar=2,
exceeding the allowance of three. Smaller H is still less possible. If
\textbar H\textbar=3 and P is exceptional, at most one adjacent source
may be exceptional, whereas ten do not fit in three positions. If P is
contained in H, then H contains a bidirected edge; a three-position set
of that form supports at most two adjacent classes, hence six copies,
again insufficient.

Therefore either \textbar H\textbar=5 and every source is contained in
H, or \textbar H\textbar=4 and at most one source is exceptional. We
exclude the latter case. At least ten adjacent sources then have their
D-intersections in H. If P is exceptional, all eleven adjacent sources
are contained.

Normalize D=\{13,14,23,24\}. An edge denotes both directions unless
individual corners are listed.

\hypertarget{the-ten-four-position-shapes}{%
\subsection*{2. The ten four-position
shapes}\label{the-ten-four-position-shapes}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. The ten four-position shapes}

Up to symmetries of the target and reversal of all directions, four
positions in a bidirected four-cycle have ten shapes. To see
completeness, count complete bidirected edges in H. There are two forms
with two such edges, four forms with one, and four orientations of the
entire four-cycle with none. The adjacent classes whose D-intersection
lies in H are as follows; C(S) means S→Sᶜ.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
H & Eligible outsets S \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
13,14,23,24 & 01;02;124;123;12;012 \\
13,14,23,31 & 01;124 \\
13,14,23,32 & 01;124;13;013 \\
13,14,23,42 & 01;124;14;014 \\
13,14,31,32 & 01;03;13;013 \\
13,14,31,41 & 01;234 \\
13,14,31,42 & 01;14;014 \\
13,14,32,42 & 01;134;13;14;014;013 \\
13,24,31,42 & 14;023;23;014 \\
13,24,32,41 & none \\
\end{longtable}

Each row follows directly by imposing the missing directed-cut
conditions on a two- or three-element outset. Rows with at most three
classes cannot accommodate ten contained adjacent sources. The ninth row
makes at least ten sources avoid the other two underlying target edges,
contradicting the nine-avoider fact.

The fourth and eighth rows contain no complete bidirected edge, so P is
exceptional and all eleven adjacent sources are contained. Their classes
share respectively corner 13 and corner 12. The common-corner
eleven-source theorem in \protect\hyperlink{arg-53}{Argument 53} solves
them.

The first, third and fifth rows remain.

\hypertarget{a-directed-rectangle-containing-all-eleven-adjacent-sources}{%
\subsection*{3. A directed rectangle containing all eleven adjacent
sources}\label{a-directed-rectangle-containing-all-eleven-adjacent-sources}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. A directed rectangle containing all
eleven adjacent sources}

In the first row P is exceptional. Write

\begin{verbatim}
A=C(01), B=C(02), C=C(124), E=C(123),
U=C(12), V=C(012),
\end{verbatim}

with counts a,b,c,e,u,v, each at most three and summing to eleven. We
prove that these sources and D,D assemble; P is unnecessary.

\hypertarget{all-four-star-classes-occur}{%
\subsubsection*{3.1 All four star classes
occur}\label{all-four-star-classes-occur}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{3.1 All four star classes occur}

If a,b,c,e are positive, use target O61=\{12,14,23,34\}. Put D,D at
41,32, and use

\begin{verbatim}
A:12; B:21; C:43; E:34.
\end{verbatim}

Two unused adjacent sources fill 14,23. Among the unused sources, at
least a+e+u+v−2≥3 see 14, and at least b+c+u+v−2≥3 see 23. Two distinct
sources can therefore be chosen.

\hypertarget{some-star-class-is-absent}{%
\subsubsection*{3.2 Some star class is
absent}\label{some-star-class-is-absent}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{3.2 Some star class is absent}

The symmetries interchanging 1,2, interchanging 3,4, and reversing
directions while interchanging the two parts of D act transitively on
A,B,C,E. Assume a=0.
