If c,e\textgreater0, use target O63=\{03,04,23,24\}. Put D,D at32,42, C
at40, and E at30. The B/V sources are universal on 03,04,23,24. Their
number satisfies b+v≥2, since c+e+u≤9 and the total is eleven. Use two
of them at03,04. The remaining adjacent neighbours of23 number at least
b+v+c+u−3=8−e≥5; those of24 number at least b+v+e+u−3=8−c≥5. Thus two
further distinct sources fill23,24.

Otherwise one of C,E is absent as well. There cannot be three absent
stars, since the three remaining classes would have total capacity nine.
We are left with one A/B star, one C/E star, and U,V. All four counts
are at least two and exactly three are three. Normalize the support to
A=C(01), C=C(124), U=C(12), V=C(012).

\hypertarget{the-four-class-residual}{%
\subsubsection*{3.3 The four-class
residual}\label{the-four-class-residual}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{3.3 The four-class residual}

Both U and V have own loops already when all four counts are two:

\begin{verbatim}
reserve two U: A:13,14; C:10,20; V:23,24;
reserve two V: A:03,04; C:13,23; U:14,24.
\end{verbatim}

The own-loop restoration argument gives the conflict edges A--U and
C--V, whose pole pairs intersect. Three further edges have the following
completions, after the indicated pair is reserved:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Reserved pair & Target class & Tricoloured completion \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
A,V & C & C:40,43; A:13; U:10,20; V:23 \\
C,U & A & A:02,12; V:03,04; C:13; U:14 \\
U,V & C(14) & C:40,43; A:12,13; D:42; U:10 \\
\end{longtable}

If U,V are both triples, their conflicting three-element repeat sets
would require six colours. If U is doubled, V is a triple and the
conflict triangle U,V,A requires at least 2+3+1=6 colours. If V is
doubled, use the triangle U,V,C instead. These exhaust the possible
positions of the unique double. Thus the first row is completely
excluded.

\hypertarget{the-row-h13142332}{%
\subsection*{4. The row H=\{13,14,23,32\}}\label{the-row-h13142332}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. The row H=\{13,14,23,32\}}

The contained classes are

\begin{verbatim}
A=C(01), B=C(124), C=C(13), E=C(013).
\end{verbatim}

The classes B,E are disjoint. If all eleven adjacent sources were
contained, their counts would be 3,3,3,2. At least one of B,E would then
be a triple with at least two disjoint sources, impossible.

Consequently P is contained and ten adjacent sources are contained.
Retain ten if necessary. All four contained classes occur, since any
three have capacity nine. The only possible P are

\begin{verbatim}
O64={02,04,23,34}, O73={01,03,12,23}.
\end{verbatim}

For P=O64, use target O63 and assign

\begin{verbatim}
D:24,42; P:23; A:03; B:40; C:30.
\end{verbatim}

The remaining positions04,32 are filled by two remaining sources from
A,C,E. At least a+c+e−2=8−b≥5 such sources remain. Each meets 04 or32,
and E supplies both, so a two-position matching exists.

For P=O73, use target O61 and assign

\begin{verbatim}
D:23,41; P:21; B:43;
four C/E sources:12,14,32,34.
\end{verbatim}

The C/E sources are universal on those four positions and c+e≥4, because
a+b≤6. Both possibilities therefore assemble.

\hypertarget{the-row-h13143132}{%
\subsection*{5. The row H=\{13,14,31,32\}}\label{the-row-h13143132}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. The row H=\{13,14,31,32\}}

The contained classes are

\begin{verbatim}
A=C(01), B=C(03), C=C(13), E=C(013).
\end{verbatim}

\hypertarget{p-is-contained}{%
\subsubsection*{5.1 P is contained}\label{p-is-contained}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{5.1 P is contained}

The only choices are P=O67=\{01,04,13,34\} or P=O72=\{02,03,12,13\}. At
least ten adjacent sources are contained; retain ten. All four classes
are positive.

For P=O67, target O66=\{03,04,13,14\} has the assignment

\begin{verbatim}
P:40; D:13,41; A:03; B:31; C:30.
\end{verbatim}

The two remaining positions04,14 are filled by unused adjacent sources.
Seven remain, each meeting04 or14, and E supplies both. Thus two
distinct sources suffice.

For P=O72 and c≥2, use target O73=\{01,03,12,23\}:

\begin{verbatim}
P:21; D:23,32; A:03; B:01; C:10,30; E:12.
\end{verbatim}

If c=1, the other three counts are all three. Use target
O74=\{01,02,13,23\} instead:

\begin{verbatim}
P:20; D:23,31; C:10; B:01; A:02,13; E:32.
\end{verbatim}

\hypertarget{p-is-exceptional}{%
\subsubsection*{5.2 P is exceptional}\label{p-is-exceptional}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{5.2 P is exceptional}

Now all eleven adjacent sources are contained, with counts3,3,3,2.
Whenever A is a triple it has an own loop, using

\begin{verbatim}
A:03; D:13; B:02,04; C:12,14
\end{verbatim}

after two A reservations. Likewise a B triple has an own loop via

\begin{verbatim}
B:01; D:31; A:02,04; C:32,34.
\end{verbatim}

The class E has an own loop already at counts two in the other classes,
using A:02,04; B:32,34; C:12,14.

If A and B are both triples, their own loops conflict at the common
pole0 and give an assembly. Otherwise the unique double is one of A,B;
the other and E are both looped triples. Their conflict edges are
witnessed as follows:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Reserved pair & Target & Tricoloured completion \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
A,E & C(034) & B:01,31; A:02; D:41,42; C:32 \\
B,E & C(012) & A:03,13; B:04; D:23,24; C:14 \\
\end{longtable}

Again two conflicting triple classes would require six repeated colours,
impossible.

Every four-position cover is now excluded. By Section 1, failure forces
a five-position set H containing the D-intersections of P and of all
eleven adjacent sources. Equivalently, three target positions are absent
from P and the entire adjacent pool.

\hypertarget{arg-57}{%
\section{Argument 57: Five-position covers with a missing bidirected
edge}\label{arg-57}}

Keep the setting of \protect\hyperlink{arg-56}{Argument 56}: the pool
consists of 2D+P, D≠P, and eleven adjacent sources with class cap three.
In a hypothetical failure, every adjacent triple has at most one
adjacent source disjoint from it. The preceding note reduces failure to
a five-position set H containing the D-intersections of P and all eleven
adjacent sources.

This note closes the cases where the three omitted D positions include
both directions of an edge. It follows in particular that the whole
neighbouring pair case is solved.

Normalize D=\{13,14,23,24\}. The omitted set consists of a bidirected
edge and a further directed corner. There are exactly two geometries,
according as their underlying edges meet or are disjoint.

\hypertarget{the-two-edges-meet}{%
\subsection*{1. The two edges meet}\label{the-two-edges-meet}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. The two edges meet}

Normalize the omitted corners to24,42,41. The containing set is

\begin{verbatim}
H={13,14,23,31,32}.
\end{verbatim}

The adjacent cuts avoiding those three corners are exactly

\begin{verbatim}
A=C(01), B=C(03), C=C(124), E=C(13), F=C(013).
\end{verbatim}

Write their counts as a,b,c,e,f.~They sum to eleven and are at most
three. The class C is disjoint from B and F.

If c=3, the disjoint-source bound gives b+f≤1, whence the total is at
most3+3+3+1=10. Thus c≤2. If c=0, all eleven adjacent sources have their
D-intersections in \{13,14,31,32\}; Section 5.2 of
\protect\hyperlink{arg-56}{Argument 56} gives an assembly using D,D
alone.

Suppose c\textgreater0. All five classes occur. For c=1, the other four
counts sum to ten, forcing all four positive. For c=2, neither B nor F
may be a triple, since either would have two disjoint C sources. Hence
b,f≤2. Omitting B or F would give total at most 3+3+2+2=10; omitting A
or E gives an even smaller bound. Again all four other classes are
positive.

Use target O66=\{03,04,13,14\}, with

\begin{verbatim}
D:13,41; A:03; B:31; C:40; E:30.
\end{verbatim}

Two more adjacent sources fill04,14. At least

\begin{verbatim}
a+b+e+f−3 = 8−c ≥ 6
\end{verbatim}

unused sources from A,B,E,F remain, and each meets04 or14. A remaining F
sees both, so two distinct sources can fill the pair. This proves the
case. P is unnecessary in the displayed assembly.

\hypertarget{the-two-edges-are-disjoint}{%
\subsection*{2. The two edges are
disjoint}\label{the-two-edges-are-disjoint}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. The two edges are disjoint}

Normalize the omitted corners to24,42,31, so

\begin{verbatim}
H={13,14,23,32,41}.
\end{verbatim}

The eligible cuts are

\begin{verbatim}
A=C(01), B=C(124), C=C(13),
E=C(024), F=C(24), G=C(013).
\end{verbatim}

Let their counts be a,b,c,e,f,g, with sum eleven and cap three. Put

\begin{verbatim}
x=c+g, y=e+f, L=a+x, R=b+y.
\end{verbatim}

The class F is disjoint from every class in the left group \{A,C,G\},
while G is disjoint from every class in the right group \{B,E,F\}. We
first show

\begin{verbatim}
x>0, y>0, L≥3, R≥3.
\end{verbatim}

If x=0, only A,B,E,F remain. Their eleven cubes have counts3,3,3,2. The
disjoint classes A,F each have at least two cubes and at least one is a
triple, contradicting the disjoint-source bound. Similarly, y=0 leaves
A,B,C,G and the disjoint pair B,G, giving a contradiction.

If L≤2, then R≥9. The three right classes have cap three, so R=9, L=2
and b=e=f=3. The F triple has two disjoint left sources, again
impossible. Interchanging the roles gives R≥3 as well: if R≤2, then
a=c=g=3 and the G triple has two disjoint right sources.

Now use target O61=\{12,14,23,34\}. Its adjacent intersections split
into two four-position halves:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Source group & Available positions \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
A & 12,14 \\
C,G & 12,14,32,34 \\
B & 23,43 \\
E,F & 21,23,41,43 \\
\end{longtable}

Put the two D copies at32,41. Since x\textgreater0 and L≥3, choose one
C/G source at34 and two further A/C/G sources at12,14. Since
y\textgreater0 and R≥3, choose one E/F source at21 and two further B/E/F
sources at23,43. These eight assignments are compatible and use distinct
physical cubes. Again P is unnecessary.

Both missing-bidirected-edge geometries therefore assemble.

\hypertarget{consequence-the-neighbouring-profile-21-is-closed}{%
\subsection*{3. Consequence: the neighbouring profile 2,1 is
closed}\label{consequence-the-neighbouring-profile-21-is-closed}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Consequence: the neighbouring
profile 2,1 is closed}

If D and P share two underlying edges, their D-intersection consists of
two complete bidirected edges. A five-position containing set H must
contain those four positions. Its three-position complement therefore
consists of one complete bidirected edge and one further directed
corner. Sections 1 and 2 exclude it.

Combining with \protect\hyperlink{arg-56}{Argument 56} proves:

\begin{quote}
Two copies of one opposite variety D and one copy of a distinct
neighbouring variety P, together with eleven adjacent cubes of class cap
three, always assemble a five-colour target.
\end{quote}

For a nonneighbouring P, the only cases still left after these
reductions have three omitted corners on three distinct underlying
edges. These edges form the three-edge path complementary to D∩P inside
D. Under symmetries of D, the three omitted directions have three
patterns: monotone, one change, and alternating. In using this
normalization one must retain both possible choices of P sharing the
remaining edge: path reversal need not preserve the marked single
variety P. Equivalently, after fixing the ordered pair D,P, direction
reversal leaves four oriented path cases, with the two one-change forms
distinguished.

\hypertarget{arg-58}{%
\section{Argument 58: The one-change missing path for
2D+P}\label{arg-58}}

Normalize D=\{13,14,23,24\}. Suppose the eleven adjacent sources, each
class of multiplicity at most three, avoid24,31,41. These directions
have one change along the three-edge path2--4--1--3.

The eligible classes, with their counts, are

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Count & Class \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
a & A=C(01) \\
b & B=C(134) \\
c & C=C(124) \\
e & E=C(13) \\
f & F=C(14) \\
g & G=C(014) \\
h & H=C(013) \\
\end{longtable}

The opposite sources are D,D,P. Containment in the corresponding
five-position D-cover forces P to be one of

\begin{verbatim}
O64={02,04,23,34}, O73={01,03,12,23}.
\end{verbatim}

Both choices are treated separately, so no symmetry of the marked single
variety is assumed. We use the common-corner eleven-source theorem in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-53}{Argument 53}.

\hypertarget{po64}{%
\subsection*{1. P=O64}\label{po64}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. P=O64}

In target P, the two D copies can fill23,32. Consider its other six
positions

\begin{verbatim}
R={02,04,20,34,40,43}.
\end{verbatim}

If the adjacent sources match any five of R, use P at the sixth and the
two D copies at23,32. Thus failure implies adjacent matching rank at
most four on R. The adjacent patterns on R are

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & R pattern \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
A & 02,04 \\
B & 40 \\
C & 20,40,43 \\
E & 34 \\
F & 40,43 \\
G & 02,43 \\
H & 02,04,34 \\
\end{longtable}

Any one, two or three positions of R contain patterns from at most one,
two or three of these classes, respectively. For the only nontrivial
bound, a three-position set containing C's pattern admits just B,C,F,
while one containing H's admits just A,E,H. Otherwise C,H are excluded.
Among A,F,G, the pairs A,F have four positions in union; A,G have three
but contain neither singleton position; F,G have three and contain only
B's singleton position. Hence a three-position set still admits at most
three classes.

König's theorem now shows that all eleven patterns lie in a
four-position set. Indeed a cover with k≤3 corner vertices and at
most4−k source vertices covers at most3k+(4−k)≤10 copies.

Equivalently, two R positions are absent from the entire adjacent pool.
The only such absences leaving capacity at least eleven are the six rows
below. Every row has four eligible classes, necessarily with
profile3,3,3,2.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Missing R positions & Eligible classes \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
04,20 & B,E,F,G \\
20,34 & A,B,F,G \\
20,40 & A,E,G,H \\
20,43 & A,B,E,H \\
02,04 & B,C,E,F \\
04,34 & B,C,F,G \\
\end{longtable}

This table follows directly from the seven patterns above: every other
missing pair leaves at most three eligible classes.

The fifth row has all eleven sources through corner10, so the
common-corner theorem solves it. Each other row admits target
O63=\{03,04,23,24\} with the explicit assignments below. Every adjacent
class is used at most twice, within its guaranteed count.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Eligible classes & Eight-corner assignment \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
B,E,F,G & G:03; P:04; D:23,24; B:40,42; E:30,32 \\
A,B,F,G & A:03,04; P:23; D:24,42; B:30,32; F:40 \\
A,E,G,H & A:03,04; P:40; D:23,24; E:30,32; G:42 \\
A,B,E,H & A:03,04; P:23; D:24,42; B:32,40; E:30 \\
B,C,F,G & G:03; P:04; D:24,42; B:30,32; C:23,40 \\
\end{longtable}

Thus P=O64 always assembles.

\hypertarget{po73}{%
\subsection*{2. P=O73}\label{po73}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. P=O73}

Use target O61=\{12,14,23,34\}. Put P at21 and one D at41. The remaining
D source sees14,23,32. The adjacent intersections on the other six
positions are

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Source group & Positions \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
A & 12,14 \\
B & 12,32 \\
C & 23,43 \\
E,H & 12,14,32,34 \\
F,G & 12,43 \\
\end{longtable}

Put x=e+h and y=f+g. This target assembles whenever

\begin{verbatim}
x>0 and c+y>0.                            (1)
\end{verbatim}

For clarity, these are sufficient by the following full Hall check. Any
set containing12 has at least11−c≥8 adjacent neighbours. For sets inside
\{14,23,32,34,43\}, corner34 has x neighbours and corner43 has c+y. The
remaining D source meets14,23,32. The only additional inequalities not
immediate from (1) and the total count are

\begin{verbatim}
a+b+x≥2, a+c+x≥2, b+c+x≥2.
\end{verbatim}

These hold because their respective complementary groups c+y, b+y,a+y
have capacity at most3+6=9, while the total is eleven. All four-position
sets have at least12−y≥6 neighbours or at least12 minus one class; the
five-position set meets all sources. Thus every Hall inequality holds.

If c+y=0, only A,B,E,H occur, and all their sources contain12. The
common-corner theorem applies.

It remains x=0, so E,H are absent. The five possible classes are
A,B,C,F,G, with total eleven. If b=0, all present classes contain13. If
c=0, all contain12. Again the common-corner theorem applies.

Assume b,c\textgreater0. Since b+c+f≤9, there are at least two A/G
sources. Use target O72=\{02,03,12,13\}, with

\begin{verbatim}
D:13,31; P:21; C:20; B:30;
two A/G sources:02,03;
one further A/B/F/G source:12.
\end{verbatim}

The last source exists: the four indicated classes have11−c≥8 copies,
and only three have been used. Every such class contains12. This
completes the P=O73 case.

Both one-change configurations are therefore soluble, by explicit
geometric and count arguments rather than inventory enumeration.

\hypertarget{arg-59}{%
\section{Argument 59: The star-and-triangle family with two opposite
cubes}\label{arg-59}}

\textbf{Theorem.} Take two arbitrary opposite-repeat cubes, with no
restriction on whether their varieties coincide, and thirteen
adjacent-repeat cubes. Suppose every adjacent tricoloured-corner class
occurs at most three times, and all adjacent classes belong to

\begin{verbatim}
C(01), C(02), C(03), C(04), C(034), C(024), C(014).
\end{verbatim}

Then the pool contains an eight-cube assembly of a five-colour target.
The same statement holds after any colour permutation or simultaneous
reversal of all directed corners.

The proof uses the common-corner eleven-source theorem from
\protect\hyperlink{arg-53}{Argument 53} and the nine-source construction
in the main article's nine-source construction. All other steps are
direct constructions or a six-position Hall argument. No inventory
search is a premise.

\hypertarget{notation-and-the-common-corner-reduction}{%
\subsection*{1. Notation and the common-corner
reduction}\label{notation-and-the-common-corner-reduction}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Notation and the common-corner
reduction}

Let I=\{1,2,3\}, and write

\begin{verbatim}
P_i=C(0i), R=C(04), Q_i=C(04i)    (i in I).
\end{verbatim}

Their multiplicities are p\_i,r,q\_i, respectively. Each lies between
zero and three, and

\begin{verbatim}
p_1+p_2+p_3+r+q_1+q_2+q_3=13.
\end{verbatim}

Put s\_i=p\_i+q\_i and p=p\_1+p\_2+p\_3. The two opposite sources are
O\_1,O\_2. An opposite source supplies both directions of every edge of
its four-cycle. We write \{a,b\}\textbar\{c,d\} for the opposite target
whose underlying edges join these two pairs.

Every class except P\_i,Q\_i supplies corner0i. Consequently s\_i≤2
would give at least eleven adjacent sources with common corner0i, which
assemble by the common-corner theorem. For the remainder, suppose the
pool fails. Then

\begin{verbatim}
s_i≥3 for every i in I.                              (1)
\end{verbatim}

Also p≥1, because the four classes R,Q\_1,Q\_2,Q\_3 have total capacity
twelve.

\hypertarget{a-positive-class-and-an-opposite-edge}{%
\subsection*{2. A positive class and an opposite
edge}\label{a-positive-class-and-an-opposite-edge}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. A positive class and an opposite
edge}

\textbf{Lemma.} Under (1), suppose p\_j\textgreater0 and an opposite
source O supplies edge4k, where j,k are distinct members of I. Then the
pool assembles.

Let i be the third member of I. Use target \{4,i\}\textbar\{j,k\}. Put
one P\_j source atj4 and O atk4. The other six target positions are

\begin{verbatim}
ij, ik, ji, ki, 4j, 4k.
\end{verbatim}

They will be filled entirely by the twelve remaining adjacent sources.
Define the three disjoint source groups

\begin{verbatim}
G_i = all P_i and Q_i sources;
G_j = all P_j and Q_j sources after the P_j reservation;
G_k = all P_k and Q_k sources.
\end{verbatim}

Their sizes are s\_i,s\_j−1,s\_k, hence at least3,2,3, respectively. The
complete neighbourhoods of the six positions are

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Positions & Adjacent-source neighbourhood after the reservation \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
ij, ik & G\_i \\
ji & G\_j \\
ki & G\_k \\
4j & R, Q\_i, Q\_k \\
4k & R, Q\_i, Q\_j \\
\end{longtable}

Here a class name denotes all its available copies. In particular, the
reservation of P\_j changes neither of the last two rows.

We check Hall's condition for every subset of these six positions. Call
ij,ik,ji,ki the first four positions and 4j,4k the last two.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  A subset containing no first position consists of one or both last
  positions. Each last position has at least one neighbour: for example,
  if r=q\_i=q\_k=0, only P\_1,P\_2,P\_3,Q\_j could occur, whose total
  capacity twelve contradicts the original count thirteen. The two last
  positions together have

\begin{verbatim}
r+q_1+q_2+q_3=13−p≥4
\end{verbatim}

  neighbours.
\item
  If the subset meets at least two of the three first-position groups
  \{ij,ik\}, \{ji\}, \{ki\}, their disjoint source groups alone supply
  at least five neighbours. A subset meeting exactly two groups has at
  most five positions. A subset meeting all three groups has at most six
  positions and at least3+2+3=8 neighbours.
\item
  Suppose the subset meets only \{ij,ik\} among the first-position
  groups. If it uses at most one last position, it has at most three
  positions and at least s\_i≥3 neighbours. If it uses both last
  positions, its neighbour count is

\begin{verbatim}
s_i+r+q_j+q_k=13−p_j−p_k≥7.
\end{verbatim}
\item
  Suppose the subset meets only \{ji\}. With at most one last position
  it has at most two positions and at least s\_j−1≥2 neighbours. With
  both last positions it has

\begin{verbatim}
(s_j−1)+r+q_i+q_k=12−p_i−p_k≥6
\end{verbatim}

  neighbours. If it meets only \{ki\}, the same argument uses s\_k≥3
  and, when both last positions occur,

\begin{verbatim}
s_k+r+q_i+q_j=13−p_i−p_j≥7.
\end{verbatim}
\end{itemize}

This exhausts the subsets. The six-position matching, together with the
two reserved sources, proves the lemma.

For any opposite source O put

\begin{verbatim}
N_4(O)={k in I : O supplies edge4k}.
\end{verbatim}

Since p≥1, the lemma implies the following restriction in a failure:
either N\_4(O) is empty, or it is a singleton \{s\} and every positive
P\_j is P\_s. Indeed, two members of N\_4(O) would give a member
different from any chosen j with p\_j\textgreater0. Moreover N\_4(O) is
empty exactly when O omits colour4. If N\_4(O)=\{s\}, the two neighbours
of4 in O are0,s.

\hypertarget{two-different-non-4-neighbours-of0}{%
\subsection*{3. Two different non-4 neighbours
of0}\label{two-different-non-4-neighbours-of0}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Two different non-4 neighbours of0}

\textbf{Lemma.} Under (1), suppose O\_1,O\_2 can be assigned to
edges0i,0k for two distinct i,k in I. Then the pool assembles.

Let j be the third member of I and use target \{0,j\}\textbar\{i,k\}.
Put the opposite sources ati0,k0. The remaining six positions are filled
as follows:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Sources & Assigned corners \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Two sources from P\_j,Q\_j & ji, jk \\
Two sources from P\_i,Q\_i & ij, 0k \\
Two sources from P\_k,Q\_k & kj, 0i \\
\end{longtable}

Every source in a row supplies both listed corners. Each row has at
least three sources by (1), so all six chosen sources are distinct. This
proves the lemma.

Now apply both lemmas to a hypothetical failure. An opposite source
omitting4 has two neighbours of0 in I. Any other opposite source allowed
by Section 2 either also omits4, or has neighbours0,s at4 and therefore
has a further neighbour t in I at0. In either case the two sources can
be assigned to distinct edges0i,0k. Thus neither opposite source can
omit4 in a failure.

Both N\_4(O\_1),N\_4(O\_2) are therefore singletons. Since some P\_j
occurs, they must have the same member s, and only P\_s can occur. Each
opposite has a further neighbour t in I at0. These two neighbours must
coincide, by the second lemma. Also t≠s, since the four-cycle has
edges04,4s and cannot close using only three vertices. Both opposite
sources are consequently the same four-cycle

\begin{verbatim}
O={04,4s,st,t0}.                                      (2)
\end{verbatim}

Let u be the third member of I.

\hypertarget{the-last-multiplicity-reduction}{%
\subsection*{4. The last multiplicity
reduction}\label{the-last-multiplicity-reduction}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. The last multiplicity reduction}

We have p\_t=p\_u=0. Since s\_t,s\_u≥3 and each class has cap three,

\begin{verbatim}
q_t=q_u=3.
\end{verbatim}

The remaining count equation is

\begin{verbatim}
p_s+r+q_s=7,      1≤p_s≤3, r≤3, q_s≤3.               (3)
\end{verbatim}

In particular q\_s≥1.

If p\_s≥2, use the opposite target \{0,s\}\textbar\{4,t\}. There is the
explicit assembly

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Sources & Assigned corners \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
The two O sources & 40, t0 \\
Two P\_s & 04, s4 \\
One Q\_t & ts \\
One Q\_s & st \\
Two Q\_u & 0t, 4s \\
\end{longtable}

The eight corners are distinct and are exactly those of the target. The
indicated source multiplicities are available by (3), and every
incidence follows directly from the relevant cut or from (2).

Therefore a failure would require p\_s=1. Equation (3) then forces
r=q\_s=3. The whole pool has the form

\begin{verbatim}
2O + P_s + 3R + 3Q_s + 3Q_t + 3Q_u,                 (4)
\end{verbatim}

with O given by (2).

\hypertarget{the-repeated-colour-completion}{%
\subsection*{5. The repeated-colour
completion}\label{the-repeated-colour-completion}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{5. The repeated-colour completion}

The nine-source subpool

\begin{verbatim}
O + 3Q_s + 3Q_t + 2R
\end{verbatim}

already assembles. Indeed, O supplies edgest. Simultaneous reversal
sends

\begin{verbatim}
Q_s=C(04s) to C(tu),
Q_t=C(04t) to C(su),
R=C(04)   to C(stu),
\end{verbatim}

and leaves the bidirected corners of O unchanged. Thus the reversed
subpool is precisely the previously proved nine-source construction

\begin{verbatim}
O(edge st) + 3C(tu) + 3C(su) + 2C(stu).
\end{verbatim}

This contradicts failure in (4) and proves the theorem. Equivalently,
Q\_s and Q\_t are own-looped triples with intersecting pole pairs tu and
su, so their repeated colours give the completion.

\hypertarget{proof-dependencies-and-scope}{%
\subsection*{6. Proof dependencies and
scope}\label{proof-dependencies-and-scope}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{6. Proof dependencies and scope}

The proof is uniform in the two original opposite varieties and in all
actual repeated colours within every adjacent class. It assumes only the
cap of three on adjacent tricoloured-corner classes.

Its two earlier inputs are:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  the theorem that eleven adjacent sources with a common directed
  tricoloured corner and class cap three assemble;
\item
  the nine-source union-class construction, including simultaneous
  reversal.
\end{enumerate}

Both inputs have independently audited hand proofs. The two new matching
lemmas and the multiplicity reduction above have passed independent root
and alternative-agent mathematical audits. The latter is recorded in the
proof-development record. No exhaustive search or new formalisation is
used.

\hypertarget{arg-60}{%
\section{Argument 60: Three opposite cubes with one doubled
variety}\label{arg-60}}

\textbf{Theorem.} Let D,P be distinct opposite-repeat five-colour
varieties. Two copies of D, one of P, and eleven adjacent-repeat cubes
of tricoloured-corner class multiplicity at most three contain an
eight-cube assembly of a five-colour target.

This note joins the component hand proofs. The proof does not use the
exhaustive fourteen-cube failure catalogue.

\hypertarget{reduction-to-a-five-position-cover-of-d}{%
\subsection*{1. Reduction to a five-position cover of
D}\label{reduction-to-a-five-position-cover-of-d}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Reduction to a five-position cover
of D}

Suppose the pool fails. A triple adjacent class has at most one adjacent
source disjoint from it: the earlier construction using three diffuse
opposite sources, the triple and two disjoint sources would otherwise
assemble.

On target D its two copies are universal, so the other twelve sources
have matching rank at most five. The argument in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-56}{Argument 56} excludes every König cover with
fewer than five corner vertices, including the four-corner cover with
one exceptional source. Consequently all twelve D-intersections lie in a
five-position set H.

If the complement of H contains a complete bidirected edge,
\protect\hyperlink{arg-57}{Argument 57} gives an assembly. This already
settles neighbouring varieties D,P: their four-position intersection is
two complete edges, so a five-position H containing it necessarily has a
missing complete edge.

\hypertarget{the-remaining-three-oriented-path-forms}{%
\subsection*{2. The remaining three oriented-path
forms}\label{the-remaining-three-oriented-path-forms}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. The remaining three oriented-path
forms}

Only the nonneighbouring case remains. The complement of H consists of
three positions on distinct underlying target edges. These three edges
form the path obtained by deleting the common edge D∩P from D. The cut
restrictions have three geometric forms, up to target symmetries and
simultaneous reversal of directions.

Normalize D=\{13,14,23,24\}. The following table records the three
forms, with every possible single P retained. Thus reversal of the path
is not incorrectly assumed to preserve the marked variety P.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Form & Absent D corners & Eligible adjacent outsets & Possible P \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Monotone & 24,32,41 & 01;124;14;014 & O67,O72 \\
One change & 24,31,41 & 01;134;124;13;14;014;013 & O64,O73 \\
Alternating & 32,41,42 & 01;02;124;123;12;023;23;012 & O67,O72 \\
\end{longtable}

The cut lists follow by imposing that each absent direction u→v forbids
u in the outset with v outside it. The possible opposite singles are
obtained by closing a four-cycle meeting D only in the remaining
bidirected edge. There are exactly two choices per edge, listed above.

In the monotone row, all eleven adjacent sources contain corner13. The
elementary theorem in \protect\hyperlink{arg-53}{Argument 53} therefore
gives an assembly without using any opposite source.

Both singles in the one-change row are covered separately by
\protect\hyperlink{arg-58}{Argument 58}.

The alternating row is covered by \protect\hyperlink{arg-55}{Argument
55}. Its proof handles P=O67. The colour permutation (1 3)(2 4),
followed by reversing all directions, preserves that absent alternating
pattern and D, and exchanges O67 with O72. It therefore covers the other
single as well.

All possible five-position covers have now been excluded. This proves
the theorem.

\hypertarget{dependency-and-status}{%
\subsection*{3. Dependency and status}\label{dependency-and-status}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Dependency and status}

The proof uses the earlier three-opposite/disjoint-source construction,
the two- and three-position cut bounds, the nine-avoider observation,
and the following new component notes:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-53}{Argument 53};
\item
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-56}{Argument 56};
\item
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-57}{Argument 57};
\item
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-58}{Argument 58};
\item
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-55}{Argument 55}.
\end{itemize}

The last alternating count configuration uses the independent
nine-source construction in the main article's nine-source construction.

\hypertarget{arg-61}{%
\section{Argument 61: Three distinct opposite sources when an edge is
absent from the adjacent pool}\label{arg-61}}

\textbf{Lemma.} Three pairwise distinct opposite varieties, together
with eleven adjacent sources whose tricoloured-corner class counts are
at most three, contain an opposite assembly if the adjacent sources all
avoid both directions of any one underlying edge.

There is no condition on whether the opposite varieties contain the
avoided edge. The proof uses no inventory enumeration.

Normalize the avoided edge to 01. Its adjacent avoiders are exactly

\begin{verbatim}
X=C(01), X_i=C(01i), i=2,3,4,
\end{verbatim}

and their reverses. Call these the forward and backward groups. On the
forward grid

\begin{verbatim}
02 03 04
12 13 14,
\end{verbatim}

X has all six positions, while X\_i omits column i and contains both
other columns. An opposite source has either a two-column rectangle or
two positions in different columns; its pattern determines its variety
uniquely.

\hypertarget{three-opposite-sources-and-one-full-column-helper}{%
\subsection*{1. Three opposite sources and one full-column
helper}\label{three-opposite-sources-and-one-full-column-helper}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Three opposite sources and one
full-column helper}

\textbf{Grid lemma.} Add to the three distinct opposite grid patterns a
helper B whose grid pattern is the full grid or a two-column rectangle.
These four sources match a two-column rectangle, unless the three
opposite patterns are precisely the three pairs on three positions, one
in each column.

The full-grid matching rank is four. Source-side Hall is immediate for
subsets containing B, whose pattern has at least four positions. Three
distinct opposite patterns have union at least three, and every one has
size at least two.

Suppose no rectangle can be matched. Let M be the rank-four transversal
matroid on the six positions, and let N be its rank-two dual. The pairs
of holes that can be omitted are the bases of N. Every same-column pair
is a nonbasis. Write H for the nonloop set of N. Alternating paths from
the two holes give

\begin{verbatim}
|N_sources(H)|=|H|−2.
\end{verbatim}

For \textbar H\textbar=2, no source meets H. But its two positions
belong to different columns, since they form a basis of N; B cannot
avoid two positions in different columns. For \textbar H\textbar=4, B
meets H, leaving two opposite sources whose patterns are contained in
the other two positions. They would be the same variety. For
\textbar H\textbar=5, one source would have its pattern in the single
remaining position. These three sizes are impossible.

Suppose \textbar H\textbar=6. Every column's two positions are parallel
in N. The parallel classes are either the three columns separately, or
two columns together and the third separately.

In the three-class case, a two-position opposite pattern would be a
basis of N, so its complement would be matchable by all four sources
while containing no position for that opposite source. Hence all three
opposite patterns must be the three distinct rectangles. On any
rectangle, one of them and B can be taken universal by choosing B's
rectangle if B is not full; the other two opposite sources each occupy a
different whole column. They match the four positions directly, a
contradiction.

In the two-class case, let V be the single-column parallel class and U
the other four positions. Dual rank gives r\_M(V)=1. There are no M
loops, because N has no coloops: each of its parallel classes has at
least two positions. Thus both positions of V are supported, and
precisely one source R meets V, containing both. If R is opposite, it is
a rectangle; B then avoids V and equals U. If R=B, the other three
sources are opposite patterns inside U. In either case all four
restrictions to U have size at least two, their union is U, and no three
restrictions lie in two positions. For the last assertion, the opposite
two-position patterns are distinct and use different columns, whereas
the restriction of a rectangle crossing V is a single column. Hall
therefore matches U, again a contradiction.

The only remaining size is \textbar H\textbar=3. B meets H, so all three
opposite patterns lie in its three-position complement. A three-position
shape in two columns contains only two possible opposite patterns. Thus
the complement has one position in each column, and the three opposite
patterns are its three pairs. This proves the grid lemma. These three
varieties form a line.

\hypertarget{a-forward-pool-with-two-copies-of-each-class}{%
\subsection*{2. A forward pool with two copies of each
class}\label{a-forward-pool-with-two-copies-of-each-class}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. A forward pool with two copies of
each class}

\textbf{Allocation lemma.} Three distinct opposite sources together with
two copies of each of X,X\_2,X\_3,X\_4 contain an opposite assembly.

Among the three opposite sources choose two, U,V, and an endpoint a of
01 such that U and V can supply incoming corners ia and ja for different
i,j in \{2,3,4\}. Here is why this is always possible.

Two distinct opposite varieties fail this selection only when one omits
0 and the other omits 1. Otherwise they share an endpoint a. If either
has two neighbours of a among 2,3,4, distinct i,j can be chosen. If both
have only one such neighbour at both endpoints, both contain 01. Their
neighbours of 0 and 1 outside 01 determine the whole four-cycle, so two
distinct varieties differ at one of these endpoints. That endpoint gives
different i,j. Three distinct varieties cannot have every pair of the
exceptional omission form.

Let k be the remaining colour in \{2,3,4\}, and use the opposite target
with parts \{a,k\} and \{i,j\}. Put U,V at ia,ja. Its other six
positions are filled by

\begin{verbatim}
X: ai,aj;  X_k: ki,kj;  X_i: ik;  X_j: jk.
\end{verbatim}

Every required class count is at most two. This proves the lemma.

\hypertarget{both-orientations-occur}{%
\subsection*{3. Both orientations occur}\label{both-orientations-occur}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Both orientations occur}

Choose the majority orientation as forward. It contains at least six
sources. On every high target with parts 01 and two of 2,3,4, four
forward sources match the whole forward rectangle. Each source meets
either both columns or one full column, and at most three forward
sources miss a specified column. Both columns therefore have at least
three source neighbours; Hall gives the four matches.

Choose one backward source B and identify its reverse grid with the
forward grid. If the grid lemma gives a four-source rectangle matching,
combine its three opposite sources and B on the backward half with four
forward sources on the forward half. This assembles an opposite target.

It remains that the three opposite patterns are the three pairs on one
position in each column.

\hypertarget{at-least-two-backward-sources}{%
\subsubsection*{At least two backward
sources}\label{at-least-two-backward-sources}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{At least two backward sources}

Choose any two of them. Each has both positions in at least two columns.
Choose different columns i,j that they respectively meet, and consider
the rectangle on i,j. Put the two backward sources in the positions
complementary to the line's distinguished position in each column. Two
distinct line sources match the two distinguished positions, because
each position belongs to two of the three line patterns. Four forward
sources fill the other directed half.

This uses only two opposite sources and six adjacent sources.

\hypertarget{exactly-one-backward-source}{%
\subsubsection*{Exactly one backward
source}\label{exactly-one-backward-source}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Exactly one backward source}

There are ten forward sources. All four forward classes occur, since
three classes have capacity nine. Write their counts as
x,x\_2,x\_3,x\_4. If x=1, then x\_2=x\_3=x\_4=3; otherwise x≥2.

Up to permuting columns and interchanging the two endpoints, the three
distinguished positions have two possible shapes.

If all lie in one row, normalize them to 12,13,14. The three opposite
sources are the line of four-cycles on 1,2,3,4. Choose k with x\_k≥2,
and let i,j be the other two column colours. Two line sources can fill
i1,j1 on target \{1,k\}\textbar\{i,j\}. The remaining six positions have
the allocation in Section 2. If x≥2, use it as written. If x=1, use an
extra X\_j for 1i and an extra X\_i for 1j instead of the two X sources;
both classes have count three.

For the mixed-row shape, normalize the distinguished positions to
02,13,14. The opposite varieties are

\begin{verbatim}
O60={13,14,23,24},
O71={01,02,14,24},
O74={01,02,13,23}.
\end{verbatim}

Use target O60. The opposite sources fill

\begin{verbatim}
O74:31; O71:41; O60:23.
\end{verbatim}

The adjacent sources fill

\begin{verbatim}
X:13,14; X_2:24; X_3:32; X_4:42.
\end{verbatim}

If x=1, replace the two X assignments by an extra X\_4 at13 and an extra
X\_3 at14. Both classes then use two of their three copies. Thus this
line shape is solved as well.

The sole backward source need not be used in these last constructions.

\hypertarget{all-sources-have-one-orientation}{%
\subsection*{4. All sources have one
orientation}\label{all-sources-have-one-orientation}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. All sources have one orientation}

There are eleven forward sources in four classes of cap three, so every
class has at least two copies. The allocation lemma of Section 2 applies
directly.

All orientation cases have been covered, proving the stated
global-zero-edge lemma.

\hypertarget{arg-62}{%
\section{Argument 62: Three distinct high opposite sources and a
one-sided rectangle family}\label{arg-62}}

\textbf{Lemma.} Suppose three distinct opposite varieties each meet at
least two underlying edges of T=01\textbar23. Suppose eleven adjacent
sources, with class cap three, have all their T-corners in the forward
half 01→23. Then the pool assembles a five-colour target.

The union of the opposite sources need not cover all edges of T. This
stronger scope is useful for the unsupported common-target case as well.

The six possible adjacent classes are

\begin{verbatim}
A=C(01), B=C(014),
P=C(04), Q=C(14), R=C(012), S=C(013).
\end{verbatim}

Write their counts as a,b,p,q,r,s. Their sum is eleven and each count is
at most three. The list follows directly by intersecting each cut with
the square T, or by choosing the side of the fifth colour after
prescribing the four square vertices.

We use two previously proved lemmas:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-61}{Argument 61}: three distinct opposite
  sources and eleven capped adjacent sources avoiding an underlying edge
  assemble;
\item
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-53}{Argument 53}: eleven capped adjacent
  sources sharing one directed corner assemble.
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{only-three-or-four-peripheral-classes-need-consideration}{%
\subsection*{1. Only three or four peripheral classes need
consideration}\label{only-three-or-four-peripheral-classes-need-consideration}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Only three or four peripheral
classes need consideration}

Only P,Q contain the underlying edge 01, and only R,S contain 23. If
either pair of classes is absent, the global-zero-edge lemma applies.
Thus at least one class from each pair occurs.

If there is just one from each pair, all occupied classes share a
directed corner of T. For example A,B,P,R all contain03; the other
choices are obtained by interchanging 0,1 or 2,3. The common-corner
lemma applies.

It remains that three or four of P,Q,R,S occur. The symmetries
interchanging 0,1, interchanging 2,3, and reversing all directions while
exchanging the two parts of T allow the three-class case to be
normalized to p,q,r\textgreater0 and s=0.

\hypertarget{all-four-peripheral-classes-occur}{%
\subsection*{2. All four peripheral classes
occur}\label{all-four-peripheral-classes-occur}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. All four peripheral classes occur}

Partition the four edges of T into the two disjoint pairs

\begin{verbatim}
E={02,13}, F={03,12}.
\end{verbatim}

Three distinct high opposite varieties collectively cover at least three
edges of T: a fixed two-edge intersection determines its opposite
variety uniquely, and the variety T itself covers all four. Hence at
least one of E,F is entirely supported by the three sources.

Any two target edges have at least two opposite-source neighbours, since
at most one of the three distinct high varieties can have its whole
intersection in the complementary pair. Therefore two opposite cubes can
fill the two reverse directions on the supported pair. Use E, say, so
these positions are20,31, and take target

\begin{verbatim}
U={01,02,13,23}.
\end{verbatim}

Put P at01, Q at10, R at23, and S at32. The only positions left
are02,13. Every one of the seven remaining adjacent sources meets this
pair: A,B meet both; P,S meet02; Q,R meet13. At least three remaining
sources meet each position. Indeed at most six sources belong to the two
classes missing that position, and at most two supporting sources have
just been used. Thus two distinct remaining sources fill02,13.

If F is the supported pair, use target \{01,03,12,23\}. The same four
peripheral assignments fill01,10,23,32, and two remaining sources
fill03,12. Their class incidences are A,B on both, P,R on03, and Q,S
on12. The same count proves the allocation.

Thus four peripheral classes always give an opposite assembly.

\hypertarget{exactly-three-peripheral-classes-occur}{%
\subsection*{3. Exactly three peripheral classes
occur}\label{exactly-three-peripheral-classes-occur}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. Exactly three peripheral classes
occur}

Normalize p,q,r\textgreater0 and s=0. Since p+q+r≤9, we have a+b≥2. Two
actual sources from A,B can therefore fill either pair E or F in its
forward direction.

\hypertarget{an-opposite-source-contains-edge-23}{%
\subsubsection*{3.1 An opposite source contains edge
23}\label{an-opposite-source-contains-edge-23}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{3.1 An opposite source contains
edge 23}

Among the varieties high on T, those containing23 are exactly

\begin{verbatim}
W_E={01,02,13,23}, W_F={01,03,12,23}.
\end{verbatim}

Suppose W\_F occurs; the other case is symmetric in E,F. Reserve that
source at32. Each of the two other opposite varieties meets E, because
W\_F is the unique high variety whose entire T-intersection is F. If the
other two can match the two edges of E, put them at 20,31 and use target
W\_E.

Otherwise both have exactly the same singleton intersection with E. As
their high patterns are distinct, they are the two adjacent edge pairs
joining that singleton to the two different edges of F. They therefore
match the two edges of F. Put them in the reverse directions30,21, and
use target W\_F instead.

In either case the three opposite sources fill the reverse directions of
E or F and the corner32. The adjacent sources P:01, Q:10, R:23, together
with two A,B sources on the forward pair, complete the target. All eight
sources are distinct.

\hypertarget{no-opposite-source-contains-edge-23}{%
\subsubsection*{3.2 No opposite source contains edge
23}\label{no-opposite-source-contains-edge-23}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{3.2 No opposite source contains
edge 23}

The high varieties avoiding23 are the following five:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Variety & Underlying edges & Meets03? & Meets13? \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
01 & 23 & 02,03,12,13 & yes \\
14 & 23 & 12,13,24,34 & no \\
04 & 23 & 02,03,24,34 & yes \\
01 & 34 & 03,04,13,14 & yes \\
01 & 24 & 02,04,12,14 & no \\
\end{longtable}

Among three distinct rows, at least one meets03, at least one meets13,
and at least two meet their union. Thus two distinct opposite sources
fill30,31 on target T'=01\textbar34.

Put P at41 and Q at40. Four forward positions remain:

\begin{verbatim}
03,04,13,14.
\end{verbatim}

The A and R sources contain this whole rectangle. Moreover a+r≥2, since
the complementary three counts b,p,q total at most nine. Put two A,R
sources at04,14. Of the seven adjacent sources still unused, at least
five can fill03 and at least five can fill13: only Q misses03 and only P
misses13, and at most three sources supporting either position have
already been used. Equivalently, the two neighbour counts are11−q−3
and11−p−3, each at least five. Choose distinct sources for03,13.

This uses two opposite and six adjacent sources and assembles T'.

All support cases are covered, proving the lemma. The only finite table
is the five high varieties avoiding one specified diagonal; it is
immediate from the six possible two-edge intersections with T.

\hypertarget{arg-63}{%
\section{Argument 63: Two opposite cubes and thirteen adjacent cubes in
the two-star family}\label{arg-63}}

\textbf{Theorem.} Let thirteen adjacent sources, each class occurring at
most three times, belong to

\begin{verbatim}
C(01), C(02), C(03), C(04), C(034), C(024), C(023).
\end{verbatim}

Together with any two opposite sources, which may have the same variety,
they contain a five-colour assembly.

Put A=C(01). Every other listed class contains the directed corner01. If
at most two A sources occur, at least eleven adjacent sources share01,
and the common-corner eleven-source lemma applies. Hence it suffices to
prove the following stronger special statement:

\begin{quote}
Three A sources, ten capped adjacent sources containing01, and any two
opposite sources contain an opposite-target assembly.
\end{quote}

Write Q=\{2,3,4\} and name the six common-corner classes

\begin{verbatim}
P_i=C(0i), D_i=C({0}∪(Q\{i})), for i∈Q.
\end{verbatim}

The ten-source common-corner pool will be called B. Its class cap is
three. All constructions below use only tricoloured corners, so its
repeated colours are irrelevant.

\hypertarget{an-opposite-source-omits-colour0}{%
\subsection*{1. An opposite source omits
colour0}\label{an-opposite-source-omits-colour0}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. An opposite source omits colour0}

Use that source's own target, whose parts are \{1,i\} and \{j,k\}, where
\{i,j,k\}=Q. Two A copies fill1j,1k. The other six target positions form
J, and the B intersections with J are:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Class & Positions in J \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
P\_i & ij,ik \\
P\_j & j1,ji \\
P\_k & k1,ki \\
D\_i & j1,ji,k1,ki \\
D\_j & ij,k1 \\
D\_k & ik,j1 \\
\end{longtable}

The five two-position patterns are the edges of the path

\begin{verbatim}
ji — j1 — ik — ij — k1 — ki.
\end{verbatim}

The four-position pattern D\_i consists of the first two and last two
positions of this path.

The ten B sources have matching rank at least five on J. To prove this,
suppose a Konig cover has at most four vertices. Four corner vertices
contain at most three of the six patterns: if they contain D\_i they are
exactly its four positions and contain only the two end path edges
besides it; otherwise four vertices of a path contain at most three
edges. Their class capacity is therefore at most nine. Three corner
vertices contain at most two path edges, hence six copies, and at most
one extra source vertex is allowed. Two corner vertices contain at most
one edge, hence three copies, with at most two extra source vertices.
One or no corner vertices contain no complete source pattern. Every case
fails to cover ten sources.

Match five B sources to five positions of J. The opposite source is
universal on its own target and fills the remaining position. With the
two A copies this is an assembly. The second opposite source and the
third A copy are unnecessary in this case.

\hypertarget{two-different-incoming-corners-at0}{%
\subsection*{2. Two different incoming corners
at0}\label{two-different-incoming-corners-at0}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. Two different incoming corners at0}

Now suppose neither opposite source omits0. If they can supply incoming
corners i0,j0 for distinct i,j∈Q, choose those assignments on the target
with parts01 and ij. Let k be the third colour of Q.

The B sources can fill i1,j1 and one position from \{0i,0j\}, using
three distinct sources. This is a three-requirement Hall matching:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  i1 is supplied by P\_i,D\_j,D\_k. Its complementary three classes have
  total capacity nine, so at least one of the ten sources supplies it.
  The same holds for j1.
\item
  At least seven sources meet \{0i,0j\}, since only D\_k misses both.
\item
  At least seven sources meet \{i1,j1\}, since only P\_k misses both.
\item
  Each pair consisting of the forward requirement and one incoming
  requirement meets every B source, as does the full three-set.
\end{itemize}

Thus all Hall inequalities hold. After those three B assignments, three
A copies fill the other three positions of the forward rectangle.
Together with the two opposite assignments i0,j0, this assembles the
target.

\hypertarget{the-remaining-opposite-source-shape}{%
\subsection*{3. The remaining opposite-source
shape}\label{the-remaining-opposite-source-shape}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. The remaining opposite-source
shape}

Both opposite sources contain0. Each has at least one neighbour of0 in
Q, because it has degree two and only one other possible neighbour is1.
If Section 2 does not apply, their neighbour sets in Q are both the same
singleton \{i\}. Thus both contain the edges01 and0i. This includes
identical opposite varieties.

Let j,k be the other two colours of Q. Consider either target

\begin{verbatim}
T_j={0,j}|{1,i},   T_k={0,k}|{1,i}.
\end{verbatim}

On T\_j, put the two opposite sources at10,i0 and two A sources at
0i,1j. The remaining positions are01,j1,ji,ij. Write the B counts as

\begin{verbatim}
a=g(P_i), b=g(P_j), c=g(P_k),
d=g(D_i), e=g(D_j), f=g(D_k),
\end{verbatim}

with a+b+c+d+e+f=10 and each count at most three. The three nonuniversal
remaining positions have neighbourhood counts

\begin{verbatim}
ji: b+d;  ij: a+e;  j1: b+d+f.
\end{verbatim}

Their matching exists exactly when

\begin{verbatim}
b+d≥1,  a+e≥1,  b+d+f≥2.                   (J)
\end{verbatim}

The pair \{ji,j1\} gives the last inequality; the other Hall
inequalities follow from the first two and the disjointness of the class
groups supplying ij and ji,j1. After these three sources are used, any
unused B source fills01.

Likewise T\_k assembles if

\begin{verbatim}
c+d≥1,  a+f≥1,  c+d+e≥2.                   (K)
\end{verbatim}

Conditions (J) and (K) cannot both fail:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  If b+d=0, then b=d=0. Capacity gives c\textgreater0, since a+e+f≤9.
  Also a+f≥4 and c+e≥4, because each complementary pair of classes has
  capacity six. Thus (K) holds.
\item
  If a+e=0, then a=e=0. Now f\textgreater0, since b+c+d≤9, and c+d≥4,
  since b+f≤6. Again (K) holds.
\item
  Otherwise b+d≥1 and a+e≥1. Failure of the third condition in (J)
  forces b+d=1 and f=0. The remaining counts satisfy a+c+e=9, so
  a=c=e=3. Every condition in (K) follows.
\end{itemize}

Hence one of T\_j,T\_k assembles. Sections 1--3 exhaust every pair of
opposite varieties, proving the stronger special statement and therefore
the two-star-family theorem.

\hypertarget{arg-64}{%
\section{Argument 64: Three distinct opposite sources with an
unsupported common target}\label{arg-64}}

\textbf{Lemma.} The opposite sources

\begin{verbatim}
P=O60={13,14,23,24},
Q=O64={02,04,23,34},
R=O68={01,03,14,34},
\end{verbatim}

together with eleven adjacent sources of class cap three always contain
a five-colour assembly.

These three varieties have the common high target T=O61=\{12,14,23,34\},
with edge12 absent from all three sources. They meet the other three
edges in their three different pairs.

The proof uses the already audited global-zero-edge lemma in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-61}{Argument 61} and the common-corner
eleven-source lemma in \protect\hyperlink{arg-53}{Argument 53}. Its
finite tables classify only small Hall corner sets against three fixed
targets; no source-inventory search is a premise.

\hypertarget{reduction-to-one-absent-direction-on-12}{%
\subsection*{1. Reduction to one absent direction on
12}\label{reduction-to-one-absent-direction-on-12}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. Reduction to one absent direction
on 12}

Suppose the pool fails. No underlying edge is absent from the whole
adjacent pool, by the global-zero-edge lemma.

On any opposite target, eleven adjacent sources of class cap three have
matching rank at least four. If their rank is at most four, all their
target intersections lie in four positions. Indeed a Konig cover of size
four with three corner vertices contains at most nine copies and at most
one extra source, hence at most ten. With two corner vertices it covers
at most six copies and two extras; with at most one corner vertex it
cannot cover eleven sources, each of which meets at least two positions.
Thus a rank at most four cover must consist of four corner vertices
alone.

If the adjacent pool supplies both directions of12, its union on T has
at least five positions: those two, and at least one on each of the
other three underlying edges. Its rank on T is therefore at least five.
Match the two12 positions first, using distinct adjacent sources, and
extend that independent position set to a five-position matching. This
is ordinary matching augmentation.

The three remaining positions lie on edges14,23,34. The three opposite
sources fill any three such positions. Every one of these edges has two
opposite neighbours; every pair of edges has all three neighbours. These
are all Hall inequalities for three positions. Thus T assembles, a
contradiction.

Consequently one direction of12 is absent from every adjacent source.
Reverse all directed corners if necessary, so the absent corner is12.
The fourteen possible adjacent outsets are

\begin{verbatim}
234,02,03,124,04,123,12,034,024,023,23,24,34,012.
\end{verbatim}

An outset S denotes the class C(S)=S→Sᶜ.

\hypertarget{the-hall-inequalities-to-be-retained}{%
\subsection*{2. The Hall inequalities to be
retained}\label{the-hall-inequalities-to-be-retained}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. The Hall inequalities to be
retained}

Use P,Q,R themselves as candidate targets. On each, its own source is
universal and the other two sources meet one distinct underlying edge
each. Denote those two edges by G\_1,G\_2 and the other two by F. For a
set Z of target positions, the number of opposite neighbours is

\begin{verbatim}
a(Z)=1+#{i : Z meets G_i}.
\end{verbatim}

Let D(Z) be the adjacent classes, among the fourteen allowed above,
which avoid Z. A Hall obstruction requires

\begin{verbatim}
number of adjacent sources outside D(Z) ≤ k(Z),
k(Z)=|Z|−1−a(Z).
\end{verbatim}

We may discard an obstruction of either of these kinds:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  k=0 and Z contains both directions of an edge: all adjacent sources
  would avoid that edge, and the global-zero-edge lemma applies.
\item
  k≤2 and Z contains both directions of two underlying edges: at least
  nine adjacent sources would avoid both edges. This already gives an
  assembly by the elementary nine-avoider lemma in
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-9}{Argument 9}, Section 1.
\end{itemize}

We may also discard k=0 whenever every class in D(Z) shares a corner, by
the common-corner eleven-source lemma. Finally any row with
3\textbar D(Z)\textbar+k\textless11 is impossible by the class cap.

\hypertarget{why-only-three-small-shapes-remain}{%
\subsubsection*{Why only three small shapes
remain}\label{why-only-three-small-shapes-remain}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Why only three small shapes remain}

All Hall sets of size at least five satisfy their inequalities. For five
positions with all three opposite neighbours, the three- position
complement contains at most three adjacent classes, hence at most nine
cubes: at least two adjacent neighbours remain. If there are only two
opposite neighbours, the complement contains both directions of the
missed G\_i edge and at most one further position; at most two adjacent
classes fit there. For six positions, at most two classes fit in the
complement, while at least two opposite sources meet the set. Sets of
seven or eight positions meet every adjacent source. These bounds
suffice in each case.

For sets of at most four positions, the formula for a(Z) leaves only
these undominated possibilities after the two discard rules:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Two positions, one on each edge of F, with k=0.
\item
  Three of the four positions on F, with k=1.
\item
  One position on each edge of F and both directions of one G\_i, with
  k=1.
\end{enumerate}

To check this directly, a three-position k=0 set either contains a
bidirected edge or contains a pair from item 1. A four-position set
meeting only one G\_i either contains a triple from item 2 or has the
shape in item 3; if its F positions are a whole edge, the two-edge
discard rule applies. A four-position k=0 set either contains a
bidirected edge or a pair from item 1. The full four- position F set is
discarded by the nine-avoider lemma. Sets with negative k give no
obstruction.

For P the two F edges are13,24; for Q they are02,04. Intersecting the
fourteen allowed cuts with the three listed shapes gives the following
complete reduced tables. All unlisted candidates in these shapes have at
most three avoiding classes, except the k=0 pair \{31,24\} for P, whose
four avoiders all contain43, and the k=0 pair \{20,04\} for Q, whose
four avoiders all contain41. Those two exceptions are discarded by the
common-corner lemma.

\hypertarget{the-two-hall-tables}{%
\subsection*{3. The two Hall tables}\label{the-two-hall-tables}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. The two Hall tables}

\hypertarget{target-p}{%
\subsubsection*{Target P}\label{target-p}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Target P}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llrl@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Row & Z & k & D(Z), written as outsets \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
a & 31,42 & 0 & 02,124,123,12,024,24,012 \\
a' & 13,24 & 0 & 234,03,04,034,024,24,34 \\
b & 13,42 & 0 & 234,02,03,123,024,023,23,24 \\
c & 13,31,42 & 1 & 02,123,024,24 \\
c' & 13,24,42 & 1 & 234,03,024,24 \\
d & 13,23,32,42 & 1 & 234,123,023,23 \\
d' & 13,14,41,42 & 1 & 02,03,023,23 \\
\end{longtable}

\hypertarget{target-q}{%
\subsubsection*{Target Q}\label{target-q}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Target Q}

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llrl@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Row & Z & k & D(Z), written as outsets \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
A & 02,40 & 0 & 02,123,12,024,023,23,012 \\
B & 20,40 & 0 & 02,03,04,034,024,023,012 \\
C & 02,04 & 0 & 234,124,123,12,024,23,24,34 \\
D & 02,20,40 & 1 & 02,024,023,012 \\
E & 02,04,40 & 1 & 123,12,024,23 \\
\end{longtable}

Let sigma interchange colours1,2, interchange3,4, and reverse every
directed corner. It preserves P, exchanges Q,R, and preserves the absent
direction12. On outsets its action is

\begin{verbatim}
sigma(S) = complement of (1 2)(3 4)(S).
\end{verbatim}

Therefore the R table is exactly the sigma-image of the Q table. For
completeness its avoiding sets are:

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lrl@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
R row & k & Avoiding outsets \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
sigma(A) & 0 & 234,03,034,023,23,24,34 \\
sigma(B) & 0 & 234,124,123,12,23,24,34 \\
sigma(C) & 0 & 02,03,04,034,024,023,24,012 \\
sigma(D) & 1 & 234,23,24,34 \\
sigma(E) & 1 & 03,034,023,24 \\
\end{longtable}

\hypertarget{the-three-targets-cannot-all-fail}{%
\subsection*{4. The three targets cannot all
fail}\label{the-three-targets-cannot-all-fail}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. The three targets cannot all fail}

Choose one obstruction row for each target and write their avoiding sets
as D\_P,D\_Q,D\_R and their bounds as k\_P,k\_Q,k\_R. Put

\begin{verbatim}
I=D_P∩D_Q∩D_R,  k=k_P+k_Q+k_R.
\end{verbatim}

Every adjacent source outside I lies outside at least one of the three
avoiding sets. Hence at most k sources lie outside I, and the class cap
gives

\begin{verbatim}
11 ≤ 3|I|+k.                                      (1)
\end{verbatim}

The displayed sets have the intersection bound

\begin{verbatim}
|I| ≤ 4−k.                                       (2)
\end{verbatim}

Here is the complete small intersection check. For each Q row and R row,
the entry (m\_0,m\_1) gives the maximum intersection size with a P row
of k\_P=0 and of k\_P=1, respectively. It is obtained simply by
intersecting the displayed finite sets; no multiplicities occur in this
table.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}llllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Q row / R row & sigma(A) & sigma(B) & sigma(C) & sigma(D) & sigma(E) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
A & (2,2) & (2,2) & (3,2) & (1,1) & (1,1) \\
B & (2,2) & (0,0) & (4,3) & (0,0) & (2,2) \\
C & (3,2) & (4,3) & (2,2) & (3,2) & (1,1) \\
D & (1,1) & (0,0) & (3,2) & (0,0) & (1,1) \\
E & (1,1) & (2,2) & (1,1) & (1,1) & (0,0) \\
\end{longtable}

Rows A,B,C and their images have bound zero; D,E and their images have
bound one. The table proves (2). If k≥1, equations (1),(2) give
11≤12−2k≤10, a contradiction.

Thus all three selected rows have k=0. To hold eleven cubes, I must have
four classes. The only entries permitting this are (B,sigma(C)) and
(C,sigma(B)). In the first case the P row must be a' or b, and the
intersections are respectively

\begin{verbatim}
{03,04,034,024},   {02,03,024,023}.
\end{verbatim}

Every class in either set contains01. The second case is the
sigma-image: its four-class intersections are

\begin{verbatim}
{124,123,12,24},   {234,123,23,24},
\end{verbatim}

and all these classes contain20. In either case all eleven adjacent
sources share a corner and already assemble by the common-corner lemma.
This is the final contradiction.

Consequently P,Q,R cannot all fail after the preceding solved cases have
been removed. The unsupported triad therefore assembles.

\hypertarget{arg-65}{%
\section{Argument 65: Three distinct opposite cubes and eleven adjacent
cubes}\label{arg-65}}

\textbf{Theorem.} Three pairwise distinct opposite-repeat five-colour
cubes, together with eleven adjacent-repeat cubes whose
tricoloured-corner class counts are at most three, contain eight cubes
assembling a five-colour 2×2×2 cube with monochromatic faces.

The conclusion permits an adjacent target. In particular the
common-corner lemma used below proves an assembly by repeated-colour
arguments; the stronger computational observation that an opposite
target always exists is not needed or asserted as proved here.

\hypertarget{the-geometric-inputs}{%
\subsection*{1. The geometric inputs}\label{the-geometric-inputs}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{1. The geometric inputs}

An adjacent class is a directed cut C(S)=S→Sᶜ on five colours, where
\textbar S\textbar{} is two or three. An opposite variety is a
four-cycle and supplies both directions of each of its four underlying
edges.

On an opposite target, every adjacent class has two or four corners, on
distinct underlying edges. At most two adjacent classes have their
entire intersection inside two specified positions, and at most three
inside three specified positions. These are the elementary cut facts in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-1}{Argument 1}, Section 1.

Call an opposite source high on a target if it meets at least two
underlying target edges. The following two four-cycle facts were proved
independently of the numerical four-opposite theorem in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-9}{Argument 9}, Section 3:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  Any three distinct opposite varieties have a common high target.
\item
  On a fixed target, a specified two-edge intersection determines its
  distinct high source variety uniquely.
\end{enumerate}

The first follows by using the line of a neighbouring pair, or by
closing a four-cycle through two edges of a missing three-edge path when
all three pairs are nonneighbours. The explicit four-row classification
is given in that note.

Three additional elementary lemmas are used:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-61}{Argument 61}: if all eleven adjacent
  sources avoid one underlying edge, the pool has an opposite assembly;
\item
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-62}{Argument 62}: if the adjacent sources are
  all confined to one directed half of a common high target, the pool
  assembles;
\item
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-53}{Argument 53}: eleven capped adjacent
  sources containing one common directed corner already assemble,
  without using any opposite source.
\end{itemize}

\hypertarget{the-adjacent-matching-dichotomy}{%
\subsection*{2. The adjacent matching
dichotomy}\label{the-adjacent-matching-dichotomy}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{2. The adjacent matching dichotomy}

On any fixed opposite target T, the eleven adjacent sources either have
matching rank at least five, or all their intersections are contained in
four directed positions.

Indeed, a Konig cover of size at most four with three corner vertices
covers at most nine source copies through those corners, and at most one
other source through its source vertex. A cover with two corner vertices
covers at most six copies and two extras. With at most one corner
vertex, every adjacent source requires a source vertex, since it meets
at least two positions. None covers eleven sources. Thus any cover of
size at most four must consist solely of four corner vertices.

Suppose, for contradiction, that the whole pool fails. The first
additional lemma lets us assume every underlying edge occurs in the
adjacent pool. Also nine adjacent sources cannot all avoid two
underlying edges: meeting edges have only two reverse avoiding classes,
while disjoint edges have four avoiding classes in two orientations;
nine copies then yield an assembly with at most one opposite source.
This nine-avoider argument is written in
\protect\hyperlink{arg-9}{Argument 9}, Section 1.

Consequently any pair of underlying edges has at least three
adjacent-source neighbours.

\hypertarget{a-fully-supported-common-high-target}{%
\subsection*{3. A fully supported common high
target}\label{a-fully-supported-common-high-target}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{3. A fully supported common high
target}

Choose a common high target T, and first suppose the union of the three
opposite varieties covers every edge of T.

\hypertarget{adjacent-matching-rank-at-least-five}{%
\subsubsection*{Adjacent matching rank at least
five}\label{adjacent-matching-rank-at-least-five}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Adjacent matching rank at least
five}

The four underlying target edges can be matched to four distinct
adjacent sources. Every single edge has a neighbour; every pair has at
least three neighbours; and every three-edge set meets all eleven
sources, because each adjacent class uses at least two of the four
target edges. Hall proves the matching.

Choose a compatible directed position on each edge. Extend this
four-position independent set to five matched positions by an augmenting
path. The five positions still cover all four underlying edges. Their
three-position complement therefore uses three distinct underlying
edges.

The three opposite sources fill this complement. A single edge has a
neighbour by full support. Any two edges have at least two neighbours:
only the unique high variety whose intersection is the complementary
pair can avoid them. Any three edges meet all three high sources. These
are all Hall inequalities needed for three positions on distinct edges.

\hypertarget{adjacent-matching-rank-at-most-four}{%
\subsubsection*{Adjacent matching rank at most
four}\label{adjacent-matching-rank-at-most-four}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsubsection}{Adjacent matching rank at most
four}

All adjacent target intersections lie in four positions H, by Section 2.
Since every underlying edge is covered, H contains exactly one direction
on each of T's four edges. Regard H as an orientation of the square.

A directed cycle is impossible: a cut confined to its directions would
have to place all four vertices on the same side. That is incompatible
with a 2\textbar3 cut of five colours.

If the square orientation has two sources and two sinks, it is
alternating, and H is one directed half of an opposite target. The
rectangle-family lemma applies.

Every other acyclic orientation of a square has a unique source a and a
unique sink b. A cut whose target intersection is confined to H has its
outset downward closed along this orientation. Its restriction to the
four target vertices is nonempty and proper, again because a whole side
cannot contain four vertices. Such a set contains a and excludes b. Thus
every adjacent source contains the same directed corner ab, whether or
not ab is an edge of T. The common-corner eleven-source lemma applies.

Both matching ranks therefore contradict failure. This closes the fully
supported common-target case.

\hypertarget{the-unsupported-common-target}{%
\subsection*{4. The unsupported common
target}\label{the-unsupported-common-target}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{4. The unsupported common target}

If the common target is not fully supported, its three distinct high
source patterns are the three two-edge subsets of three target edges.
Indeed there cannot be only two supported edges, by uniqueness of a
two-edge high pattern. None of the sources is T, so each meets exactly
two edges, and distinctness gives all three pairs. The supported edges
form a three-edge path.

There is only one unmarked geometric form. A convenient representative
is

\begin{verbatim}
O60={13,14,23,24},
O64={02,04,23,34},
O68={01,03,14,34},
\end{verbatim}

on common target O61=\{12,14,23,34\}, whose edge12 is unsupported. Every
two-edge pattern determines its variety, so this normalization leaves no
hidden choice of opposite source.

The complete closure is \protect\hyperlink{arg-64}{Argument 64}. If the
adjacent pool supplies both directions of12, extend those two positions
to a five-corner adjacent matching; the opposite sources fill any three
positions on the other three edges. Otherwise normalize the absent
direction to12. The three source varieties themselves are used as
candidate targets. Their possible Hall obstructions reduce to seven rows
on one target and five rows on each of the other two, which are
symmetric.

Each row requires all but at most k=0 or1 adjacent sources to lie in a
stated avoiding set. Three selected rows have common avoiding set I
satisfying \textbar I\textbar≤4−(k\_1+k\_2+k\_3). The class cap makes
every choice with a positive k impossible for eleven sources. The only
zero-k intersections large enough are four-class common-corner families,
already solved by the common-corner lemma. The complete small
set-intersection certificate is displayed in that note.

Thus the unsupported target case also assembles, completing the proof
for three distinct opposite sources.

\hypertarget{arg-66}{%
\section{Argument 66: Three opposite cubes and eleven capped adjacent
cubes}\label{arg-66}}

\hypertarget{theorem-6}{%
\subsection*{Theorem}\label{theorem-6}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Theorem}

Take three opposite-repeat five-colour cubes and eleven adjacent-repeat
five-colour cubes. If no adjacent tricoloured-corner class occurs more
than three times, eight of these fourteen cubes assemble a five-colour
2×2×2 cube with monochromatic faces.

There is no restriction on the multiplicities of the three opposite
varieties. The conclusion permits either an adjacent-repeat or an
opposite-repeat target. All sources are physical cubes, and an assembly
uses eight distinct sources with compatible proper rotations.

\hypertarget{proof-by-the-opposite-multiplicities}{%
\subsection*{Proof by the opposite
multiplicities}\label{proof-by-the-opposite-multiplicities}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Proof by the opposite multiplicities}

There are three possibilities.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  \textbf{Three distinct varieties.} Apply the theorem in
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-65}{Argument 65}. Its proof uses Hall's theorem
  on a common high target. A fully supported target reduces to a
  rank-five matching, a directed rectangle, or a common-corner family.
  The unique unsupported triad is handled by displayed small
  Hall-obstruction tables and their set intersections. Each of these
  component proofs has been audited.
\item
  \textbf{One doubled variety and one different variety.} Apply
  \protect\hyperlink{arg-60}{Argument 60}. Hall's theorem on the doubled
  variety reduces failure to a five-position cover. A missing bidirected
  edge is impossible; otherwise the three missing directions form a
  path. Its monotone, one-change, and alternating orientations are all
  solved in the accompanying hand proofs, retaining both possible single
  varieties when a symmetry does not preserve the marked source.
\item
  \textbf{Three identical varieties.} The earlier elementary
  classification in \protect\hyperlink{arg-22}{Argument 22} says that a
  fourteen-cube failure containing three identical opposite cubes has
  adjacent class sizes 5,3,3. This contradicts the present adjacent
  class cap of three.
\end{enumerate}

These cases exhaust the three opposite sources. No inventory search is a
premise of the argument.

\end{document}
